This is a Life (This is Our Life)

by thetalee

Summary

With Bobby taking Athena on a cruise for their belated honeymoon, it is Hen's turn to wear the captain's hat again. Which is a very good thing, because Buck's plate is filled. He's got one kid hitting the terrible twos, and another going full throttle into teenagedom. Not to mention, as his sister's Man of Honor, he has the very serious job of helping her plan it.

Now if only Buck could figure out why he and Eddie are suddenly getting so many follow requests on their Instragrams...

Notes

Title is from "This is a Life" by Son Lux

See the end of the work for more notes

Chapter 1: Buenos Dias

There was a benefit to waking up before their alarms, both the one they set the night before and the one tucked into bed in the room next door. The benefit was this: in the peace of the early morning hours, before the chaos of their lives came to crash down upon them again, Buck got to simply watch. Watch the way Eddie breathed, watch the way his face went slack in his slumber, watch the way the sunlight that came through the gaps in their curtains played against his skin.

It was one of Buck’s favorite things to do sometimes, watching his husband. It was right up there, in fact, with watching Chris and Gavin. He didn’t get to do it very often, with the way things in his life had him in constant motion. So he took any chance he could, any moment where he wasn’t being called away to save a life or prevent a mess or solve a problem, to simply take in the view.

Eddie’s hair was a mess, sticking up everywhere thanks to Buck’s fingers running through it the night before and then from the way Eddie slept on it. Half his face was smashed into his pillow, the fabric wet from bits of drool. The bedsheet was pulled down to just above his hips, revealing golden skin that, if he wasn’t afraid of waking him up, Buck would trace shapes against and chest hair that Buck would comb his fingers through and scars that Buck would press his lips to. One of Eddie’s arms was buried under his pillow, the other stretched out and clutching at Buck’s waist.

“You’re staring at me,” Eddie complained, apparently not as deeply asleep as Buck assumed. The words came out thick and heavy, half-muffled by his pillow.

“No, I’m not,” Buck lied with a grin. He gave in to the urge to touch his husband, now that he knew he was already awake. He ran his fingers along Eddie’s cheek, his grin growing bigger at the way Eddie’s face scrunched up and how a small bit of sunlight glinted off the silver of his wedding band. “How do you know what I’m doing? Your eyes are closed.”

“I always know what you’re doing, mi sol,” Eddie told him. He opened his eyes, big and brown and so very beautiful. “And you’re always staring at me.”

“I’m not always staring at you,” Buck denied, leaving his hand where it was cupping Eddie’s face. “Sometimes my eyes are closed. Sometimes I’m staring at other people.”

“Who are these other people?” Eddie asked, squeezing at Buck’s hip. “I’ll fight ‘em.”

“Oh, yeah, my big buff husband.” Buck laughed and leaned in to kiss him, morning breath be damned. “Gonna get into some old-fashioned fisticuffs over me? Duel them for my honor?”

“You’ve watched too much Bridgerton.”

“Bridgerton has a lot of hot people to stare at.”

Eddie rolled his eyes, the hand on Buck’s waist slipping beneath the bedsheet to trail along bare skin. Buck shivered and laughed as Eddie tugged him closer, so they were pressed against each other.

“We have time,” Eddie said. The smile that unfurled across his face was full of wicked promise. “I think I can make sure you don’t look at anyone else.”

“Oh, you do, do you?” Buck brushed their noses together, his own hand making a wandering trail down Eddie’s side. “Gonna have to work hard for that.”

“So much effort.” Eddie grabbed his thigh, pulled Buck’s leg over his hip. “Are you worth it?”

“The answer to that question better be yes.”

Eddie laughed and started to roll him onto his back. And then, as if he knew exactly what they were about to start, Gavin began to shout for them, the sound audible both through the wall and the baby monitor on their dresser. Buck laughed as Eddie buried his frustrated groan in the pillow.

“This is your fault,” Buck said, pushing at him until he rolled off. “You jinxed it.”

Eddie lifted his head to scowl at him.

“How did I jinx it?”

“You said we had time.” Buck blindly felt around on the floor, grabbing the pair of sweats he wore to bed last night. “I’ll take care of the kid.”

Eddie let out another groan and dragged a pillow over his head, blindly flipping Buck off when he laughed again.

In the hallway, Buck found Hoover curled up in front of Gavin’s door. The dog lifted his head when Buck stepped out, tail thumping against the floor. Buck reached down to give him a pat before stepping over him to carefully ease the door open. A couple months before, they switched Gavin’s crib out for a toddler bed after he started climbing the railings. They hadn’t wanted to risk him making a successful jailbreak and falling on his head, and thanks to Buck’s paranoia the room was already baby proofed.

Gavin, standing just inside, let out a happy squeal and began to hop in place when Buck poked his head in.

“Hey there, little guy,” Buck said, pushing the door open wider. “Are you ready to start the day?”

“Yeah!”

Starting the day, in this case, meant wrangling the almost two-year-old into holding still long enough for Buck to change him into a dry pull-up and then into at least a shirt. The moment that was done, the moment Gavin’s feet were on the ground again, he was running off down the hall with Hoover chasing after him. Buck followed at a much more sedate pace, knocking on Chris’ door on the way past, and headed for the kitchen.

Senorita, the fluffy black cat that Eddie brought home as a kitten the summer before, twined between his legs and did her best to trip him until he bent down and scooped her up. She immediately went boneless, flopping in his arms and purring up a storm. Buck rolled his eyes as she carried her into the kitchen, where HILDY had the coffee ready and Abuela was making breakfast. 

“Buen día,” was how she greeted him. She gave the cat a narrow eyed glare. “That one knows she isn’t allowed in here while I’m cooking.”

The cat, tail flicking, gave a mrrp sound.

“I bet if it was Hoover, you wouldn’t have a problem,” Buck said as he one-handedly dug through the cabinet for his favorite coffee mug, the one he accidentally stole from the station and never returned.

“He cleans up messes,” Abuela told him, a bit haughtily. “That one just makes them.”

“Uh-huh.”

Buck placed the cat, ever so gently, onto the floor before pouring his coffee and then shaking out his daily dose. The bupropion was helping. It was helping a lot. He was no longer randomly not happy for no reason, or at least it didn’t happen as often and it wasn’t as bad when it did, and he’d even managed to get through the Leadership Academy in the fall without crashing and burning. At that point it was less of a subtle “fuck you” to the brass, as the goal had been at the start, and more simply because Buck was a stubborn little shit who wanted to prove to himself that he could.

It looked very nice and shiny on his resume.

“Hey, Gavin?” Buck called out, leaning out of the kitchen to where the tot was running circles around the dining room with Hoover. “You wanna make sure your brother’s up for me?”

Chris hit the teenage years hard, leaving him moody and grumbly and even more difficult to get up in the mornings. There was even a first date being held at their house, between Chris and a girl from school named Penny, on Saturday. Actually, Buck wasn’t certain if it was actually a date, but he also wasn’t stupid. Eddie was handling it much better than Buck thought he would.

Little feet thumped against the floor as Gavin sprinted for Chris’ door. Little hands slapped against the wood, accompanied by cries of,

“Chris! Chris!”

Buck took a seat at the dining table, happily drinking his coffee, as he watched. Was this technically child labor, using the toddler to annoy his brother into getting up in the morning? Probably, but it was also very effective. As proven when the door opened up and a bleary eyed Chris, his hair a bird’s nest, stood in the doorway to glower at the world.

“Morning Chris!” Buck said, raising his coffee in a toast. 

The teenager grumbled something in response that might have been a “good morning” or it might have been an aspersion upon his parentage. Buck couldn’t really tell, and chose to believe it was the former.

“Chris!” Gavin repeated, hopping in spot, until his older brother rolled his eyes and reached down to hug him. And then the little tot ran off giggling straight into Eddie’s legs, who picked him up.

“Don’t forget your stretches, mijo,” Eddie said on his way by.

“Yeah, yeah,” was the grunted reply.

The morning stretches were important for Chris; they loosened up his muscles, reduced the strain on his joints, and allowed him to move about much easier. The problem was that Chris needed to get up with enough time before school to get them in, and most teenagers wanted to sleep in. Chris was one of those teenagers. Sure, they could switch the stretches to the evening, but then they’d have an entirely different battle on their hands.

“You take your meds?” Eddie asked, entirely unbothered by his oldest son’s gruffness as he stepped into the dining room.

“Yup.” Buck drained the last off his coffee, getting up to set the mug on the breakfast bar. He reached out and poked Gavin in the nose. “Okay little guy, d’ya wanna help your dads get big and strong?”

Gavin’s curls bounced as he nodded.

After push-ups and sit-ups, done with Gavin clinging to their backs and shoulders like a monkey, they did their normal morning waltz in the bathroom as they got ready for the day. And then, afterwards, they pulled on their uniforms - more out of habit now than from being truly pressed for time - and sat down at the dining table for breakfast. They greeted Carla when she walked in, snickered to themselves as Chris zombied his way through eating, and made sure Gavin didn’t feed too much of his own meal to Hoover and Senorita. Then, before they could leave for the station, they obediently lingered by the front door while Abuela trailed a cross over their chests and kissed them goodbye.

“I have another one,” Buck announced as he slid into the driver’s seat of the jeep, frowning at the screen of his phone.

“You’re popular,” Eddie told him.

Over the last couple of months, Buck and Eddie both suddenly had an uptick in people requesting to follow their Instagrams. People neither of them knew nor recognized. Their social media was all private, Eddie’s since the very start and Buck’s since that kerfuffle with the catfisher, and at this point the requests were getting denied without either of them actually looking at the person who sent it.

They had pictures of Christopher, Gavin, and Jee-Yun on their pages, they weren’t about to give random strangers access to see them.

“Kinda wish I knew why, though,” Buck said as he set his phone in its holder on the dash, sending the request off to the void to wither and die with the others. “We didn’t go viral, did we? We’d know if we’d gone viral for something, right?”

At the very least, Taylor Kelly would be showing back up at their door.

They were halfway to the station when Buck’s phone began to ring. He ran his finger across the screen, letting out a cheerful,

“Morning, Pops! You’re on speaker.”

“Hey, Bobby,” Eddie said, giving the phone a small wave.

“Good morning,” Bobby said. In the background, Buck could hear wind and seagulls and people talking. “Figured I’d call you while we waited to board.”

“Fingers crossed that you both actually manage to get on without something happening, this time,” Eddie said.

“Yeah,” Buck agreed. “The Carters are both alright still, right?”

“As far as I am aware, neither Sam nor Beatrice have driven their car into their home.”

There was a muffled conversation, followed by the sound of Athena’s laughter.

“Mama and Daddy are both fine, Buckaroo,” she said, having apparently taken the phone from Bobby. “Ain’t nothin’ stopping us from getting on this boat today.”

She sounded… Well, she sounded like she was trying to be enthusiastic, but her heart wasn’t quite in it. It had Buck frowning at the road ahead of him.

“On the brightside,” Athena said, before Buck had the chance to ask if something was wrong. “Hen should do better this time. No medical school to stress her out.”

“Which means we won’t get to watch her trash talk a dog statue again,” Eddie said. “Darn.”

“Never say never,” Buck said, and his husband pointed at him with eyebrows raised.

“Try not to get into any trouble while we’re gone,” Bobby said. Either he took the phone back or his end was on speaker, too. “No speeding across the city to rescue anyone, no amateur arson investigations.”

“I promise not to do anything you wouldn’t do,” Buck said. He heard Athena let out a laugh and grinned. “Eddie will keep me on my best behavior. Right, Eds?”

“Right.”

The other end of the line fell silent. Well, mostly silent, since they could still hear the sounds of the ocean and the seagulls. Buck’s grin got bigger when he glanced at Eddie and saw the offended expression growing on his face.

“I’ll call Isabel later,” Bobby finally said. “Maybe May, if she’s not busy with school.”

“Wow,” Eddie intoned.

“We all know you’ll just follow him into whatever mess he gets himself into,” Athena said. “You don’t get to be insulted.”

“Uh, yes I do.”

“We’re almost to the station,” Buck broke in, snickering at his husband. “I’ll try not to lead Eddie into anything illegal or life-threatening before you get back.”

“That’s all we ask,” Bobby said.

They hung up just as Buck was pulling the jeep into the station’s parking lot, Eddie grumbling about being insulted the entire time. Buck just smiled and rolled his eyes. They grabbed their work bags and jogged into the station, waving to everyone as they stuffed said bags into their shared locker, and then going upstairs to find everyone else. 

Hen was sitting at one of the round tables with a laptop open in front of her, nodding along to whatever Chimney was telling her as she typed away. Ravi and Albert were at the dining table, eating breakfast they clearly picked up on their way in. Toby, the driver engineer that transferred over after Jones’ death last year, was standing by the station's coffee maker as he waited for it to finish its job.

“La capitana!” Buck belted out as he crested the top of the stairs. “Buenos dias!”

“Hello, Diazes,” Hen greeted them, not even looking up. “Bobby and Athena ship off okay?”

“They were still waiting to board when we hung up,” he said, not even batting an eye at the fact she guessed they called him. “But nothing’s happened yet, so fingers crossed.”

“Don’t jinx it,” Chimney warned, rapping his knuckles against the tabletop.

Buck didn’t have to look Eddie’s way to know he was rolling his eyes. For someone who practically broke out a thesaurus in his efforts to avoid saying the dreaded Q-word at the station, he sure did like to pretend he was above such silly things like superstitions.

“I don’t think this table is actually wood,” Buck said, poking at it with a frown.

Chimney’s brow furrowed, and then he leaned to the side to knock his fist against one of the wooden beams, instead. 

“But hey, when you last talked to Ma,” Buck said, slipping into the seat across from Hen. “She sounded like she was looking forward to the cruise, right? She was excited for the last one. I think.”

The question had Hen looking up, lips pursed in thought.

“I think so?” She shrugged. “She didn’t say anything to me about not looking forward to it, so…”

“If she didn’t want to go on the cruise,” Eddie said, leaning against his chair and laying his arm around Buck’s shoulders, “do you really think she’d go?”

“That… Is a good point.” Buck nodded and pointed at his husband. “A very good point.”

“It’s almost time for our shift to officially start,” Hen said, glancing first at the screen of her laptop and then at the watch on her wrist. “Do me a favor, one of you go round everyone up for the daily brief?”

“Si, si, capitana!” Eddie gave her a mocking salute, before jogging off to do just that.

Hen rolled her eyes, first at him and then at the way Chimney and Buck were snickering.

Chapter 2: The Red Wire

Chapter Summary

In which something about this call is very familiar.

It said something about Buck’s life that a fighter jet sticking out of someone’s living room was not actually the weirdest thing he had seen on a call. Sure, it was concerning, but like… It was just a plane, y’know? Planes crash all the time. Maybe not necessarily military planes, at least not in California, but they’d responded to far worse plane crashes before.

“Bobby sure picked the right day to leave for his honeymoon,” Hen commented after they spent a moment taking in the scene. There were twin trails of flames going across the previously pristine lawn, leading to where the fighter jet had demolished half the house. “Ravi, Toby, get that fire taken care of.”

“Got it, Cap!” Ravi said, jogging with the red-haired driver-engineer to get the hoses.

“No way anybody survived this,” Chimney claimed as they headed into the house.

“Pretty sure people have said that about the both of us,” Buck said.

“You didn’t survive,” Eddie pointed out, throwing him a smile. “Technically.”

“Not even God Himself could keep me down for long.”

“If you’re both done.” Hen didn’t sound amused at all, the debbie-downer.

The inside of the house, or at least the inside of what had once been the living room, was about as much of a mess as Buck expected it to be. There was smoke and dust in the air, and debris coating the floor. There was a woman just inside, who looked relieved when they came bursting in.

“Oh, thank God. The firefighters are here, Abe,” she said. “My husband, he’s stuck in his chair. Which is not unusual.”

The last bit was said with a touch of bitterness, but that wasn’t important right now. What was important was the way that her husband, Abe, wasn’t just stuck in his chair. He was being pinned to it by the jet’s wing. Or rather, by what looked very much like a missile on the jet’s wing.

Chimney went to look the husband over, while Al took the wife to the side with a,

“Lemme take a look at you, ma’am. What’s your name?”

“Edna.”

“Is there anyone else in the house?” Hen asked her.

“No, it’s just us,” Edna said. “We never had children.”

“And animals don’t like her,” Abe chimed in.

“Sir, try not to move your neck,” Chimney instructed.  “Can you wiggle your toes for me?”

“No,” the guy said with a groan. “And I can’t feel my legs, either. It’s kind of… It’s kinda hard to breathe.”

The four of them - Buck, Eddie, Chimney, and Hen - shared a look over the wing of the plane. That was very much not good.

“Alright, we’re gonna get you out of here,” Chimney told Abe. “Buck, Eddie?”

Buck and Eddie moved into position, and then on Buck’s count, they tried to get the chair and its occupant free. “Tried” being the operative word, because all they accomplished was some ominous creaking and Abe letting out some pained grunts. He was properly wedged beneath the wing and the missile.

“Okay, what if we chain the wreckage to the ladder truck?” Buck suggested. “Try and tow it off him?”

“No.” Hen shook her head. “This plane is holding up whatever’s left of this house.”

Buck looked up and grimaced. She was right, he realized. Pulling it out would result in a mess similar to the one Gavin made, the last time he got to one of Christopher’s Lego creations. No one had been happy that night.

“We’re gonna have to cut him out of this chair,” Chimney announced. Something that got two very conflicting responses from the married couple.

“No! Don’t cut the chair!” was Abe’s, coming out in a pained groan.

“Yes! Please cut the chair!” Edna sounded far more excited. “He loves that chair more than he loves me.”

“For once, I’m not arguing with her.”

It was probably not the proper time to suggest the couple go whale watching, which was much cheaper than a divorce, but Buck still thought it. He grinned at Eddie, who laughed and shook his head, because he thought it, too.

“Al?” Hen gave him a look; the paramedic nodded.

“Ma’am, come with me outside?” he asked Edna. She looked at him for a moment, before nodding and letting herself be escorted out of the house.

The sound of Eddie coughing and gagging tore at Buck’s attention. He was kneeling by the plane’s fuselage, face turned away from a fast and heavy leakage.

“I think this is hydrazine,” Eddie announced.

Buck crept over and took a sniff over his shoulder. He immediately reeled back from the ammonia-like odor that hit him.

“Which means if we start cutting this chair, I worry about sparks,” Hen said. Considering hydrazine was used for rocket fuel, it was a fair worry. Buck didn’t know a lot about it, or about the rockets and planes that used it, but he knew that containers of it got slapped with a lot of scary warning labels. “We just need to clear a space so that we can slide him out.”

She crouched down next to Eddie, the light on her helmet fixed on the ominously missile-shaped object pinning Abe to his chair.

“What do we think this is?” she asked.

Eddie didn’t even bother giving her a bitchy look for the stupid question, simply saying,

“Exactly what you think it is.”

Specifically, it was a dummy bomb. According to the pilot of the fighter jet, who’d ejected when the systems on said jet started failing and ended up stranded somewhere, it wasn’t enough to take out the whole neighborhood. Just the house they were in, and maybe also the one next door. The dummy bomb could be ejected, and then safely moved off their patient, it just needed to be defused first.

“So let me get this straight,” Buck said once Hen, still on the line with both Joe the pilot and Maddie at dispatch, caught them up to speed. “We have a bomb that’s keeping us from taking our patient to the hospital, and because the military isn’t going to get here before our patient runs out of time, we’re having to remove it ourselves. While hoping it doesn’t explode in our faces.”

“That’s right,” Hen confirmed with a nod.

Buck reached over and slapped Eddie on the arm.

“Babe! And it’s not even our anniversary.”

“Hopefully this time doesn’t cost us another ambulance,” Eddie said. “I don’t think the city appreciates how many municipal vehicles we go through.”

“Both of you shut up.” Hen rolled her eyes, because she just did not appreciate good humor. “Chimney, you’re on our patient. You two chuckleheads, go get what we need to play bomb squad.”

That wasn’t actually that tall of an order. There was an assortment of hand tools stored on the trucks for when they had to disassemble something to get to a patient. Really, they just grabbed a tool kit and hurried back inside.

“Okay, Joe,” Eddie said, power drill in hand. He and Buck were crouched in front of the dummy bomb. “We’re ready.”

“Okay, first carefully remove the trigger housing from the nose.”

That was a metal plate attached in place with screws. The drill whirred as Eddie worked, Buck giving regular squirts of saline to reduce friction. The last thing any of them wanted was an errant spark to light up the hydrazine. The removal of the plate revealed a mess of colorful wires and cords.

“Trigger cap is removed,” Eddie reported, putting down the drill. ”What now?”

“Okay, go ahead and snip that red wire.”

Eddie took out the wire cutters and went to do exactly that.

“But first-” They all froze, Buck not even daring to breathe. He saw Eddie’s eyes go wide, because he was literally a second away from cutting the wire. “Disconnect the white low voltage coupling.”

Eddie let out an annoyed huff, switching the wire cutters for a pair of pliers. On the other side of the missile, Buck could hear Chimney and Abe talking. Judging from the way Abe talked, his life with his wife was not one of happy matrimony.

The white low voltage coupling was, apparently, a white cord on the far end of the mess of wires. Eddie, at least, knew what it was even if Buck didn’t, which was why he was the one doing this. He used the pliers to pull the coupling loose, letting it dangle down the side of the missile.

“Voltage coupling disconnected.”

“Okay, go ahead and cut that green wire.”

What?

“What?” Buck blurted out. “Green? You- You said red.”

“Did I?”

“Yeah.” Eddie was using that tone of his, the one that made an appearance whenever he was getting irritated but was trying to keep a lid on it. Sometimes, on days that he felt like being a brat, Buck liked to poke at his husband until that lid blew off. The consequences were always fun. “You said red.”

“Are you sure?”

“Yes! Yes!” That was Maddie, also still on the line with them. “You said red!”

“Okay, uh. Let’s go with red.”

What in the everloving-

“What the hell is wrong with this guy?” Buck hissed at Hen.

“He fell ten-thousand feet,” she said. “It’s possible he’s not thinking that clearly.”

“Hey, guys!?” Chimney spoke up from the other side. “We need to get this thing off of him. Like, now.”

“What are we gonna do here, Cap?” Eddie asked.

Hen closed her eyes and took a breath. And then her eyes popped open when something on the plane burst with a crackle and started to hiss.

“Cut the red one,” she said, voice tight and tense. “His disorientation is just now presenting, let’s go with his first instinct. Cut the red one.”

Eddie looked at Buck; Buck shrugged at him. It was always the red wire in those movies, wasn’t it? And besides, if it was the wrong wire, they wouldn’t have long to regret it. The boys would be taken care of, Abuela and Pepa both knew where their wills and the list were.

“Red one it is,” Eddie said, reaching back in with the wire cutter.

Buck held his breath, eyes focused on the red wire that may or not may blow them all up. And then once again, just as Eddie was about to cut it-

“Guys!” Chimney shouted, startling them. He poked his head around the tail end of the dummy bomb. “It’s a recliner.”

“Chingada madre,” Eddie swore, throwing his head back.

“Second that,” Buck said. And then he squirted the saline right into Chimney’s face.

Since it was a recliner, Chimney just pulled the lever and the guy dropped back and down a bit. Which meant they were free to pull him out from under the missile that wasn’t actually pinning him in place anymore. They carried him in the chair to the ambulance, hefting it into the back while Hen talked to the military guy that finally showed up.

“Abe?” That was Edna calling for her husband, and she was coming closer.

“Do me a favor,” Abe said, pulling off his oxygen mask. “Tell her I died.”

Edna rounded the corner, an expression of relief falling over her face.

“Oh, thank God!” she said. “It’s a miracle.”

“It was touch-and-go there for a minute,” Chimney told her. “But I think he’s going to be just fine.”

“What? No.” Edna shook her head as she looked at Chimney as if he were stupid. “I meant it’s a miracle that hideous chair is finally out of my house.”

She stomped off again, mumbling under her breath, leaving a bewildered Chimney behind.

“Hey, uh.” Buck bent down so he could talk to Abe. “Have you ever considered taking your wife whale-watching?”

Eddie smacked him.

“Do you two have any plans for the weekend?” Albert asked, voice a bit muffled by the shirt he was pulling over his head. “Ravi and I are gonna go go-karting in the desert.”

It was the end of the shift and everyone was more than ready to head home, changing out of their uniforms and into their civvies.

“Oh! Go-karting sounds fun!” Buck said, perking up from where he was sitting down and tying his shoes. “Babe, next date night?”

“Sure, mi sol.” Eddie was only half-dressed, chest still bare, and Buck felt it well within his rights to leer. “Next date night. ‘Buela won’t mind watching the kids.”

“So that’s a no to this weekend, then?” Ravi asked, leaning against the glass wall of the locker room.

“This weekend is Christopher’s date night,” Buck threw a grin at his husband. “Our baby boy’s growing up.”

“Chris invited a girl from his math class over on Saturday,” Eddie explained, finally tugging on a shirt. He rolled his eyes at Buck’s exaggerated pout. “Her mom’s bringing her over. There’ll be video games and dinner.”

“Aw, that’s cute,” Albert said. “I remember when I first asked a girl out. I was so nervous, I stammered my way through asking her. I had to repeat myself twice.”

“As opposed to your first time asking a boy out, where you just stuck your tongue in his mouth in the storage closet,” Buck said, smirking at the pair of them.

“It was my tongue in his mouth,” Ravi corrected. “But yes.”

“Well, if how he does at the school dances is anything to judge by, I think Chris’ll be fine,” Buck told them. “Kid has game. He gets it from me, obviously.”

Eddie rolled his eyes and then reached over the locker room bench to smack him.

“I’m the one that asked you out, remember?”

“Babe.” Buck reached up, took Eddie’s hand in his own, and looked him in the eye. “I was almost fellating a beer bottle in front of you, and it still took you over a month to figure it out.”

Eddie huffed and rolled his eyes again, using the grip Buck had on his hand to pull him to his feet.

“C’mon,” he said, handing Buck his work bag, which Buck shrugged onto his shoulder. “Let’s get going.”

“Fine, fine, Mr. Grump.” Buck looked over at Albert and Ravi, the pair of them snickering. “Tell me how the go-karting goes, if the place sucks or whatever.”

“Tell us how the date goes,” Ravi called after them as they headed out of the locker room.

“I’ll send you pictures of how nice he dresses up!” Buck threw over his shoulder. “We can use ‘em to embarrass the kid later!”

“He specifically made us promise not to embarrass him,” Eddie pointed out, still holding Buck’s hand as they walked through the truck bay and into the parking lot. “I’m pretty sure making him pose for pictures counts.”

“I had my fingers crossed the entire time,” Buck said, waving away the concern. “‘Sides, I promised not to embarrass him too much. There’s no way he’s getting out of this without a little embarrassment, he knows that. He knows who his dads are.”

“That’s true,” Eddie agreed, nodding. “You give me secondhand embarrassment all the time.”

Buck glowered at him. Eddie just smiled prettily.

Chapter 3: Jell-O

Chapter Summary

In which Christopher has more in common with one of his fathers than he does the other, to everyone's despair.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“So how is the first date going?” Maddie asked, sounding like she was about to laugh.

Buck leaned back in his chair to peer into the living room. He could easily see Christopher and Penny, the girl from his math class, sitting on the couch and focused on the television as they committed heinous violence upon pixels. There were a pair of plates with the remnants of dinner sitting on the coffee table, along with soft drinks and a bag of chips.

“It looks like it’s going good,” Buck answered. He had his phone on speaker as it sat next to his laptop on the dining table, not that it mattered; both teenagers were too focused on the game and each other to even realize he existed right now. “Gavin played wingman for a bit, before we put him down for bed.”

“Women can’t resist a guy with a baby.”

“Tell me about it, walking Hoover goes five times faster if I do it without Gavin. And it’s not just women,” Buck said with a laugh. “Hey, uh, has everyone RSVP’d yet?”

There was silence for a moment on the other end of the line, broken by a tired sigh.

“Yes, Buck,” Maddie said. “Why?”

“Are you sure?” he asked, turning back to his laptop. The screen showed multiple spreadsheets open in separate tabs. “Because some of the guests still don’t have a yes or no on the-”

“Oh my God.” Maddie let out a groan. “Yes, Buck, everyone who is coming has said they’ll come.”

“Are you sure?” he asked again. “Maybe I should leave a table empty, just in case…”

“You know,” his sister said, “I have heard all the horror stories about Clipboard Buck. I have seen Clipboard Buck in action. But I didn’t realize that it extended to Google spreadsheets and wedding planning.”

“Hey, you asked for my-”

“The hell I did,” Maddie interrupted. “You inflicted your assistance upon me, though I have no clue why. You barely even planned your own wedding, Athena and them did it for you. Not that there was much to plan.”

“I didn’t hear you complaining about my help when you were trying to decide between venues,” Buck said. “Which, I still don’t know why you can’t just have it in Pops’ and Ma’s backyard, too.”

“Because it’s my wedding.”

Buck rolled his eyes at her tone.

The backdoor opened and Hoover came trotting in from doing his business. He stopped by Buck for his tribute of headpats and scratches, and then he went down the hall to curl up in front of Gavin’s door. Eddie followed him inside a few moments later, taking a seat beside Buck at the table. Immediately, Senorita jumped up to paw at him for attention. She apparently had her claws out as she did so, because Eddie let out a yelp before gathering her up in his arms.

“Senorita Dolores Jeronima Diaz,” he tutted. It would probably have more of an effect if he wasn’t rocking the purring ball of fur as he did. “We do not show our love by hurting each other.”

“Not without a safe word,” Buck muttered under his breath and earned himself a smirk from Eddie.

“Did he just full name the cat?” Maddie asked. “Is that the cat’s full name?”

“I had to make up for not getting to name the kid or the dog,” Eddie said, defending himself. “Oh, I see Buck has the spreadsheets out again.”

“The spreadsheets are spreading,” Maddie complained. “We used one to plan our dates for the next two months.”

“Uh.” Buck shared a look with Eddie. “What?”

“He came home worried about us calcifying. Because of the couple with the fighter jet in their living room.”

“Oh, them. I just suggested that they go whale watching.”

“...Why?”

“Because going out into the ocean on a boat is cheaper than getting a divorce,” Eddie explained, though Buck knew it didn’t really clarify their in-joke at all. “Wait, Chimney came home worried about the future after a conversation with an old guy? That’s more of a Buck thing to do.”

“Hey!”

“You know it’s true, mi sol.”

“Doesn’t mean I can’t still be offended,” Buck complained. “So, hold on. Why do your dates require a spreadsheet and two months prep?”

“We’re forever dating. It’s supposed to be dating every day, but I managed to haggle it down to three dates a week. He wanted five, but I figured that was pushing it with Jee.”

“Three dates a week,” Eddie repeated.

“Obviously they don’t all have to be fancy or involved, they can be simple. The point is that you don’t want to take your partner for granted, keep things from… Calcifying.”

“Uh-huh.”

“Do you feel like I take you for granted, babe?” Buck asked, opening up a new tab on his laptop.

“Every damn day,” Eddie answered, and then, “Ay, maldita sea.”

“What?”

“He’s pulled up a fresh spreadsheet. I blame you for this.”

Buck grinned at him.

“Hey, I can’t let Chimney out romance me,” he said. “We can do four dates a week, right?”

“Sure,” Eddie said, nodding. Senorita was still happily curled up and purring in his arms like a jet engine. “And the first one we go on can be whale watching, because the very idea of this sounds exhausting.”

“I know,” Maddie whined. “But he’s worried about us growing old and falling out of love, and it’s very sweet.”

“See, my doctor put me on medication for that,” Buck said. “And we’re all happier for it.”

“Pretty sure that wasn’t the main reason.”

“You’re prettier when you don’t talk.”

“Aw, muchas gracias, mi sol. So are you.”

“I’m going to hang up now,” Maddie cut in. “Before I have to listen to any more of this… Is this flirting? Foreplay? Whichever it is, I don’t want to listen to it.”

“Your jealousy is noted,” Buck said. “Have fun with your forever dating.”

“Oh, I’ll try.”

The screen lit up with her face as the call ended. He spotted the time at the top and got to his feet to pad over to the living room.

“Alright, guys,” he said, bursting the teenagers’ little bubble. “Penny, it’s almost nine. Your mom should be here soon.”

“Okay, Mr. Diaz!”

Buck decided he liked Penny, especially when she didn’t complain after he asked the two of them to carry their dishes and such to the kitchen. Her mother arrived not long after and though there wasn’t a kiss goodbye, which might have been because of all the parental units watching or it could have been because they just weren’t there yet, she and Chris did hug it out while promising to talk to each other later.

It was all very cute, though none of them said it aloud.

“I told you,” Buck said, rinsing off the plates before sticking them into the washing machine to be run later. “The kid’s got game.”

“It wasn’t all that different from when he hangs out with his guy friends, to be honest,” Eddie said.

“Babe.” Buck fixed him with a look, and then gestured between them. “Really?”

“Point taken.”

The sound of giggling from down the hall drew both of their attention. Two people giggling, actually, Chris and a girl. Presumably on the phone, because Buck really, really hoped their kid wasn’t already at the stage of sneaking people into his bedroom.

“Seriously? She just left.” Eddie chuckled and shook his head. “And he’s supposed to be brushing his teeth. I’ll go take care of it.”

As Eddie left the kitchen and went down the hall, Buck busied himself with finishing cleaning up. Then he opened the fridge, fully intending to go through and add things to their grocery list. Except there were suddenly raised voices from down the hall, Eddie’s and Chris’, followed by a door slamming shut.

“Wasn’t happy that you made him stop talking to Penny?” Buck asked. Though, when he turned around, the expression on his husband’s face told him something was up. “Eds? What is it?”

Eddie tossed Chris’ phone onto the counter. Buck frowned at it, and then at his husband.

“He wasn’t talking to Penny,” Eddie said. He unlocked the phone, and then started snooping through. “It was some girl that got really upset that he was two-timing her.”

“What.”

“My baby boy was- Jesús, María y José!” Eddie was staring at the phone, eyes wide.

“What?” Buck asked, both curious and also fearful of the answer. This was a teenager’s phone. A teenager who was talking to girls. There were so many horrible things that could be causing his husband to react like that. “What is it?”

“There are conversations with, I dunno, I think five different girls on here.”

“Oh my God, our son’s a player.” Buck craned his neck to see for himself. “I told you he had game.”

“Buck!”

“I’m sorry! I’m just relieved that that’s all it is.”

“That’s all it is!?”

“Babe, you started looking through our teen son’s phone and then looked horrified,” Buck told him. “We should both be thankful that all you found was evidence he’s into polygamy.”

Eddie’s face scrunched up in confusion, and then he looked very, very ill as the meaning registered.

“That is-” He shook his head, flipped the phone screen down in disgust. “Where did this come from? It’s not from me, I married the first girl I dated.”

“You mean the first girl you slept with,” Buck pointed out. “And then you married the first guy you slept with. It’s a bit of a theme with you.”

“The point is,” Eddie said, scowling at him. “I’m a nester. I nest.”

“Chris might be a bit too young to nest.”

“He’s still in the nest!”

Buck rolled his eyes, and then poked at Chris’ phone like it was some venomous creature. He was a bit curious about the conversations his husband had glimpsed, but he also didn’t want to go snooping too much. Not because he respected their son’s privacy, though he did, but because he was legitimately terrified now of what he might find if he did.

“So,” Eddie said. “What are you gonna say to him?”

“What am I gonna say to him?”

“I nest!” Eddie repeated. “I don’t know what the hell to do here.”

“And you think I do?”

“Better than me!”

Buck threw his hands up and rolled his eyes.

“Fine!” he said. “We work tomorrow, I’ll talk to him after school on Monday.”

“Thank you, mi sol.”

“Oh, shove it.”

Sex-related calls were among the worst, in Eddie’s opinion. Dignity was already a victim in most emergencies, but it was absolutely murdered by these. For the people they were helping, and also for the first responders themselves.

For this particular sex-related call, they were at a fancy looking mansion for a reported heart attack. They found their victim- Er, victims in the backyard, in a jacuzzi on the other side of a pool. The caller was still straddling her boyfriend, because she was… Stuck.

“Help!” the woman called out while they rushed for her.

Chimney knelt and felt at the unconscious man’s neck.

“He’s alive,” he announced.

Eddie dug out his stethoscope, because with Hen wearing the captain’s hat they were down a paramedic. And Albert had been out on another medical call with Ravi.

“This is so embarrassing,” the woman said, not meeting any of their eyes.

“It could be worse,” Buck blurted out.

“How!? I am stuck on his- I’m stuck on him.”

“We responded to a call last summer,” he said. Eddie glanced up at him and grimaced, because he knew exactly what story his husband was about to tell her. “A couple was stuck like you are, except it was because… Uh, well. A certain type of body jewelry he was wearing got um. Caught. In her.”

The woman’s face twisted in horror.

“Okay, yeah,” she breathed. “Could be worse.”

“Perspective is everything,” Chimney remarked, pulling the unconscious guy’s arm out of the jacuzzi so it was splayed along the edge and started taking his blood pressure.

“Does your friend have a heart condition?” Hen asked, bent over at the waist.

Eddie reached up and blindly smacked Buck when the man let out a snort at the use of the word “friend.” He took off the stethoscope and let it dangle around his neck, and then he gripped the guy’s arms to keep him from slipping down into the water.

“Not that I know of,” the woman said.

“Is he on any medication?”

“Rogaine. I think.”

“Okay,” Hen said, straightening up. “Buck, go be useful and check the bathroom.”

“Copy,” Buck said, bounding off in the exact same way as Hoover.

“I don’t even know how this happened,” the woman told them. “I- I mean, I know how it happened. We were, you know, doing it, and then he got woozy and started complaining about chest pains and he went limp.” She grimaced. “Mostly limp. Every time I try to get free, I can’t. What is happening?”

“Vaginismus resulting in penis captivus,” Hen answered.

“Vaga-what now?”

“Vaginismus,” Hen repeated. “It’s the involuntary tensing of the- of the muscles surrounding the vagina. It’s sometimes a stress response.”

“Okay!” Buck called out, returning from his seeking trip. He was holding up medication in his hands. “He has a heart condition, and male potency pills.”

“Alright, so I don’t think he had a heart attack. Probably just severe hypotension from mixing the nitro with Sildenafill,” Hen concluded. “What we need to do is get him out of this hot water.”

“Well, what about me?” the woman demanded. “I- I mean, I’ll go to the hospital with him, but please, not like this.”

A beat of silence, where they all looked at each other expectantly.

“Maybe give her something to help her relax?” Chimney suggested.

Eddie let out a sigh and then glanced at Buck over his shoulder.

“Switch with me?”

Buck frowned in question, but he knelt down and took the guy’s weight from Eddie. Eddie, in turn, scooted to the side a bit and leaned closer to the unfortunately stuck woman.

“Hey, what’s your name again?” he asked her.

“Uh, Sofia.”

“Hi, Sofia, I’m Eddie. Do you like Jell-O?”

That earned him a trio of curious stares from the others.

“Um.” Sofia was clearly caught off guard. “I mean, it’s fine?”

“What’s your favorite flavor?” he asked her.

(“Buck,” Chimney whispered, leaning in. “Your husband is being weird.”

“Shut up and let him work,” Buck hissed back.)

“I don’t know. Lime?”

“Alright. So, a while back, I used to have panic attacks,” Eddie explained. Buck, perhaps instinctively, shifted closer to him. “And one way I learned to handle them was a game called “Jell-O Body.” So I want you to close your eyes for me.”

Sofia looked rather dubious with the instructions, but she did as he asked.

“Okay, now I want you to think about your feet. Feel them turning into lime Jell-O,” he instructed. “Feel that?”

“Uh-huh.”

“Good! Let that Jell-O go up to your calves,” he said. “Your thighs… Your pelvis… Your stomach. Your whole body is turning into squishy, bouncy Jell-O. Now let it run up to your chest… Your arms… Your head. You are one giant Sofia Jell-O mold.”

Nothing happened at first, and then Sofia’s eyes snapped open with a gasp. She scrambled backwards, off the man’s lap. Buck and Eddie worked together to drag the guy out of the water.

“You know,” Buck said, once they got their patient onto a gurney and were wheeling him out to the ambulance. They left Hen and Chimney behind, to tend to Sofia. “I don’t think I have ever seen a man turn a woman off with such skill.”

“It’s a gift,” Eddie said.

Buck let out a snort.

“Well, RIP her, but I’m different,” he said with a grin. “You could never turn me off by talking to me, babe. But if it was you, apparently all anyone would have to do is call you daddy.”

“I just don’t get why a guy would be turned on by their partner calling them the same thing their kids do!”

“Yeah, yeah, whatever.”

In which Chris has a talk with his pops.

“Oh!” Eddie waved his own phone. “I got two.”

“You’re Mr. Popular.” Buck knew he meant two new follower requests to his Instagram. Both of which undoubtedly got rejected the moment Eddie saw them. “C”mon, let’s get out of here so you can give me my encouragement for the conversation with Chris, later. And distract me from whatever’s going on with Ma and Pops.”

“Si, mi sol.”

Buck was very much not looking forward to this conversation. In the years since he and Eddie got their shit together, in the years that Buck gradually moved from “best friend” to “dad’s boyfriend” to “Pops,” he’d been forced to have several conversations that he’d dreaded going into. Like the one day he and Eddie sat Christopher down and fumbled their way through a conversation about the birds and the bees, which had all three of them red faced and embarrassed. And clearly, unfortunately, they’d failed to cover some very important things at the time.

Chris was sitting on his bed, going through what looked like his biology textbook, and he looked up when Buck knocked against the doorframe. The kid’s expression flattened and he let out a sigh.

“Dad sent you to talk to me?”

“Yup,” Buck said, popping the “p” and resisting the urge to look over his shoulder. Eddie was lurking just out of sight, because while he didn’t want to have the conversation himself, he was perfectly willing to eavesdrop on it. “Can I come in?”

“If I say no, will you go away?”

“Nope.”

Another sigh.

“Fine.”

Buck stepped fully into the room, crossing the space until he could take a seat on the bed just by Chris’ feet. One of the picture frames on the bedside table was facedown, something he took a moment to frown at before focusing back on the task at hand.

“Dad isn’t… He’s not like us,” Buck said, struggling to find the words. He didn’t really understand it himself, not truly. But his understanding of it was not a requirement for it to be real. “Y’know, before me, there was only your mom. So he doesn’t really get it enough to talk about things like this.”

(“Of course I think she’s pretty,” Eddie said, taking a sip of his drink as he answered the question about the woman in the movie they were watching. Buck was briefly distracted by the way his throat bobbed. “So’s the ocean. I don’t want to have sex with it, either.”

“Okay, but what about him?” Buck asked, gesturing with his own bottle at the swaggering cowboy that the camera just cut to. “He’s hot, right?”

“I have a Stetson in the closet.” Eddie grinned at the way he flushed. “And I don’t know. I guess? Kinda reminds me of one of those marble statues.”

“But you don’t…?”

“Nah. Neither of ‘em are doin’ it for me.”)

“Uh-huh,” Chris mumbled, head once more buried in the textbook as he worked through problems on a worksheet.

“So, Penny. She’s- She’s someone that you like. Right?”

“She’s nice.”

“Yeah, I thought so, too,” Buck said, nodding a little. “But she isn’t… She isn’t the only girl that you’re talking to.”

“I like to keep things loose.”

“Okay, and I- I get that.” Boy howdy did he get that. Hadn’t that pretty much been his modus operandi before Bobby fired and then un-fired him, back during his probational year? “And it’s- It’s okay, you know, to like more than one girl. To like more than one person. But uh… But it’s less okay to let each one of them think they’re special, though. You know, it- it’s kinda like lying.”

“It’s not a lie,” Chris said, like Buck was being obtuse. “They’re all special.”

“Okay, yeah, I get that, but I think… I think we’re talking about something else, maybe?” he said, fumbling and clutching. “When you tell someone that they’re special, it makes them think they’re the only one. And then when they find out you’ve been saying that to a bunch of other people, it makes them feel not-so-special anymore.”

Christopher looked up from his homework, but he didn’t say anything.

“And then,” Buck continued. “They talk and you get a reputation for being a not-so-nice guy. And you want these ladies, and any future ladies, you want them to think you’re a nice guy. Right?”

“Does it matter?” Chris asked.

Buck was momentarily thrown by the question, left to gape stupidly at his son.

“Uh, what? What do you mean, does it matter?

“They just end up leaving anyway,” he said in the same tone that he’d maybe use to report the weather and recite the alphabet.

“What?” Buck repeated dumbly. “Why would you say that?”

“Because Mom did.”

Oh. Oh God.

“Chris,” Buck said, his tone as gentle as the tone he placed on the boy’s leg. “Your mom died.”

“Before that,” Chris said, looking at him for the first time this entire conversation. “We loved her, and she left anyway. I can’t remember her voice anymore.”

This time, Buck couldn’t keep himself from glancing in the direction of the open door and the hallway beyond. Eddie was right there, he knew. Eddie was right there and hearing this, too. What the hell was Buck supposed to say to this?

“Yeah,” Buck agreed, the word coming out as a sigh. “She did.”

Chris went back to his homework and they both fell silent, but Buck didn’t get up to leave. Because that was awful and it was horrible, and it was definitely not something he was going to touch without Eddie right there with him. Right there and in the room, not just lurking out in the hall to eavesdrop. But it also didn’t change the fact that the conversation, as deep and heavy as it had gotten, still wasn’t over.

However much the both of them might like to let it drop and die.

“When Dad interrupted you the other night,” Buck said. “He said the girl you were talking to didn’t sound very happy that you’d been talking to Penny.”

“I guess.” Chris shrugged. “She ignored me at school.”

Buck couldn’t blame her any.

“Did you… You’ve been talking to all these girls at the same time. Did you talk to any of them about it, first?” he asked. “Did you ask them if they were okay with it?”

The boy didn’t answer, he just shrugged again.

“What I said before,” Buck said. “About telling these girls they’re special and then them finding out that you were saying that to other girls? How it makes them feel not-so-special? How they’ll talk and you’ll get a reputation as a not-so-nice guy?”

“What does it matter?” Chris asked again.

“It matters because it’s cruel, Chris. It matters because you’re being cruel to them,” Buck told him, Chris’ shoulders rising up around his ears as he spoke. “It matters because Dad and I taught you better than to be cruel to people. It matters because we both know you’re better than that and we’re disappointed that you’re not acting it.”

 The words were coming out of his mouth, but they sounded so very much to him like something Bobby or Maddie would say.

Chris didn’t say anything in response. His gaze was focused on his worksheet, though he was blinking his eyes rapidly and squeezing his pencil in a death grip. Buck let out a sigh and leaned forward, pressing a kiss into his curls.

“I love you,” Buck reminded him, before pushing off the bed and leaving the room.

He closed the door behind him and let his shoulders drop. Sitting on the floor, his head resting against the wall, was Eddie. The man had his knees drawn up to his chest and he looked miserable.

“He can’t remember her voice anymore,” Eddie whispered. “He thinks everyone will just leave him anyway because she did.”

“Yeah.” 

God, did Chris think he was going to leave? That was something to spiral over later, when Eddie wasn’t in the middle of his own. Instead, he held out his hand and waited until his husband took it, using his grip to help the man to his feet.

“Thank you,” Eddie said, and Buck knew which action he was expressing gratitude towards.

“What do you want to do?” he asked. “Watch a telenovela? Curl up in bed? Cuddle with Gavin?”

“Curl up on the couch,” his husband answered. “Watch a telenovela while we cuddle with Gavin.”

“Sounds perfect.” 

Buck led him through the dining room and to the couch. Abuela, sitting in her chair, frowned at them in concern. Buck shook his head at her. When Eddie sat down, Senorita immediately dropped down from the back of the couch to sprawl over his lap and began purring the moment he started petting her.

“Load up an episode,” he ordered. “I’ll get the kid.”

“Thank you, mi sol.”

There was too much stuff being kept in their closet, Eddie thought. They’d downsized a lot when they moved houses, but they clearly hadn’t gotten rid of enough. Then again, it wasn’t like there was often a need for either of them to dig into the back like this.

After a couple episodes of distracting drama, with Senorita and Gavin competing for space in his lap and Buck pressed into his side, Eddie came to the realization of what he needed to do. Or at least, the best thing he could do. Now he was looking for their safe, fireproof and waterproof. It had been Buck’s originally, coming with him from the loft when he moved in with them. It was where they kept all their important documents that they didn’t want to lose in a fire. Their wills, the just-in-case list, birth certificates and social security cards, the info for Gavin’s adoption, insurance policies.

“There you are,” Eddie grumbled aloud, tugging the safe out. It wasn’t that big, just large enough for the aforementioned documents, but it was sturdy and hefty.

Getting into the safe didn’t take much effort beyond putting in the code - Maddie’s birthday, the same as it had been since Buck first set it - and turning the knob. Inside were neatly stacked papers and certificates and cards. He ignored all of them in favor of the light blue envelope in the far back.

He really should have done this years ago, Eddie knew. The letter from Shannon wasn't even meant for him, it wasn’t his to keep. But he could just never quite bear to part with it, to give away the last words he had from his first love. Even if he couldn’t quite bring himself to read those words again after the first time, alone on the beach where, for only a moment, they’d been a family again, and allowing himself only a small window of time to mourn.

Chris’ door was wide open, with Chris himself sitting at his desk working through homework. Eddie took a moment just to watch him, just to look at him, just to take in how much his baby boy had grown, before announcing his presence.

“Hey, buddy. How’s the homework?”

“Almost done,” Chris said.

Eddie nodded and then walked in, rounding Chris and the desk to sit at the foot of the bed. He happened to glance at the small table next to it and saw that Chris’ picture of himself and his mother at the beach was facedown.

“When your mom…” Eddie started, paused, wet his lips. Chris looked up from his homework to frown at him. “After your mom left, she wrote a letter. To you. I should have given it to you years ago, but I couldn’t…”

He trailed off with a sigh and held the blue envelope with the letter inside out to Chris. Chris took it from him, but he didn’t open it right away. Instead he set it down on the desk, off to the side, and asked,

“What’s for dinner?”

“Spaghetti,” Eddie answered after a beat. “Pops is making the sauce. I think there’s garlic bread, too.”

“Cool.”

Eddie waited another moment, and then let out a sigh. He got off the bed, bent to place a kiss atop Chris’ head, and left the room. The distance from there to the dining room is only a couple steps. On his right, in the living room, he could see Abuela sitting in her chair with Hoover at her feet, Gavin treating the patient pooch like a personal jungle gym.

He went left, heading into the kitchen. Buck was puttering about in front of the stove, an apron over one of the ridiculous I Am Kenough hoodies he got for Christmas. There were three, because apparently the others hadn’t bothered to coordinate their gifts. 

The moment Eddie got close enough, Buck raised the spoon he was using to stir the sauce pan, holding his hand under it to keep from dripping spaghetti sauce on the floor. Eddie obediently opened his mouth and took a taste.

“It’s good,” he said, and got an eye roll for the effort.

“Why do I keep asking you?” Buck grumbled, taking a taste for himself and frowning. “It’s always ‘it’s good,’ and not anything helpful like what it’s missing.”

“I’m so terribly sorry that I love everything you cook for me.”

“You ought to be.” Buck pawed through the spice rack, choosing them to shake into the pan at seemingly random. “How did it go?”

Eddie leaned against the counter with a sigh, hooking his chin over Buck’s shoulder.

“Chris barely even looked at it,” he said. “Took it, set it down, moved on.”

“He was probably just waiting for you to go away before reading it,” Buck said. “You two are a lot alike.”

“I should have given it to him years ago. Maybe this wouldn’t have happened if I had.”

“Maybe. I understand why you didn’t, though.”

“Still, I-”

Buck’s phone was sitting on the counter beside the stove, forgotten until then. It began to ring and they both turned their heads to stare at it. On the screen, in plain letters, was a single name: Phillip.

“Um,” Buck said.

“Um,” Eddie concurred.

It had been almost exactly two years since the Buckley parents last cast a shadow over their lives. Buck had efficiently and effectively cut them out of his life for good, as had Maddie, and the Buckleys had eventually given up. Not that there’d been much of an effort to start with.

Eventually the ringing stopped. They eyed the phone for a bit longer, waiting to see if Phillip would try again. A voicemail notification popped up instead. Buck swiped his finger over the screen to get rid of it.

“I vote we just pretend that didn’t happen,” Eddie suggested.

“Excellent idea, babe.”

Chapter End Notes

Have a look at Buck's contacts list.

Chapter 5: Error of Judgment

Chapter Summary

In which Captain Wilson may or may not have made a bad call.

Buck hesitated outside the door, spending a moment to psyche himself up, before knocking on the door. He didn’t have to wait long for the grumpily shouted response of,

“I’m awake!”

“I know, bud,” he replied, snickering to himself a little. “Could I… Could I come in, really quick?”

“Yeah, sure.”

He pushed open the door, poking his head in first before entering the rest of the way. Chris was sitting on his bed as he finished getting ready for school, pulling on a sock with a look of concentration. Buck himself was wearing his uniform, and was just waiting for Eddie before they went in for work.

Feeling awkward, Buck went over and sat beside his kid. Chris, sock fully on now, frowned at him.

“What?” Chris asked, sounding hesitant.

“I just… I wanted to talk to you about something,” Buck said. He’d noticed the picture on the night, one of Shannon with a younger Chris, was sitting up once again. “About- About what we talked about yesterday.”

Chris rolled his eyes, putting his whole head into it, and then grabbed his other sock to put on.

“Do we have to?”

“Yeah, we do. Because- Because you said…” Buck wetted his lips, not really sure how to bring this up but desperately needing to. “Your mom, you said- you said that she loved you and that she left anyway.”

Chris ducked his head, focusing on his sock.

“Yeah,” he said.

“Right, I just… You know I’m here to stay, right?” Buck asked him, raising his hand but leaving it to hover over Chris’ back. “I love you, and I’m not gonna leave. Not willingly. You know that, right?”

Therapy, medication, maturity, maybe they were coming together to keep Buck from spiraling over every little thing. He was fully capable, for example, of meeting a couple who’d been married for decades and had grown to hate each other without going into a panic that he was getting a glimpse into his own future. But that didn’t mean he didn’t spiral at all anymore.

Last night’s sleep was not a particularly restful one. Because maybe he managed to keep from convincing himself that the older couple’s current present was his inevitable future, but he couldn’t keep from worrying about what his kid thought. He couldn’t keep his brain from grabbing hold of that thread and following it all the way down as he unraveled. 

Buck needed Chris to know that he wasn’t going to leave.

“You do know that, don’t you?” he asked again, when the answer wasn’t as quick to come as he’d prefer.

Chris lifted his head to frown at him again and, instead of saying anything right away, leaned over to wrap his arms around him. Buck hugged him back, because he didn’t know when Chris would decide that showing affection was for nerds and he didn’t want to miss the opportunity at hand.

“I know,” the teenager said.

Buck let out a sigh of relief.

“Good.”

Apparently done with the hug, Chris started to pull away. Buck, who wasn’t done, tightened his arms and held him in place.

“Pops,” Chris whined, wiggling. “Lemme go.”

“Fine, fine.”

Buck let him go and Chris pulled away, pulling in giant breaths of air like Buck had been strangling him or something. Because Diazes were dramatic little shits.

Chris went back to getting ready for school, grabbing his bag and pulling it close as he started going through this. Buck, deciding not to impose on him any further now that his fears were suitably assuaged, got up and started to leave.

“Hey, Pops?”

Buck stopped, turned back.

“Yeah, Superman?”

“Um.” Chris wasn’t looking at him. “What do I… Do?”

“What do you do?” Buck repeated. “About…?”

“The- The girls,” the kid answered.

“Oh, you mean Penny and all the other girls you were talking to that thought they were special.”

Chris nodded shyly.

“I don’t know,” Buck admitted with a shrug. “I think you might have to take the hit on this one. They’re not gonna be happy when they find out, and they are gonna find out. Especially since one of them already knows about Penny, because she’s gonna say something to her, if she hasn’t already, and then…”

“Okay, but what do I do?”

“That depends on if they’re willing to give you the time of day,” he said. “But you’ll need to apologize. Apologize a lot. Maybe give ‘em all flowers with handwritten, ‘sorry I’m a butthole’ notes.”

Chris let out a despondent sigh.

“Okay.”

“You’ll be fine. Good luck at school today, try to take the anger like a-” Buck was gonna say like a man but he suddenly had Hen’s voice popping up in his head to complain about sexism. “Like a, er… An adult? Like an adult! Try to take the anger like an adult.”

Chris let out a groan and Buck, unable to help himself, broke into laughter as he walked out of the room.

“What the hell are you idiots doing?”

Buck looked up from the game in front of him to smile at Hen.

“Go Fish,” he answered. “Well, they’re playing Go Fish. They kicked me out of the game ‘cause they’re a bunch of sore losers.”

“You were counting the cards,” Albert complained, frowning at the ones in his hand.

“I was not!”

Eddie reached over and patted his knee. It was probably meant to be consoling, but it just came across as sarcastic.

“Yeah, you were, mi sol.”

“You,” Buck said, “are a traitor and I’m not-” He cut himself off and glanced at the others. “Let’s just say that you’re gonna be awfully cold and lonely on your side of the bed, Mr. Diaz.”

“Somehow I doubt that,” Eddie said, not looking bothered at all. He could’ve at least pretended to be worried about Buck following through with the (obviously empty, who was Buck kidding?) threat. “Ravi, got any twos?”

“Uh, yeah.” Ravi passed a card across the table to Eddie. “Hey, how did Chris’ date go? You guys never said.”

“Chimney, twos?” Eddie asked. “And the date itself went okay.”

“Go fish,” Chimney said. Eddie sighed and drew a card from the deck in the middle. “What do you mean, the date itself went okay?”

“Well…” Buck and Eddie shared a look.

“Alright, spill,” Hen said, taking a seat at the table with them. “What happened?”

“We had dinner, they played video games,” Buck said. “Penny’s heart was stolen by Gavin, y’know how cute he is.”

“Maddie mentioned something about him playing wingman.” Chimney tapped his cards on the table. “Al, gimme a four.”

“No, go fish.”

“Aw, shoot.”

“Then, after she got picked up by her mom,” Eddie picked up the thread, “Chris goes to his room, and we hear him on his phone, talking to a girl.”

“Liked talking to her that much?” Ravi asked, but Hen had her mouth covered as she slowly shook her head.

“Oh, no,” she said, because she caught on quicker than the rest of them did.

“Oh, yes,” Eddie confirmed. “The words “I will drag her skinny ass” were uttered. I’d forgotten how scary teenage girls could be. Chris definitely got to find out, or is going to.”

“Holy shit.” Albert’s eyes had gone wide. “What did you do?”

“Yeah, babe?” Buck asked, putting his elbow on the table and propping his chin on his fist. He gave Eddie his most sarcastic smile. “What did you do?”

Eddie glared at him.

“I delegated.”

“He means he made me handle it.”

“You probably do have more experience in that area,” Chimney said.

The words had Buck and Eddie sharing a frown. It felt like at least one of them got insulted, somehow. Perhaps even both, Chimney was talented like that.

“Anyways, I talked to him,” Buck said, pushing past that bit of awkwardness. “If they aren’t so angry they form a mob, we talked about him giving them all flowers with apology notes.”

“All?” Ravi repeated. “Give them all flowers?”

“Kid cast a wide net, apparently.” 

Buck’s phone buzzed in his pocket. When he pulled it out, he saw it was a message from May in the Adopted Nash Siblings chat, complaining about a paper she was having to write. On the other side of the table, Albert was digging his own phone out, because he’d been in the group chat since shortly after the lightning strike.

Seeing it, though, led to Buck checking and realizing that neither Athena nor Bobby had replied to any of his texts that day. He wasn’t even certain they’d read them.

“Hey, uh, Hen?” He lifted his head to look at her. “Have you heard from Ma or Pops?”

“Athena FaceTimed me yesterday,” she said. “Something about a murder and a sacrificial tentpole? I dunno, the service was bad on her end.”

“Sounds like an Indiana Jones movie,” Chimney quipped. “Whatever it was about, Bobby didn’t want us encouraging her.”

“Indiana Jones is the dude who saved Christmas from Professor Snape, right?” Buck asked, grinning brightly at the flat glare that earned him from his soon-to-be brother-in-law. “Zip-a-dee doo-dah, or whatever?”

“It’s yippee ki-yay, you mother-”

The alarm interrupted Chimney’s insult. The cards were dropped onto the table as they all rushed for their gear and the trucks. Over the station’s PA system, a dispatcher gave details about a motor vehicle accident. It sounded severe, involving multiple cars, and it sent both trucks and both ambulances rushing down the road.

They were met by Officer Williams when they arrived, the man running up to them as soon as they started hopping out of the engine’s cab.

“Sports car T-boned the SUV, causing it to roll onto the sedan.” He pointed at the cars as they walked; the SUV was a crumpled mess that was - thankfully - resting on top of the sedan’s hood instead of its roof. “We’ve got two trapped inside, mother and a daughter. The mom’s not responsive.”

“Help!” the driver, a teen girl, begged. “My mom, she’s hurt!”

“We’re gonna get you both out of there,” Hen assured her, leaning through the driver’s side window a little. On the other side, Albert and Eddie were looking over the unconscious passenger, the mom. “What’s your name?”

“Lexi. My mom was just giving me a driving lesson, when that guy ran the light. He came out of nowhere.”

Buck, from where he was lingering back a little as he waited for orders, could see who he was pretty certain was the guy in question. He appeared uninjured as he paced behind a sports car with a crumpled front, talking agitatedly on his phone.

“Mom’s pulse is thready,” Albert reported from the other side of the SUV. “But it’s there.”

“Okay, my friend here-” Hen gestured at Chimney beside her. “-is gonna have a quick look at you. Alright?”

“Okay,” Lexi said. From his viewpoint looking through the back window, Buck watched as she gave a jerky nod.

Hen stepped out of the way, allowing Chimney to get in with his pen light. He shone it into the girl's eyes, letting out a contented hum.

“Pupils are equal and reactive,” he said. “Can you wiggle your toes for me?”

“Yeah, I’m fine,” Lexi insisted. “Just please help my mom.”

“I want this car secured and stabilized for extraction,” Hen ordered, having moved around to the front to get a better look at what they were working with.

“Copy that, Cap,” Buck said, almost disappointed that Hen didn’t glare at him in annoyance for it anymore. He jerked his head at Eddie, and then at Ravi and Toby, the four of them going to the engine to get everything they would need.

He took note, distantly, of Hen talking to Officer Williams and then sending Chimney to talk to the driver of the other vehicle. Buck wasn’t certain what was being discussed, because he was too busy pulling out gear and was also too far away to successfully eavesdrop.

It was unavoidable to hear, while they got the cars secured, the way the guy shouted for Chimney to,

“Get out of my face!”

Hen went rushing over there to defuse the situation, leaving Buck to share a look with the others before getting back to work. The next time he took the chance to see what was going on, the guy was being handcuffed while Williams - loudly and insistently - informed him that he wasn’t being arrested, just detained. Hen and Chimney, meanwhile, were coming back.

“Did they even look him over?” Buck asked, frowning in their direction.

“Not our problem,” Eddie reminded him.

“Yeah, right.”

Buck took one of the power saws that Ravi held out while Eddie took the other, flipped down the visor of his helmet, and together the pair of them started cutting apart the front of the SUV. The frame of it was twisted enough to make simply popping the windshield out impossible, or at least not without far too much time and effort spent on it. Spark flew up as the saw chewed through fiberglass and steel, while others worked on other points of the vehicle.

“Good on my side,” Eddie announced, setting his saw on the hood. “Yours?”

Buck nodded and set down his saw as well, and then they worked together to take out the windshield. They carried it over out the way, dropped it without ceremony, and then went back to help finish turning the SUV into a convertible. With certain points cut clean through, the roof was peeled back quite similarly to the lids of the fancy canned food that Eddie bought for Senorita.

(Eddie sulked for a full day when it was pointed out to him just how much he spoiled the cat. Though that might’ve been because everyone teased him for it. And everyone, in this case, included Abuela and Pepa, along with his sisters. Because Buck was an awful husband and shared a picture with his sisters-in-law of Eddie and the cat.)

The mom roused as they got the c-collar on her, much to her daughter’s clear relief. She was disoriented at first, asking for her daughter that was right next to her and asking what happened.

“There was an accident,” Albert explained, taking the c-collar from Eddie and wrapping it around the woman’s neck.

“Oh, no. Is- Is anyone hurt?”

“Everyone survived,” Hen reassured her, to the response of,

“Praise Jesus.”

Getting them out after that was relatively easy. Ravi and Toby brought a gurney over to the mother’s side, because she was the one they were most concerned about, before bringing the other one for the daughter. After slicing through their restraints, both women were gently placed onto the gurneys and then led to the ambulances to be loaded up.

And then Officer Williams was shouting for Hen, because,

“He’s not breathing!”

The “he” in question was the other driver, the one that they’d detained (not arrested) after Hen spoke to him. He was on the ground, laying on his side and still in a far too familiar way. Chimney rushed over to him, Hen and Eddie and Buck not far behind.

“Uncuff him!” Chimney barked, dropping to his knees. “I need him on his back!”

Officer Williams hurried to do as he was told, scrambling to unlock the cuffs and helping to roll him onto his back. Chimney felt at the guy’s neck, relayed the lack of a pulse, and started doing compressions.

“What happened?” Eddie asked.

“He was up and talking,” the officer said. “Then his words became gibberish. He just collapsed.”

That very much sounded like a brain bleed, Buck thought. The guy had seemed okay from afar, but Buck hadn’t looked him over so he couldn’t be sure. No one had looked him over, he didn’t think. Too busy with the mother and daughter in the car, was the kinder explanation. Too impatient and too unsympathetic to deal with the belligerent driver that caused the wreck, was perhaps closer to the truth.

“Chim, switch,” Hen ordered, kneeling on the other side and taking over compressions as soon as Chimney was out of the way. “Radio for another ambulance, and call the closest level-one trauma center. Have a neurosurgeon standing by.”

“Copy that,” Eddie said, grabbing his radio and relaying the captain’s words.

Buck refrain from pointing out that there probably wasn’t any need to rush.

Chapter 6: Consequences of Judgment

Chapter Summary

In which Captain Wilson regrets not following the maxim of CYA.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Hen ended up going with the guy to the hospital, riding in the back of the ambulance that answered their call. The last Buck saw of her, she was still trying to revive the guy. Then the ambulance doors shut and they were both being carted off. This particular fact was very problematic, considering she was the acting captain.  

Then again, Chimney and Bobby had both done the same, hadn’t they? When Shannon was hit by the car that killed her, Chimney had fallen out of the role of Interim Captain and back to being a paramedic, leaving them behind to handle the scene without leadership. And Bobby had rode the ambulance with them after Buck was struck by lightning, or so Buck had been told, putting everyone left behind at the apartment fire in the same situation until the 133 arrived to take over.

Buck would like to say he wouldn’t do the same and thankfully, during those months he played captain, he’d never been placed into the position to prove that he damn well would have. At least if it had been one of his people who were injured.

Anyways, they’d returned to the station sans a captain. In the time it took for Hen to return, their own ambulances came back. Not long after that, the station was invaded by serious looking people in the same uniform Buck wore as a fire marshall, people who none of them ever wanted to deal with: the LAFD Professional Services Division.

Internal Affairs.

After they were gathered together and it was explained to them why these strangers were there, as part of an inquiry into the death of the driver, they were taken aside one at a time and questioned. They started with Chimney, as the other paramedic who tried to treat the guy, and worked their way through everyone from there.

Everyone except Buck, at least: he was called to the captain’s office, where the fire chief had set up camp.

Chief Simpson took over as fire chief when Alonso retired, some time before the bridge collapse that killed Jones and put so many of them out of commission. Buck wondered if the two men were who Battalion Chief Williams meant by “someone reached retirement, someone else got promoted” when informing him what had changed that they were allowing him to fill the captain’s chair. 

Simpson didn’t have an easy start to his tenure, all things considered. One of the first things he’d had to deal with was a probationary firefighter - Dalton Wolfe, their probie - turning out to be an arsonist. Something made significantly more awkward by the fact that his uncle was Deputy Chief Randolf Torres.

Former Deputy Chief Randolf Torres: he’d been “encouraged” to resign in the wake of it. He probably would’ve gotten through the scandal mostly okay, with just a bit of professional embarrassment, except he apparently spent months trying to leverage his authority to defend his nephew and push the blame onto someone else. Specifically, Buck and Eddie, not that they’d noticed it at all, because others in the department (namely Williams, as well as Eleanor Boone from the union) kept blocking all his attempts.

(Buck only heard about all this after the fact, from Josh and Maddie and Linda. The fire chief’s office was now in the same building as Metro Dispatch and first responders of any kind were a gossipy bunch.)

When Buck entered the office, Simpson was sitting behind Bobby’s desk. He looked quite comfortable, something that had Buck feeling a bit of irrational irritation that he had to smother down.

“You asked to see me, Sir?”

“Yes.” The chief gave no invitation for Buck to sit, so he stood behind the chairs at parade rest. “They explained to you what’s going on?”

“They’re taking our statements regarding the MVA and the driver who died,” Buck answered. “They didn’t tell me why you wanted to speak to me. Sir.”

“Why don’t you give me your statement first?” Simpson suggested. “And then I can tell you.”

“There isn’t much to it,” Buck said. “I was assigned to help secure the cars and to extract the two women inside. The driver of the sports car seemed okay when we arrived and while we worked, if a bit… Unhappy. When we got there, he was on the phone with someone and pacing. Hen- Captain Wilson sent Chimney- I mean Paramedic Han- Er, the older Paramedic Han to assess him, but the driver got upset. I didn’t see what happened, but there was shouting and the captain went over. And then the next thing I know, Officer Williams is putting him in cuffs and both Captain Wilson and Paramedic Han are coming back.”

“So you didn’t interact with him at all?”
