“No, sir.” He shook his head. “The closest I got to him was after he collapsed while we were loading up the occupants of the SUV he crashed into and I ran over to see what was going on.”

“Were his vitals ever taken? Was he ever made to do a field sobriety test?”

“I…” Buck swallowed, felt his stomach twist. Because he was having flashbacks to the ordeal after Taylor Kelly and Jonah Greenway, and just like then, he didn’t want to throw his friends under the bus. But he also didn’t want to lie. “I’m not certain.”

“You’re not certain?” Simpson asked, clasping his hands together on the desk.

“No, sir. I was busy doing the tasks I was assigned. I can’t say what did or didn’t happen with him in that time. Sir.”

Simpson nodded to himself.

“The driver’s name was Kyle Ortiz,” he said. Buck wondered if he was related to Javier Ortiz, who drove their ladder truck, and then dismissed it as unlikely considering the man’s utter lack of reaction regarding everything. “His mother’s Councilwoman Olivia Ortiz.”

“Oh.” Shit. That definitely explained why they were moving so fast on the inquiry.

“Oh, indeed,” Simpson agreed. “While we’re conducting the inquiry into any and all mitigating factors of the death of the councilwoman’s son, we have to make sure that we do everything by the book. Which means, unfortunately, that the One Eighteen needs a new captain until we’re through.”

“A new- You’re suspending Hen!?”

“If we’re to follow protocol, then she can’t serve as acting captain while her actions and choices are being investigated,” he explained, which made an unfortunate amount of sense. It’s why Bobby had been suspended and they got stuck with Interim Captain Han, after all. “Which is why I wanted to speak to you, personally.”

Oh. Shit.

“I see no reason to look out of house for someone to fill in,” Simpson continued. “You did the job well last summer, so I’m assigning you to the position until the inquiry has reached its conclusion.”

“Yes, sir.”

“You’re dismissed, Diaz,” the fire chief said. “When you see Wilson, whenever she finally comes back, pass on the message that I wish to speak to her.”

“Yes, sir,” Buck said again, before nodding and exiting the office.

It was with a building feeling of dread that Buck walked back into the truck bay. Everyone else was still giving statements, or waiting for their turn to do so. He spotted Eddie sitting in the locker room as he was interviewed, and Toby going through the same on a bench near the gym. Chimney, the first one to give a statement, appeared to have finished, though. Buck found him perched on the side of the engine.

“Waiting for Hen to get here?” Buck asked as he joined him.

“Yeah.” Chimney let out a sigh. “This is a mess.”

Buck let out a hollow laugh, because the man didn’t know the half of it. He would soon, though, because another ambulance was pulling up in front of the station. The passenger door opened and Hen climbed out. Chimney shot their guests an anxious glance and stood as she approached them.

“He was pronounced at the hospital,” Hen said, to which Chimney replied with a grim,

“I know.”

Hen finally looked around, taking in all their white-shirted guests.

“What’s going on?” she asked.

“LAFD Professional Services division,” Chimney answered. “They’re taking statements.”

“Internal Affairs is here taking statements,” Hen said. “Why? What for?”

Buck and Chimney shared a look.

“Chief Simpson is in Bobby’s office,” Buck told her. “He wants to talk to you.”

“That didn’t really answer my question,” she said, frowning at him, but she left for the captain’s office anyway.

“Why does the chief want to talk to her?” Chimney asked as they watched her go.

“Maybe so that she didn’t shoot the messenger,” was Buck’s glib answer.

It landed between them like a dead fish.

Just as Buck knew she would, Hen came out of Bobby’s office in a foul mood. She shot Buck a dirty look on her way passed him to the locker room and he gave her a wide berth. Eddie, who’d finished giving his statement while she was talking to Simpson, scowled after her.

“What was that about?”

“Something neither of us are happy about,” Buck answered, because he hadn’t yet told anyone that Hen had been removed from the captain’s seat and replaced with him. Sure, it was only temporary, but still! Just thinking about it left him feeling dirty and guilty and awful.

Chimney went in when she was changed into her civvies, with Buck and Eddie following behind. She glanced at them - at Buck - and let out a humorless snort, before telling them what Buck already knew. That the dead guy was a councilwoman’s son, that she was removed from acting captain until everything was through.

“It’s a bum deal,” Buck told her, which got him side-eyed. He ducked his head to avoid it.

“It really is,” Chimney agreed, frowning between the two of them.

“The chief isn’t calling it an official suspension,” Hen said, going back to making sure her work bag was packed to go. “Just a pause.”

“So who’s in charge now?”

“Yeah, Buck.” Hen looked at him straight on, challenging him to say something. “Who’s in charge now?”

He saw Chimney look between them, and knew Eddie was doing the same. And then the paramedic let out a quiet,

“Oh.”

“Yeah.” Buck rubbed at the back of his neck, feeling awkward. “He- He didn’t see the reason to look for someone out of house…”

“That- That makes sense,” Chimney said, looking back at Hen. “At least it’s someone we know, right?”

“Right,” was her totally (not) sincere sounding agreement.

“Okay, but what if the tox screen comes back negative?” Eddie asked, giving voice to the question that they were all wondering. “You know, what if he wasn’t under the influence?”

“The truth is, I don’t know.” Hen zipped up her bag and picked it up, heading for the locker room exit. “Councilwoman Ortiz is looking for someone to blame.”

“Well, that- they can’t fire you for one bad call,” Buck told her, trying to be reassuring.

She stopped at the door and turned to glare at him. So it hadn’t come across as reassuring as he wanted.

“Who said it was a bad call?” she demanded. “Did they?”

“N- No-”

“Did you?”

“No! I- I didn’t say anything,” Buck told her. “I- I didn’t really see anything. Y’know, you had us cribbing the other car and…”

“We didn’t even get a good look at him until he was coding,” Eddie added. “We all thought he was drunk.”

“He was. I- I mean, we could all smell it,” Hen said. “Right? You could smell it, Chim.”

Chimney didn’t answer. He sat silently on the bench of the locker room, and didn’t meet Hen’s eyes. Buck saw her face drop.

“Chim?” she prodded.

“I’m sorry,” Chimney said. “I didn’t smell anything.”

“How is that possible?” Hen demanded. “You were closer than I was! How could you not smell that?”

“I don’t know.” He shrugged. “Was I supposed to lie?”

Hen leaned back, reeling.

“Is that what you think I’d want?” she asked, sounding upset and offended.

“No, of course I don’t think you’d want me to lie,” he said. “I didn’t even want to talk to those guys.”

“Right, nobody did,” Buck said, supporting him.

“We just answered their questions,” Eddie added.

“And nobody blamed you.”

“Uh-huh.” Hen did not look to be believing them for one second, her glare moving from one of them to the next.

“You were understandably concerned for the victims in the other car,” Chimney said.

“His victims.”

“Exactly! If the roles were reversed, any one of us might have made the same call.”

That… Wasn’t necessarily true, actually. Buck wouldn’t say it, not to Hen and not to anyone official, but he knew he wouldn’t have just let the guy go without at least being checked for injuries. Hell, last spring with the bank robber, he brought up this very thing with Bobby and then made Eddie go look the bank robber over. Because he remembered, way back when he was still Buck 1.0, having Athena shouting at him about how he didn’t get to choose who lived or who died just because they’d done something he thought was wrong.

So no, Buck at least wouldn’t have done the same thing. And honestly, he loved Hen, but she shouldn’t have just dismissed the guy for being an asshole.

Not that he would ever, ever say that to anyone that wasn’t Eddie.

“And if the roles were reversed, I’d find a way to have your backs,” Hen shot back. She was absolutely pissed at them. “And I wouldn’t have to lie to do it. So I’ll see you around. Maybe.”

With that last parting remark, and one finally venomous look for all of them, Hen stomped out. Awkward silence was left in her wake, during which the three of them didn’t look at each other. It wasn’t broken until Albert and Ravi came in, the pair of them craning their necks to watch Hen’s departure.

“Did Hen get fired?” Albert asked them.

“What?” Chimney stared at him and then shook his head. “No, this is just… The chief said it was a pause.”

“So if she’s gone, who’s in charge?” Ravi asked. “Buck again?”

“Yup.” Eddie slapped Buck on the shoulder, jostling him a little. “You’ve got Captain Diaz again.”

“Oh, joy,” Albert said, and then smiled brightly when Buck scowled at his lack of excitement. “I mean, oh! Joy!”

“Uh-huh. What’s everyone else doing?” Buck asked them.

The people from the Professional Services Division had left, along with Chief Simpson, so he knew everyone was done giving their statements. It hadn’t taken very long, mainly because there wasn’t much for anyone to say. With the sole exception of Chimney, they all would’ve had the same story to tell as Buck.

“I think they’re upstairs gossiping,” Ravi answered. “Want us to go make sure they’re all together?”

“Please.”

Ravi nodded, and then he and Albert left through the same door they came in, jogging up the stairs just outside. Chimney went with them, at a slower pace and still trailing a cloud of guilt. That left Buck and Eddie alone in the locker room.

“Do you remember,” Eddie said to him, “when you asked if they looked the guy over and I told you it wasn’t our problem?”

“Yup.”

“I’m gonna let you say it now.”

Buck just grinned at him, because he didn’t need to say it, and lead him out of the locker room and up to the station loft by his hand. He dropped it when he found their coworkers all gathered together, each of them raising their heads to look at him in expectation.

“Okay!” Buck clapped his hands together. “So, I’m sure you’ve all heard the gossip that the last call involved the son of a councilwoman. Until they’re finished looking into everything, you’re all stuck with Captain Diaz again.”

The synchronized groans from everyone at that announcement were completely unnecessary.

Afterwards, Buck went and hid in the captain’s office. He fiddled with his phone for a moment, debating, before pulling up the contact for Bobby and swiping his thumb across the screen to call him. It didn’t even ring through, instead just going straight to voicemail. When he tried calling Athena, it got the same result.

He was about to try someone else - Maddie, maybe - to make sure it wasn’t his phone acting up when someone called him. Buck stared at the screen, at his father’s name flashing on it, and very deliberately rejected the call. Now, not only was he worried about Bobby and Athena, but now he was… Curious, that was a good word for it. He was curious about Phillip calling him for a second time after a year of radio silence. And not only that, but Buck knew it was a late hour in Hershey.

Too many questions, too many things to be worried about, and none of it enough to distract him from feeling guilty over something that wasn’t his fault.

Chapter End Notes

Fun fact! Firefighter Ortiz is from season 5: there was an Ortiz from the 133, mentioned during the episode dispatch burned - I changed that Ortiz's name - and then there's another Ortiz present in the season 5 finale, when the ladder truck takes a header. All of this to ask, Why the hell can't they vary up the names used in the show? I know they have a limited number of coats for the extras, probably why a Kinard was present at the bridge collapse, but still!

Chapter 7: Did You Lose a Boat?

Chapter Summary

In which Hen goes to Maddie for a favor.

After all that she’d been told, Maddie expected Hen to be at home with her wife and kid. Maybe complaining and venting over a glass of wine while Karen lended her a sympathetic ear. She did not expect to be seeing the other woman step off the elevator onto the dispatch floor, wave at her the moment they made eye contact, and then jog over.

“Hey,” Hen greeted her. “I’m so sorry to bother you are work.”

“It’s no problem,” Maddie assured her. “Shouldn't you be on your shift?”

Shit. The moment the words were out of her mouth, Maddie wanted to kick herself.

“You haven’t spoken to Chimney?” Hen asked.

“About what?” she tried, but could only play dumb for so long. Especially since it was clear that Hen wasn’t buying her bullshit tonight. “Okay, they told me everything and it sucks.”

“Yeah,” the paramedic agreed with a sigh. “But that’s not why I’m here.”

That sounded ominous to Maddie’s ears, and like the beginning of a conversation best held somewhere that wasn’t the middle of the dispatch floor. She led Hen to the nearby conference room, the glass walls making it easy to see that it was empty, and held the door open for her to step inside.

“Why are you here, then?” Maddie asked, letting the door close. It muffled the constant drone of voices and ringing phones, but it didn’t block the noise completely.

“I was hoping you could do me a favor.”

“Name it.”

“Can you call the Coast Guard?

Maddie couldn’t help the way she laughed at the question, so totally out of left field from what she expected. Not that she really expected anything, but if she had, it wouldn’t have been that.

“Did you lose a boat?” she joked, only to have the humor sucked dry when Hen nodded and said, very seriously,

“Yes.”

“Okay, explain.”

“Bobby and Athena’s cruise ship, the Uno? I can’t find it,” Hen said. She pulled out her phone and thumbed the screen on, an app already showing. It showed the ocean and part of the coast of Mexico, the blue populated by labeled dots. “This is every boat out on the Pacific right now, and theirs isn’t on it. I’ve tried to call Bobby and Athena, but it just keeps going straight to voicemail.”

“Yeah, Buck complained about the same thing,” Maddie said. “Like I told him, they’re at sea and there’s a tropical storm down there.”

“Hurricane,” Hen corrected. “It’s been upgraded.”

Double shit.

“I’ll make a call,” Maddie promised, hurrying out of the conference room.

“Cap!” Toby called up from the bay below. Buck went over to the loft’s railing and peered down. “Phone’s ringing in the office.”

“And you couldn’t answer it to find out who it was?” Buck threw back, even as he headed for the stairs.

The driver-engineer just shrugged and held out his hands, both of them visibly grease covered even at this distance. He’d been shoulder deep in doing maintenance on the engine, so Buck couldn't really blame the guy for wanting to avoid putting his dirty mitts everywhere, especially since the guy had clearly wiped at his face at some point and left a smear of grease across a freckle-covered cheek.

“It’s probably the chief.” Eddie was right on his heels.

“Probably,” Buck agreed. “Here’s hoping it’s good news.”

The phone on Bobby’s desk was still ringing, loud and shrill, when Buck got to the office. He hurried over, clearing the space in just a couple long strides, and answered it with a crisp,

“Captain Diaz, One Eighteen.”

“Captain Diaz, it’s Chief Simpson.”

“Sir.” Buck stood up straighter, even though Simpson couldn’t even see him. “Is this about Hen?”

“It is,” the man said. “I’m sure you’ll be glad to know that Wilson has been cleared. The tox results showed Kyle Ortiz to be at more than double the legal alcohol limit, with traces of methamphetamine in his system. As far as the LAFD is concerned, she acted professionally and appropriately.”

“That’s a relief to hear, sir,” Buck told him, even though he didn’t necessarily agree. 

He was still firmly of the opinion that Hen should’ve had the guy looked over regardless, not that he was ever going to say that to her or the chief or anyone whose last name wasn’t Diaz. But also, the guy’s death was very much out of anyone’s control, so Hen definitely shouldn’t be getting punished for it. Kyle Ortiz actually being drunk and, apparently, at least a little high on meth was a stroke of luck that they should all be thankful for.

Buck definitely was.

“You’ll be doubly relieved, then. Wilson is being put back onto active duty, effective immediately. When she returns to the station, you will be handing command of the One Eighteen to her.”

“Yes, sir.”

The chief didn’t stay on the line much longer, the pair of them exchanging polite farewells, and Buck was soon placing the phone back into its cradle. He turned and leaned back against Bobby’s desk, shooting Eddie a smirk and shaking his head when the man opened his mouth to, no doubt, ask about the good news. Buck checked the time on his watch, jerked his chin over at the doorway, and got an eye roll and a smile in return. Eddie glanced at his own watch and held up his fingers, three in total. Buck silently scoffed, and just held up the one.

They stood in silence for not even a minute before Chimney finally barged into the office.

“Well!?” his soon-to-be brother-in-law demanded, throwing up his arms. “What was a relief to hear!?”

“Ha!” Buck pointed at Eddie. “I win.”

“You couldn’t have held out for a couple more minutes, man?” Eddie tutted and shook his head. “I had faith in you.”

“You’re both dicks,” Chimney said. “Come on, was that the chief? Did he say anything about Hen?”

“You didn’t hear?” Buck asked, because he was a little shit sometimes.

“I heard you sounding like a responsible adult, and that was very terrifying.”

Chimney managed to miss the entirety of Captain Diaz’s tenure last summer, not recovering from his injuries enough to return to work until some time after Bobby. They’d only had one call out since Hen’s captaincy was “paused,” and that was a basic medical emergency, so Captain Diaz honestly hadn’t been doing much captaining tonight.

“Almost as terrifying as when it’s coming from you,” Buck shot back. “Hen’s good, turns out the guy actually was drunk, and there was something about meth. Hen should be coming back soon.”

“Good.” Chimney let out a relieved breath. “When did he say she’d be back?”

“He didn’t,” he answered with a frown, glancing back at the phone on Bobby’ desk. “I’m guessing she’ll be his next call.”

“We should probably pass on the good news.” Chimney pulled out his own phone and started tapping at the screen. “Let everyone know that your tyranny is over once more.”

“It is!?” Albert’s head was poking out from around the corner. “Oh, thank God.”

“Not until Hen gets back,” Buck pointed out with a mock scowl. “Go get your toothbrush, I saw a giant stain on the bay floor that needs scrubbing.”

“If you actually made him do that, we’d just grab yours,” Ravi said, patting Albert on the head as he walked into the office. “So if Hen’s coming back, that means the guy really was drunk, right?”

“And on drugs,” Eddie said. “So everyone’s cleared for the death that none of us could’ve done anything about.”

Albert gave a dull, not-very-cheery sounding, “Yay.”

“Save the yay,” Chimney told his brother. He’d pulled out his phone and was glaring down at it. “She isn’t texting me back, so she might still be a little bit… Upset. When she gets back.”

“Or she’s talking to the chief,” Buck pointed out. “It hasn’t been that long since I got off the phone with him.”

“Or that.”

“At least she’s getting the texts, though, right?” he asked, pulling out his own phone. The ones he’d sent to Bobby and Athena all had Failed to Send notifications attached to them. He pressed his thumb to one and re-sent it, only for his phone to spin its wheels for a brief while and tell him to get lost. “I’m still not getting anything to go through to Ma or Pops.”

“The storm must be messing with everything,” Eddie suggested.

“Yeah, probably.”

The phone on the desk rang again, startling all of them. Buck reached over and picked it up, answering it again with,

“Captain Diaz, One Eighteen.”

“I just told Captain Wilson in person,” Simpson said. “She-”

“In person, sir?” Buck interrupted. It hadn’t been that long since they got off the phone, surely not long enough to make the trip to the Wilsons’ home.

“Yes, she was visiting someone at dispatch, so I made the walk,” the chief said. Buck’s frown felt like it involved his entire face, something that had the others broadcasting confusion and curiosity. “I informed her that she will be reporting for duty at your firehouse as its captain. She should be on her way.”

There was something pointed about the chief’s tone, Buck couldn’t help but note. 

“Um, yes sir. Thank you for telling me.”

Simpson didn’t say goodbye that time, there was simply a click and Buck was left staring at the receiver for a moment before placing it down.

“Hen’s at dispatch,” he announced. “But she should be on her way.”

“Why is she at dispatch?” Ravi asked, but Buck could only shrug.

“Hell if I know.”

Calling the Coast Guard was a bust, unfortunately. They had their hands full with the hurricane, assisting the various ships and their passengers they could actually locate that got caught in the storm’s path. They didn’t have the time or the manpower to hunt down a ship that went missing off another nation’s coast.

Maddie was still trying to find some other avenue to help, when she spotted Chief Simpson coming in. He went into the conference room where Hen was waiting, and Maddie watched them talk through the glass. She got up from her station and went over to linger just outside.

It didn’t seem to have been a good conversation, and neither of them looked particularly happy as Simpson left.

“That didn’t look super fun,” she remarked, after she pushed the door open and stepped inside.

“Could have gone better,” Hen admitted.

“Are you fired?” God, Maddie hoped she wasn’t.

“Not yet, but I probably will be.” The paramedic put on a mask of determination. “What’s the number for the Nine-One-One in Mexico?”

“It’s Nine-One-One,” Maddie answered a bit dumbly. “Why?”

“I was just thinking, if something happened, then people would be calling about it. Right?” Hen said. 

“Well obviously I can’t just call up Mexico and ask,” Maddie pointed out. “I’d need to know the number of the dispatch center nearest to their location, and hope their calls didn’t actually end up routed to somewhere else.”

“Their last reported location,” Hen said, pulling up the app on her phone again, “was somewhere between Mazatlán, in Sinaloa, and Cabo San Lucas, in Baja California Sur.”

“Great, I’m gonna be making international calls between two Mexican states,” Maddie said, turning to head back to her station. Hen was right on her heels. “Let’s hope someone there speaks better English than I speak Spanish, because I never got to nautical disasters in my high school Spanish class and it’s not something that’s ever come up in Buck’s kitchen.”

“Buck’s kitchen?” Hen sounded a little confused.

“You’ve never sat in on them while Buck and Isabel cook,” Maddie said, sitting down in her chair. Hen grabbed another one nearby and rolled it over. “It is very much an English-free zone, unless Bobby’s there.”

And since the Diaz women apparently adopted her, Maddie was getting dragged into the Spanish lessons whenever she visited with Jee-Yun.

“Okay.” Maddie slipped her earpiece back on. “First, let’s see which city is bigger.” That turned out to be Mazatlán. “And then we’ll look up the non-emergency number for their dispatch center…”

Or maybe it would be better to call the local consulate? Maddie wasn’t certain, she’d never done this before. She’d try that next, if this didn’t work.

“Hola,” was how the person on the other end answered. “[How can I help you?]”

She understood that much, at least. Maybe this wouldn’t be such a painful experience after all.

“Um… ¿Hablas inglés?”

“No.”

Shoot. Well, time to make Isabel proud. Or disappoint her entirely, it could go either way, really.

The conversation that followed was choppy, and she pulled up Google Translate partway through to help her. But! She managed to get her questions across, and get the answers, so it wasn’t that bad. She was put on hold while the person she was talking to, Anette, asked around. When Anette got back, and passed on what she found out, it was pretty much what Hen had been saying.

Hen, who kept getting calls from someone while Maddie was on the line with Anette and kept declining them.

“Okay, I think you’re right. Something definitely happened,” Maddie told her, once Anette hung up. “At around ten this morning, more than two dozen Nine-One-One calls came into coastal emergency centers in Central Mexico. And all of them dropped.”

“That’s about the time the Uno disappeared from radar. That can’t be a coincidence.”

“Probably not,” Maddie agreed. In front of her, her phone began to buzz with a call from Chimney. Hen, in the middle of gathering her things to rush out the door, suddenly stopped.

“Do me a favor,” she said. “Don’t answer that, at least not until I’m out of the building?”

“Why?”

“Because I don’t feel like being second-guessed right now.”

Just because Buck was currently the acting captain didn’t mean he didn’t do chores. Sure, he could probably shove the ones he’s been assigned onto someone else’s shoulders, but that was a bit of an asshole move. Plus, helping to make sure the engine was shining and everything was tip-top after Toby was done doing engine maintenance kept him properly distracted from the things he would have been spiraling over a year ago. 

Chimney was supposed to be helping, but he’d finally got someone - Maddie, not Hen - on the phone, and so he’d stopped in-between the trucks to have his conversation.

“Yeah, Buck took over when they sent Hen home,” he said. “But you knew that already… No, I’ve been reaching out to her ever since she got benched, but she won’t answer my calls or return my text. At this point I’m just like, fine, if she wants to blame us, she can.”

Buck paused in the middle of scrubbing at a particularly stubborn portion of the engine’s side to shoot the paramedic a look.

“I don’t know about us,” Buck said. Though Hen did seem more than a mite annoyed with him for being the one they replaced her with, temporarily or not, but he couldn’t help that and she’d get over it.

“Yeah,” Eddie agreed, from where he was doing the same at the ladder truck. “Think it was more you.”

Chimney turned to glare at the pair of them, phone still pressed to his ear.

“Yeah, we know she got cleared to come back,” he said. “The chief told Buck she was supposed to be on her way, but she’s still not answering me and there’s been no sign of her. Why was she at dispatch, anyway?... What kind of favor?”

Buck tossed the rag he was using at Ravi, hitting him square in the head and dodging Albert’s own rag, before going with Eddie to grab a hose. He helped his husband roll it out while keeping an ear on Chimney’s half of the conversation, just like he knew the others were doing, because they were all curious about what was going on with Hen.

“Missing?” Chimney suddenly blurted out. “What do you mean missing?”

“Who’s missing?” Buck asked. The other man held up a hand and turned, pacing down the way a little.

“Why would Bobby and Athena’s cruise ship be anywhere near a hurricane?”

At those words, everyone stopped pretending to be interested in their actual tasks. They all - Buck and Eddie, Ravi and Albert - converged on Chimney.

“Cap and Athena?” Eddie said. “That’s who’s missing?”

“Cruise ships turn away from hurricanes, Maddie,” Chimney said, putting his phone on speaker and setting it down on top of one of the tool boxes.

“Right, and we think that it did,” Maddie said, voice a bit tinny through the speaker of Chimney’s phone. “Probably. Look, like I was saying, it’s not officially missing, but it is possible that some emergency calls have been dropped in the vicinity of the ship, and Hen seemes very concerned.”

“I can see why. Did she tell the chief?”

“And why didn’t she tell any of us?” Buck asked, only to get shushed by Chimney.

“She did, he asked her to report back to work anyway.”

“Yeah, two hours ago.”

Overhead, the PA burst into life as a dispatcher rattled off information and a request for their engine. They all shared a grim look, before clambering into the engine’s cab, Buck once again going for the captain’s seat because the captain that should be there, wasn’t.

“Buck,” Chimney asked, once they all had their headset on and Toby was pulling them out of the station. “What would you do, if you were Hen right now?”

“Something stupid,” Buck immediately answered, turning in his seat enough to face him. “Like, I don’t know. Fly down there myself. I kinda want to do that now.”

Chimney nodded.

“So,” he said. “Hen and I both know a guy…”

Chapter 8: S.O.S.

Chapter Summary

In which the gang technically steal a municipal vehicle and get away with it.

Hen had never been to the Harbor Station before, never had the need. The 217 was attached to an airport, and was where the LAFD’s air support units were all based out of. The water bomber that put out the gas main fire, way back during Eddie’s first year with them, had come from here. But the reason she was here was for the helicopters, one of which should - keyword there - be able to make a trip to the last known location of Bobby and Athena’s cruise ship.

But first, before she could even get on one of those helicopters, she’d need to be able to convince the 217’s captain that everything was legit.

It was remarkably empty inside the main hangar, though there were a pair of desks sitting right in the middle of the floor. Everything was white, and the hanger doors were wide open. There was one helicopter just inside, with someone in a flight suit doing maintenance, and another helicopter was just outside on the tarmac. A man in the regular firefighter uniform and not a flight suit started heading her way the moment the door closed, the noise reverberating through the building.

“Can I help you?” he asked.

“Acting Captain Henrietta Wilson from the One Eighteen,” she introduced herself, meeting him halfway, at one of the desks. She held out the forms she’d snuck into the 118 to print out, and hoped he hadn’t been talking to any of the chiefs. “I have an emergency requisition for a helicopter and pilot. A search and rescue.”

“You want to take an LAFD chopper down to Mexico?” the man asked, reading through the form. He was making no attempt to hide his dubiousness over the request.

“Off the coast of Mexico, yeah,” she said. “About a ninety-minute flight.”

“A cruise ship? I haven’t heard anything about a missing cruise ship.”

“Situation’s fast-moving down there,” Hen told him. “Due to the hurricane.”

Come on, come on, stop asking questions.

“Wouldn’t this be under the Coast Guard’s jurisdiction?” he asked, already reaching for the phone on his desk.

Hen’s heart dropped, because she’d honestly hoped she would at least make it into the air before this house of cards collapsed on her.

“Actually,” said a man in a blue flight suit, someone Hen hadn’t seen in many a year. He was crossing the hangar to join them. “Anything off the coast of Mexico would fall under the Mexican Maritime Search and Rescue. I assume that’s who put in the initial request?”

“Uh, yeah,” she agreed, taking the rope he was throwing her and clinging to it. “Could be.”

“It’s good to see you, Hen.”

“You too. I forgot you were stationed out of Harbor.”

“Could be why I haven’t been getting your Christmas cards,” Tommy Kinard said, the words barbed. 

Tommy had been at the 118 when Hen first joined, had been there before Chimney, even. In the years between Gerrard getting booted from the captain’s chair and Bobby coming in from St. Paul, everyone had transferred or quit or been fired, except for the three of them and Sal Deluca, who hadn’t made it long into Bobby’s tenure as captain. And Tommy himself transferred to Harbor, leaving a space that would eventually be filled by Buck.

And just like all the others that had been there during that sexist, racist dinosaur’s time as captain, neither she nor Chimney made much effort to keep in touch with him after he was gone. Hen was pretty sure Chimney still had his number, but he’d only ever reached out to Tommy a grand total of once, to ask for the favor that resulted in that water bomber doing its run. Hen definitely hadn’t reached out to Tommy, and Tommy hadn’t reached out to either of them.

“Captain Wilson and I used to work together at the One Eighteen,” Tommy explained to the person Hen had been talking to. “And look at her now.”

“It’s been a minute,” Hen said.

“Speaking of minutes, the longer we wait, the tougher it’s gonna be to fly through that storm,” he said, and started to walk away. “We should move, I’ve got your bird fueled and ready to go.”

He didn’t need to tell her twice. Hen rounded the desk, and hurried to catch up.

“Wait a minute,” the other man called out to him. “You knew about this?”

“Yeah, you didn’t get a call from Central Bureau?”

“Uh, no.”

“I’ll have words,” Tommy promised him.

“What is going on right now?” Hen asked him under his breath as they stepped onto the tarmac.

“What do you mean? I’m just helping out an old friend.” Tommy was leading her to the second helicopter she’d spotted, slightly bigger than the one getting the tune-up and painted bright yellow. “I thought you’d be a little more grateful.”

“I am,” Hen assured him. “I’m just a little confused. Who even called you?”

“Who do you think? The guy only ever calls to ask for a favor.”

The door of the cockpit was already open when they got to it and she came to a halt to stare like an idiot. Just inside was a group of idiots that she most definitely should’ve been expecting.

“What?” Chimney asked her, sitting between Eddie and Ravi, each of them wearing the lighter hi-vis jackets. “You wouldn’t return our calls.”

Eddie held out a jacket and Hen took it, before saying the first thing that came to mind:

“Where’s Buck?”

“I told you.” Eddie looked stupidly smug as he turned to the other two idiots. “I knew that would be the first thing she asked.”

“He’s holding down the fort,” Chimney explained to her. “Get a move on, we’re wasting time.”

Well. Alright, then.

Considering this was the first time Eddie had been in a helicopter since the crash, discounting that brief time when they first had the misfortune of meeting Taylor Kelly, he felt he was doing pretty well with it. Sure, there was a minor thrum of anxiety beneath his skin, but that was honestly mostly due to the storm they were flying into. Rain pelted against the windows and the wind shook the helicopter, and in the distance he could occasionally see flashes of bright lightning that rumbled moments later with thunder.

But while there was a hurricane raging outside, inside was a chilly blizzard. None of them had really spoken since Hen climbed into the copilot’s seat and they’d taken off. The icy tension was coating everything in hoarfrost, making the flight even more uncomfortable than the weather already was.

“Okay, this is ridiculous,” Ravi said, breaking the silence over their comms. Eddie couldn’t see what he was doing, but he figured he was elbowing Chimney based on the way the older man jerked. “Grow a pair and apologize to her. Or at least say something so we don’t all die in awkward silence.”

“I should say something?” Chimney sounded offended. “Why should I say something?”

“It is kind of tense in here,” Eddie helpfully pointed out.

“How is that my fault? How much more proof does she need that we have her back, other than us skipping out on the rest of our shift in order to aid and abet in the virtual theft of an LAFD chopper for an unauthorized jaunt to a foreign land?”

“Who asked you to?” Hen curtly asked.

“Woops, my bad.” Ravi did not sound apologetic. “Forgot we were on an open channel.”

If it was Buck, Eddie might almost believe that, because his husband most definitely would forget that everyone could hear him before speaking over the comms. But since this was Ravi… Eddie leaned forward a bit, and Ravi smiled brightly at him from the other side of Chimney.

“You didn’t do anything wrong,” Hen said, twisting in her seat to face them. “Okay? You don’t have to prove anything to me, so let’s just drop it.”

“No, no, no, we’re not dropping it,” Chimney said, arms folded across his chest as he scowled at the side of Hen’s head; she’d turned back around after saying her piece. “If I didn’t do anything wrong, you didn’t do anything wrong in the first place.”

“Okay.”

“Fine!”

“Good.”

“Good!”

“Come on, guys!” Eddie broke in, having enough of this. “Stop agreeing with each other like you’re fighting. It’s weird, and it’s giving me flashbacks to listening to my sisters argue. Seriously, stop it.”

“So are you guys good?” Ravi asked. “Because being stuck in here while you two fight isn’t making me less regretful about coming along.”

“We were always good,” Hen said.

“So why didn’t you reach out to us?” Chimney asked her.

“Because I knew you’d insist on coming, and I didn’t want you putting everything at risk over some wild hunch that I can’t even prove.”

“And yet, we’re here anyway,” Ravi pointed out. The helicopter shook with a particularly powerful gust of wind, and he glared out the window at the storm. “And I am regretting every moment of it.”

Hen turned around again to fix him with a look.

“Why did you even come along?”

“Buck made me.”

“He didn’t make you.” Eddie rolled his eyes. “He asked, you said yes.”

“Asking implies there was a question involved, or even that he would’ve accepted being told no.”

“C’mon,” Chimney said. “It’s Bobby and Athena.”

Which was exactly what Buck had said, when it seemed like Ravi was about to waver.

“I’m just thankful there was an excuse to keep Buck from coming,” Eddie said, catching another flash of lightning that was close enough for the thunder to rattle everything. “Not sure how well he’d handle being in this weather.”

“What?” Tommy broke in, laughing a little. “Is he afraid of storms?”

“Well he was once killed by lightning, so.”

Tommy let out a little “huh” and didn’t press any further.

Buck sat behind Bobby’s desk, a mug of chamomile tea clutched in his hands. He still didn’t particularly like the taste, but these days, after so many sleepless nights at the station spent with Bobby and the tea he’d made for them both, the smell was comforting. Chamomile was supposed to be relaxing and to help him fall asleep, but even though it was on the wrong side of midnight, he wasn’t actually looking to fall asleep. Not with half his family stuck in a hurricane.

Eddie texted him when they were all in the air and heading to where Bobby and Athena should be. That was over an hour ago, and he hadn’t heard much of anything since. They should be getting to their destination soon, which was - a quick glance at the weather map to confirm - currently being pummeled by a hurricane.

He still didn’t like the taste of chamomile.

“Uh, Cap?”

Buck looked up to see Albert standing at the door. He looked nervous.

“Yeah, Al?”

“You’ve got, uh…”

“Diaz.” Simpson stepped into view, sending Albert scurrying away and Buck leaping to his feet. The fire chief did not look pleased. “I checked the roster earlier, and I noticed that you’re still listed as the acting captain.”

“Well, sir, you see-”

“And,” Simpson interrupted, “I think you’re missing some people.”

“Sir…”

“Unless I’m mistaken and they’re in the bunks? I’m sure if I asked you to have everyone line up for inspection, right now, that you wouldn’t be missing anyone. Right?”

Buck blew out a breath.

“Okay, sir, the truth is…” Buck chewed on his lip, trying not to visibly cringe beneath the chief’s glare. “It’s karma.”

Simpson raised an eyebrow. It was a very judgmental eyebrow.

“Karma?”

“Yes sir. Usually Bobby does the cooking duties, but with him gone, I took over. And how do my friends, my family, my husband, show their gratitude? By going out and getting sushi to eat.”

“Sushi.”

“Yes sir.” Buck nodded frantically. “I slaved away over a hot stove to make enough food, good food, for everyone, and they eat sushi instead. Of course, the joke’s on them, because now they’ve all got food poisoning. The bathroom was an absolute mess, sir. I made Albert clean it, it was his brother and his boyfriend that made most of it, after all. But I saw it and it was just… Awful, sir. The smell, the colors, the texture.”

“So Panikkar, the other Diaz, the older Han, they’re all at home with food poisoning.”

“Yes sir.” He picked up the phone and held it out to the chief. “If you don’t believe me, you could call my house phone. I mean, Abuela’s asleep and she’ll kill us both for waking her up, but she can tell you. And it's cordless, she could even take it into the bathroom so you can hear for yourself.”

The chief’s scowl was tinged with disgust, now, and Buck smiled wider.

“They’re all three out with foot poisoning. Not, instead, on an LAFD Copter? On an unapproved flight across international borders?”

“That sounds like something they’d be fired for, sir,” Buck said. “Eddie and I have a mortgage and children, he wouldn’t do anything that would risk getting himself fired. And Chimney, he’s got a daughter and about to get married and has his own mortgage, so he definitely wouldn’t, either.”

“And Panikkar?”

“Um. He’s smarter than us?”

Chief Simpson did not believe one word of bullshit coming out of Buck’s mouth, that much was clear. But he also didn’t have proof that Buck was lying, so he couldn’t just call him out on it. Instead, he just nodded and marched back out of the office, leaving Buck to collapse back into the chair.

Albert poked his head in a minute later.

“Are you fired?”

“Not yet,” Buck told him. “Ask me again when we get off shift.”

“This should be the general vicinity where the Uno last pinged,” Tommy announced, after what felt like forever.

“Took us ninety minutes to get here, and it’ll take ninety to get home,” Eddie said. “How long can this scud run out here?”

“We have about twelve minutes to play with.”

“That’s not enough time,” Hen complained.

“Unless you feel like swimming back, that’s all we’ve got.”

“LAFD Copter Seventeen-Oh-One, do you read?” asked a woman over the rado. Tommy and Hen shared a look. “Firefighter Kinard, come in, please.”

“Go for Kinard.”

“Hold for Chief Simpson.”

Eddie silently cursed, and was certain the others were doing the same.

“Firefighter Kinard,” the chief said, voice clear in all their ears. “I know you’re not doing what I think you’re doing. Wilson and the missing One Eighteen members better not be with you, either.”

“Um,” Tommy said, and then, “Chief Simpson- Kssssh. Trouble reading- Kssssh. Can’t hear- Kssssssh.”

Then he turned off the radio.

“Think he bought it?” he asked Hen.

“Oh, we are all getting fired.” Ravi already sounded resigned to that future. “Okay, where’s this stupid boat?”

“It should be around here somewhere,” Hen said, doing her best to peer through the storm like the rest of them.

“A cruise ship that size, it should be standing out like a floating Christmas tree,” Chimney complained. “Shouldn’t it?”

“Yeah, except a Cat Five hurricane passed through here,” Tommy pointed out. “We could be miles off target.”

“...is this not the hurricane?” Ravi asked, sounding a bit hesitant.

“This is intermittent showers.”

Eddie had never been through a hurricane, they didn’t really reach El Paso, so he’d take the man’s word on it. He knew Buck had been through a couple, when he was still on the east coast, and Maddie had been in Boston for at least a few hurricane seasons. Apparently, in some places, the severity of a storm was measured not by wind speeds but by whether or not the local Waffle House was serving full meals afterwards.

They flew in circles, trying their best to peer through the darkness and the rain to find what they were looking for. But their window was small and limited, and Tommy eventually called it, turning the radio back on to announce that they were heading back to base.

And then, to the left of them, an orange star rising from the water. 

“That’s it!” Hen cried. “That’s it!”

The joy of finding them didn’t last long, however. Under the light of the flare, they saw the bottom of an ocean liner. With a giant gaping hole in the hull. Tommy sent a mayday over an open emergency line, giving their location and requesting assistance. Then, because they only had so much fuel, they landed on the bottom of the boat.

The gaping hole was their best option for getting in, considering all the actual doors were beneath the water level. Everyone but Tommy got their harnesses on, attached ropes to themselves and to the helicopter - it being the only reliable anchor point they had because, again, they were on the bottom of the boat - and rappelled down into an upside-down room that was steadily filling with water.

That was where they found Athena, along with several others: a woman and man that both looked familiar, the latter with a bandage on his torso; a woman in a life jacket that was being floated on a make-shift raft; two crew members, one that looked to be the ship's captain; and another man lying still in the water.

There was no sign of Bobby.

“Where’s Buck?” was literally the first thing out of Athena’s mouth, after several moments where she kept glancing between their group and the hole they’d come in through, getting more and more visibly confused when no one else came down.

“Where’s Bobby?” Hen shot back, she and Chimney already assessing everyone.

Eddie felt for the pulse man that was simply lying in the water and shook his head at the paramedics.

“Julian fell, trying to shoot off the flare,” she said. “Bobby’s trying to find Eliza’s- That’s her.” She pointed at the woman in the life jacket. “She got crushed by a door, tore her axillary artery, but Bobby’s gone to find her son. Norman there has a gunshot wound-”

“Gunshot?” Ravi blurted out.

“There were pirates,” Athena explained, which created so many more questions.

“Wait, Norman?” Eddie looked closer at the man, realizing why the man looked so familiar. “As in, See Me, Norman? Lola’s Norman?”

“Yup, that’s me.” The guy waved.

“So then that’s…” Eddie glared at the soaking wet woman who he now knew was Lola. “You pointed a gun at my husband.”

“Eddie, Ravi,” Hen said, not bothering to raise her head from Eliza. “Go find Bobby.”

“He went to a maintenance tube,” the captain said. She pointed through a doorway that was above them, because the room they were in had a high ceiling and they were standing on it. “Eliza said her son, Cory, was in their stateroom on deck twelve. The sergeant’s husband was going to use the tube to get to it. There’ll be a ladder and a hatch on the floor. The ceiling, but it’s the floor now.”

“Got it.”

The hatch was easily found, after a short climb to the doorway and a hike through a trashed hall. The hatch to the maintenance tube was closed, the wheel jiggling a little like someone was trying to turn it on the other side. Bobby’s face, when they opened it, was almost comical.

And like his wife, the first question out of his mouth was,

“Where’s Buck?”

“That’s all three,” Eddie bragged to Ravi, who rolled his eyes and reached to grab the kid that was clinging to the ladder beside the fire captain. “I should have put actual money on that bet.”

“You couldn’t say, ‘hi Ravi, thanks for risking your job by flying into the middle of the Pacific to save me’?” Ravi complained. “Or even, ‘oh wow, considering no one even radioed for help, it’s amazing you’re here at all, Ravi.’ No, instead it’s, ‘where’s my son, I’d much rather see him.’”

“Hi, Ravi. Thank you for risking your job by flying into the middle of the Pacific to save me, which is amazing considering no one radioed for help.” Bobby’s tone was utterly deadpan as he allowed Eddie to help him through the hatch. “Is that better?”

“Yes.”

“Now where’s Buck?”

Ravi’s eye roll was almost audible.

“Good news, babe!” Buck cheerfully announced over the phone. “It doesn’t look like we’re getting fired.”

The station’s TV was set to Channel 8, showing Chief Simpson’s press conference about the cruise ship sinking where he was taking full credit for authorizing the rescue mission. As much as Alonzo might’ve held a grudge against him, Buck kinda missed the guy. Simpson seemed to be much more of a politician than a firefighter. Buck briefly wondered if Taylor Kelly was in the throng of reporters, and whether or not it would be morally acceptable to text her that Simpson was straight-up lying about approving anything.

(Sure, they hadn’t really spoken since her book came out, but she’d be happy to have something to make a juicy story out of.)

“That is a relief to hear,” Eddie said, speaking up to be heard over the wind in the background. “I just watched Bobby and Athena have one of those romantic, running to each other in slow motion, moments. Oh, I think Lola’s getting arrested again.”

“Lola?” Buck squinted at nothing. “The Lola who pulled a gun on me after flashing everyone on the highway? See me, Norman? That Lola?”

“The very same,” Eddie confirmed. “She and Norman were on the cruise ship, too. I don’t actually know why she’s being arrested.”

“What, did she actually shoot someone this time?”

“Uh, no. But apparently Norman got shot by a pirate.”

“By a-” Buck shook his head. “We are never going on a cruise ship. Not unless one of us actually decides to kill the other to avoid a divorce.”

“Oh, hell no, mi sol. If we ever go on an actual honeymoon, it’ll be on land.”

Chapter 9: Friends

Chapter Summary

In which Buck is having a minor crisis, and the author maybe gets a little too meta.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“Holy shit,” Buck blurted out when he peered out the window of the fire engine. “It’s the Bachelor Mansion.”

Eddie slapped his hand over his face, grumbling out an “Ay, Dios.”

“You watch it?” Albert laughed at him.

“Unfortunately,” Buck’s faithless husband complained.

Just like with Speed Wedding, Eddie was often forced to either sit through watching the Bachelor with Buck or find something else to do. Buck didn’t feel very guilty about it, because he sat through - okay, slept through - plenty of baseball games for his husband, so the man could suffer through an hour of cheesy dating shows once or twice a week.

With Speed Wedding, it was Athena whom he texted with about what was going on. But with the Bachelor, it was Maddie. Which, actually…

“Hey, Cap.” Buck leaned forward as best he could. Because he knew two things: there were several stations much closer to the mansion than them, and Maddie was working tonight. Normally, those two things were entirely unrelated, but they didn’t normally get sent to the filming location of a show that she was a fan of. “D’ya know who was the dispatcher that took the call?”

Bobby looked up from the tablet, mouth twitching like he was trying not to smile at something.

“Your sister.”

“That explains it.”

When the engine and the ambulance pulled to a stop in front of the mansion, they were greeted by various production crew members. Off to the side and out of the way was a group of women dressed for a fancy date.

“What’s her name?” Bobby asked after they’d hopped out.

“Bailey,” the host answered. “She’s a superfan. She took Conchata’s spot.”

“Took her spot, how?”

“Stole the limo when the driver was ten-one.”

“That means he was in the bathroom,” Buck helpfully translated for his friends.

“Hey, Bailey, are you ready to meet the firefighters who are here to rescue you?” the host asked, talking down to the woman who glued herself to the pavement in front of the mansion.

“I don’t need rescuing,” the woman insisted, calling for the bachelor’s attention while the paramedics looked her over.

“What kind of glue did you use?” Hen asked her.

“I’m not telling you.”

Bailey didn’t have to. The bottle of it was sitting, open, on the ground near her. Buck picked it up and took a cautious sniff.

“Ugh. Smells like industrial-grade stuff,” he said, letting eddie take it from his hand for his own sniff-test.

“Yeah,” he agreed. “I don’t know if we have anything on the truck that’ll break this down.”

“Let me break it down for you.” The pair turned, finding the group of women had gathered behind them. “If we don’t get lucky in the mansion, we might have better chance at the fire station.”

“I love dalmatians,” one woman said, trying and failing to sound seductive. Another one, dressed like a flight attendant for some reason, followed it up with,

“I feel so lightheaded, I’m having palpitations.”

“Sorry ladies.” Eddie did not sound sorry at all. “We’re taken.”

He held up his hand, wiggling the finger with the bright red silicone wedding band he wore. 

“But uh, hold that thought.” Buck turned, spotted his target, and shouted, “Toby! C’mere!”

Looking wary and somewhat confused, the driver-engineer made his way over to them. When he got close enough, Buck grabbed him and tugged him forward. The guy let out a startled yelp as he was thrust in front of both Diazes.

“This is Toby,” Buck told the ladies, gripping him by the shoulders. “He’s a nice Jewish boy, single, drives the engine and does the maintenance on it.”

The group of women all looked very intrigued. And also very predatory, all of a sudden. It was honestly more than a bit frightening..

“What’s going on?” Toby asked.

“Don’t worry about it,” Eddie told him with a pat on the back.

“Just have fun, and be yourself,” Buck said.

They abandoned him to the tender, possibly non-existent mercies of the women and made their way back over to Bailey and the others. Hen was conferring with Bobby while Chimney and Albert kept track of their patient’s vitals.

“There’s significant dermatitis,” Hen was reporting when the Diazes joined them. “Given the large area of glue to skin, she could be soaking up a lot of toxins.”

“Diphenhydramine keep her from anaphylaxis?” Bobby asked.

“It should.”

“If she’s got dermatitis, she won’t react well to acetone,” Eddie said. “Or anything we have on the truck.”

“So how do we get her off this driveway, then?” Buck asked, frowning down at the puzzle before them.

“We don’t,” Bobby answered. “We travel her with the driveway.”

Buck could feel the grin growing on his face, with a matching one on Eddie’s.

“Jackhammers coming up,” he declared, skipping off to the engine and to the compartments where they kept the big toys- Sorry, the heavy-duty tools stored.

“You practically danced in place like Hoover whenever he hears someone say walk,” Eddie teased, dragging out the spare turn-out coats and helmets and gloves. “If you actually had a tail, it would be wagging.”

“Like you’re any better,” Buck shot back, setting the jackhammers to lean against the engine as he shrugged on the coat. “Hey, should we get Toby? Let him show off a bit?”

Toby was still entertaining the group of hopeful bachelor contestants. It looked like they were trying to swarm him and, while what they were saying didn’t carry over to where Buck was, he could tell that Toby’s face was nearly as red as his hair.

“I think we’d need the Jaws just to get him away from them,” Eddie said, and Buck threw his head back to laugh.

When they got back to Bailey, suited up and jackhammers ready, Chimney was nowhere to be found. Hen was holding the bag of fluids that they were running, and Albert was tracking her vitals, and the bachelor was talking with Bailey, keeping her calm. They all three moved as much out of the way as they were able when Buck and Eddie started ruining the mansion’s property value.

They were only part way through when Chimney shouted for assistance, because he’d found an unconscious Conchata in the backseat of a limo. Bailey had ambushed her with chloroform when she stole the vehicle. They called another ambulance for her, because there was no way she was going to fit in theirs with a chunk of a driveway shoved into the back of it. Also, it was still against the regulations to put a person on the same bus as their victim.

A still very red-faced Toby drove them back to the station afterwards, and he refused to tell them whether or not he got any of the ladies’ numbers.

Apparently, when Eddie went off with the others to rescue Bobby and Athena from a sinking cruise ship, he’d made a new friend. Specifically, he’d made a new friend in the form of the guy that Buck replaced at the 118, Tommy Kinard. Apparently, he was the one who flew them through the storm, which was pretty cool.

It was also pretty awkward, because… Well, Tommy had been over to Casa de Diaz thrice in the two weeks since then. And the very first time he was over, Buck answered the door to greet him, only for the man to stare at his face - at the birthmark above his eye - and then point at him while blurting out,

“Firehose!”

“Um. Hi?” Really fucking awkward.

“You don’t recognize me at all, do you?” Tommy asked. At least he sounded amused rather than offended.

“No, sorry.”

Buck felt guilty for it, but also inappropriately proud of himself. After all, if the guy called him Firehose, that meant it was someone he’d hooked up with as Buck 1.0. And apparently he’d left a big enough impression for Tommy to remember him literally years later. Feeling a little smug was allowed, right?

At the start, everything was fine. Tommy was cool, in Buck’s opinion. Mostly. He didn’t seem to really know what to do with Gavin and he insisted on calling him “Evan” rather than Buck. The first time Chris heard Tommy call him by his actual name, he turned in his seat on the couch and asked,

“Who the fuck is Evan?”

“Chris!” Eddie yelped, while Buck choked on his own spit and Tommy awkwardly laughed. “Language!”

Chris, in full teenage fashion, rolled his eyes with a dramatic huff.

“Fine, sorry,” he said. “¿Quién carajo es Evan?”

“That was not what I meant,” Eddie groaned, covering his face. “And I think we need to start monitoring your calls with your tias.”

“Please,” Buck scoffed. “He probably learned it from Pepa.”

“I meant your, uh… Your other dad,” Tommy said, sounding a bit unsure.

Christopher made a face at him.

“Pops’ name is Buck.”

Chris knew that Evan was his name, but he also wasn’t wrong, because the only people who regularly called him by that were his parents and his therapist. Hell, they’d used “Buck” in the wedding vows. It would be like someone popping up and calling Chimney, “Howard.”

That was not his name, Buck didn’t care what his official documents said.

Besides those two hiccups, Tommy was mostly okay. Tommy himself wasn’t the problem, not really. No, the problem was that Eddie and Christopher were both so damn enamored with the man. It seemed like Buck had been hearing nonstop about the guy since Eddie got back from helping rescue Bobby and Athena, and Christopher joined after the first time Tommy came over. 

Tommy flies the helicopter, isn’t that cool? Tommy was in the army, too, Buck, how cool is that? He likes watching two half-naked guys beat each other up! He’s into cars! He, too, is absolutely wrong in his opinion about Revenge of the Sith being better than Return of the Jedi. What kind of psycho even thinks that?

(Nevermind that Tommy had been agreeing with Chris when he said it. But Chris was young, he still had time to grow out of his objectively incorrect opinions.)

And! And! If Tommy was so cool, why wasn’t he on the list of wedding guests, huh? Some guy named Eli, in Boston, was invited, but not Tommy.

“So where’s Eddie now?” Maddie asked, when there was a break in Buck’s ranting. She’d come over with Jee-Yun, to let the two rugrats chase Hoover around in the backyard. Chimney, apparently, had dragged Albert out for brotherly bonding or something.

“With Tommy,” Buck said, ignoring the way his sister made a face at the derisive way he said the man’s name. “Apparently he’s buddies with the promoter for some fight in Vegas, and he got tickets for ringside seats.”

The invitation had been extended to Buck, as well, but he actually wasn’t a fan of watching two guys beat the shit out of each other. If he wanted to watch two buff, mostly nude guys homoerotically pin each other on the ground, he had the internet for that. Or he could just do it with Eddie, but skip all the fighting parts.

But, perhaps even more importantly, Buck did not feel like sitting through something that he held little interest in and also be ignored by his husband the entire time in favor of someone else. There was only so much of that he could deal with before he started throwing a tantrum and biting people like Gavin.

(Gavin had only done it the one time so far, thankfully, but it had made his birthday party last week - which Tommy hadn’t made an appearance at despite the invitation Buck knew Eddie had extended to him - a bit awkward. At least the parent of the other kid, who went to the same daycare as Jee and who was one of the ones Maddie had recruited when Buck expressed his worries about Gavin not having enough friends his own age, had been understanding in the face of Buck’s apologetic fretting.)

“When did they leave?” Maddie asked. “It’s a six hour drive there, isn’t it?”

“Tommy flew them.”

Buck glanced over at her in time to see his sister looking at him weirdly, her mouth downturned in a curious little frown.

“What?” he demanded.

“Is everything okay?” she asked.

“Everything’s fine,” he insisted. “He’s allowed to have other friends. Even stupid ones with stupid opinions.”

“Oh yeah.” Maddie scoffed. “Everything definitely sounds fine.”

“Whatever, shut up,” he told her, and then changed the topic. “Hey, has Dad called you recently?”

“I have both of their numbers blocked,” she said with a shrug. “Why, don’t you?”

“No, I, uh. I never got around to it.” More like Buck, even now, couldn’t quite crush that childish spark of hope that lingered even now. “But um. He’s been calling me. A lot.”

“Why?”

“I don’t know, I haven’t been answering and he hasn’t left any messages,” he said. “But they’ve all been late at night, really late. For someone in Pennsylvania, I mean. And he just started, like, just after Ma and Pops went on their cruise.”

“Maybe he sensed his competition was out of town,” Maddie suggested.

Buck barked out a laugh that cut off early when his phone began to ring. For a moment, he had the insane thought that Phillip had known he was talking about him, somehow. But no, that wasn’t his ringtone. It was, in point of fact, a video call from TK.

“Hello there, sunshine,” was how his friend greeted him. “Where’s your other half?”

“Uh, not here right now. Maddie is though.” Buck angled his phone so his sister could wave. “What’s up?”

“That’s too bad. Uh, so remember when you were talking to me about all those random people sending you follow requests on Instagram?”

“Yeah, why?”

“I think I know why,” Marjan’s voice came from off-screen, initially. There was a moment of dizzying motion, and then she was right there. “One of my followers noticed we follow eachother on it-”

“Why would anyone care that Firefox is following me?”

“Okay, see, funny story.” It must’ve been funny, because she was smirking as she said it. “TK said you are, or were, friends with some news report named Taylor Kelly?”

“What does Taylor have to do with this?”

“Not her,” TK broke in to say. “Not precisely. But the book she wrote about you guys.”

“Alright. What about it?”

Buck was not expecting the answer that his friends gave him. He was not expecting the link that TK sent him. He definitely wasn’t expecting what he saw, when he grabbed his laptop to pull it up. He simply sat at the kitchen table, Jee-Yun and Gavin having been moved inside, and stared at the laptop screen.

“So,” Maddie said. She'd pulled a chair as close to his side as possible to look as well. “You’re Oliver-”

“Yes.”

“And Eddie’s Ryan-”

“Yes.”

“And these stories,” she said, finger hovering just over the screen, which was currently showing the webpage full of stories. “They’re all about the two of you.”

“Yes,” Buck said for a third time, unable to tear his eyes away.

Maddie threw her head back and laughed.

Chapter End Notes

Xzibit voice: "I heard you like fanfics, so I put fanfics inside your fanfic."

Chapter 10: They Aren't Going Into the Sewer, Ravi

Chapter Summary

In which someone gets stuck in a drain, and there's a small (adopted) family reunion.

“So all these people,” Eddie said, hopping out of the engine. “They’re fans of that awful book?”

“Yup,” Buck said, opening up the compartment so they could get their kits.

Eddie had come in too late last night for Buck to give him the full rundown of what TK and Marjan had told him, and of what he’d seen, but that just meant everyone else got to hear about the insanity at the same time. At the moment, they were responding to a - according to Maddie - person supposedly stuck down the drain at a restaurant. So simple and easy, Bobby hadn’t even bothered coming with them.

“What book?” Albert asked, joining them with medkit already in hand; he’d ridden over in the ambulance.

“I think they’re talking about Taylor Kelly’s,” Ravi answered.

“What happened to the copy we had at the station?”

“It got set on fire,” Buck said, closing the compartment door. “Bobby claimed it was for a demonstration.”

“Yeah, yeah, whatever.” Eddie started leading them inside. “And these people, they’re fans of us.”

“Of Oliver and Ryan,” Buck corrected.

“Okay, yeah, but they know they’re us,” his husband counted. “Because Taylor pretty much directed everyone to that first piece she did on us. And they’ve been writing stories. About us.”

“Ohoho, have they been writing stories.” Buck smiled at him when Eddie held the door open for him. “So many stories. So many… Stories.”

“I don’t think I like the emphasis you put there,” Albert said, but they had to go into professional mode before Buck could explain himself.

The employee who made the call met them just inside and let them into the kitchen. The drain in question was attached to an industrial sink. Buck, Ravi, and Albert checked around it - the pipes, the sink itself - just in case, while Eddie used their handheld thermal imager to look for someone trapped behind a wall. But they were all coming up with bupkis.

“I’m not seeing or hearing anything in the walls,” Eddie said, while Buck peered down the sink drain. “Sure the voice didn’t come from the restaurant? Lots of metal here to reflect the sound.”

“No, no,” the worker denied. “It wasn’t like a customer was demanding more marinara sauce, okay? It was more… Plaintive, though not wistful.” That was oddly specific. “And it wasn’t coming from the walls, it was coming from inside the sink.”

“Not sure that’s possible,” Ravi told the guy as he pulled out from under the sink and stood back up. “The drainpipe’s only three inches.”

“Didn’t you guys rescue a baby from a pipe once?” Albert asked.

“It was a preemie,” Buck said. “And it didn’t speak, more cried. A lot.”

He remembered they’d thought the guy was hallucinating it because of drugs, when they first showed up. Which, he’d been friends with enough people who worked in kitchens as he bounced across the hemisphere. It wasn’t entirely out of the realm of possibility that-

“Help!”

They all froze, a bit like deer when a twig snapped suddenly. All their ears were perked up, heads snapping to attention.

“Do you hear that?” Eddie needlessly asked. Buck nodded, as did the others.

“Somebody!” It was muffled, distant. Kinda tinny. And it was coming from… “Please! Somebody help me, please!”

Buck stared at the drain in the bottom of the sink’s basin, where the voice was coming from. What in the world?

“You’re not going to believe this.” It was the restaurant manager, Buck was pretty sure. She held out a tablet for them to look at. “I just pulled this off the security camera.”

Ravi took it from her and they crowded around him to look, Albert having to crane his neck to peer over his boyfriend’s shoulder. On the tablet, they watched as a man on a motorized scooter was hit by a car, knocked over, and then went right into a storm drain.

“Did he just slide into the sewer?” Ravi voiced the disbelief they were all thinking. And then, when they watched the car drive off without checking on the guy, he did it again with a derisive,  “Seriously?”

“I already called the cops,” the manager informed them.

At least, after that, it was easy to locate the guy. There was a manhole just outside the restaurant. They grabbed a hook from the engine to tug it free, revealing their victim down at the bottom. And also hitting them with the smell of, well sewage that had Buck scrunching up his nose.

“Thank God!” the man declared, voice echoing a little. “I was starting to think you’d never find me.”

“Are you hurt?” Eddie asked, raising his voice to be heard without needing to put his face anywhere close to the opening.

“Just twisted my leg!”

“We’ll get you right out of there,” Buck told the guy.

“Please hurry!” the guy replied. “I don’t even want to think about what I’m sitting in.”

“Guess someone’s going into the sewer,” Ravi said.

Buck and Eddie stared at him and Albert.

“Yes,” Buck agreed with a nod. “One of you is going into the sewer.”

The pair groaned, and then Ravi groaned again when Albert started pouting at him.

“I guess I’m going into the sewer,” he grumbled, rolling his eyes.

“I’ll make it up to you!” Albert promised. “After a shower.” He took a sniff of the air and grimaced. “Maybe two showers.”

Albert helped his boyfriend get geared up to go down, with the helmet and the harness. Ravi complained the entire time about the unfairness of it, but Buck and Eddie just ignored him. It could be worse, the guy could’ve been stuck in a septic tank. That was a smell that clung, Buck knew that from unfortunate experience.

After Ravi lowered himself into the hole, precariously clutching the backboard as he climbed down the ladder, the others went to get the wench and pulley set up. Buck grabbed the large tripod from where they had it sitting out and waiting, carrying it over to the manhole. Eddie and Albert helped him stand it up.

“Okay, so I actually am wondering.” Albert kicked one of the tripod legs a bit further out. “What kind of ‘stories’ are these people writing?”

Eddie’s face scrunched up, but Buck knew he was just as curious as the rest of them.

“Maddie and I read a few,” Buck answered. “They were actually… Kinda good? But uh. Some of them got a bit spicy.”

Maddie had stolen the laptop from him, laughing until she was red in the face as she read one out loud.

“Spicy how?” Eddie demanded.

“Um.” He tried to figure out how to put this delicately while he attached the pulley and rope to the tripod stand. “Well. They had, um. Interesting and descriptive ideas about the, um. The roles we fill- Uh, our roles in the-” Buck let out a sigh. “They wrote about us having sex.”

Albert let out a loud guffaw, hurriedly clapping his hand over his mouth. It did next to nothing for muffling the laughter. Eddie, meanwhile, was red-faced and slack jawed.

“Wh- Wh-” Eddie stammered, seemingly having trouble getting the word out. “Why would they…?”

“Babe.” Buck clapped his hands on Eddie’s shoulders. “They’ve probably seen the puff piece Taylor did on us. They know what we look like. That’s why.”

Eddie was a goddamn snack, Buck would always be the first to say so. And, however vain it was to say it, Buck knew he himself was just as attractive. They both put work into their bodies, and it showed.

“I feel so violated.” Eddie sounded haunted.

Buck wasn’t surprised. His husband hadn’t reacted well to the idea of their sex life being a topic of conversation between Buck and TK, there was absolutely no way he’d be comfortable with the concept of people, utter strangers, writing about it. Okay, sure, they were writing about the sex lives of two characters in a book, but those characters were just them with different names.

“How are we looking down there, Rav?” a still laughing Albert called down.

“Vitals are good!” Ravi called back up. “No sign of spinal or TBI, I’m splinting the leg now!”

“What are you going to do?” Albert asked them. “Y’know, since people are apparently writing porn of the two of you.”

“Ay Dios.” Eddie groaned. “Why did you…”

“I have no idea,” Buck admitted as he worked the pulley and fed the rope into the manhole for Ravi. “Oh, God, I’m gonna have to tell Pops about it.”

That was going to be an awkward as hell conversation.

“Changing topics, please, God, let us talk about something else,” Eddie just about begged, and then nudged Buck's shoulder. “You shoulda come with us yesterday. Just to ride in the chopper, too, if nothing else.”

“Where did you ride a chopper to?” Albert asked him.

“Vegas,” Eddie answered off-handedly. “Seriously, we were so close to the ring, it felt like I was the one throwing punches. You were missed.”

Right, sure.

“Maybe next time,” Buck said, without much feeling.

Albert frowned and glanced between them, but when he opened his mouth, he was interrupted by Ravi shouting,

“I’m ready to come up!”

“Copy that!” Eddie replied, heading to the wench. He pressed a button and it started winding up the rope. “What about Wednesday? He’s invited us to some karaoke bar trivia. Random trivia is practically your specialty.”

“Sure,” Buck agreed as he made sure the line stayed steady. “Sounds like it’ll be fun.”

He wasn’t sure he’d ever told a lie so big before.

The worst thing, perhaps, about going to so much therapy and being on meds that worked was that Buck could recognize when his mind was about to drag him down a spiral of insecurities. Sure, yeah, okay, that was usually a good thing, but not always. The sad fact was that just because Buck knew when it was happening, it didn’t mean he was capable of pulling the brakes on the train ride to hell.

Thankfully, he had a support system. Dr. Copeland was really big on those, and identifying everyone in his support system was the very first exercise she had him do after he told her about the darker details of his post-lightning strike coma dream. Normally, when he realized he was the cusp of a spiral or even in the middle of one, he went to Eddie or to Maddie, sometimes Bobby or Athena, and let them be the outside voice of reason when his internal one was failing him.

Except, this time, Buck couldn’t bring himself to go to one of them. It all sounded so childish and immature and, frankly, stupid even to himself. There was no way he’d be able to go to any of them for this. Not his sister, not his not-quite-parents, and especially not Eddie. Luckily, he did have someone else that he could go to.

He made an excuse in-between calls, heading up to the station roof with his tablet after making sure she was available. Then he got himself comfortable in one of the lawn chairs they kept up there as he waited for the video call to come in, answering it before the first ring.

“Hello, Evan.” Copeland was frowning in obvious concern. She was sitting at the desk in her office, Buck could tell. Her framed degree was hanging on the wall just behind her head. “Is everything okay?”

“Yeah- I mean no, I-” Buck let out a sigh. “I need to talk to you about something, and I didn’t want to do it with Eddie there. And I don’t want to tell him I want to talk to you alone, because then he’ll get worried and-”

“Alright,” the doctor said. “That is certainly a cause for concern to begin with. What is it that’s troubling you, then?”

“God, it sounds so dumb,” he told her, already cringing. “I think my best friend has a new best friend.”

On the screen, Copeland blinked and tilted her head in quiet befuddlement.

“This certainly sounds like your insecurities regarding being replaced,” she said. “And something easily addressed simply by talking with Eddie. But you know this.”

“I- I do, it’s just…” Buck let out a sigh. “It’s different this time? I mean, it’s not, but it is!”

“Okay…”

“See, it’s… God, it’s gonna sound so stupid.”

“Don’t worry about that, it’s not stupid if it’s bothering you enough to ask for an emergency session.”

That did help a little, actually.

“Okay.” Buck wetted his lips. “See, Shannon was Eddie’s best friend first, before they were anything else. And then I was his best friend first, before we were anything else. And now he’s got a new best friend…”

“Oh,” was her simple response to that, and then, “Why don’t you tell me more about what’s been going on?”

Harry Grant had apparently decided to fly into LA and surprise his mother and step-father, sneaking into the house and waking them up before Bobby’s shift with breakfast. It certainly caught Buck by surprise, because Harry hadn’t mentioned it at all in the Adopted Nash Siblings chat. And the timing was weird, because Buck could’ve sworn that Harry’s spring break was just ending, not starting.

Or he was just mistaken, that was extremely likely.

Regardless, Buck and the Grant kids made plans to get together, dragging Albert along with them for proper Adopted Nash Sibling bonding time. Buck had very little qualms about leaving Eddie to fend for himself with Abuela and the kids, all things considered. Eddie flew out to Vegas with Tommy to watch fights, Buck went to the movies with non-blood family. It all evened out, probably.

(Copeland had done her best with convincing him to talk to Eddie, which he absolutely should do. But being worried about his husband having a new best friend made Buck feel so stupid and childish, and the fear sprouting up from it was just insulting to the man. Eddie wasn’t cheating on him, Eddie would never cheat on him.)

After the movie, they found a place to eat that wouldn’t break Buck’s wallet, since he was the one paying for it. This meant they went to an In-and-Out, which none of them complained about. 

“I don’t like this,” May complained as they found a table to sit at. “I’m the shortest, now.”

She was. Harry had shot up in his time away, reaching Buck’s height. May looked tiny compared to the two of them and Albert.

“You can complain about it with Maddie, later,” Buck told her.

“I will,” she declared with a sniff, holding her nose in the air. She didn’t even glance Harry’s way when she smacked his hand away from her fries. “Hey, how’s Chris doing?”

“Oh! That’s a good question!” Harry leaned forward, putting his elbows on the table. “He mentioned a girlfriend - Penny? - but when I ask about her he keeps going quiet.”

“Ah, yeah.” Buck grimaced as he unwrapped his burger. “They went on a single date and broke up.”

“Date that bad?”

“We all wish that was the problem,” Albert commented, squirting an excessive amount of ketchup next to his fries. 

The Grant siblings turned their gaze on Buck with the exact same kind of expectant stare as their mother. Even the eyebrow raise was the same. He was used to it coming from May, not so much from Harry.

“There were other girls,” Buck said. “They didn’t know about each other.”

Harry sucked in a breath between his teeth.

“Oh man,” he said, shaking his head. “Penny found out?”

“Yeah…”

“How bad was it?” May asked, sounding genuinely concerned.

“He wasn’t tarred and feathered, so it could’ve gone worse,” Buck said. “But when I went to the PTO meeting last month, I had two of their mothers giving me death glares. He apologized, and now he’s keeping his head down until it blows over.”

“This is why you boys need sisters. Harry would never do something like that.” May turned and gave her brother a pointed glare. “Right?”

“I would never,” Harry hurried to say, shaking his head frantically.
