“Good.”

“How long are you going to be in town, Harry?” Albert asked him.

“Uh.” Harry scratched at the back of his neck in sudden awkwardness. “I don’t know. A few days, maybe? Dad’s busy with some project in Haiti…”

That had May frowning, face scrunched up like she was trying to work out a problem. Whatever she was thinking, though, she kept it to herself. Instead, she shook her head and said,

“Maddie mentioned something about Taylor’s book?”

Buck let out a groan; Albert threw his head back and laughed.

Chapter 11: Trivial Matters

Chapter Summary

In which the boys talk to Bobby about their growing popularity, Buck feels like a third wheel at a trivia night, and Hen meddles.

What was facing them might not have been the most awkward conversation of their lives, but neither Buck nor Eddie were looking forward to it. When they arrived at the station at the start of their shift, already dressed in their uniforms, they stashed their work bags into their locker and hunted down Bobby. The captain was upstairs, talking to Hen while he finished up breakfast.

“Good morning, Diazes,” was how he greeted them.

“Yeah, good morning,” Buck mumbled, sharing a glance with Eddie. “Uh, could we talk to you, Cap?”

“It’s kinda important,” Eddie added.

Bobby and Hen frowned at each other, and then frowned at the pair of them.

“Of course,” Bobby said, turning off the burner on the stove and setting the spatula he was using to the side. “Here or the office?”

“Um.” Buck glanced at Eddie again, who shrugged. “Office?”

The captain’s frown grew even more pronounced.

The trip to the office was short and silent, with Bobby leading the way and the Diazes following like ducklings. Bobby closed the door behind them when they entered and, after a moment’s hesitation, went to sit behind his desk while they sat in the other two seats.

“So what is it you needed to talk to me about?” he asked them.

“Well, uh.” Buck scratched at an ear. “You remember Taylor’s book?”

A shadow fell over Bobby’s face.

“Yes,” he said.

“Apparently it’s gotten popular,” Eddie said. “It has… Fans.”

“And they’re fans of us,” Buck added.

Bobby glanced between them, eyebrows steadily rising.

“Fans of us,” he repeated.

“Well- Well, fans of us,” Buck reiterated, gesturing at himself and his husband. “Big fans, apparently. Creative… Fans…”

“They’re writing porn of us, Cap,” Eddie blurted out. 

Buck slapped a hand over his face while Bobby’s own went through every stage of grief in rapid progression.

“Porn,” he repeated dully.

“Not just porn,” Buck said. “And it’s not really porn of us, it’s porn of the characters in the book…”

He fell silent at the look Eddie gave him, allowing what he was saying to trail off and die.

“Marjan and TK were the ones who told him about the stories ,” Eddie told Bobby. “But we’ve both been getting a lot of people trying to follow us on social media recently that we don’t recognize.”

“Everything’s private,” Buck hurried to remind Bobby, when the man sat up looking worried. “And we’ve just been rejecting them, ‘cause we didn’t know who they were. But we figured we should tell you.”

Bobby let out a sigh that already sounded exhausted, considering their shift was just starting.

“I’ll call someone in the chief’s office,” Bobby said. “Let it be their problem. No one’s started accosting us on calls, at least. Have there been any pictures of you two posted anywhere? Or the boys?”

Well that was a scary thought. Eddie clearly agreed, with how hard his expression turned.

“Um,” Buck said, no longer quite as amused and flattered by all this as he had been. “I- I don’t know? TK sent me the link to the stories, but we haven’t really gone looking for anything else.”

“Good, don’t,” Bobby told them, already reaching for the phone on his desk. “The department will have someone do it, and hopefully it won’t be anything beyond awkward for all of us involved.”

“And what about in the meantime?” Eddie asked.

“Keep doing what you’ve been doing,” the captain said. “Someone in the Community Liaison Office will probably want to talk to you both.”

Oh, joy.

The karaoke bar that Tommy invited them out to was in the middle of downtown, and it wasn’t one Buck had been to before. Finding a parking spot on a late Wednesday afternoon wasn’t as much of a pain as it might’ve been during the weekend or later in the day. When they went inside, they found Tommy waving to them from a booth off to the side. 

The night didn’t go too terribly bad, not like Buck was worried it might. He’d gone into it expecting to end up a third wheel next to Eddie and his new bestie, but that wasn’t what happened. Not entirely, anyway. Sure, he’d ended up listening to them going on about the fight they went to and whatever else, but to Eddie’s - and also Tommy’s, if Buck was feeling charitable - credit, he did do his best to include Buck in everything. For the most part, at least.

It also helped that, since Buck was the designated driver for between them for the evening - he tried not to drink too much ever since starting the bupropion, because he knew his luck and he didn’t want to push it - Eddie was free to drink however much he wanted. And while a tipsy Eddie wasn’t nearly as bad as a drunken Buck, he still ended up pressed into Buck’s space even more than usual. Feeling like a third wheel was very tolerable when Buck’s husband was practically in his lap.

Plus, Buck knew most of the answers to the trivia questions, including some of the more recent pop culture ones. Hell, he even knew the answer to a baseball one, which was entirely Eddie’s fault.

When the guy running the trivia game announced a break at half-time, Eddie announced he was running to the bathroom. Which left Buck alone with Tommy, which was a bit awkward, all things considered. Buck had been too busy nursing a grudge against the man for taking his spot as Eddie’s best friend, and so he hadn’t made much effort to get to know him during the times he was over at the house.

The uncomfortable silence left behind in Eddie’s wake was broken by Tommy.

“So,” he said, drawing the word out. “I kinda figured calling yourself Firehose back then was a bragging thing, I didn’t realize it was a pun.”

“I didn’t come up with it,” Buck said with a shrug, though he did happily use it.

“Better than whatever I was using back then, probably “Discreet” or “DL,” or something like that.”

“I’m guessing you weren’t out at work, either.”

“Nah, the One Eighteen was a pretty regressive place under Captain Gerrard.”

Buck started to nod and then paused, frowning. Because while neither Hen nor Chimney liked to talk about Gerrard all that much, they’d told him enough over the years that he knew Gerrard didn’t last very long once Hen got there. After that, there was a procession of captains until they got Bobby, who was there for a year or two before Buck.

“Even after he was gone?” he asked. “Hen got me a cake when she found out, and Bobby and everyone else have always been fine with it.”

“I was still trying to figure myself out,” Tommy said. “It was only after I transferred to Harbor that I stopped lying about who I was. Hell, I dated a woman at dispatch for a while. That was a mistake.”

“So did I!” Buck plucked a fry out of the basket he and Eddie were sharing. “And then she went off to Europe and came back engaged, so.”

“Ouch.”

Buck just shrugged as he munched on the fry. He was long over Abby, by this point. 

“I’m glad you came this time,” Tommy told him with a smile. “And not just because you somehow knew who the “Unsinkable Molly Brown” was.”

“I know a bunch of random trivia.” 

Though that one wasn’t truly random: Athena and Bobby nearly died when their cruise ship sank, so of course Buck would end up down a rabbit hole that ended with him reading up on the Titanic.

“Oh, I know,” Tommy said. “Eddie wouldn’t shut up about it when I mentioned tonight to him.”

“So that’s why I got invited,” Buck joked. Or tried to, anyways. The words felt like they fell flat out of his mouth. “For my trivia knowledge.”

“Well.” Tommy’s smile morphed into a smirk and he leaned forward a bit, planting his elbow on the table and his chin on his fist. “Not just for your trivia knowledge.”

Buck opened his mouth to ask him what else he could be there for, when Tommy and Eddie could both clearly enjoy themselves just fine without him. But by that point, Eddie had returned from the bathroom. He slid into the booth beside Buck, pressing into his space and kissing him in greeting, like Eddie had been gone for five years rather than just five minutes.

The final, game winning question of the night was MMA related, and of course Tommy knew the answer. Sure, they won because of it, but the cost was having to listen to Eddie praise Tommy for knowing the answer. Puke.

Part of the problem, which Buck was being forced to acknowledge, was the fact that he wasn’t used to having to share Eddie’s attention anymore. As the man liked to tell him, often and repeatedly, he was Eddie’s sun. Sure, yeah, he didn’t get every last bit of his husband’s attention all the time, there were portions of it dedicated to Chris and to Gavin and to their patients on scenes, but it was never a difficult thing for him to gain when he wanted it. They both just gravitated to one another whenever they were near, constantly aware of and tuned into the other. 

A glance and a smile was usually all Buck needed.  Or, if the mood struck and he didn’t feel like being subtle, a piece of clothing thrown at his husband’s head. Really, though, Buck didn’t have to do anything to get Eddie to pay attention to him.

Or at least that had been the case before Tommy.

It started when they had a bit of downtime in their shift and they decided to hit the gym. Except, as they got to the locker room to change into clothes to work out in, Eddie’s phone began to ring. Eddie sat down on the bench and answered with a,

“Hey.”

Buck would like to at least pretend that he was a full-grown adult, with a job and kids and a mortgage. He managed it at first, but when he took off his pants to put on his shorts and Eddie didn’t even glance his way, Buck felt his inner brat rear its ugly head. Eddie just smiled and nodded at him as he walked past, as he walked right in front of him, on the way to the station’s gym.

But that was fine. Eddie had a clear line of sight to watch him as he worked up a sweat, if only he would look Buck’s way. It was fine. His husband would forget all about stupid Tommy and pay attention to him. He would!

Except he didn’t. Not even when Buck loaded up the weights on the barbell and started lifting on the bench. No, Eddie was too busy laughing at something on the phone.

“Do you need a spot?” Ravi asked him. He was coming from the loft stairs, Albert by his side.

“No,” Buck gritted out. “I’m good, thanks.”

Ravi and Albert frowned at each other, turned to look at Eddie in the locker room, glanced at each other again, and then walked away to leave Buck to stew in his attention-starved aggravation. He slid the bar back onto its rack and checked to see if- Nope, Eddie still wasn’t even looking his way.

Buck threw his head back with a sigh.

“Okay.” That was Chimney; apparently Albert and Ravi had fetched him and Hen. “What is this? What is happening here?”

“Nothing,” Buck said, rubbing his hands down his face.

“It sure doesn’t look like nothing,” Albert remarked.

Buck was about to tell them all where to stuff it, but then Eddie got up off the bench in the locker room and started walking. Buck sat up, feeling his metaphorical tail start to wag, only to immediately droop when his husband jogged up the stairs to the station’s loft without even looking his way.

“Who is he on the phone with?” Ravi asked.

“Tommy,” Buck spat out, with enough vitriol that the others leaned back to avoid being hit by it. 

“Aw, is Eddie not paying enough attention to you?” Hen teased. “Were you trying to get him to come over by working up a sweat?”

It hit a little close to home, so Buck just shrugged and looked away from them.

“If it makes you feel better, it caught our attention,” Albert told him, gesturing between himself and Ravi.

Buck shrugged again. It did make the bratty gremlin in his chest feel a little better, actually, but still.

There was a silent, half-whispered conversation between the other four. He didn’t really pay attention, too busy sulking, but it ended with Hen sending the other three away. Then she perched herself on the end of the bench he was stradling.

“I once made a mistake,” she said, “of rushing to a conclusion without actually talking to either of you-”

“He’s not cheating on me, Hen,” he said, cutting her off to glare at her. Not that she seemed to be affected by it any.

“Good. But there’s clearly something wrong. Since when has he been talking to Tommy?”

“Since he flew you guys to Mexico.” Buck shrugged. “He’s cool, I guess. Chris likes him.”

“Al said he flew Eddie to Vegas.”

“To see some fight,” he said. “And we went to a trivia game yesterday.”

Hen slowly nodded, eyes narrowed and focused on him like a problem from one of her old medical textbooks.

“I remember how Eddie used to get whenever Taylor so much as breathed in your general direction, even if he denied it,” she said. “But you’ve never really had a problem with that, not until now. Actually, the last time I remember you acting anything like this was when Eddie first started here.”

“It’s nothing, Hen,” Buck insisted. “It’s stupid and it’s childish and it’s- It’s nothing. He’s got a new best friend, so what? I’ve already talked to Copeland.”

“Did she say it was stupid and childish?”

“No, but she wouldn’t, anyways.”

“So what did she say?” Hen pressed.

Buck let out a sigh.

“That just because Shannon was his best friend, and then I was his best friend, it doesn’t mean-” Buck cut himself out, shook his head. “She said that I’m being stupid, she just didn’t say it in those words.”

“Buck-”

“I’m gonna go take a shower,” he announced, doing his best to avoid hitting her with his leg when he swung it over. “I worked up a sweat.”

He ignored the way she frowned as she watched him leave, and he didn’t see how she got up and headed for the stairs.

The calls picked up for the rest of their shift, leaving them rather busy, which was a blessing in Buck’s opinion. It meant there was no time for him to sulk about Eddie and Tommy, which in turn meant he didn’t have to spend any time feeling stupid about it. The ride home was silent, with Eddie frowning out the window the entire time.

When they pulled into the driveway and got out, they were greeted as always by Hoover and then Gavin at the door. They said quick goodbyes to Carla before she was out the door to head home, Eddie took both their work bags to the bedroom and Buck took both child and dog to the backyard. 

Buck stood on the back porch and watched one chase the other around. He was so distracted by it that a pair of arms wrapping around his middle startled him completely, even though he knew those arms by feel alone.

“Babe?” Buck leaned back into Eddie’s chest, relishing having his husband’s undivided attention for the first time all day.

Eddie rested his chin on Buck’s shoulder and let out a little hum.

“I’m sorry,” he said.

“For what?” Buck asked him, twisting his head around a little.

“For making you feel ignored,” he said, and then, “Hen talked to me today.”

“Eds-” Buck tried to pull away, but Eddie tightened his hold on him.

“Do you really think that Tommy could replace you?” Eddie asked. “He’s new, he’s shiny, he’s cool-”

“Yeah, I know,” Buck said with maybe a bit more bitterness than he meant. “I got that, thanks.”

“But he isn’t you,” Eddie continued. “He’s not my best friend, he’ll never be my best friend, because I don’t have as much fun flying into a hurricane with him as I do just going grocery shopping with you. And I’m definitely not going to fall in love with him, either.”

Buck winced. He wasn’t sure if Hen had cottoned on to that particular fear and brought it up herself, or if she simply told Eddie enough of what had been said that he had figured it out himself.

“I- I know you wouldn’t…”

“You should have talked to me, mi sol,” Eddie told him, pressing a kiss into his neck. “You should have told me I was hurting you.”

“It was stupid,” Buck insisted. “I was being stupid.”

“No, I'm pretty sure I was being stupid if I didn’t notice that you were upset,” his husband said. “I told you, you were missed when we went to Vegas. If you’d gone, you would’ve spared Tommy from having to listen to me talk about you so goddamn much. I’m serious, the entire flight there. I’m lucky Tommy didn’t decide to throw me out to make me shut up.”

“I’m sorry,” Buck said. “My stupid brain…”

“I love your stupid brain,” Eddie told him, pressing another kiss to the side of his head. “It helped win us a gift card, remember?”

Buck ducked his head, grinning, and relaxed against Eddie’s chest. He tried to, anyway, because Eddie chose that moment to start pulling away which meant Buck nearly fell backwards. After regaining his balance, he watched, curious, as Eddie made his way to Abuela’s garden.

“What are you-” he started to ask, only to cut himself off with a yelp and a “Eddie!”

His husband, having apparently lost any and all survival instincts, bent down and plucked one of Abuela’s tulips. Then, with a satisfied little smile, he walked back over and presented it to Buck.

“She is going to kill you,” Buck told him, even as he took the flower from him.

“I’ll hide behind Chris.”

Chapter 12: Public Relations

Chapter Summary

In which Buck is a big brother, Maddie has a request, and the Firefighters Diaz are given an offer.

Chapter Notes

CW: Harry talks about being racially profiled and discriminated against, even though it's never out-right stated.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

The bratty gremlin that had spread itself out in Buck’s chest had fallen silent and was currently in the process of withering and dying. Buck himself was curled up on the couch with Eddie, pressed into his side with the man’s arm around him. Hoover was spread out at their feet, Gavin happily leaning against him while he played with his toys, and Senorita had draped herself along the back of the couch.

The tulip from Abuela’ garden was tucked behind Buck’s ear. When they’d come in from the backyard, Abuela had taken one look at the flower, and then at Eddie, and shook her head with a quiet, grumbly complaint. But she hadn’t said anything else, and Eddie hadn’t had to hide behind Chris for his own safety, so it was good.

They were, at the moment, catching up on one of the telenovelas they watched with Abuela. They were a couple episodes behind, what with the way their schedule at the station worked out.

“Mi sol,” Eddie said, frowning at the characters on the screen. “I need you to promise me something.”

“What?”

“If I ever start cheating on you with my dead wife’s doppelganger-”

“Not that you would,” Buck interrupted.

“But if I do, take me to the doctor and get me checked out for, I dunno. A brain tumor,” Eddie said. “And if Chris ever asks to move back to El Paso with my parents, and if I let him, just take us both out back and shoot us. We’ve clearly been replaced by pod people, and the invasion has begun.”

“I don’t think I could shoot either of you, Eds, pod people or not,” Buck told him, scrunching his face up. “I’ll let Ma do it.”

“Probably for the best.”

Knocking at the door popped their little bubble of peace. Hoover got up, dislodging Gavin, and started barking at their unseen visitor. Buck and Eddie shared a look, both of them thinking the same thing - Maybe if we just ignored them, they’d go away. - before Eddie let out a sigh and started to his feet. This meant Buck had to sit up, since Eddie was serving as his very comfortable pillow, which led to him glaring at the closed door.

“I’m pretty sure I’m having deja vu,” Eddie announced when he answered the door. “You didn’t take an Uber here again, did you?”

“No,” was Harry’s reply, voice filled with laughter. “I walked. You’re not that far from Mom’s.”

“That was one of the points in this house’s favor,” Buck said, getting up as well. He pat Hoover on the head as he went by, the dog still standing and staring at the door, despite having quieted down. “Please don’t tell me you’re here because Ma slapped you again.”

He was not used to being the one fussing at Athena over something, even if it had been through Bobby. He didn’t particularly want to do it again, especially since she was still very capable of scaring the hell out of him.

“We just had a fight,” Harry answered, walking in when Eddie got out of the way. He knelt down with a wide grin. “Hey, Gav! You remember me, right?”

Gavin clung to Hoover’s side, burying his face in the dog’s fur in an attempt to hide from the (near) stranger in their home.

“C’mon, Gavin,” Harry whined. “Be nice to your Uncle Harry.”

“It was a while ago,” Eddie pointed out, leaning back against the now closer front door. “Nearly half his life.”

“If you’re Gavin’s uncle,” Buck said, thinking aloud. “What does that make you to Chris?”

Harry grimaced, clearly having not thought about that before, and stood back up.

“That’s the dog Bobby stole, right?” he asked.

“Yup, that’s Hoover,” Buck confirmed. “So what was the fight with Ma about?”

“Uh.” The teenager scratched at the back of his neck. “That’s, um. Not important. Hey, where’s Chris?”

Buck met Eddie’s eyes over Harry’s shoulder. His husband nodded at him and stepped around the teenager, going to Gavin and scooping him up in his arms.

“Why don’t we go bother your brother in his room,” he said, hip checking Buck before leaving the room. “How ‘bout that, mijo? Wanna see what Chris is doing?”

“Yeah!” Gavin squealed. He kicked his legs and called out, “Chris! Chris!”

“I’ll just-” Harry started to follow them, but Buck clapped his hands over his shoulders and directed him towards the kitchen, instead.

“I need to get started on dinner,” Buck told him. “Are you staying?”

“Well- Actually, I was thinking-”

“Perfect!” He shoved Harry into the kitchen, letting go of him long enough to start pulling out what he needed for the spaghetti they were going to have. “Maybe I should invite Ma and Pops over.”

“No, no.” The teenager hurriedly shook his head. “That’s not- We shouldn’t bother Mom.”

“Sure, sure.” Buck eyed him as he dumped a jar of homemade sauce into a pan to heat it up. “So what was the fight about?”

“You’re worse than May.”

Buck’s phone, still sitting where he left it in the living room, chimed to announce that there were new texts to be read. He heard Eddie’s phone go off at the same time, from Chris’ room.

“I could probably just go in there and check my phone,” Buck told Harry. “That was the tone I set for Ma and Pops, I bet they’d tell me.”

Harry heaved out a sigh and went to throw himself into one of the stools at the breakfast bar.

“Mom took the cops’ side,” he complained. “Again.”

“Well, she is a cop,” Buck pointed out.

“She’s also my mom!”

“Yeah.” Buck let out a sigh. “So what happened that there even is a cop side to take?”

“There was this guy at a convenience store,” Harry finally said. “The guy had it out for me the moment I walked inside. Staring me down, following me. Blocked the door when I tried to leave and started screaming at me, accused me of stealing.”

“What a dick.”

“Thank you!” Harry exclaimed. “When I told Mom, d’ya know what she said? She asked me if I did!”

“Well…” Buck let it trail off. “I can’t really defend that. So, what? Did he call the cops on you because he thought you stole from the store?”

“No, he…” Harry ducked his head. “He put his hands on me so I punched him.”

“Huh. I thought you were a Grant, not a Diaz.” At Harry’s confused expression, Buck explained, “Eddie once got arrested for punching a guy. It was during the whole… Thing, back in the day.”

The lawsuit thing, to be specific.

“Wait,” Buck said, details finally clicking together. “You punched a guy and then you fled the state to avoid the cops!?”

Harry hunkered down in his seat, his shoulders rising to his ears.

“Harry,” Buck groaned, dragging a hand down his face. “You can’t do that, I’m pretty sure that makes you a felon.”

“That’s your take away from all this?” Eddie was standing in the doorway to the hall, phone in his hand. “Whether or not fleeing the state was the wrong thing to do? ‘Cause I remember when Chris hit Chimney, you grounded him.”

“The Tyler Durden cosplayer does not get an opinion on this,” Buck told him, leaning around the kitchen doorway to point at him with a wooden spoon. “Did you tell them he’s staying for dinner?”

“Yup,” Eddie confirmed with a nod.

“Excellent,” he said, and then turned the spoon on Harry. “You can set the table.”

Harry rolled his eyes, but he got up all the same.

The best part of Sunday dinners, in Maddie’s opinion, was not the company or the food, though both were excellent. No, the best part of them was seeing in person just how happy Buck was everytime they all came over like this. Her baby brother beamed and glowed, just like the sun that Eddie always compared him to. It was a stark contrast to how he had been before he left Hershey, downtrodden and broken.

This Sunday dinner was a little bit more crowded than typical: there were Albert and Ravi, who didn’t always come, and also Harry, who hadn’t been to one since before he moved to Florida. He was, presumably, going to be showing up at more in the future. Maddie wasn’t entirely certain of what happened, but she knew he was grounded and having to do community service, and that he was staying in Los Angeles for the foreseeable future.

After dinner, when everyone was done eating and were happily digesting, they would always break up in groups. Some helped start on the cleaning, never the ones who helped cook. Some went into the backyard with the kids and the dogs. Some lingered in the dining room or the living room to keep talking. 

Maddie kept an eye on Bobby, waiting to find an excuse to catch him alone. Her opportunity came when he volunteered to take Hoover outside, and so she quickly did the same with Jee-Yun and Gavin. Chimney, who knew what she was wanting to do that night, and proving why she loved him so much, quickly distracted anyone else that thought about going into the backyard with them.

The moment she opened the door, Gavin and Jee-Yun rushed out to cling to Bobby’s legs, before being distracted by each other. Maddie stepped up beside the man, and the pair both watch the kids and the dog run around before it was time to go to bed.

“How’s the wedding planning going?” Bobby asked her. “Got everything ready for the big day?”

“Oh I’m sure Buck will find something to harass me about,” she said, which made him laugh.

“I’ve heard how he’s been about it,” Bobby said. “There’s a reason everyone flees the station when they see him with a clipboard.”

“He just wants me to be happy and have a better wedding than my last one,” she said. “Buck was the one who walked me down the aisle, last time.”

“I know,” he said. “Just between you and me? I was kinda looking forward to walking him down, but then he told me he was planning on the pair of them going together. There was something about brides being handed off like cattle.”

Maddie grimaced at the comparison.

“I think he feels guilty for it, just a little, even if he hasn’t said anything,” she said. “You know, for being the one to give me to Doug like- like prized cattle. But it’s not like anyone else was going to do it, Mom and Dad didn’t even show up.”

“Are they going to show up for this one?”

“No.” She shook her head. “I haven’t even told them I’m getting married again.”

“Good.” Something that, on a worse person, might’ve been vindictive pleasure crossed Bobby’s face for just a moment. The man really did not like her parents, not that she could blame him.

“I did want to ask you something, though,” Maddie said. “I know that everyone’s forgiven Chimney for what he did-”

“Mostly,” Bobby cut in to say. She nodded.

“Mostly,” she agreed. “And like I said, I think Buck still feels guilty for his part in my last wedding. And I don’t really want to put him through handing me off to someone that hurt him, even if he’s definitely forgiven Chimney for it. So I- I’m not going to have Buck walk me down, this time.”

“Are you doing it alone?”

“No. I mean, I will if I have to, but…” She glanced at him, nervous and shy. “I was wondering if you would?”

Maddie watched Bobby blink repeatedly as he took in the question, and then his eyes went wide and he turned his entire body around to face her.

“Me?”

“Only if you want to,” she hurried to say. “It’s just… Our parents don’t even know about it, like I said, and… And you’re Buck’s dad, so…”

Maddie shrugged, feeling self-conscious now. And then she squeaked when the man suddenly pulled her into a hug that threatened to crush her bones.

“It would be my honor,” Bobby told her, sounding suspiciously like he was about to start crying.

Buck wasn’t really sure what he expected to happen after he and Eddie told Bobby about their- Okay, about the fans of Taylor Kelly’s book, who had apparently attached themselves to their barely fictional counterparts. Buck knew that the captain had sent the problem up the food chain, and Bobby had warned that someone would want to talk to them.

He just didn’t really expect for their Monday morning to be interrupted by Battalion Chief Williams walking into the station with another firefighter. They showed up during Bobby’s morning brief, and the captain trailed into silence at the sight of them.

“Good morning, chief,” he said, shaking Williams’ hand. “How can we help you?”

“We,” Williams said, gesturing between herself and the dark-haired man she came in with, “need to steal the Diazes.”

“Ah.” Bobby nodded. “The office is free. Buck, Eddie.”

Williams nodded back and then, without even glancing at them, started off towards Bobby’s office, the unknown firefighter right behind her. Buck and Eddie hurried to catch up, feeling everyone’s gazes following them even as Bobby continued on with the briefing.

When they got to the office, Williams rounded the desk and dropped into the seat with a sigh. She placed her elbows on the desk, knitted her fingers together, and fixed both Diazes with an unimpressed stare.

“Why,” she said, “is it always this station? Why is it almost always one of you?”

“We’re special?” Buck offered, trying for a smile. Eddie nudged him in the side with his elbow. “What, uh. What did we do this time?”

Williams, instead of answering, directed her gaze to the firefighter standing beside her. He was holding a tablet in his hand and was tapping away at it.

“Technically you didn’t do anything, not really,” the man said, and then raised his head. “Oh, sorry. I’m Tomas Wade, I’m from the Community Liaison Office.”

“Oh,” Eddie said. “Then you’re here about the…”

“That reporter’s book,” Williams confirmed with a nod.

Buck and Eddie looked at each other, and then they both sat down in the chairs in front of Bobby’s desk

“We put out a statement shortly after the book was initially published,” Tomas said, attention back on his tablet. “It was mostly to say that we didn’t have a statement regarding the book’s contents, actually, beyond it being heavily dramatized and that no one in the department was consulted about it. I’m assuming you two have read it?”

“Unfortunately,” Eddie said. Williams smirked at him.

“Well apparently, so have other people,” she said.

“Oh, I know,” Buck said. “Bobby told you about the, uh…”

“The fan creations, yes,” Tomas said. “It’s the same thing you’d see with most other pieces of media with any level of popularity. We’re most concerned because Ms. Kelly made it so easy to find your actual identities. Your captain said you’ve had people trying to follow you on your personal social media?”

“Yeah,” Eddie said. “All of it’s private, though.”

“Mine’s been private since the, uh. The catfishing, my probie year,” Buck told them, a bit sheepishly. “We post pictures of the kids, so we’ve been especially careful about who sees anything. It was what caught our attention at first, the fact that we suddenly had all these people trying to follow us.”

“Bobby was concerned about people taking and posting pictures of us,” Eddie said. “Did you…?”

“There’ve been a few,” Tomas confirmed. Buck and Eddie both blanched at that. “All of them taken while you were working,”

“There’s not much we can do about that,” Williams told them. “If there’s an accident, there’ll be people taking pictures.”

“But I didn’t find any of you out of uniform, nor of your family.”

“Oh thank God,” Buck said at the same time as Eddie. “So- So what do we do?”

Williams and Tomas glanced at each other, neither one speaking up for a long moment. Finally, the battalion chief sighed and leaned forward.

“You’re probably not going to like it,” she said. “And you don’t have to do it.”

“Do what?” Eddie asked, eyes narrowed in suspicion.

“The department is always after good publicity,” Tomas told them. “Even though the public generally has no problem with firefighters, not like they do with police, we’ve still had issues that have been reported on. Like with that firefighter arsonist or that article the LA Times published, both from just last year.”

Buck knew what article Tomas was talking about. It was the one that reported on the Public Liaison Officer who, in the middle of his shift, used a department phone to hire and have sex with a prostitute. The article that talked about the firefighter who was pulled over with a blood-alcohol level nearly two and a half times the legal limit, who resisted arrest, who battered a cop. The article that talked about the fire captain who kicked a homeless man in the head.

The article that made a point of mentioning how none of those firefighters had gotten fired for it.

(Maybe Buck 1.0 was never in any danger of being fired for his shenanigans, after all.)

“And the department- You,” Buck pointed at Tomas, who was apparently serving as the Mouth of Sauron for the department’s PR people. “Want us to do what, exactly?”

Tomas sat the tablet down in front of him and Eddie. On the screen was the sign-up page to make a new Instagram account.

“Oh,” the Diazes said together.

Chapter End Notes

It took all I had to not have Daniel suddenly jump fics to voice his ACAB opinions to Harry. (I'm sorry if that statement confused anyone who reads this series but not my others.)

The LA Times article mentioned here is real. It conveniently came out shortly before I was writing about Dalton Wolfe, which was just a case of fortuitous timing.

Chapter 13: Grab Him By The-

Chapter Summary

In which a victim gets a little too handsy.

Chapter Notes

cw: is this sexual assault? I'm gonna put a SA warning on here, just in case.

Los Angeles, by its very nature, hosted a lot of self-improvement seminars led by self-help gurus. Whether or not they were actually helpful wasn’t something Buck could decide on, though he knew Eddie was of the firm belief that it was all hogwash. What Buck could decide on was that none of them would ever be worth the price of admission, not when he had a therapist that was (almost) entirely covered by his insurance. The books sold by the people leading the seminars were also much cheaper, he’d found.

The 118 responded to an incident at one particular self-improvement seminar, this one titled “I’m the Boss of Me,” because the guy leading it was attacking himself. Apparently, in the middle of his lecture, the guy started acting weird. Weird as in, he started beating himself in the face with the microphone. Weird as in, he got into a slap fight with his own hand. Weird as in, the guy was losing a fight with his own hand.

When they got there and were escorted into the large hall where the seminar was being held, it was easy to find their victim. He was the guy on the stage being bear-hugged by a bunch of others, his arm flapping about like an angry snake.

“Looks like we’re looking at a possible Fifty-one Fifty, Cap,” Hen remarked.

5150 was the California Welfare and Institutions code for the involuntary hold of someone who was in the middle of a mental health crisis and was a danger to themselves or others. In impolite terms, Hen basically declared that the guy had gone a bit batshit.

“Everybody, give him some space, okay?” Bobby ordered, shooing away the civilians that had been restraining the guy. The moment they did, he started slapping himself one more. “Sir, whatever’s going on here, you don’t have to hurt yourself.”

“You think I want to hurt myself?” their vic asked, grabbing himself by the wrist and entering a struggle for control with his own arm. “It’s my hand! It has a mind of its own.”

“I think he’s suffering from alien hand syndrome,” Hen said.

Eddie was quick to scoff, saying,

“That’s not a real thing.”

“I’m pretty sure it was in an episode of House,” Buck added. It was one of the shows he and Eddie binged when they had the time. There were a couple episodes in the first season with someone who looked just like Chimney.

“It’s a really rare neurological condition,” Hen informed them all. “It causes a person’s limbs to act out, seemingly of their own will, and it usually indicates urgent brain danger.”

That did not sound good at all.

“Buck, Eddie, get up there,” Bobby ordered. “See if you can help him restrain himself.”

Their patient’s alien hand was, currently, choking him out. Buck and Eddie climbed up onto the stage, Eddie steadying the guy while Buck started wrestling with the arm. It should’ve been easy, he didn’t look like he worked out at all.

Looks were apparently deceiving, however.

“I’m thinking lorazepam,” Hen said, unslinging her medical bag while the Diazes were busy.

“And five milligrams of lidocaine?” was Chimney’s suggestion, already digging through his own bag. “Aim for the ulnar nerve.”

“He’s uh, he’s stronger than he looks,” Buck informed the others, straining to loosen the hand’s grip on the guy’s throat.

“It’s not me,” the guy protested. “It’s- Ah, thank you.”

With a grunt, Buck finally pulled the arm away. He couldn’t rejoice for too long, however, because it turned out to be a slippery thing. It escaped his hold, going back to its angry snake impression before Buck could wrangle it back down again.

“Please, just cut it off!” the poor guy begged.

“Sir, is there any chance you’ve been treated for epilepsy?” Hen asked.

The part of Buck that wasn’t focusing on trying to keep the arm still - seriously, it was worse than Senorita the last time they gave her a bath - recalled how, in that episode of House, that was the source of that patient-of-the-week’s alien hand. Something about brain surgery to fix the epilepsy involving disconnecting the two sides of his brain or whatever.

“No,” was their current patient’s response.

“How about blood clots?” Hen asked. “Any recent contusions or bruises?”

“Not until today.”

“What about irregular heartbeat?” Chimney asked.

“Uh, yeah, I had an operation four weeks ago for my AFib,” the patient answered. Hen and Chimney, syringes held in their gloved hands, shared one of their Eureka! looks. “What?”

“I think it’s possible your heart threw a clot that went to your brain,” Hen explained.

“So I’m having a stroke!?”

“I believe so,” she said. “But we should be able to get you to the hospital before there’s any permanent brain damage.”

Because Buck’s luck sucked, that was the moment he once again lost his grip on the crazed limb. Because his luck sucked a lot, it immediately started choking him. He realized that, before this moment, he didn’t truly know what they meant by “kung-fu grip.” He did now, though.

Bobby immediately hopped onto the stage, yanking at the murderous appendage. The captain managed to get it loose, letting Buck collapse as he gasped for breath. Buck didn’t see what happened with the hand then, but he knew Bobby didn’t have a hold of it, not with the way he was checking over Buck.

There was a loud, horrified, “Oh!” from everyone in the room. Eddie let out a high pitched scream, shriller than anything Buck had heard him let out before.

“It’s not me!” he heard their patient say, followed by his husband’s squeaky,

“It’s me!”

Buck looked up to see what was going on and saw that the hand had grabbed Eddie’s crotch. From the expression on Eddie’s face, it was not being any gentler with its handling of things in that moment than it had been with anything else.

“Hey!” Buck croaked out, still on his knees. “That’s mine, let it go!”

Bobby left Buck to assist the other Diaz. Like before, he managed to get the hand’s latest victim free. And also like before, he failed to get the arm restrained before it swung back around, once more grabbing Buck by the throat.

“Yeah, let me get in there,” Chimney said, the paramedic finally deciding to intervene. “Hold on, hold on.”

Buck glared at him as best he could with his trachea being crushed. Hold on? Hold on? Seriously?

Chimney unbuttoned the cuff of the patient’s sleeve, pushing the fabric out of the way so he could stab him with the syringe. The patient immediately started complaining that it hurt, which had Buck redirecting his glare at him. He was the one being strangled to death here, the guy could deal with a little pain.

Thankfully, whatever was in that particular syringe seemed to be fast acting. The grip on Buck’s throat disappeared, allowing him to drop to the ground as he heaved in much needed breaths before Bobby helped him back to his feet.

“Did you kill it?” the guy asked. His arm was hanging limply at his side, and Buck was suddenly very keen to follow through with the earlier request of cutting it off.

“No, I just put it to sleep,” Chimney said. “Lidocaine acts as a numbing agent.”

“I am so sorry,” the guy said, using his other, apparently pacifist hand to straighten Buck’s collar.

“It’s- It’s all good,” Buck lied.

Their patient went to apologize to Eddie, who was still bent over at the waist as he cupped himself, and got slapped away before he could finish getting the words out.

The others brought in the gurney and loaded their patient up, strapping him down before the arm could cause any further mayhem. They took him away to the ambulance while Buck and a limping Eddie went to the engine. Climbing in proved difficult, however, because the moment Eddie tried lifting his leg, he let out a pathetic whimper and dropped his foot back to the ground.

“Do you need help?” Buck asked, voice still coming out rough.

Eddie gave a shaky nod. So Buck, using the muscles he worked so hard for, bent down a little and wrapped his arms around his husband’s middle and lifted him up into the engine’s cab before he clambered in after him. In the driver’s seat, a simultaneously amused and worried looking Toby watched them through the engine’s mirrors.

“Are you guys okay?” he asked.

“No,” Eddie grunted, trying to find a comfortable way to sit in the jumpseat he’d collapsed into. 

The front passenger door was yanked open and Bobby climbed in. Once seated, he turned to look between the two Diazes, face contorted in sympathy pangs for Eddie, and then fixed his gaze on Buck.

“That’s mine, let it go?” the captain repeated, shaking his head. “Really, Buck?”

Buck felt himself flush.

“I- I mean, I did put a ring on it,” he said. He saw Toby’s reflection tilt his head as his face turned as red as his hair, and he saw Bobby’s face twist up in horror. It took a moment for Buck to realize what they heard, and then he was horrified. “Wait, no, I meant I put a ring on him! I mean-”

“Mi sol.” Eddie reached over and tried to pat the side of his head but, as he was still slightly bent over and in pain, he mostly missed and smacked Buck’s face instead. “Please shut up.”

“Shutting up.”

The ambulance pulled away, heading for the nearest hospital. The engine followed for a bit, before turning off to go back to the station. Everytime they hit a particularly bad bump, Eddie let out a little squeak of pain that Buck knew he shouldn’t find adorable, and yet he did.

Once back at the station, Buck helped Eddie to get out of the engine. After a moment where he was very clearly having a debate with himself, Bobby sent Eddie off to the station showers and then had Buck get a cooling pack from the other ambulance before joining his husband. Someone needed to check the man over to make sure he wasn’t seriously injured, the captain pointed out, and that someone was obviously going to be Buck.

“Alright, drop your pants,” Buck said when he joined Eddie in the station’s showers. He set the cooling pack in one of the ledges meant for soap and shampoo.

“I thought we had a rule against this kind of thing at work,” Eddie said, even as he gingerly undid his belt. “You’re not posting about this.”

“‘Course not, babe,” Buck agreed as he knelt down. “Now keep thinking unsexy thoughts and pray to God that Pops keeps everyone out.”

The department’s response to Buck and Eddie’s rise in internet popularity, thanks to Taylor Kelly’s book about the 118 and the lack of effort she put into actually keeping their identities private, was to pimp them out for good publicity. It took the form of an LAFD sanctioned Instagram account that they made under the watchful eyes of Tomas Wade, from the Community Liaison Office, and Chief Williams. The name wasn’t very creative, FirefightersDiaz_LAFD, but at least they were allowed to come up with it themselves.

Everyone in their circle of family and coworkers found it hilarious, and the first followers their little account had came from there. Then Buck made the mistake of telling TK, who then told Marjan, who then shared a screenshot of one of the few posts they’d uploaded so far and tagged them in it. With most of their friends, that wouldn’t matter. But Marjan wasn’t just anyone, she was Firefox.

Eddie had a minor freak out when he saw the number of people following them after that.

It was through an unspoken agreement between them that Buck was the one in charge of the account. Mostly because his husband was a recovering technophobe. There was a problem, though, in that Buck didn’t really know what to post, and neither Tomas nor Chief Williams had given him much of a directive. He was certain that, at some point, the Mouth of Sauron would show up on his doorstep - or in his inbox - with a request that wasn’t a request, but that hadn’t happened yet.

Mostly, so far, he was sharing photos of Eddie. Because he loved his husband, and he found his husband very attractive, and he liked to flaunt to the world that his husband was his husband. There was also, so far, a couple “safety” lectures that mostly consisted of him telling people not to do the stupid thing they just had to save some idiot from doing, a news article he saw about a rescue they’d done, and - the one time Eddie had deigned to post anything - a video of him yammering on about whatever niche piece of information he was fixated on that day while he cooked. Buck hadn’t even known that Eddie was recording at the time.

All-in-all, Buck was very much enjoying himself with it.

(He’d always been something of an attention whore, after all.)

Some time ago, Deidra called the Wilsons about a baby girl. Buck wasn’t very certain with the details, but he knew she was currently in her elderly grandmother’s custody. For the same reasons that he and Eddie were dubious about depending on Abuela for childcare, the baby girl’s grandmother made the heavy decision to put her up for adoption. As tragic as it was, the Wilsons were also very obviously excited about it.

With the official adoption day quickly approaching, Buck and Eddie loaded up Gavin, along with a bunch of the clothes he’d outgrown, and went to Maddie and Chimney’s. It was the perfect opportunity, they all decided, to get rid of things that were just taking up space. It was also a good excuse to hang out with their friends and family, not that they ever really needed one.

“Aw,” Maddie squealed, holding up a tiny, floral printed dress. The coffee table was covered in similarly sized clothing, all neatly folded. “Don’t you wish you could just shrink them back down to this size?”

“Yes,” Eddie immediately said, scowling at a shirt with a cartoon shark on the front. “I don’t even remember buying this.”

“I’d just be okay with shrinking her down to this size,” Chimney said, coming in with another box of clothes. He sat down on the couch, next to where Jee-Yun and Gavin were going through a picture book together, and pulled out a tiny pair of footie pajamas. “She never even got a chance to wear this.”

“Okay, we have nine, twelve, eighteen, and twenty-four months,” Maddie said, pointing at each stack as she listed them off. “That should hold you over for a while, but if anything doesn’t fit…”

“Keep it anyway,” Chimney said.

“Thank you,” Hen said. “We will definitely put all of this to good use. Including the boys’ clothes.”

“Hey, gender is a construct, remember?” Buck dropped a balled up pair of probably never worn socks, which were so cute and so tiny, and poked at her leg. “Are you really gonna say no to free baby clothes?”

“No, we are not,” Karen declared.

“Maybe we should just put it all into a community box,” Eddie suggested. He was looking at a pair of Gavin’s pants, his expression having turned wistful. “You know, for any future rugrats.”

Buck grinned and bumped his shoulder against Eddie, but his husband ducked his gaze to focus on sorting through more of the baby clothes.

“You guys must be so excited,” Maddie said. “A little girl!”

“I know.” Karen pulled out her phone, messed with it for a moment, and then held it out for them all to see the picture she’d pulled up. “How perfect is this little face?”

Buck craned his neck to look and let out an “aw” with his sister.

“I can’t wait until tomorrow,” Hen told them.

Karen’s phone chimed.

“Oh!” she said, having turned it back around to check what it was. “It’s Deidra. She wants to see us.”

It was with an air of excitement and anticipation that the pair quickly gathered up the baby clothes that had been laid out for them so far, tucked them away in a box, and rushed out the door. Buck pushed himself up off the floor, where he’d been kneeling with Eddie, and claimed the chair that Hen just vacated. He nudged at Eddie with his foot.

“So,” he said, smiling widely. “Future rugrats, huh?”

“Cállate,” his husband grumbled.

“Where would you put them?” Chimney wondered aloud. “Pretty sure all your rooms are filled up.”

“Isn’t your garage fully finished?” Maddie asked.

“It is,” Buck confirmed. “It currently just has our exercise equipment and some boxes.”

“I told you to shut up,” Eddie groaned.

Buck was about to keep poking at his husband, but his phone began to ring in his pocket. When he pulled it out to check and saw who it was, something must’ve shown on his face, because Maddie scowled and asked him,

“Is it Dad again?”

“No,” Buck told her, shaking his head. “It’s too early for that.”

He got up and padded away from them before he answered.

“Hello,” the woman on the other end said. “This is Buck Diaz, right?”

“It is.”

“It’s Sylvie, Amy’s aunt. Do you remember me?”

“I do,” Buck said, turning slightly to glance at the couch. From here, he couldn’t see Gavin and Jee-Yun, but he could hear the pair chattering away to each other.

“I’m going to be in the city tomorrow.” Sylvie sounded hesitant and uncertain. “And I was hoping you had the time to meet…”

Chapter 14: Irony

Chapter Summary

In which Buck and Eddie meet with someone from Gavin's biofamily, Albert and Ravi do their uncle duties, and Bobby has an unexpected (and unwanted) visitor.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Buck had spent more time in courtrooms in the last couple of years than he ever had his entire life prior. Twice he went through the song and dance of stepping in front of a judge with Eddie in family court, first for the step-parent adoption of Christopher and then when they officially adopted Gavin. Then there were the times he was called up as a material witness in criminal trials, for Jonah Greenway, the paramedic floater with a hero complex; for Dalton Wolfe, their probie that became an arsonist; and for a Los Angeles police officer named Clifford Tate.

Two summers ago, during the week TK and Carlos were visiting, Buck received a phone call from Gavin’s mother, Amy, asking for help. That resulted in him speeding across the city, TK a hapless passenger in the jeep, to rescue her from her step-father. That was how he met Amy’s aunt, Sylvie; she came to the hospital where they took Amy, and they exchanged numbers before he left. They hadn’t crossed paths at the trial for Clifford, so it had been a few months short of two years since he heard from either of them.

Sylvie met them at Lincoln Park, on the other side of downtown LA from where they lived. It was a bit out of their way, but she was coming from Barstow, so everything was subjective. Buck and Eddie found her sitting on a bench by the playground, between two elephant statues. She looked like an older, tired version of her niece, with dark hair that fell past her shoulders in ringlets, and she was sat with a purse in her lap as she watched kids climb all over the playground equipment.

“Hi, Mrs. Vega,” Buck said when he got close enough.

She turned her head to smile at them, gesturing for them to take a seat with her. Buck did, but Eddie stayed standing.

“Sylvie, please,” she said. “And you’re Eddie, right?”

“Yes, ma’am,” Eddie said with a nod.

“I don’t think I’ve thanked you enough for what you did,” she told Buck. “I don’t think I’ll ever be able to thank you enough.”

“You… You don’t have to,” Buck said. “She called and asked for help, what else was I going to do?”

Behind him, Eddie let out a soft snort and leaned against the back of the bench.

“My sister always did have the worst taste in men,” she said, old sadness lacing her words. “That boyfriend of hers that knocked her up and then ran for the hills, and then Cliff.” It was impressive, how much derision and venom she managed to fit into one syllable. “Gabe and I - my husband - we tried to tell Lindsay he was no good, but she didn’t listen. And then she died and I tried to talk Amy into moving to Barstow with us, but she wanted to stay here with all her friends. I should’ve tried harder.”

“I’m sorry,” Buck said, for lack of anything better to offer. Eddie’s hand moved to his shoulder and gave it a squeeze.

“How is Amy doing?” Eddie asked.

“Better,” Sylvie answered. “She turned eighteen last May and graduated. I managed to convince her to take a gap year, told her to just focus on healing. And she has been.”

“Good,” Buck said. “That’s- That’s good.”

“That’s why I asked to talk to you,” Sylvie said. “I bring her into the city, once or twice a month, to see her therapist. He’s been talking to her about everything. Her mother, Cliff. The… Baby.”

It was a reflex for Buck to tilt his head back and meet Eddie’s eyes.

“She said that, when she was in the hospital, you offered to show her pictures of him,” she said. “Do you still have them?”

“I- I do.” Buck nodded, slowly and uncertain. “Would you… Would you like to see them?”

Sylie tilted her head in thought.

“I think I would, actually, but I didn’t drag you out just for that,” she said. “But she said you had pictures of him, you told her that one of your coworkers was his foster parent. I figured, I hoped, that you could help me find him.”

“Uh.” Buck looked to Eddie again. “M- Maybe.”

“Why?” his husband asked her, his hand a solid weight on Buck’s shoulder still.

“Her therapist thinks it would help her,” Sylvie said. “Seeing him, I mean. I’m not sure about that, but she wants to.”
“Does she, uh.” Buck cleared his throat. “Does she… Does she want him back?”

Sylvie was quick to shake her head, which had Buck melting into the bench a little with relief.

“No. I asked her that, when she first brought it up,” she said. “She just wants to see him, see how he’s doing. Make sure he’s safe.”

“Then, yeah, I… I think we can help with that.”

Buck pulled out his phone, thumbing it on. He went into the gallery and the album that was filled with nothing but pictures of his kids and Jee-Yun. The newest one was of a sleeping Gavin starfished on the floor, using Hoover as a pillow while one fist clutched tightly to his vibrant orange bunny.

Sylvie frowned at the screen when he held it out for her to see.

“That is a toddler,” she said.

“We named him-”

“We?” Eddie interrupted, sounding amused.

“Whatever.” Buck rolled his eyes. “I named him Gavin.”

“Gavin looks very adorable,” Sylvie told them.

“He is, isn’t he?” Eddie leaned in to peer at the phone, too, as Buck flipped through the pictures.

“But what does this have to do with Amy’s…” Sylvie trailed off with a frown and took Buck’s phone from his hand to go to the next picture herself, this one of Eddie holding Gavin as he splashed in the pool at Albert and Ravi’s apartment building. “This is him, isn’t he? He looks like Amy at that age.”

“After Amy dropped him off at the station, we made the mistake of leaving him with Buck while the rest of us went on a call,” Eddie explained. “We came back to find that he’d named him. It was probably inevitable, after that.”

“Good, that’s good.” Sylvie half-whispered the words. “Gavin…”

“Gavin Robert Diaz,” Buck said.

“It’s a good name.” She handed Buck his phone back. “And you would be okay with letting Amy see him?”

Buck tilted his head back again to meet Eddie’s eyes, checking in with him.

“She did a very brave thing, when she brought him to the station,” Buck said, turning back to Sylvie.

“Yes, we’re fine with it,” Eddie added.

“I’ll talk to her therapist, then, and with her, make sure she really is up to it,” she said, and then, “Would you mind sending me some of those pictures? Maybe a couple of him as an infant? I think that would be an easier first step for her, seeing him in pictures.”

“Yeah, of course.” Buck was already picking out a handful to do just that. He had to scroll way down to get to the oldest photos of Gavin, though, the ones that he had offered to show Amy nearly two years ago.

“Thank you. Like I said, I’m not sold that seeing him would help her,” Sylvie said. “But I honestly don’t know how she’ll react to seeing him.”

That had Buck feeling even more uncertain about this than he already was.

“Why would she react badly?” Eddie asked.

“He has Cliff’s eyes,” was her toneless answer.

It took a moment for Buck’s brain to process just what that meant, to make the connection between Amy’s step-father and Amy’s baby. Then, when it did, everything he had eaten that day threatened to make a reappearance as Eddie let out a series of multilingual swears that earned him an impressive glare from a passing mother. The question of Gavin’s father wasn’t one that Buck had ever contemplated, because it simply hadn’t mattered. He hadn’t thought, not for a moment, that it had been-

“Thank you for meeting me today,” Sylvie said, tearing him out of his horror. “For the pictures, too, and… I really can’t ever thank you enough for what you did.”

“You’re welcome.”

She stood, nodded at them both as she clutched at her purse, and left in the direction of the nearby parking lot. Eddie rounded the bench and dropped into it beside Buck, and dropped his arm around his shoulders.

“I know it might make me a hypocrite, considering my own experience with horrible family secrets,” Buck said as the pair watched Sylvie leave. “But I don’t think I ever want Gavin to know.”

“Agreed.”

In the eternal struggle to be proven the best uncle, Albert was willing to subject himself to many things. Today, that meant he was sitting patiently and uncomfortably in an undersized chair while Jee-Yun clumsily painted at his nails with a look of utmost concentration. She was using the nail polish that Buck had surprised his sister with, after Maddie mentioned offhandedly that Jee-Yun wanted her nails to be colorful like hers.

Apparently, there was a brand specifically marketed as safe for kids. Who knew?

“I think that color suits you,” Ravi commented, peering over Albert’s shoulder.

“I look good in any color.” Jee-Yun had gone with a bright, glittery pink that was ending up on his fingertips as much as it was his fingernails. “Also, screw you.”

“Rude.” Ravi poked at Albert’s face with a fingertip smeared with red and softly smelling of watermelon. “You know Maddie will be upset if she repeats that.”

“We’ll just blame Chimney,” Albert told him, batting at his boyfriend’s finger.

“Hey!” Jee-Yun glared at him. She reached up and gave his hand a tug, trying to bring it back down. “No moving.”

“Right, right, sorry, Jee.”

Currently, they were at Maddie and Chimney’s house, babysitting. The pair were having a date night and, because Buck and Eddie were busy with something, no one bothered telling him precisely what, Albert jumped at the chance. Ravi was, obviously, dragged along with him.

“Speaking of Chimney,” Ravi said. “Did he tell you what’s going on with Hen?”

“He did, actually.” Albert’s brother had almost burst out with the gossip when they came over. Ravi would’ve heard it, too, except he’d allowed Jee-Yun to drag him away to see something. “Apparently the baby girl fell through, something about her grandmother changing her mind.”

“That sucks.”

“Yeah,” Albert agreed. Jee-Yun finally finished his nails, leaving his pinky finger half-painted in pink beyond the nail. “It’s very pretty, Jee. Thank you.”

“D’nada!” Jee-Yun chirped. She capped the tiny bottle, dropped it carelessly onto the table, and then started digging into the small bag that it and the others Buck bought her were all kept in, pulling out a bottle of bright yellow that she held out to them. “Mine now?”

“Uh.” Albert eyed it warily. “Jee, I’ve never…”

Ravi reached over and took the bottle of nail polish.

“I’ll do it,” he said. “Don’t want to ruin your nails.”

“No, seriously, screw you.”

“What are Hen and Karen going to do now?” Ravi asked, uncapping the nail polish. The air suddenly smelled of bananas. “Should we, I don’t know, send them condolences?”

“Oh, apparently Deidra told them about some other girl,” Albert said. “She’s older, nine instead of a baby. Dead parents, in a group home. They took her in today.”

“That was fast.”

“I don’t think it’s any faster than their other placements.” Albert watched as Ravi carefully drew the brush of Jee-Yun’s tiny nails, covering them one at a time in bright, vivid yellow. His face was a mask of concentration, and Albert couldn’t help but lean over to kiss it.

“Al!” Ravi’s attempt to be stern was ruined by his laughing. “Don’t make me mess her nails up.”

“Yeah!” Jee-Yun wagged a finger at Albert. “No messing!”

“Sorry, princess.”

The house was silent and empty in a way that reminded Bobby a little too much of the apartment he lived in before Athena. Athena herself was on shift, while Harry was at Buck and Eddie’s, catching up with Chris. Bobby knew that’s where he was, and that that was what he was doing, because Athena was enforcing near-Orwellian levels of supervision on the teenager.

She was taking him being in her “custody” for his community service very seriously. Harry was just lucky his mother hadn’t LoJacked him.

Usually, Bobby didn’t mind the silence too terribly much. He, unlike a certain young man, was perfectly capable of enjoying it, even basking in it. Large parts of his life were dictated by noise and chaos. He’d take, and appreciate, any breaks from that when he was offered them.

Except, far too often, these moments of peace were yanked away. Like now, just as Bobby was about to sit down with the book that Buck had been bothering him to read, someone made their presence known at the front door. The list of possibilities for who the unexpected guest might’ve been was long, but it was easy to cross a few suspects off the list.

It obviously wasn’t Athena, she’d just walk right in, and so would Harry. May, ever since that embarrassing incident when she walked in on him and her mother… taking advantage of an empty nest, had taken to ringing the doorbell and knocking, but she knew Athena wasn’t home. She would’ve just let herself in, as would Buck.

Hen and Karen, he knew, were busy settling in with the new addition to their family. Chimney had mentioned a date night with Maddie. Albert and Ravi rarely, if ever, came over uninvited. Eddie might’ve come over by himself, but there wasn’t anything going on in his life that would have the man in need of one of their talks. Not that Bobby knew about, anyway.

Or was there? There was something causing strife between Buck and Eddie not too long ago. He tried to stay out of their relationship, because there was the unspoken notion that he’d automatically take Buck’s side no matter what. The worst part was that Bobby couldn’t immediately deny it, and he didn’t want to find out if it was true. Plus, being their captain added another layer of complications to it all.

It didn’t mean that it wasn’t a temptation to step in on the rare occasions that they were hitting a rough patch with one another. Luckily, Hen had decided to meddle this last time before that temptation became too much to resist.

Whoever it was at the door hadn’t gone away. They rang the doorbell again, reminding Bobby of their presence. He heaved out a sigh, set the still unopened book aside, and got up to go answer it.

It was only his manners, taught to him by his mother and enforced by decades of practice, that kept Bobby from slamming the door closed. He still couldn’t find the strength to sound anywhere close to friendly when he said,

“Hello, Phillip.”

The last time he saw Phillip Buckley, he was watching with pride as Maddie ran both of her parents out of the house and then out of the city. The time since had evidently not been kind to him. He looked as if he had shrunk and withered, his edges growing sharper and more prominent.

“Hello, Bobby.” Phillip even sounded tired.

“What are you doing here?”

“Well, I don’t know where either of my children live now,” the man said. “And I don’t think Buck would appreciate a visit at the station.”

“I’m not sure I appreciate this visit,” Bobby bluntly told him. “Where’s Margaret?”

“Back in Hershey, still,” Phillip answered. He came to Los Angeles by himself? “Could I come in and sit down, please?”

If Bobby was a worse person, a pettier person, he would’ve denied the request. He should deny the request. But the same manners that kept him from slamming the door in the man’s face had him letting him inside once again. That, plus how tired Phillip looked from just standing there.

Bobby watched the man make his slow way inside, gripping the railing as he went down the stairs. Phillip went for the nearest chair and lowered himself into it, looking relieved as he did. Bobby retook his seat on the couch.

“You have new pictures,” Phillip remarked, looking at one on the end table beside them. It was a framed photo of Bobby and Buck with Chris and Gavin, sitting next to one of Bobby and Athena with all three of their kids. He pointed at Gavin. “Who’s this?”

“Buck and Eddie’s youngest,” Bobby answered, and tried to ignore the vindictive pleasure at the pained expression that crossed Phillip’s face.

“Their youngest,” Phillip repeated. “Next you’ll be telling me that Maddie’s gotten married again and didn’t invite us.”

“Not yet,” Bobby said. “Why are you here?”

“Buck won’t answer my calls,” Phillip said. Bobby wasn’t aware that there had been calls to answer. “And I’m pretty sure Maddie blocked my number.”

“Are you surprised?”

“No.” The honest answer was refreshing, and surprising. “I wasn’t a very good father, was I?”

“No,” Bobby agreed.

“I loved them,” Phillip said. “But… Buck was right, at that dinner here. I have always cared more about Margaret. I loved him, just like I loved Maddie and Daniel. But I’ve always loved Margaret more than them.”

“Why are you here, Phillip?”

“I was hoping… I don’t know what I was hoping for,” he said. “That my children would let me waltz back in like I deserved it.”

“I don’t think that’s going to happen.”

“You’re probably right,” Phillip said, shaking his head. “Because I don’t deserve it.”

If Bobby spoke, he would only say unkind things. So he kept his mouth shut.

“We’re Episcopalian, but I’ve never been all that religious,” Phillip continued, laughing. It was a dark laugh, entirely devoid of both humor and cheer. “But there must be a God, and he must have a sense of humor. Why else would I be dying of leukemia?”

What was Bobby supposed to say to that?

Chapter End Notes

Don't worry, this is still an unsafe space for the Buckley parents.

Chapter 15: A Foregone Conclusion

Chapter Summary

In which Bobby has no good choices in front of him.

Chapter Notes

At some point while writing this fic, Darling, So It Goes, as a series, broke 500k words.

“So,” Athena said, eying her husband over the top of her glass of wine. She was leaning against the dining table, watching Bobby as he absolutely butchered the vegetables he was cutting up for dinner. “Are you going to tell me what has you in such a mood tonight?”

The sergeant came home from work after a shift that, although it wasn’t any worse than usual, left her feeling rather drained. She was looking forward to having a glass of her finest wine while curling up on the couch with Bobby, maybe convince him to give her a massage. It wouldn’t have been very hard, not really. She would have just needed to ask.

Except, when Athena walked through the door, it was to find an extremely agitated husband and a son that was hiding in his room like it was a bunker.

“He’s been like that since I got home,” Harry told her when she went to make sure he actually was in his room and hadn’t snuck out. “I don’t know why, I don’t need to know why. I’m staying out of his way.”

It was nice to know that her youngest was inheriting his parents’ intelligence. Especially after how foolheaded he’d been acting not that long ago. Still, though, his willing ignorance meant Athena was still at a loss as to what was bothering Bobby. Which meant she had to go to the source and as much as she loved her husband, she could admit that sometimes getting him to tell her what was bothering him, or even to admit that something was bothering him, was about as easy as holding Buck back from leaping headfirst into something dangerous.

Walking into the kitchen, Athena didn’t immediately start the interrogation. She observed him for a bit first as she pulled out the bottle of wine, a California Merlot that Maddie said was a “congratulations on surviving the Poseidon” gift, along with a glass. Normally, when Bobby cut up his ingredients, there was a certain delicate art to it. Normally, his hands were steady, the motions of the knife even, the results nearly uniform.

Normally.

This time, it really did seem like he was butchering the vegetables in front of him. One piece was large and chunky, another was almost paper thin. Everytime the blade came down, it was with a loud thunk that had her wondering if they were going to need a new cutting board. His shoulders and neck weren’t stiff, but there was a dark cloud over his face. 

Bobby didn’t say anything to her, barely greeted her beyond a quick glance and a nod before he went back to taking out his frustrations on innocent stalks of celery. That, too, was a sign of how much he was bothered by whatever was bothering him. Sure, they joked that Buck was a Golden Retriever that someone cursed into human-shape, but Bobby wasn’t all that different. At least when it came to greeting her after a long day apart.

After taking in her observations, and taking a fortifying swig of wine, Athena leaned against the dining table and asked,

“So, are you going to tell me what has you in such a mood tonight?”

Bobby looked at her over his shoulder before dumping the bits of celery into a bowl. A package of mushrooms was torn open and poured out onto the cutting board in the celery’s place. They were soon receiving the same brutal, indelicate treatment.

“We had a visitor,” Bobby finally said.

“I take it that this visitor wasn’t a neighbor asking for a cup of sugar,” Athena said. “Or someone from the station.”

“No.” The knife slammed into a mushroom, cleaving through it and hitting the board beneath with another thunk. Bobby had to yank it loose from where it got stuck in the wood. “It wasn’t.”

“I can start making guesses of who it could’ve been,” Athena said. “It wasn’t anyone here about Harry, they would’ve gone through me and Elaine. My mother is still in Florida, so it couldn’t have been her. It wasn’t anyone from the station, that wouldn’t put you in this mood. Was it someone from the department brass, making noises about your back again?”

“No,” Bobby said. “It was Phillip.”

Athena straightened up as her grip on the wine glass tightened enough that, if she could spare the attention for it, she’d worry about shattering it.

“Phillip who?”

“Hey, when is…” Harry trailed off as Athena turned her head to look at him. He was leaning halfway into the kitchen, and he blanched at whatever expression he saw on her face. “Nevermind, I’m going back to my room.”

Smart boy, Athena thought as she watched him go.

“Buckley,” Bobby said when Harry was gone, answering her question. “Phillip Buckley.”

“Phillip Buckley was here?” Athena snarled, setting her wine down hard enough that it sloshed a little over the edges. She ignored that minor mess as she stalked to her husband’s side. “In my house?”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“He has leukemia,” Bobby told her.

“Well ain’t karma a bitch.” Athena shook her head. “What, did he come crawling here hoping you’d help him convince the savior sibling to help save him?”

“I asked him that,” Bobby said. The massacre of mushrooms was finished, but he didn’t pour the victims into the bowl with the celery. He set the knife down instead, and put his hands flat on the counter on either side of the cutting board. “He pointed out that they had Buck to be a match for Daniel, since none of them were.”

“So he’s here to beg Maddie, then,” she guessed, but her husband shook his head. 

“He’s not,” Bobby said.

“He’s not?” She didn’t believe that for a moment. “Then why is he here? For that matter, where’s Margaret?”

“In Hershey, still. He said that she hasn’t been handling it well.”

Athena felt a brief flare of empathy for the woman, reminded as she was of Michael’s brush with brain cancer. It made her nauseous, and she quickly crushed it until not a spark remained. She did not ever want to empathize with Margaret Buckley.

“So he came out to LA alone,” she said. “Why?”

“Apparently Maddie’s blocked their numbers.” Good for her. “And Buck hasn’t been answering.”

“It’s been two years since either of them darkened their children’s lives,” Athena pointed out. “Why now, if he’s not here to beg them for a donation?”

“Because he’s dying.”

Well, that she could understand. Looking death in the face, having it looming over you like an ominous specter, could easily rearrange a person’s priorities. But she hated that this was what it took for Phillip Buckley to actually prioritize his children, that he couldn’t have been a decent father years ago, back when it actually mattered.

“What are you going to tell Buck?” Athena asked her husband. “And Maddie?”

“I don’t know,” Bobby admitted. “They deserve to know.”

“They do.”

“I don’t want to make them feel like they have to see him, just because he’s sick,” he said. “You know that’s how they’ll feel. That’s how Buck will feel, at least. He’ll let both of those awful people back into his life, ignoring how much damage they’ve done and how much damage they’ll do.”

“He will.”

That was, sadly, not in any doubt. Buck may not forgive and forget, but he would definitely ignore his own feelings for the sake of someone else. He would be perfectly willing to let his biological parents back into his life out of a sense of obligation and guilt. Eddie wouldn’t like it any more than she or Bobby, Athena knew, but he’d support Buck in whatever he decided to do.

“Maybe,” Athena said after a moment. “Maybe you don’t tell him about the cancer.”

Bobby turned his head to frown at her.

“We just agreed that he and Maddie deserve to know.”

“We did,” she said. “But you said Phillip has been calling Buck. If he was leaving messages, do you think Buck would simply not listen to them?”

Her husband shook his head.

“No, he’s too curious.”

“Exactly. So Phillip wants to talk to them, but he hasn’t told Buck about the cancer for whatever reason. Maybe he doesn’t want them to know about it.”

“Maybe,” Bobby agreed, sounding unsure and uneasy.

“So tell Buck that Phillip is in town, tell him that he wants to see him,” Athena said. “Don’t tell him anything else, and let Buck decide from there. No guilt, no feelings of obligation. If he gives Phillip the time of day-”

“He probably will,” Bobby said, glaring down at the counter top.

“He probably will. And when he does, Phillip can tell him about it then.”

“And if he doesn’t tell Buck?” Bobby asked.

“Then he doesn’t.” Athena shrugged. “It’s his health, he’s allowed to keep it a secret from his children.”

“I hate them,” he said, which was a very strong statement for her husband to make.

“I know.” She went back to her wine glass. “So do I.”

There was something on Bobby’s mind, that much was very obvious once Buck and Eddie made it up to the station’s loft. It was only to be expected that Buck would notice that kind of thing after knowing the man for the better part of a decade. He just couldn’t think of what it might be.

The moment the captain spotted him, he summoned him over to help cook. Which, okay, that wasn’t too unusual. Except that they were just making breakfast, simple bacon (pig and turkey) and eggs, nothing too fancy. Nothing that really required two people. It wasn’t something Buck would ever turn down, though, so he just bounded over and fell into place beside him.

A couple times, Bobby opened his mouth like he was going to say something. Both times, though, he just closed it and shook his head and then asked how Chris was doing in school and what else was left to do for Maddie’s wedding. Both of which were, Buck could admit, perfect questions to ask him if someone wanted him to take over the conversation for a bit.

Okay, more than a bit.

“I’m still working on the seating chart,” Buck said while Bobby nodded along. He wasn’t really helping the captain cook anything at this point, instead he was mostly hovering in a way that would have, by now, greatly irritated his birth parents. “It would be easier if everyone they sent an invite to actually replied one way or another. Like, I know Maddie sent out wedding invites to some of her old friends from Hershey and Boston, but most of them never sent anything back. So I’m thinking there’s just going to be an ‘And All the Rest’ table stuck in the back for-”

“Oh God, is he going on about wedding planning again?” Albert broke in with a groan.

Buck turned his head to glare at him. Albert flung himself into the bar seat next to Eddie, who had been perfectly content to sit at the kitchen island and listen to Buck ramble on.

“Where’s Ravi?” Eddie asked.

“Downstairs,” Albert answered, raising a hand to gesture lazily behind him. Buck caught a glimpse of something on his fingernails that glittered as he did. “Toby kidnapped him to help with the engine.”

“Nice color,” Buck commented, getting a closer look to see that Albert’s nails were clumsily painted a bright, pale pink. “It suits you.”
