It was Albert’s turn to glare, though it wasn’t very effective. Honestly, Buck was struck by the similarity to Senorita when they annoyed her too much. The man even hissed like her when Buck pulled out his phone to take a picture of them.

“They are not going on your stupid page,” Albert said, clutching his hands to his chest, fingers curled so the nails weren’t visible.

“Oh, come on,” Buck wheedled. “Don’t you want to have a picture of our niece’s art work for posterity?”

“No.”

“You are the worst uncle.”

“Who’s the worst uncle?” a tired sounding Hen asked. She dropped into a seat at the nearby dining table, hunched over and rubbing her neck. Just looking at her made Buck want to go back to bed.

“Albert is,” Eddie answered. “You okay?”

“Rough night,” was her obvious answer.

Bobby tapped Buck’s shoulder, motioning for him to keep an eye on the food, and got down two mugs from the cabinet. He poured coffee into both of them and went to join Hen, setting one of them down in front of her before taking a seat for himself.

“This might help,” he said.

“Yeah,” Hen sighed, rubbing at the back of her neck. “That and maybe a handful of ibuprofen.”

“You got a headache?”

“More like an everything ache.”

“How’s the little one settling in?” Bobby asked.

“What’s her name?” Buck cut in, nudging at one of the skillets of bacon. 

“Mara,” Hen answered. “Something happened to that little girl.”

“What do you mean?” Albert asked, having turned around in his seat.

“Last night, she had an episode,” Hen told them. “Some kind of night terror, maybe. I ended up sleeping on the floor next to her bed.”

“Ah, I was going to suggest looking into a new mattress,” Bobby said. “Maybe what you need is carpet.”

Hen let out a small, tired chuckle before turning serious again.

“She’s only been with us a day and a night,” she said. “But she hasn’t uttered on single word.”

“A day and a night’s not very long,” Bobby told her. “I’m sure once she realizes she’s safe, she’ll open up.”

“Yeah, that’s what I keep telling myself.”

On the table, laying next to her arm, Hen’s phone let out a pair of chimes. She picked it up and unlocked the screen with her thumb. Whatever she saw had her looking wide awake and horrified as she scrambled out of the chair.

“Oh, God, I’ve got to call Karen,” was her only explanation as she hurried to the other end of the loft, phone pressed to her ear.

“That’s never been a good reaction to a text,” Eddie remarked.

“No, it isn’t,” Bobby agreed, getting up as well. He glanced in Buck’s direction, and then looked at Albert. “Al, could you go see if Chimney or Ravi need help with anything?”

Albert shared a look with the two Diazes, confusion very clear on his face, before sliding out of his seat.

“You could just say you want me to leave,” he said, as he headed for the stairs.

“Uh, what’s up, Cap?” Buck asked, feeling uneasy. Eddie wasn’t doing any better, he could tell.

Bobby didn’t answer right away as he rejoined Buck at the stove. He glanced the food over, turned off the burners, and nudged Buck to move.

“Why don’t you two go take a seat,” he said.

Buck and Eddie did so, slowly and with an air of anxiety between them. They sat next to each other, as they almost always did, pulling the chairs to be as close to one another as possible. Together, they watched as Bobby piled two plates up with bacon and eggs, which were then sat down in front of them with silverware. Then, the captain poured them both cups of coffee, making them the way they both liked it, including the minor mound of sugar that went into Buck’s that Bobby always made remarks about.

“Hey, Cap.” Hen sounded on the verge of frantic. “Denny’s at the hospital.”

“What happened?” Bobby asked, setting the coffee in front of Buck and Eddie.

“I’m not sure, Mara threw a coffee mug.” Shit. “I need-”

“Go,” Bobby said. 

The word was barely out of his mouth before Hen was rushing down the stairs. The three watched her go, Buck and Eddie poking at their food, before the captain let out a sigh and sat down across from them. He reached over to his own coffee, left there from earlier, and dragged it over. 

“So, Cap, not that we’re not grateful,” Eddie said, gesturing at their plates. “But what’s going on?”

Bobby let out a sigh.

“Phillip’s in town.”

Silverware clattered as Buck dropped his fork onto his plate. The bit of egg he’d just eaten went down his throat like a rock.

“Wh- What?”

“What do you mean, Phillip’s in town?” Eddie demanded, leaning forward a bit.

“He showed up at my door yesterday,” Bobby said, clasping his hands together in front of him on the table. “Because he doesn’t know where you or Maddie live at now, and he at least thought better than to show up here unannounced.”

“Just Dad?” Buck asked. “What about Mom?”

“She’s still in Hershey.”

“Okay, why is that bastard here?” Eddie looked, and sounded, angry. “He’s been calling Buck, and now he shows up in the city. What does he want that’s so important?”

“I couldn’t say,” was Bobby’s answer. “He wants to see Buck and Maddie, obviously.”

Eddie let out a disgusted scoff, shaking his head as he grabbed his coffee.

“How long is he staying?” Buck asked. “Did he say?”

“I don’t know,” the captain said. “A few days, maybe.”

The three of them fell silent. Buck picked up his fork again, but didn’t do much except poke at his food. After a few moments, Bobby got up and rounded the table to give Buck a comforting squeeze to the shoulder, before heading down the stairs to tell everyone that breakfast was ready. It also, conveniently, left the two Diazes alone.

“You want to talk to him.” It wasn’t a question, because Eddie knew him too well for that.

“He’s been calling and I’ve been ignoring him,” Buck said. “And then he comes all the way out here, alone. I have to, don’t I?”

“You don’t have to do anything when it comes to them.”

“Yeah, but.” He shrugged. “He came all the way to Los Angeles to talk, Eds. He went to Ma and Pops’ house. What if he shows up here?”

“Bobby’ll kick him out,” was Eddie’s response.

“And I… I kinda want to know what’s so important.”

“Of course you do,” Eddie said, even as he reached out to grip Buck’s hand.

Chapter 16: Sowing, Reaping

Chapter Summary

The tragedy of realizing too late that you should have done better.

This was, somehow, the first time Buck had been to Metro Dispatch since the fire that devoured the old building. That was two years ago, and yet Buck had never swung by before now. There simply hadn’t been any reason to visit. Although, considering he hadn’t actually laid eyes on Josh for a while, maybe he ought to do it more often. Maybe he should start visiting his sister for lunch sometimes, he could bring Gavin along to win the hearts of all her coworkers through sheer adorableness.

This was not a visit that Gavin needed to be present for, however. Honestly, Buck didn’t want to be making this visit - didn’t want to go in there and have this conversation - but he didn’t want to put it off, and he didn’t want to have it over the phone. This was very much an in-person conversation. Hell, he and Eddie specifically hadn’t mentioned anything to Chimney, because they didn’t want him letting it slip to Maddie before Buck could talk to her today. And they didn’t want to torture him by asking him to keep it to himself until then, either.

He swiped his department ID badge at the front desk and was waved through, taking the stairs instead of the elevator. The new floor was set up vastly different from its predecessor. On one side, the workstations were in tiered rows with steps leading up and down them. On the other side were glass rooms, one the new break room and one that might’ve been a conference room, judging from the table.

It was occupied.

“Hey.” Maddie was cheerful as she greeted him, taking a detour from going back to her workstation to meet him halfway. “What are you doing here?”

“I, uh, I wanted to talk to you about something,” he answered, still focused on the couple in the conference room, the pair of them leaning in to look at something on a laptop. “It’s not really a phone conversation. Is that Hen and Karen?”

Maddie glanced over her shoulder.

“Yeah,” she said. “They’re listening to an old Nine-One-One call.”

“Why?” he asked, but his sister just shrugged at him. She probably knew, she just decided it wasn’t any of his business and wasn’t going to tell him. Which was absolutely fair.

“So what is it that you wanted to talk to me about?” Maddie led him to the break room as she talked. It was currently devoid of people, which he was thankful for. Buckleys never did like having their family drama aired out for everyone else. “Wait, is this about the phone call the other day? You said it was Gavin’s aunt, right? You never told me what she wanted.”

“His great aunt, and uh…” Buck scratched at his neck, letting the glass door of the breakroom close behind him. “Amy, his bio mom, wants to see him.”

“Oh.” Maddie scrunched up her nose as she got a bottle of water from the fridge. “And how do we feel about this?”

“Um.” Buck thought for a moment. “I mean, she was a kid when she had him, not that she still isn’t a kid, and she walked to the station and gave him up because she knew it would be better for him. It’s not like she stuffed him down a pipe and tried to act like he never existed.”

“Or keep him and then regret not giving him up because looking at him was upsetting.”  

The derision in his sister’s voice had Buck wincing. But that was, unfortunately, a very good segue to what he was actually here to talk to her about.

“So, speaking of Mom and Dad-”

“Oh, God.” Maddie sat down at the breakroom’s island and rolled her eyes. “Did Dad call you again? Or is Mom joining in now?”

“Uh, no, no.” Buck stayed standing, anxiously shifting his weight between legs as he drummed his fingers on the back of a chair. “Dad’s in town.”

Maddie had the water bottle raised to take a drink, but froze with it halfway to her open mouth. She slowly, almost deliberately, lowered it and screwed the cap back on.

“Dad’s what?”

“He apparently visited Ma and Pops the other day, since, y’know, he doesn’t know where we live now. Just Dad, Mom’s still in Hershey. Or so he told Pops.” Buck made a face as he heard the words that just came out of his mouth. “You know, it’s a good thing we met both of our husbands before the family stuff got really complicated, or else we’d have to figure out if flowcharts are a third or fourth date thing.”

“I’ve already prepared myself for questions from teachers if they ever have Jee do a presentation on the family,” Maddie said. “Why is Dad in Los Angeles? What does he want?”

“To see us,” Buck answered with a shrug. “I- I mean, he’s been calling me for weeks now, right? And they’ve been late at night, Maddie. I mean late late. Late for us, which means it was even later for him.”

“I told them both to stay out of our lives.” She shook her head. “You want to talk to him.”

Like with Eddie before her, it was a statement of fact and not a question. Because they both knew him far too well to ever for a moment doubt that he would cave like this. And since it wasn’t really a question, it didn’t really need an answer. Buck just gave his sister a sheepish shrug.

“And what does Eddie think about this?” she asked.

“He’s going to support me in whatever I decide to do.”

“He hates the idea.”

“Oh, absolutely.”

Maddie let out a sigh, set her water bottle down, and then reached over the island, hand closed in a fist but with her pinky finger out. Buck reached back, entwining his own pinky with hers.

“United front,” she reminded him. “Let’s hear what he has to say.”

God, he loved his sister.

Someone rapped at the glass door to the breakroom, and the pair dropped their hands to look. Hen opened the door and poked her head in, Karen visible beside her through the glass. Buck thought they looked vaguely sad, the both of them wearing an expression similar to the one he saw on his own face in the rearview mirror of the jeep after the conversation with Sylvie.

“Hey, Buck,” Hen said, pushing the door open all the way. “Sorry for interrupting.”

“You guys done?” Maddie asked, leaving her bottle of water where it was as she rounded the island to go to them.

“Yeah.” Hen shared a glance with her wife. “We found out what we needed.”

“Good, I’m glad.”

“Thank you again, Maddie,” Karen said.

“No problem, I’m just glad I could help.”

The Wilsons said their goodbyes, thanking Maddie again as they did, before taking their leave. Buck followed Maddie as she went to fetch the laptop they’d been using, no doubt to give it back to IT or whoever it belonged to.

“Everything’s okay, right?” he asked her. “With Hen and Karen, I mean.”

“Yeah,” Maddie was quick to say. “Like I said, they were just listening to an old call, I think it had to do with something Athena found for them.”

Buck was curious, he was always curious. It was what drove him to do things like sneak a peek at Bobby’s journal his probie year and almost go dumpster diving for evidence with Taylor, that one time. But Maddie was very good at keeping secrets, perhaps the best, and if she decided that it wasn’t any of his business, then she wasn’t going to tell him anything more.

“So, you’ll go with me to talk to Dad?” he asked, instead of pressing further about Hen and Karen.

“United front. Just tell me when and where.”

Phillip Buckley, once upon a time, used to pride himself on being a good husband. He loved his wife so very much, with all his heart and all his soul. The day he met Margaret, their first date, when they got engaged, when they got married, those were still, after so very long, the moments in his life that he treasured the most. Those were the memories he held close to his chest to keep himself warm through the darkest of times.

In Plato’s Symposium, Aristophanes told the story of how, at the beginning, humans once had two heads, two hearts, two souls. They were fearless and powerful and prideful. They threatened to conquer the gods themselves, to rule in their stead. Zeus, king of the gods, split them in half. He tore them asunder, ripped them from their other halves. Humans were thereafter filled with despair and loneliness and longing, forever searching for their other halves so they could be whole again.

Phillip believed, had always believed, that Margaret was his other half. She was his person, and he was hers, and he loved her more than anything else in the world. He loved her too much, maybe. Because he really did love her more than anything else in the world.

Phillip Buckley wasn’t sure if he had ever been considered a good father. The years before Daniel’s diagnosis were happy ones, even if the memories were now tainted with mourning. But the problem had always been that he was a better husband than he was a father. But the problem had always been that he loved his wife more than he loved his children.

Daniel got sick, and none of them were a match to save him, so they had Evan. But it didn’t work. The doctors had their reasons and excuses, but none of it mattered. All that mattered was that the child they wanted to save was gone, and they were left with the one that had failed. That had the looks, and the whispers, and the judgment from everyone around them for hanging all their hopes on another child.

Margaret was in no shape to handle that, not with how lost in mourning she was. The choice to pack up and move was an easy one, because Phillip would do anything for the love of his life. Erasing Daniel’s memory from their lives, that had been harder. He’d still done it, though, because being reminded of what she had lost had hurt Margaret ever so much. So Maddie was sworn to absolute secrecy, and all the reminders were donated or thrown out or hidden away.

Almost all the reminders.

Two years ago, Evan- Buck, Phillip reminded himself. He went by Buck now, and had for years. Two years ago, Buck stood in the Grant-Nash kitchen and accused Phillip of caring more about Margaret than he did his youngest. And the awful truth was that it was the truth. Phillip cared more about his wife, loved her more, than he did Buck. He loved her more than he loved Daniel, than he loved Maddie.

But that didn’t mean he didn’t love them at all. After Daniel was buried, when they started the process of erasing him from their lives, there was the question of what to do with little Evan. The son they had hung all their hopes on, which were now withered on the vine. Just seeing a picture of Daniel was enough to have Margaret burst into tears, so it went without saying how upset she got every time Evan - the son they had made, the one she had carried for nine months, just to save Daniel - reminded the world of his existence.

They talked about it then, whispered it in the dark where Maddie could not hear. He couldn’t remember which one of them brought it up first, the idea of giving little Evan up, of donating him to some other family just as they were doing with all of Daniel’s clothes and toys. He couldn’t remember if it was said in jest or in seriousness at first. But they talked about it, maybe even, perhaps, fought about it.

They didn’t go through with it, obviously. And Evan- Buck that day, when he was revealing even more of their family’s dirty laundry for all the world to gossip about, he had said that he didn’t know why they hadn’t. Phillip thought the answer was obvious, it should have been obvious. But Phillip was also always a better husband than he had been a father, so maybe it wasn’t so obvious to his son as it should have been.

He loved both his sons, and he loved his daughter. He just… He just didn’t love them as much as he loved his wife, that was all. And loving Evan didn’t keep him from feeling regret, as the years went by, for choosing not to give him up. When Evan got older and looked so very much like Daniel; when he got older still and they were taunted with the idea of what Daniel might have looked like. When every time he got himself hurt and ended up in the hospital, Phillip had to comfort his wife as she was reminded - viciously, cruelly - of the days and nights they spent at Daniel’s bedside.

Once upon a time, he prided himself on being a good husband. He doted on his wife, took her out on dates even after years of marriage, surprised her with beautiful bouquets of flowers. He prioritized her above everyone else, because that was what he thought he was supposed to do. Because that was what he wanted to do.

That was before, though. Before he sat in that examination room - alone, because he couldn’t bear to do that to Margaret - and got the news from a doctor who spoke with such detached kindness. Before he heard “leukemia” and “treatment plans” and “six to twelve months.” Before he realized that God absolutely did exist, if only because there had to be a thinking entity behind such irony.

He remembered the stages of grief they went through with Daniel, after his diagnosis. Denial, anger, bargaining, depression, acceptance. It had taken years for him and Margaret to move through all the stages then. It only took days for Phillip to do it this time. Days in which he was struck in the face with how little time he had left. Days in which he looked back and felt regret.  

The death of a son had not rearranged Phillips’ priorities, but the looming specter of his own did.

Once upon a time, he prided himself on being a good husband. But he wasn’t a good father, no matter how much he loved his children. He could’ve been a better father, he should’ve been a better father. But he hadn’t been, because he was too busy being a good husband, and where did that leave him? His daughter declared that he and Margaret were dead to her. His son had replaced them both with utter strangers.

And no matter how often he called, in the dark hours of the night after Margaret cried herself to sleep and he found courage in a bottle, his children did not answer. Because he’d loved his wife more than he loved them, and this was what he got for it.

After Maddie agreed to go with him to see their father, Buck called the man to set up a time and a place. Phillip answered almost immediately, sounding surprised and overjoyed. They hammered out where and when to meet, with Phillip being agreeable to all of Buck’s suggestions.

“Are you sure about this?” Eddie asked as Buck waited to be picked up by his sister. The place they were meeting Phillip, Syd Kronenthal Park, was just a few streets away from Athena and Bobby, so Maddie was picking Buck up on the way. “You can still change your mind, cancel on his sorry ass, and go do something fun with Maddie.”

“Or if you wish,” Abuela said from the dining table, where she was frowning down at a half-finished jigsaw puzzle, “Eddito and I can go with you both.”

“Eddie might start throwing hands if Dad says the wrong thing,” Buck told her, leaning into his husband. “And I’d have to shake you down for knives.”

She gave him a withering look, but she didn’t argue the point.

“‘Sides, ‘Buela, you’re dragging me to the crafts store, remember?” Eddie said.

Abuela had decided that she wanted to get back into knitting. Coincidentally, the other day, Buck got to listen to her as she complained about how one of her church friends wouldn’t shut up about the blanket she was knitting for her granddaughter, though that was certainly unrelated. He and Eddie were expecting her to give it up again before too long, however, since her arthritis hadn’t magically disappeared just because she was as stubborn as her grandson.

Then again, she was very stubborn. Buck wouldn’t put it past her to tough it out until she had a bigger and better blanket to show off, aching joints be damned. And hell, maybe he’d join her. Learning to knit might be fun.

“Maybe Gavin can help you pick out the yarn colors,” Buck said. His phone chimed in his pocket, though he didn’t need to check it to know what message just came in. “Maddie’s here.”

“Good luck,” Eddie whispered to him, giving him a kiss and a quick pat on the rump.

Buck rolled his eyes as he went out the door. Maddie’s car was parked on the curb, and it was a quick walk down the driveway to clamber into the passenger seat. She turned down the radio while he got buckled in and lowered her sunglasses to look at him.

“We don’t have to do this,” she said. “We can still-”

“Cancel on him and go do something fun together?” Buck finished for her, grinning. “Eddie just gave me the same spiel.”

“We do need to go do something fun together,” Maddie declared as she pulled away from the curb. “A brother-sister day. No kids, no husbands.”

It was not a long drive to the park, and Buck wished it was longer. The park wasn’t in Los Angeles, it was in Culver City. Though depending on who you asked, that might still be considered part of LA. They’d actually been called to the aftermath of a fight, once, about that particular distinction. Athena had not been amused.

The park was not very big, most of it taken up by a large green space that was partitioned into sports fields - softball, baseball, soccer - with a small playground by the parking lot and beside a building that… Buck actually wasn’t certain what it was for. A daycare, maybe? It had its own playground, separate from the park, that had a bunch of tiny tots running around it when they arrived.

Phillip was easy to spot. He was sitting at a picnic table beneath one of the shelters, just on the other side of the public playground. Buck saw him glance at them and then sit up, able to see them through the windshield. He looked older, Buck thought. Older and thinner. The years had not been kind to him, evidently.

“Alright,” Maddie said with a sigh as she shut off the car. She raised her hand between them, pinky extended. “Let’s do this.”

“Let’s do this,” Buck agreed, wrapping his pinky around hers and giving it a little shake.

It was the middle of a school day, so there weren’t very many others present, barring a woman chasing around a little girl with pigtails that couldn’t have been any older than Gavin. They didn’t pay the two of them any mind as they marched past, Phillip standing at their approach. The man made an aborted motion like he was about to go in for a hug and then thought better of it.

“Hi, Dad,” Maddie said, eying their father warily. 

“You came.” Phillip sounded surprised. Deservedly so, because Buck had been severely tempted to go with the suggestion of ditching the plan for something else.

“Well you keep calling me.” Buck eased himself down onto the bench across from where Phillip had been sitting, twisting around so his long legs weren’t under the table. Maddie followed suit, sitting beside him. “And then you showed up at Pops’ house, so…”

He watched his father wince at the word Pops as he sat back down, too.

“Well,” Phillip said. “You weren’t answering.”

“Why would I?” Buck blurted out before he could think better of it.

“Right. You’re right. Why would you?” His father sounded pained. “I saw that your… your captain, Bobby, right? I saw he had a new photo of your family. What’s the little one’s name?”

Buck shared an uneasy glance with Maddie.

“Gavin Robert Diaz,” he answered after a moment. 

“That’s- That’s a nice name.” Phillip’s smile was strained. “And Christopher? Jee-Yun? How are they?”

“Chris is fine. He had some girl troubles earlier.”

“He is getting to be that age.” Phillip let out a small laugh. “I remember when you-”

“Why are you here?” Maddie interrupted the attempted trip down memory lane. Buck was thankful for it, because there was no way it wasn’t going to end in anything but awkwardness for everyone. “I told you that I wanted the both of you out of our lives, and you seemed perfectly fine with that the last two years. Why are you here? Why now?”

Phillip stared at her and then dropped his head with a sigh, setting his hands flat on top of the picnic table.

“I haven’t been the best of fathers,” he said. “Have I?”

“No,” Buck immediately answered. “Wait, is that why you’re here now? Because you’re just realizing that?”

Phillip took a breath and opened his mouth, but instead of saying anything, he just let the breath out and focused his gaze on his hands.

“Dad?” Maddie prompted, frowning at him.

“I’ve been thinking lately,” Phillip finally said. “I’m getting on in years, you know? Long in the tooth. I’ve been thinking, remembering. And yeah, I am just realizing that I wasn’t a very good father. I was too busy trying to be a good husband, because I-”

“Because you love Mom more than you love us,” Buck finished for him. Even though he already knew that, it still hurt when Phillip nodded.

“But that doesn’t mean…” Phillip raised his head to look at Buck. “You said you didn’t know why your mother and I didn’t give you up for adoption. And I couldn’t understand that, because I thought the answer was obvious, but… I wasn’t a good father, I’m realizing that now.”

“So?” Buck demanded. “Why didn’t you let some other family have me?”

“Because you’re my son.” His father shrugged. “And I love you.”

That was not the answer that Buck was expecting. He thought the answer would be something like guilt or obligation, or to keep from having something else for people to whisper about behind their backs. He wasn’t expecting this to be the answer, and he didn’t expect it to hurt more than the alternatives.

“Where was that when I was growing up?” Buck asked him, hating how his voice cracked. “Why didn’t…”

Ten, twenty years ago, he might have sawed off his own leg just to hear his father say those words. But now that part of him was nothing but scar tissue, and they were far too late to make any difference. He pushed himself to his feet, ignoring the way his father called his name, and stomped back to Maddie’s car. She unlocked it with her fob just as he reached it, so he was able to just climb in and huddle in the passenger seat.

Through the windshield, he could see Maddie had gotten to her feet and was saying something to their father. He couldn’t tell what it was, but it didn’t seem friendly. Whatever Phillip had come here for, whatever reason he had for badgering his way back into their lives, he wasn’t getting it.

He looked so very small as he watched his daughter walk away from him.

Maddie was silent when she got to the car and climbed into the driver seat, and she kindly ignored the way he was wiping at his face. She didn’t say anything until they were back on West Jefferson.

“So I know for a fact that Bobby’s home,” she said, even as she got into the lane to turn onto La Cienega, which would take them to Bobby and Athena’s neighborhood.

“Yeah,” Buck said, sniffing a little. “Let’s do that.”

He really did have the best sister.

Chapter 17: Yeah, Why Not?

Chapter Summary

In which Bobby deals with a visitor to the station, and Chris has a question.

The shift was a calm one so far. Not quiet, never quiet. Bobby tried not to even think that word these days, it was far too risky of an imposition. Calm was okay, though. Or maybe not, there was always a calm before a really bad storm. Maybe Bobby should take cues from George Orwell for ways to talk and to think around the issue without invoking the wrath of any higher powers that might decide to take umbrage with it.

The shift was unloud.

There, that was better.

So far, all they’d really had to deal with was an old woman whose hip had given out on her while her daughter was visiting, a reported house fire that turned out to be nothing more than the smoke coming from someone’s ruined breakfast, and a dog that somehow managed to climb onto someone’s roof and then couldn’t figure out how to get down. Bobby had allowed Buck to be the one to rescue the pooch, even ignoring the way he had Eddie take pictures of him doing so for that stupid account of theirs. His kid needed the reprieve, he thought, after the rather bad - or, perhaps, the ungood - encounter with his biological father. And if that reprieve took the form of the internet getting to see him carry a seventy-five pound golden retriever like a puppy, then so be it.

Bobby had known that Buck’s meeting with Phillip was not going to end well. Anyone with any level of familiarity with the Buckleys could’ve guessed that. Maddie had prepared for it, calling him up on her way to her brother and making sure he would be available to help with the aftermath. When she arrived with Buck, the younger man was subdued and upset, and gladly accepted a hug that lasted as long as it needed to.

They ended up in the kitchen, obviously, where Maddie watched from the dining table as Bobby walked Buck through the steps for making a Kentucky Butter Cake. Which, it was baked in a Bundt pan, so he didn’t understand why a lesser state was trying to lay claim to a dessert that so clearly belonged to Minnesota. While they did so, Maddie shared some gossip from Metro Dispatch and Buck talked about how Isabel was about to get into a knitting competition with someone from church.

They’d also talked, somewhat, about what happened with Phillip, about what Phillip had said. Most importantly was what wasn’t said. Neither Buck nor Maddie mentioned anything about the cancer, which meant Phillip hadn’t told them about it. Either because he didn’t have the chance before he put his foot in it, or because he just chose not to. Bobby didn’t know which it was, and he wasn’t about to speculate.

Though it did leave him in a rather awkward position.

Buck was seemingly doing better now. Maybe he hadn’t come bounding into the station that morning with his usual amount of cheer, but there was certainly more cheer being expressed than when Maddie brought him to the house. The last Bobby had seen him, before retreating to his office to get through the bureaucratic parts of his job that he so often tried to put off, he was arguing with Albert about…

Bobby wasn’t actually sure what they were arguing about, really. He just knew they’d been getting rather animated about it when he took his leave, and he trusted the others to keep things from getting out of hand.

“Hey, uh, Cap?” 

When Bobby looked away from the computer screen, and the emails he was trying to decide needed to be replied to or not, he found an uncertain Toby hovering in the doorway to the captain’s office. 

Bobby wasn’t sure, when he first came back, how he felt about the driver-engineer. He was just so very different from Jones, and his presence was a constant reminder at the beginning that, despite how much Bobby ached at the time - how much he still ached now, if he neglected to do his stretches - he was still so very lucky. It was still off-putting at times to actually have him join in on conversations in the truck, where Jones had been perfectly content to just listen while keeping his thoughts to himself.

“What is it?”

“There’s a visitor.” Toby shifted his weight where he stood. “He seemed kinda upset, so Javier-” Ortiz. “-sent me to get you. They’re saying they’re Buck’s dad?”

Bobby’s office chair nearly hit the wall behind him with how fast he stood.

“Where are they?”

“Just outside,” Toby answered, moving out of the way to let Bobby stomp past. “I think he’s drunk, sir.”

That was double-plus-ungood. He knew he only had himself to blame, this was what he got for even thinking the Q-word earlier. Orwell had the right idea.

“Where’s Buck right now?”

“Upstairs. Why?”

“Go make sure he stays there, please.”

If Bobby hadn’t already known that the kid was dependable, the way that Toby - still looking uncertain - simply nodded and hurried for the stairs would’ve cemented that fact for him. But he had no time to dwell on that, because he needed to get this situation handled before there was a chance for it to spiral out of control.

Just like Toby had said, Phillip was right outside the bay doors. He also wasn’t alone, there was Ortiz acting as a human wall to keep him from getting inside while Clarke watched on, arms crossed and glaring. Phillip was very obviously drunk, clothes disheveled, stumbling a little when he tried to step around Ortiz, and slurring while he spoke.

Bobby got Clarke’s attention as subtly as he could.

“Call dispatch, ask them to send Sergeant Grant over,” he ordered. 

The woman nodded and, with one last glare at Phillip, went off to do just that. It was only then that Bobby approached the other two. He knew when Phillip spotted him, because the man spat out,

“You!”

“I got this,” Bobby said. “Go on back inside.”

“Copy that, Cap,” Ortiz said, reluctantly doing just that. Bobby wouldn’t be surprised if he stopped and lingered just within, though.

“What are you doing here, Phillip?”

“I want to see my son,” the man answered. “You can’t keep me from him, he isn’t yours!”

“Yes, he is,” Bobby said, doing his best to keep his cool.

“Because you took him from me!” Phillip shouted, loud enough that Bobby worried that Buck might’ve heard. “You took them both from me! And I’m telling you right now, you’re not keeping them!”

“I didn’t take anything from you. Buck and Maddie are both adults, and they’re both capable of making their own decisions. But you’re not seeing Buck.”

“He’s my son-”

“Buck doesn’t want to see you,” he bluntly told the man. “He gave you a chance, one he didn’t have to give you, and it didn’t work out. You can’t just show up at his place of work, drunk, and demand to see him.”

“What else am I supposed to do?” Phillip demanded. “He won’t talk to me! Neither of them will.”

“I’m sorry, but I don’t have the answer to that,” Bobby said. “I just know that this is not the way to go about it. I’m not letting you into my station, not like this.”

Phillip drew himself up and Bobby prepared himself for things to get messy. He wasn’t certain how drunk the man was, but it was clear that he was upset and not thinking straight. Which meant there was no telling how he was going to react now. For example, Bobby expected him to try and force his way into the station. Instead, Phillip started to cry.

 It was an unfortunate fact of the job that this was something Bobby had experience with. He may not like the man, in fact he loathed him utterly, but that didn’t stop him from taking Phillip by the shoulders and gently guiding him to sit down on the sidewalk, just out of the way of the bay doors. Hopefully the alarms weren’t about to sound, because Bobby didn’t know what he would do then.

It was always awkward for Bobby to sit with an upset drunk. He knew, through hazy memories and secondhand accounts, how he himself acted when he drank too much. He wasn’t like Buck, who got progressively more lively and bouncy the more alcohol he consumed. Or like Hen, who once tried to compose a sonnet about Karen’s eyes.

Thankfully, God seemed to be smiling down upon him today. Athena’s cruiser pulled into the station’s parking lot after a few minutes of Bobby awkwardly standing next to the crying Phillip. She climbed out, took in the scene from behind the darkened lenses of her shades, and shook her head.

“I wondered what was going on that you had dispatch send me specifically,” she said as she approached them. “Kinda figured you weren’t just inviting me for a social visit.”

“You know I’d take any excuse to see you,” Bobby told his wife, which had her breaking the stern expression with a flash of a smile. “Phillip here is having a bad time, and I think he needs to go somewhere to sleep it off.”

Athena took off her sunglasses and eyed the man sitting on the sidewalk. Phillip had stopped crying, but his face was a mess.

“How much has he had to drink?” she asked. “Any idea?”

“Too much,” Bobby answered. “He was shouting earlier.”

“I just want to see my children,” Phillip said, pulling at his hair.

For a moment, something like pity flashed across Athena’s face.

“You’re in no state to see either of them right now, Mr. Buckley,” she said, reaching out her hand to help him to his feet. “Come on, I’ll give you a ride to wherever you’re staying.”

Bobby watched as the man shuffled across the pavement to Athena’s cruiser. She held the passenger door open for him, until he pulled his feet inside and he was slumped in his seat. Once that was done, she went back to Bobby, after making sure Phillip wasn’t going anywhere.

“Does he know?”

Bobby didn’t have to ask who Athena meant.

“No.” He gave a small shake of his head. “I didn’t think it was a good idea.”

“Good. I’ll see you at home.”

She leaned in for a kiss, before climbing into the driver seat of her cruiser. Bobby watched her drive away with Phillip, and he did not relax until they were out of sight.

One of the best things about Eddie knowing Buck so well, in Buck’s opinion, was that he knew when to just let Buck go and when he needed to step  in before things got a little too out of hand. Okay, sure, half the time he was helping Buck drive the chaos, but the other half of the time he was the one preventing Buck from going full steam off the edge of the cliff, Looney Tunes style. It wasn’t needed so much now, after he finally got around to getting the diagnosis and got put on the bupropion, but there were still times when his husband felt the need.

Like now, for example, when he interrupted Buck’s rant mid sentence by catching his flailing arm and using it to pull him away from Albert.

“I think that’s enough, mi sol,” he said. “You’ve been arguing about this for over half an hour now.”

“If Al would just admit that his music tastes suck-”

“He won’t.” Eddie forced him to sit down on the couch, next to the chair where Chimney had been gleefully watching everything with a bowl of popcorn. “He’s enjoying winding you up too much.”

Buck started to get up again, but Eddie stopped him. So he contented himself with twisting around in the seat and glaring at the younger man. Albert, in turn, was smiling brightly in his direction.

“C”mon, Eddie,” Chimney complained. “There’s nothing good on TV, and I’m pretty sure we were about to see Buck try and throw my brother down the stairs.”

“I’d prefer not to see that,” Ravi said, even though he had made no effort to try and break it up himself. He’d seemed perfectly content with playing on his phone while the two argued.

“How’s Mara doing?” Eddie asked Hen, sitting next to Buck and tugging at him until he was turned back around. “The other day, you said she threw a mug at Denny.”

“Turns out she had a rough life,” Hen answered, setting down the book she’d been reading while she ignored them all. “She’s protective of her things - Denny moved her blanket - and wants to watch us sleep because she’s afraid we won’t wake up again.”

“Ah, man.” Buck had forgotten about his argument with Albert now. “That sucks.”

“Yeah, but she actually spoke to Karen last night, so we’re making progress,” she said. “Oh! Karen and I haven’t been over in a couple weeks. We can introduce her to Gavin and Chris.”

“Um.” Buck and Eddie shared a look. “Is that… Is that a good idea?”

A stormy expression passed over Hen’s face.

“Is that a good idea?” she repeated. “What does that mean?”

“It- It’s just-” Buck flailed about for the words. “She threw a mug at Denny!”

Hen sat up, looking angry and upset now. Chimney, wide-eyed, drew his legs up into his chair while he munched on his popcorn. 

“I told you,” she said. “Mara’s had a rough life. That does not mean we should just give up on her.”

“I didn’t…”

“You just said she threw the mug because Denny touched her blanket,” Eddie reminded her. “Gavin’s two, Hen. We can tell him not to touch something all we like, but he’s still going to do it. And who knows what other things he might do that could trigger her. If she does something that has us rushing my kid to the ER for stitches, I will not be held accountable for my actions.”

Hen puffed up, clearly ready to argue back, only to deflate with a sigh at the last moment. She dragged her hand down her face, looking miserable.

“No, you’re right,” she said. “Interacting with her has been like walking through a minefield, blind, and Denny’s been so patient with her. But you’re right, Gavin’s two. It wouldn’t be fair to either of them.”

“But hey, we could come over to meet her,” Buck offered. “We could bring Chris. Maybe Maddie and Chimney, too?”

As one, Buck and Eddie turned their heads to look at Chimney. The man’s cheeks were bulging slightly with popcorn, so he just frantically nodded in agreement.

“Yeah,” Hen agreed. “That would be nice, I think.”

“Alright, everyone!” Bobby called out, cresting the top of the stairs. Buck spotted Toby trailing slightly behind him, along with Clarke and Ortiz. “I have an announcement to make. It has been decided that everyone involved in the cruise ship rescue last month will be awarded the Medal of Valor.”

“Oh, nice!” Buck cheered, nudging Eddie with his elbow.

“Why are you so excited?” Albert asked him. “Neither of us went, so it’s not like you’re getting one.”

“Oh, I’m sure I could convince Eds to let me wear his,” Buck answered, leering at his husband.

“Gross,” Chimney said, scrunching up his nose. “Remember when we all thought we were going to be fired for that?”

“I remember having to lie to the chief when he asked where you all were..”

“What did you tell him?” Ravi asked.

“That you were all dying from bad sushi.”

“I guess this’ll be another thing for you to show off to your fans,” Hen teased, which had Eddie making a face at the idea.

The comment did make Buck think to check his department email. If they were informing Bobby about a medal ceremony coming up, then maybe- Ah, yup.

“Hey, Eds.” Buck waved his phone at him. “Playtime’s over, the Mouth of Sauron’s sent us a request.”

His husband let out a groan as the others laughed, Bobby included.

“What is it?” the captain asked.

“They want us to do a PSA about wildfires,” Buck said. It made sense, it was currently the middle of April and the fire season began at around the start of May. “Look, babe, they even gave us a script. Or, well, what they want us to say at least.”

“Great,” Eddie drawled, taking Buck’s phone so he could see for himself. “Because this is what I dreamed about doing when I joined the academy.”

“You’ll live,” Buck told him, pecking a kiss on his cheek before turning to the others. “Alright, who wants to play cameraman?”

Eddie wasn’t entirely certain how he felt about receiving another medal. It took him so very long to actually feel okay with even talking about his Silver Star, much less displaying it in any way. Hell, even now it was kept in its small box, sitting next to Shannon’s photo in the bedroom, so it still wasn’t really on display per se. 

But this medal, at least, would not come packaged with trauma and a sense of failure that lingered no matter how loudly or how often he was praised. Maybe, even, he could wear it and not feel like a liar when people called him a hero. Even if he and the others were almost definitely getting the medals just because it would reflect well on the department and Chief Simpson, both.

Honestly, Eddie figured that was probably the reason they pressured him and Buck into opening that instagram account. They wanted to be able to point at them and go, “Behold! We have a gay, interracial married couple working for us! Look how progressive we are!” Tomas Wade, or the Mouth of Sauron, as Buck called him, just about said as much on the day he and Chief Williams swung by the station.

At the very least, Abuela was nearly as excited for him as Buck was, when they told her about it. She squealed loudly enough that he was sure Hoover tried to cover his ears, and then wrapped him in one of her tightest hugs. Afterwards, she grabbed the house phone and started spreading the news, starting with Pepa.

It was a coin toss whether or not his parents would be told before or after her church friends.

One person who did not seem excited about Eddie getting awarded the Medal of Valor was Christopher. Or, no, he was excited for it, but at the same time Eddie could tell that something was bothering him about it. Luckily, he didn’t have to wait long before his son opened up about it.

“So you’re getting it for rescuing Grandpops and Nana ‘Thena and everyone on their boat?” he asked Eddie, while Buck was down the hall, busy changing Gavin’s diaper.

“Yeah, I am,” Eddie said. The answer, for whatever reason, had his oldest scowling. “Chris? What is it?”

“Why didn’t Pops get one, then?”

“Because he didn’t go with us,” he said. “He was acting captain, so he stayed behind to help people here.”

“No, that’s not-” Chris cut himself off and shook his head in agitation. “I mean after the tsunami, when he saved me and all those other people. Why didn’t he get one for that?”

“I…” Eddie trailed off. He hadn’t thought about that before, actually. “You know what, mijo? You have a very good point.”

“I know,” his bratty teenager of a son said with a huff. “So?”

Eddie gave Chris a dry look, even as he tried to formulate a way to fix this obvious msicarriage of justice. Obviously, he couldn’t just go up to the people in charge and demand that they recognize how wonderful his husband was.

Actually, hold up. They were doing this for PR purposes, which was the exact reason they had Buck and Eddie doing that stupid Instagram account. Maybe he could do that…

“Let’s call your grandpops,” Eddie said, pulling out his phone even as he led Chris to the back patio, where they’d get forewarning if Buck was about to interrupt them. “And we’ll get this sorted out.”

Chapter 18: The Stag and the Hen

Chapter Summary

In which neither Chimney nor Maddie actually get a choice in celebrating.

“Does this look right to you?” Buck asked, holding up the mess of sunshine yellow yarn that was entangled around the needles. 

Hen lowered the book she was reading to squint at it from behind her glasses.

“Uh, that depends,” she said. “What is it supposed to be?”

“A scarf.” Buck frowned at it. “The start of one, at least. I think I messed up somewhere, but I can’t figure out where…”

“Why are you knitting now, anyway?” Hen asked. “How did this start?”

“Abuela’s picked up knitting again,” Eddie explained, coming over to sit down on the couch beside Buck. He carefully moved the bag that the yarn was sitting in onto the floor by their feet. “And he thought it looked fun.”

“She just sits down in her chair, turns on one of her shows, and the next thing I know she has like, half a blanket in her lap,” Buck said, sticking his tongue out as he focused on what he was doing. “And then she yells at Eddie because he keeps using her yarn to play with Senorita.”

“She likes pouncing on it,” Eddie said with an unrepentant shrug. “Don’t let Abuela fool you, I’ve seen her toss an entire ball of yarn just to watch the cat chase after it.”

“Cute,” Hen drawled.

“It is!” Buck nodded, switching the needles between his hands and starting on another row of stitches. He felt clumsy and rather slow, especially compared to how fast Abuela could get going, even with her arthritis hobbling her. “I think the blanket she’s working on right now is for Gavin, maybe? She’s using yarn that’s a similar gray to the blanket he came into the station with.”

“Is Chris gonna get one?”

“He’s currently pretending that he’s too cool for it,” Eddie said. “But probably. She’s been asking him what colors he thinks Gavin might like, so.”

“And I bet they just so happen to coincide with colors Chris likes,” Hen said, laughing. “Clever, clever.”

“She’s on her third generation of moody teenagers now,” he pointed out. “She’s figured out how it works.”

“But all of that depends on if she’ll even- Ah, shit.” Buck glared at the dropped stitch, before bundling the entire thing up and tossing into the bag with all the knitting supplies. He could fix it later, or start over, or just toss the entire thing into the street. He’ll see how he felt about it in the future. “All of that depends on if she’ll even finish the blanket she’s knitting now.”

“Abuela’s got arthritis in her fingers,” Eddie explained to Hen, who nodded slowly in understanding. “She’s on steroids, and they help, but she’s mostly just being stubborn because she wants to show-up a woman from her church.”

“Luisa was bragging too much about the blanket she made for her granddaughter. She needs to be taken down a peg.”

“And I think you spend too much time with Abuela.”

“Oh like you don’t happily sit there with me and listen to all the latest gossip.” Buck rolled his eyes at his husband’s attempt to look offended by the implication, turning in his seat to turn his back on him and face Hen. “So about Chim’s bachelor party-”

“Oh, my God.” Hen put her entire head into the act of rolling her eyes. “He’s told you that he doesn’t want one.”

“He didn’t mean it.”

Beside him, Eddie smacked himself in the face.

“Buck,” Hen said, lips drawn into a tight line. “His exact words were, “no” and “I don’t want a bachelor party.” I’m not sure how clearer he could’ve been.”

“Okay, but he’s getting one anyway,” Buck said, leaning forward as his friend threw up her hands. She nearly sent her book flying with the motion. “No, no, listen. He’s your best friend, don’t you want to take him out and celebrate this momentous, er, moment in his life? After everything he’s been through? All the ups, all the downs? All the times he’s nearly died?”

He gave her his brightest smile. She gave him the flattest look in the world.

“Your emotional manipulation is obvious and blatant,” she told him.

“But is it working?” Eddie asked, very obviously amused.

“Unfortunately.” Hen rolled her eyes when Buck cheered. “It better not be anything crazy.”

“It won’t be!” Buck hurried to assure her. “I’ve got it all figured out. It’ll be at a karaoke bar, just a small group of people. You, Eddie, Ravi, Albert, maybe one other. That’s all. And Chimney, obviously.”

“Ravi and Albert already agreed,” Eddie said. “Kinda easily, actually, so it was happening with or without you.”

“Okay, but hold on.” Hen pointed at Buck. “Are you not going to be there? After all the whining and pushing?”

“Hen,” Buck said, trying to put on as much of an air of gravitas as he could manage. He was failing, but he thought he was putting in a good effort. “I am the Man of Honor. I already did your job organizing the thing, but I’ll be busy that night doing my solemn duty as a-”

“He’s dragging Maddie to a bachelorette party,” Eddie explained, cutting to the chase. He ignored Buck’s pout for interrupting him. “I honestly have no idea what they’re doing with that.”

“Josh rented a place,” Buck said. “But the point is that we’re going to celebrate the both of them separately, as individuals, before we celebrate them as a couple. Because they’re our family and we love them.”

He turned his smile on Hen again. She shoved a hand into his face.

“I already agreed to it,” she grumbled. “You don’t have to keep up with all of… That. But nothing crazy, right?”

“Scout’s honor,” Buck said, raising his hand. “Nothing crazy.”

Hen eyed him.

“Were you ever even in the boy scouts?”

“Semantics.”

With mere days left to go until her wedding, Maddie was feeling the jitters. It was so unlike the days leading up to becoming Mrs. Kendall, when she spent the days smiling prettily and the nights ruthlessly quashing any and all doubts that threatened to bubble up in the silence. She was actually, honestly, excited to be walking down the aisle this time. The dread that had shadowed her every step last time, that she had managed to convince herself were simply nerves, was nonexistent with Chimney.

And the wedding itself was going to be different, too. She was confident about that. Her last one had been all about appearances. She barely got a say about any part of it, because her wedding hadn’t been about her. It had been about Doug, a chance for the man to show off his perfect bride with a perfect church wedding. A perfection marred, of course, by the fact that the only member of the bride’s family that bothered to show was her gangly limbed little brother. But then again, considering their mutual dislike, Doug was probably glad that the Buckley parents skipped out.

But this wedding was going to be different. Buck, however much he had become an unholy terror about things, was dead set on Maddie’s wedding being exactly the way she wanted it. To the point where she had to, sometimes pointedly, ask Chimney what he wanted. Except, of course, he would just turn around and say - truthfully, earnestly - that he was perfectly fine with anything she decided, that they could say their vows on a street corner in Inglewood while wearing clown costumes if that's what she wanted, whatever made her happy.

(But also, please, don’t make him wear a clown costume to his wedding.)

It was sweet. Extremely unhelpful, but sweet.

With the countdown nearly coming to a close, they were running out of things for Maddie to do to keep herself busy. Her vows were written, she’d broken in her wedding shoes, gone in for a final dress fitting, and they’d done a final walkthrough of the venue they were using. Just that morning, Athena took her out for a mani-pedi. There was simply nothing else for her to do but wait until it was time to put on her dress and let Bobby walk her down the aisle.

At the moment, Maddie was playing with Jee-Yun in the living room. Chimney had just gone down the hall, complaining about a headache, which was surely not going to be helped by the sudden pounding at the door. She gave Jee an exaggerated frown, which had her daughter giggling, before getting up to answer it.

And then she had to step to the side as Albert and someone she didn’t recognize stormed in, both of them shouting out,

“Howie!”

“What is this?” Maddie demanded, trying to decide if she should be upset or amused.

“Your brother,” Hen answered.

Said brother smiled cheekily as he walked in with Harry, of all people.

“Don’t forget his shoes,” Buck said, pointing them out. “And his wallet, he’ll probably need that.”

Maddie watched as Eddie and Ravi fetched both items, the shoes from where they were lined up neatly in the corner and the wallet from where it had been haphazardly tossed onto a table with Chimney’s keys. She could hear his complaining, getting louder as he came closer.

“Eli, Al, let me go!” he demanded, as they rounded the corner into the living room. He was being held aloft between the two men. “I told him I didn’t want this stupid party!”

“Too bad, Howie,” the man that Maddie guessed was Eli - Chimney had talked about him before, but she’d never actually seen him - said. “You’re getting one.”

“You are the worst- Hen!?” Chimney’s expression of betrayal at the sight of his friend was almost comical. “Et tu?”

“He made a convincing argument,” Hen told him with an unrepentant shrug.

“Maddie, help me!”

Maddie, of course, did not do that. What she did instead was crouch down next to Jee-Yun and join her in waving goodbye until he was carried completely out of sight.

“I’ll make sure he comes back in one piece,” Eddie told her, giving Buck a quick kiss before he, along with Ravi and Hen, followed after the other three.

Buck, however, remained behind, as did Harry, which Maddie found to be very suspect.

“Aren’t you going with them?” she asked, squinting at him.

“Not with them,” her brother said, and no, she did not like that smile at all when it was directed at her. “You should get your shoes on.”

“Buck, no!”

“I’m pretty sure he might just carry you out,” Harry said. He knelt down in front of Jee. “Hi!”

“Hello,” Jee-Yun chirped back with a wave of her tiny hand.

“C’mon,” Buck urged, tapping at his watch. “Everyone’s waiting.”

“I hate you,” Maddie lied, even as she went to grab her things. “I liked this better when it was happening to someone else.”

“C’mon, it’ll be fun,” he told her. “A night with your friends, no kids, mixed drinks…”

“Why are you even trying to convince me?” she asked him. “I’m not even getting a choice.”

“Because it’s polite.”

Maddie rolled her eyes.

This was not actually as bad as Eddie feared it might’ve been. They were tucked away in the private room that Buck had rented, in the back of a karaoke bar. There were drinks, there was bad singing, it was… Not that different from how things went when they all went out together, whenever they found the time. 

At the moment, he was sitting in a booth watching as Albert and Chimney argued over song selections. Up on the tiny stage, Hen had managed to cajole Ravi into doing a duet of what Eddie was almost certain was a Rick Astley song. He wasn’t sure if it was better or worse than what Buck was currently going through - apparently Linda was making them watch the Notebook - but he was absolutely certain he could adequately describe suffering through this as a labor of love.

“Yeah, honey.” Eli took a seat next to him, phone to his ear and smiling softly. “Okay, enjoy your spa… Love you, too.”

“Your wife?” Eddie figured he wasn’t in a position to just assume something like that, all things considered.

“Molly,” Eli answered, tucking his phone away. “She’s back at the hotel, taking advantage of the girls being hundreds of miles away and me not being there to bother her.”

“Buck’s apparently stuck watching a Nicholas Sparks marathon.”

The other man let out a bark of laughter. Then he glanced at the others, at Ravi and Hen, singing on the stage, and at Chimney and Albert, still arguing over Journey versus Duran Duran, and laughed even harder.

“What?” Eddie asked, curious.

“Just imagining Gerrard’s face,” Eli said, chuckling still. “I bet he had a conniption when Hen joined.”

“Yeah.” Eddie grimaced, remembering what Hen and Chimney both shared from that time. “I heard it wasn’t pretty.”

“If he walked into the One Eighteen now and saw how it was, he’d probably have a heart attack.”

“I’ll make sure to reach out and invite him to visit for Hen’s birthday, then,” Eddie said, which got more laughter.

“Who all’s even left there from my time? Just Howie?”

“Just Chimney, yeah.”

“Probably- No, definitely for the best,” Eli said. “I remember how Sal was. He probably would have made some comment about you or your boy, and then someone would’ve gotten punched.”

“No wonder Tommy waited until he was at Harbor.”

Eli blinked and then stared at him.

“Wait, Tommy?” he asked, sounding incredulous. When Eddie nodded, he let out a shocked little, ”Huh.”

“Eli!” Chimney called, waving for the man to join him. “Come on, you’re singing with us.”

“Oh, God, why.” 

Eli heaved out a sigh, downed the drink he brought with him, and dutifully went over. The spot was not vacant for long, however. Ravi and Hen were soon filling the space. Ravi pressed a drink into Eddie’s hand.

“No, trust me,” Ravi said, when Eddie tried to turn it down. “I listen to Al sing along to the radio every morning on the way to the station. You need this.”

“There is no way he’s that bad,” Eddie said. Ravi just let out a snort and shook his head, draining his own drink quickly.

It turned out that, yes, Albert really was that bad. And unfortunately, while everyone else around him could dull the pain, Eddie was stuck being the sober one. Buck was very lucky that Eddie loved him so much.

“You are so damn lucky I love you.”

Buck laughed, adjusting the phone where it was pressed between his ear and his shoulder.

“I know, babe.” He ran his fingers along the various supplies spread out on the counter in front of him. Ever since they arrived, he’d been pretty much stuck in this spot as he fixed everyone’s requested drinks. “I tell myself that every day.”

“Al sounds like a dying cat,” Eddie complained. “We all thought Ravi was being cute with the cat nicknames, but no. It turns out he’s been warning us this entire time.”

“Truly, your suffering knows no bounds.”

“Buck!” May called from where the others were all grouped around the couch. They’d moved on to another Nicholas Sparks movie, this one involving a bull rider. It was technically the third one of the night; Safe Haven got nixed about five minutes into the first scene, once Josh and Linda remembered what it was about. “Slow bartenders don’t get tips!”

“He’s a mixologist, May,” Maddie corrected her, a mocking lilt to the words that had the others laughing.

“I’m not being paid for this at all,” Buck grumbled, only just loud enough for Eddie to hear. He grabbed a bottle, flipped it just to show off, and started making the next round of drinks. “Listen, if you have to, just turn off Albert’s mic. It works on Gavin.”

They had a non-working controller just for him, now, that he could pound and press without a care. Gavin got to think he was playing video games, too, and Chris got to actually play without being bothered. It was a win-win for everyone involved.

“Al’s not a toddler, mi sol.”

“Eh.” Buck placed a finished drink on the edge of the counter and moved to the next one. “Get him drunk enough and you wouldn’t know the difference.”

“Ay, Dios.” Eddie’s voice was filled with dread. Buck was about to ask what was wrong, when he followed it with, “They’re trying to get me to sing, now.”

“Hey, you can’t sound any worse than Al.”

“Or you.”

The call dropped before Buck could respond to that, leaving him to glare impotently at his phone. He rolled his eyes, tucked it back into his pocket, and finished the requested drinks. A shrill whistle had Josh and May hurrying over, the latter taking hers, along with Linda and Maddie’s, back to where they were sitting with a quick,

“Thanks!”

“This is actually really good,” Josh told him, sipping at his fruity cocktail. “I wish I remembered to get whipped cream.”

“Why?” Buck grabbed a towel and wiped up where he spilled some grenadine. “So that you could tell everyone I gave you a Blow Job?”

Amaretto and Irish cream, topped with whipped cream. Blow Jobs weren’t very heavy on the alcohol, easy to make, and stupidly popular with anyone who giggled at the name.

“Maybe.” Josh shrugged. “But I’ll make do with forcing you to make me another Sex on the Beach when I’m done with… What’s this one?”

“Purple Rain.”

 “It’s good.”

There was knocking at the door, firm and insistent. Buck frowned at Josh and went to answer it. The frown got even heavier when he saw it was a cop that was knocking. Though, something about his uniform didn’t look quite right.

“Uh. Sir?”

“There was a noise complaint,” the man said.

“A noise…?”

“Oh!” Josh hurried over, pushing Buck out of the way slightly. “Come in, come in.”

Buck watched, very confused, as Josh led the officer inside. Though that confusion turned to horror when Josh pulled out his phone and started playing music. He closed the door, unable to look away, even as Maddie came over to stand beside him.

“I swear,” Buck told her. “I did not hire a stripper.”

“I know.” His sister let out a sigh. “This was Josh.”

“C’mon, I’ll make you a Zombie.”

“I don’t know what that is, but I’ll take two.”

It was very late when Buck pulled up in front of Maddie and Chimney’s house. Eddie was sitting on the porch, but he got up and came over to help get Maddie out of the Jeep.

“Eli and I dumped Chimney in the bed,” Eddie explained. “And then he went back to his hotel.”

“Oh, so you had a sober buddy tonight.” Buck tucked Maddie into his side, snorting at her half-hearted mumbling. “Lucky you. I left Josh and Linda back at the place, since Josh has it until tomorrow.”

May was in the back, slumped over against the car seat and half-covered by Buck’s jacket. Eddie could see a plastic bag dangling from her hand.

Between the two of them, it was easy to get the bride-to-be into the house and to her bedroom. Eddie watched as Buck gently laid her out next to Chimney, the pair of them immediately rolling closer to each other, and then busy himself filling two glasses of water and shaking out tylenol to be placed next to each one. When that was done, he went back to the living room, where Harry was sleeping on the couch, while Buck got together stuff for Jee-Yun.

The teenager woke with a jerk when Eddie shook his shoulder, raising his head and blinking blarily at him.

“Buck’s getting Jee-Yun to take her home with us,” Eddie told him. “We’ll drop you and May off at your mom’s.”

“Yeah-” Harry interrupted himself with a yawn. “Yeah, okay. How drunk is May?”

“Very,” Buck answered, coming in with a sleeping Jee-Yun huddled against his shoulder, diaper bag dangling from his other. “She tried the Zombie I made for Maddie and demanded one of her own.”

“What’s a Zombie?”

“Uh. A lot of rum with some pineapple and lime juice, mainly.”

“Could you-”

“No,” Buck and Eddie said together.

Harry huffed, but he let it go as he grabbed his shoes, pulling them on without bothering with the laces. They made their exit as quietly as they could, Eddie using Buck’s spare key to lock the door behind them. Jee woke up briefly as they got her buckled into the jeep’s car seat, but settled easily. Apparently, according to the teen, she spent their time together running herself - and him - to exhaustion. It showed.

“We should probably warn Athena and Bobby that we’re coming,” Eddie pointed out as the jeep rumbled to life and Buck pulled away.

“Yeah, you’re right.”

Buck dug out his bluetooth and stuck it on, before pulling up “Police Mom ICE” in his contacts on his phone and hitting call.

“Hey, Ma-... Yeah, I know what time it is.” He glanced at the clock on the dash and grimaced. “I just figured I should-... Yeah, yeah, I’m bringing them back now… Oh, May is gonna suffer tomorrow…”

Chapter 19: Runaway Groom

Chapter Summary

In which a Very Important Event is missing a Very Important Person.

Buck had spent the last few months being an - and he could freely admit this about himself - annoying little shit in regards to helping plan his sister’s wedding, being pedantic about guest lists and seating charts and every minute detail he could think about, trying to account for anything and everything that might go wrong. That was why there were empty chairs in the back row, and an entire empty table where they were having the wedding reception, dedicated to guests that hadn’t said they were coming but showed up anyway. That was why he’d asked for someone to bring a sewing kit, for any wardrobe malfunctions, and why there was a designated room off to the side for the tiny tots to have their inevitable little fits. That was why there were three - three, he made sure - working fire extinguishers on hand.

But what he failed to account for was, apparently, the bride and the groom themselves. Strangely enough, Buck figured those two were the least likely to cause problems on their big day. Silly him.

“He is half an hour late.” Maddie was pacing, while Athena dutifully held the train of her dress to keep it from dragging along the floor and tangling around her feet as she did. “Why is he half an hour late to our wedding?”

“Let’s not jump to any conclusions,” Bobby urged. “He might’ve just overslept. Maybe he set his alarm for PM instead of AM?”

“Al and Ravi went over to the hotel,” Buck said, trying to reassure his sister. For some reason, she insisted on following tradition and decided they couldn’t see each other the day before the ceremony, so Chimney spent the night at a hotel. “If he did oversleep, they’ll-”

He cut himself off as his phone began to ring. The screen, when he pulled it out, read “Chainsaw.”

“Ravi, thank God,” he said when he answered. “Please-”

“We have a problem.”

“I know we have a problem,” Buck hissed back, turning away from Maddie and lowering his voice. “Wake his ass up and get him to his wedding.”

“I would love to, but he’s not here.”

It took a moment for the words to register in Buck’s head, and then he was turning back around to face the others. He lowered his phone from his ear and switched it to speaker, so they could all hear it.

“What do you mean he’s not there?” he demanded.

“I mean that he’s not here,” Ravi repeated himself. “He wasn’t answering his phone, and he wasn’t opening the door, so we got one of the hotel people to let us in. His tux is hanging up, and the bed’s not been slept in. He is not here.”

Maddie just about dove for her phone, which had been thrown on an ottoman before she started her pacing. Athena moved with her, so as not to tear the train of fabric that she was still holding onto for her.

“Are you calling him?” Buck knew it was a stupid question, but he still blurted it out.

“Yes,” his sister snapped at him, holding the phone to her ear. “But he isn’t picking up.”

Buck understood the logic that led to her trying, even after Ravi just said the man wasn’t answering. Because just as surely as he assumed he would always be the exception with Eddie, if he didn’t pick up the phone for anyone else, Maddie doubtlessly thought the same with Chimney.

“Do you have him on Find My Friend?” Athena asked, and then explained that, “I forced it onto Harry, it’s the only reason I don’t have him under house arrest.”

“Yes!” Maddie quickly pulled up the app and while Buck couldn’t see the screen from where he was, the way her face scrunched up in confusion did not fill him with comfort. “He’s on Century Park East. Why is he on Century Park East? Why isn’t he answering me? Why-”

“Okay, okay.” Bobby laid gentle hands on her shoulders. “Breathe, Maddie.”

She took in a shuddering breath, and then another, with Bobby nodding with each one.

“Good, that’s better. Ravi?”

“Yeah, Cap?”

“You and Al come back here. Buck, go get the others.”

“Got it, Pops.”

It did not take much effort for Buck to round up everyone else. Soon, they were all back in the room Maddie had been using to get ready, prior to everything going off the rails. Eddie and the Wilsons, the Lees, Josh, even Eli, whose wife - Molly - opted to help keep Jee-Yun and Gavin out of the way, excited at the prospect of fussing over kids that she could give back at the end of the day. Athena had called in a favor while Buck was gathering everyone else and had an officer go to where Find My Friend claimed Chimney’s phone to be. They had nothing to do except sit around in anxious silence as they waited for her phone to ring, which it did just as Albert and Ravi finally arrived.

“What’d you find?” was how she answered, her sergeant voice out in full.

“The phone was right where you said it would be.” Buck recognized Officer Williams’ voice, even as it sounded slightly tinny. “But Mr. Han’s not with it.”

“What about his car?”

“No sign of that either.”

There was a collective sigh of despair from everyone gathered. Buck saw Anne Lee, worry clear as day on her face, turn to her husband, who would have been the epitome of stoicism if not for the way his fingers were clutching at the fabric of his suit pants.

“Alright,” Athena said. “Put out a BOLO for both him and his car.”

“Officer Williams,” Maddie spoke up. Athena held her phone out closer to her, so the officer could hear her better. “Are there traffic cameras visible where you are?”

There was a moment of silence, presumably where the officer looked around, before,

“A few actually.”

And Buck watched as a familiar glint of determination flared into life in his sister’s eyes.

Eddie wasn’t certain how he ended up as a member of the party that went to Metro Dispatch. Buck stayed behind to deal with everything there, including all the guests that were probably starting to wonder what happened to the wedding they showed up for, while Athena handled the coordination efforts. But somehow, he ended up in a limo with Maddie and Josh and Hen and Eli, and off to Dispatch they went. They probably made a very comical scene, the whole group of them marching into the building, dressed for a wedding. Josh was trailing along behind Maddie, helpfully clutching at her dress’s train and keeping it from being caught in any doors or in the elevator.

“I’ll check the Century Park East cameras,” Maddie announced as she stormed off the elevator. Everyone on the floor turned to look as they followed after her. “See if I can find him.”

“I’ll check the call logs to see if there's been any calls from that vicinity,” Josh said, doing his best to keep up with Maddie’s pace.

“Runaway bride?” a bewildered Sue asked as the entire group passed her.

“Runaway groom,” Hen answered.

“Howie’s done a runner,” Eli explained.

Maddie and Josh claimed a pair of workstations opposite to each other, which made it easy for the group to gather behind them both and hover anxiously. Sure, they were in the way if anyone needed to get by, but none of them particularly cared about that at the moment. Josh’s search through the call logs yielded no results, but Maddie was able to pull up some footage from one of the traffic cameras in the area and had it on fast forward, cars zipping down the road.

“This is where his phone was?” Sue asked, leaning over Maddie’s shoulder just as the rest of them were doing.

“Yeah,” Maddie confirmed, eyes fixed on the computer screen. “Officer Williams found it in the middle of the road. But he didn’t find Chimney or the car…”

They found Chimney’s car right away, just sitting on the side of the road. Eddie didn’t know how long it was there, Maddie only accessed footage from about an hour before the wedding, but he was willing to guess that it was at least overnight, since Ravi said it looked as if Chimney didn’t spend the night at the hotel. And all the while, cars just kept driving by. Because Los Angelinos were not the most neighborly of folk.

There was one person, some random guy, who finally went up and knocked on the window. They could see them talking, and then Chimney was getting out and letting the stranger into the driver seat. Just as Chimney stumbled around to, maybe, get into the passenger seat, the guy drove off in the car. He made a very illegal u-turn, tossed something out the window - Chimney’s phone - and sped off.

“What an asshole,” Eli stated.

“What is he doing?” Josh asked.

To Eddie, it looked as if Chimney was simply standing there, swaying in place a little, as he stared at where his phone lay in the road. And he kept doing it for some time, until-

“Oh my God,” Sue gasped as Chimney stepped off the curb and walked into traffic.

Despite already knowing that Chimney wasn’t going to be hit, since Officer Williams didn’t find anything and there’d undoubtedly be call logs for a traffic accident, Eddie still found himself holding his breath with the others as a bus came barreling down on the man on the screen. There was a collective sigh of relief when the bus came to a stop just short of him and then, for some reason…

“He’s getting on that bus?” Hen asked, voicing everyone’s confusion.

As they watched, Chimney did just that. He left his phone where it lay and instead climbed into the bus after the driver opened the doors. And then the bus drove off the screen, running over top the phone in the process.

“That was the metro express,” Josh said. “Did anyone see the bus ID number?”

Maddie wordlessly grabbed a pad and a pen, scrawling the bus’s number onto it and holding it out to Sue. Her supervisor took it and left to contact the Metropolitan Transportation Authority, in the hopes of finding out where the bus went and if anyone on it happened to keep track of Chimney. Eddie was more than a little concerned by how the driver nearly hit someone that was standing in the middle of the road and then, for whatever reason, just let that person onto the bus before driving away.

Eddie kinda thought that was the sort of thing to get called in. Then again, he didn’t use public transportation, so what did he know? Maybe this was perfectly normal in context.

“Is it just me,” Eli said, leaning in to whisper to him so the others couldn’t hear, “or did he seem really off on the video?”

“Besides the way he just walked into traffic?” Eddie shrugged. “He was swaying in place. And he let a total stranger drive his car.”

“He was fine the other night at the bachelor party.” Eli frowned, uncertain. “Wasn’t he?”

“I thought so, but he was pretty drunk by the time we got him home, remember?”

Thanks to Sue, they could access footage from the security camera that was on the bus. They were able to pull it up on Maddie’s workstation, once more forwarding through everything until Chimney got on. They all watched as he stumbled through the aisle to collapse into one of the seats, where he sat alone for the rest of the journey.

Or, at least, he wasn’t with anyone that the camera could see.

“Is his mouth moving?” Maddie asked.

“He looks like he’s having a conversation with someone,” Hen said.

“Except there’s nobody there. He’s just talking to himself.”

“Maybe he’s hallucinating.”

“Could this be some kind of delayed reaction to his previous brain injury?”

“Previous brain injury?” Eli asked.

“He uh, had a piece of rebar go through his head,” Eddie told him, tapping the spot on his own forehead where there scar had been on Chimney’s when he first met the man. “A few months before I joined them.”

“Jesus, Howie…”

“I suppose it’s possible,” Hen said with a shrug, half-turned to face them. “But I don’t think so. Not after this long, or at least if it happened, it wouldn’t be like this.”

“Maybe he’s having a breakdown,” was Eddie’s suggestion. Honestly, it was probably an inevitability for one of them to finally crack like a glass cuckoo bird.

“Could be. I mean, when Chimney gets stressed, he deflects,” Hen said. “Makes him a little hard to read sometimes.”

“Marrying me was stressing him out,” Maddie said, sounding like she was about to cry, and Eddie really only saw one appropriate response to that.

He reached over, took a lock of her hair, and gave it a sharp yank. Just as he would’ve done to Adriana or Sophia, if they said something just as dumb.

“Ow!” She reached up to clutch at her head, turning in her seat to glare at him. “Eddie, what the hell!?”

“You were saying stupid things.” Eddie shrugged and slipped off the jacket of his suit to put over her shoulders.
