The dispatch floor was being blasted with AC, and her wedding dress did not look very warm. “Seriously, you think marrying you stressed him out? Maybe there’s something to Buck’s idea that the younger siblings get all the brains.”

The words had her glowering at him, all puffed up with indignant offense even as she tugged the jacket tighter around herself, but at least she didn’t look like she was about to start crying. Mission accomplished.

“Listen.” Hen laid her hand on Maddie’s arm, catching her attention. “Marrying you is the one thing he didn’t stress about. The only thing I’m sure about right now is that he couldn’t wait to do it.”

They were rejoined by Sue and Josh, and all four of them straightened up to hear what the pair had to say.

“We talked to the bus driver,” Josh informed them. “She said he seemed a little out of it, but otherwise normal.”

“Did he say anything to her?” Maddie asked, attention once again focused on a freeze frame of Chimney talking to thin air.

“That was the weird thing,” Sue said. “According to her, he kept talking about being late for work.”

“Work?” Hen brow pinched in confusion. “This is a crosstown bus, it wouldn’t go anywhere near the firehouse.”

“So then, what stop did he get off at?” Eli asked.

The answer to that was, apparently, near some bakery. They found themselves in the employee break room, gathered around Maddie’s phone. She’d sent Athena the location, and she and Bobby went to go check it out, where they found a lead in the form of someone who’d seen and spoken with Chimney.

“He said he did seem agitated and he looked feverish. The witness also said that Chimney thought that this bakery was a karaoke bar,” the sergeant informed them. Eddie saw Eli’s head shoot up and he became laser-focused on the phone laying on the table, furrowing his brow. “He was just here not that long ago. I have some officers searching the area, Bobby and I will stick around in case he comes back or we hear something new.”

“Thanks, Athena.”

“We’re gonna find him.”

“I know,” Maddie said, before reaching off and ending the call.

“Eddie, you guys took him to a karaoke bar for his bachelor party, right?” Josh asked him. “Maybe that’s where he’s been trying to get back to?”

“No.” Eli shook his head. “He worked at a karaoke bar before he was a firefighter. Him and his brother, Kevin.”

“Not just any karaoke bar,” Hen said, holding out her own phone. There was a news article on the screen, which included a picture of a much younger Chimney. “The karaoke bar that used to be at that bakery. It burned down nearly twenty years ago.”

“He said he was late for work, and he went there,” Eddie said, tapping his fingers on the tabletop. “It is possible he’s just… Forgotten about two decades of his life?”

“And he might not be the only one.”

It was extremely difficult, Buck found, to juggle a catering dish, two bags of clothing, and two empty garment bags, all at the same time. He managed it, though, somehow sliding his department ID at the front desk and riding the elevator up to the dispatch floor without spilling anything. When he arrived, he spotted Hen pacing in the conference room with her phone pressed to her ear while Maddie, Eddie, and Eli watched from the break room. An awkward attempt at knocking managed to get their attention, and Eddie hurried to open the door for him.

“Thanks, babe.” Buck gave him a quick kiss, before holding out his burdens. “Change of clothes for you and Maddie, and food from the catering.”

“Thanks, Buck.” Maddie, with Eddie’s suit jacket draped over her shoulders, gave him a short hug before taking the catering dish from him and sitting it down on the table. The bags, the empty garment ones and the ones filled with clothing, joined it.

“Everything’s taken care of, you don’t have to worry about a thing,” Buck assured her. “Aren’t you glad I pressed you about the wedding insurance, now?”

“You get one pass about your paranoia.”

With their luck, Buck knew that getting wedding insurance was a no-brainer. Of course, at the time, he’d been thinking about the disasters that seemed to plague their lives at work. He had not been expecting for the groom to just not show up. Really, that was at the exact bottom of his list of possibilities, even below the improbable event of his parents somehow learning about the wedding and deciding to care enough to crash it.

“Uh, Abuela and Molly are both still keeping an eye on the kids, they’re at ours,” Buck said, partially directing his statement to Eli. He liked the man’s wife, she was rather bubbly. “She seemed kinda taken with Hoover. Sorry in advance if she starts wanting a dog.”

Eli gave him a flat glare and then let out a sigh.

“As long as she doesn’t start talking about having another kid.”

“Who is she talking to?” Buck asked, gesturing at Hen on the other side of the glass.

“She’s checking on a patient,” Maddie answered, and then hurriedly followed it with, “Not Chimney.”

“Remember the guy at the hotel a couple weeks ago?” Eddie asked him. “The voyeur husband, stuck in the ventilation ducts?”

“Yeah. Chimney volunteered to climb in to assess him.” Buck frowned. “He’s still in the hospital? He wasn’t even hurt.”

On the other side of the glass, Hen finished up the phone call, let out a sigh, and pushed the door open to join them.

“Viral encephalitis,” she said. “The guy had been traveling in Central America, they think he brought it back with him.”

“And Chimney was in close quarters with him.” Buck was suddenly very thankful his shoulders made him too big for those kinds of rescues. “So this is what we think it is? That Chimney has encephalitis?”

“All of us should get checked,” Eddie said, and then pointed at Maddie. “Especially you.”

“Chimney was the only one of us that spent time nose-to-nose with a carrier,” Hen pointed out. “But yeah, we should.”

“What’s the prognosis?” Eli asked, crossing his arms.

“If he were in a hospital, it’d be good,” she said. “But at the moment, with him on the lam and acting as he has been? Every minute that passes, the swelling in his brain is gonna get worse, increasing the odds that he suffers from…” Hen grimaced. “Permanent brain damage.”

“The patient, the guy you all rescued,” Maddie said, the words coming out tentative and hesitant. “What’s his status?”

Buck knew the answer before Hen even said it, just from the way her face fell.

“He died this morning.”

Chapter 20: I Choose You (And You Choose Me)

Chapter Summary

In which a missing person is located.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

They received a lead on their runaway groom not long after Hen’s grim announcement. A car drove into a street market, and someone matching Chimney’s description was seen at the scene. Buck and Eddie, along with Eli, drove over to meet up with Bobby and Athena and Albert. First responders were already there and in the middle of working the scene when their group arrived, and Buck spotted Bobby talking to the captain of the 146.

They were stuck waiting around with Athena and Albert until he was done, giving the other captain his thanks and strolling over, narrowly missing being hit by a gurney as it was pushed past by paramedics.

“He was definitely here,” Bobby told them.

“But he didn’t cause this, though,” Buck said, stuffing his hands into the pocket of his hoodie. “Did he?”

According to all accounts, Chimney was acting erratically. Eddie said he just walked into traffic and then was seen talking to himself on the bus, while Athena said that the guy she and Bobby talked to at the bakery claimed he got spooked by something and literally fled the area. If Chimney had done something like that here, if he ran into the road in a blind panic, that very well could have caused the mess here.

“No.” Bobby shook his head at that, so sure that even without details Buck knew that wasn’t the case. “But the captain did confirm that, according to witnesses, a man fitting his description triaged the area before first responders got here.”

“So maybe he remembers who he is,” Eddie suggested, sounding hopeful.

“He’s still claiming to work at a karaoke bar, so I don’t think so.”

“Well, he may not remember who he is,” Athena said. “But he’s still who he is, that hasn’t changed.”

Buck honestly wasn’t surprised. At a certain point, this kind of thing just became automatic. Muscle memory, almost. He did the same thing after the tsunami, when he was basically existing on autopilot, and that was with only a couple years of doing it as a basic EMT under his belt, as opposed to Chimney’s couple of decades as an actual paramedic.

“It’s been less than half an hour,” Bobby said. “He can’t have gotten too far.”

“That’s if he didn’t run out in front of another bus and got picked up,” Eli pointed out.

“For now, let’s assume he didn’t. Fan out and start looking.”

“Hey, uh, actually.” Albert was looking around them with a frown. More specifically, Buck saw him staring intently at the street signs on the street corners. “I don’t think we’re that far from Anne and John’s place.”

“Do you think he would have gone there?” Athena asked, gaze moving between Albert and Eli. “Either of you?”

“I never met this version of him, not really,” Albert said with a shrug, and then turned to Eli, who also shrugged.

“They’re Kevin's parents, aren’t they?” he said. “So probably.”

“Okay, then you-” Athena pointed at Albert. “-go to the Lees’, in case he shows up there. I’ll have PD focus on this area.”

“Alright, the rest of us, like I said, we’ll fan out and start looking,” Bobby instructed. “We’ll find him.”

Buck and Eddie bumped wrists before heading in opposite directions, while Bobby and Eli went off on their own and Albert headed back to his car. Athena was left behind, already calling whoever she needed to in order to have the police start canvassing the area.

Buck was, vaguely, reminded of the first time he met Abby in person. Abby’s mother, suffering from dementia, had wandered off on her own and Abby was desperate to find her. Buck remembered standing around a table with her and Carla, looking over printed out maps of the city and trying to guess how far an old woman could walk. The answer turned out to be pretty fucking far, with Abby’s mother, Patricia, being found all the way in Southern Los Angeles.

There was the niggling fear in the back of Buck’s mind that, just like back then, they were underestimating how far Chimney could walk - or run away - in the time since he was last seen.

“Chimney!” Buck shouted as he wandered down the sidewalk, ignoring the looks that the strangers around him were giving him. “Chim! Chimney!”

Wait, shit. Chimney was stuck back to before he became a firefighter, before he became Chimney. He probably wouldn’t answer to that, would he?

“Uh, Howie!?” Buck started shouting instead. “Howard!”

Not that he honestly thought this was going to work. Chimney didn’t seem to be in his right mind, with his brain literally in the process of boiling into soup. Who was to say that he would hear any of them and realize it was him they were shouting for? Who was to say that, if he did make the connection, that he would actually respond and not just start running again? Something seemed to keep spooking him, and none of them had any ideas what that something could be.

But there weren’t very many other options in front of him. So Buck kept shouting for Chimney, and he kept hoping that the man would show.

The moment Albert stepped inside, Anne and John were leaping to their feet off the couch and rushing for him.

“We haven’t found him yet,” he said, cutting them off and giving the answer to the question they were about to ask. “Everyone’s still looking. But he was last seen not too far from here, so I just thought…”

“We haven’t seen him either,” John said, though Albert guessed that easily enough. “Do they know anything?”

“Uh… Yeah.”

Anne looked at his face and then, gently, grabbed him by the sleeve to lead him to the kitchen. She made him sit down before filling a glass with water and setting it down in front of him, for which he was very thankful.

“We think he got something called viral encephalitis from a patient a couple weeks ago,” he told them, once they were both sitting down across from him. “It causes the brain to swell and-”

“He complained about a headache.” Anne was pressing her fingers to her mouth, looking stricken. “When he was over for the jesa for Kevin. He blamed it on Buck.”

“Of course he did,” Albert said, laughing a little.

“He was clumsier than usual, too,” John added. “He knocked over a glass at dinner, said he was feeling jittery. We-”

“We all missed it.” Albert knew that was what was going through both of their minds right now, if they were thinking back like this. “But um, it seems like he’s suffering from memory loss. According to people he’s talked to today, he’s been claiming to work at a karaoke bar.”

“He and Kevin worked at one,” Anne said. “But it burned down, that’s what led them both to becoming firefighters. He doesn’t remember that?”

Albert could only shrug at the question. Everything he knew was second- or third-hand.

“I don’t know which virus, exactly, the guy might’ve given him. Encephalitis is just the term for brain swelling,” he told them. “After we find Howie, Buck’ll probably end up being able to list them all off for us. But we’ve all been around him since then, so we should all get checked.”

The two of them absently nodded in agreement. They knew, after everything, how quickly and how easily a virus could spread before anyone even realized it was happening. It was better to just assume they’d all been exposed and were, each of them, a ticking time bomb waiting to go off.

“You said everyone’s still looking,” Anne said. “That he was seen not too far from here. What was he doing?”

“Helping people,” Albert answered, and saw John smile proudly at that. “Uh, a car drove into some booths…”

He told them about how Howie triaged people at the scene, before any other first responders arrived. Then he told them about the trail that the others had followed after Officer Williams found Howie’s phone, tiptoeing around just how erratic the others said he was acting in the footage of him they saw. They didn’t have much else to do while waiting for an update from one of the others, so after that topic of conversation dried up, Albert told them about the bachelor party and some of the calls from work.

Just as he was telling them about the date he took Ravi on the other day, after Anne asked about it, there was the sound of breaking glass and someone stumbling through the front door. The three of them jumped to their feet and rushed into the other room. They found Howie standing, swaying, into the living room, staring at the picture of Kevin Lee on the mantle and with his back to them. The motion made him stumble a little. 

“Howard!” Anne gasped out, which had Howie turning around. “Oh my God, are you okay?”

Albert had not seen the footage from the traffic cameras and the bus, he’d only been given a description from those who had. Words did not do it justice, though. His brother was sweaty and feverish looking, with his clothes disheveled and his hair in disarray. He was also covered in blood, with it splattered all across his front and covering his hands. Albert desperately hoped that it was from the people he helped triage at the accident, and not because he had gotten hurt in the periods of time that no one could account for him.

“Everybody’s been looking for you,” John said. “Where have you been?”

“Where’s Kevin?” Howie asked.

There was a heavy pause, and then Anne hesitantly stepped forward to tell him that,

“Kevin’s dead.”

Howie stared at them uncomprehendingly. Then he shook his head, swaying even more until he listed to the side, collapsing. Albert rushed for him, managing to catch him just before he hit the ground.

“John, call Nine-One-One. Anne, call Maddie.” Trusting them to do as he asked, Albert focused on his brother. His skin was burning to the touch, radiating enough heat to cook an egg as Albert checked him over for injuries. “Are you hurt anywhere? Is any of this your blood?”

Howie blinked up at him, seemingly not quite able to focus.

“Who are you?” he asked.

“Albert, your- Your other brother.”

“You’re not Albert,” Howie denied, slurring a little as his eyes fluttered closed. “Albert’s little…”

And then, because Albert’s brother was as dramatic as the rest of them, he passed out in his arms like a bastard.

They spent too much of their lives keeping vigil beside hospital beds, Maddie thought. Chimney hadn’t even made it a full calendar year between brushes with death, the imaginary “number of days since…” sign getting reset just a few scant weeks before the anniversary of the bridge collapse. This time it wasn’t even a collapsing bridge or a spear of rebar through the head or a knife to the gut. No, this time it was because a microscopic organism, one that people argued technically wasn’t even alive, decided to remind them all how things they couldn’t see were capable of rending their world asunder.

Because that was a reminder they oh-so-desperately needed.

After Chimney ended up at the Lees’, after Anne called her, there was a mass rush to the hospital he was being taken to. The others were all, even now, floating around the halls somewhere. Probably stuffing themselves full of Isabel’s food, as the woman showed up not only with Pepa and Molly and the kids, but also with stacks of tupperware.

Others had simply showed up with bouquets of flowers and Get Well Soon cards, which were now decorating the hospital room they’d moved Chimney to. The man himself was unconscious on the bed, as he had been since- No, he was waking up.

Maddie got to her feet just as he opened his eyes, and then closed them again with a wince. It was too bright in here, she should’ve thought of that. She went to the light switch and flicked off everything but some of the wall sconces.

“Hi,” she said as she hesitantly approached the bed. His mouth moved, but nothing came out except a hoarse grunt. “Don't feel like you have to talk. But do you know where you are?”

Chimney’s eyes darted around, and then he gave a little shake of his head.

“Do you know who…” I am? she almost asked, but she knew she wasn’t strong enough to hear the possible answer to that question. Albert had told them, quietly, of how Chimney hadn’t recognized him. “Do you know who you are?”

Chimney cleared his throat, looking exhausted by even that small effort, and said,

“Sorry…”

Maddie felt her heart drop.

“No, no, it’s okay,” she reassured him. “It’s fine-”

“I’m sorry,” he said again, staring right at her. “I missed our wedding.”

She let out a shuddering breath, relief filling her lungs in its place, and sat on the edge of the bed. His skin, when she gripped at his hand, was not nearly as feverish as when he was brought in.

“You remember?”

“Yeah,” Chimney said. “Kind of wish you didn’t, you must be pissed.”

“I’m too relieved to be anything else,” Maddie told him. “Though maybe Bobby and Athena had the right idea.”

There was the pitter-patter of little feet, and then Jee-Yun was suddenly sprinting into the room with a cry of “Daddy!” Maddie managed to catch her, sweeping her up so she was sitting in her lap. Buck was at the door a moment later, clutching at the frame so he didn’t trip over his own feet as he came to a stop.

“Sorry, she slipped away. I think she had Gavin distract us,” he said, and then, “Chim! You’re awake!”

“And just like that, my headache’s back,” Chimney said, with about as much heat in it as ice. He let go of Maddie’s hand so that he could snag Jee-Yun’s smaller one. “She’s just like her daddy, small but fast. Aren’t you?”

“She didn’t get onto a bus that nearly hit her.”

“I did that?” Chimney scrunched his face up and then shook his head, before telling Maddie, “I was trying to get back to you, the both of you, the whole time.”

“We always find our way back to each other somehow,” she said, ignoring how, while the words were nice and sent a thrill of warmth through her, she knew it wasn’t quite true. His fever-addled mind led him to Anne and John, and to Kevin, because she didn’t exist for the Howie that Chimney used to be.

“Can I ask you a question?”

“Anything.”

“Will you marry me?”

Maddie couldn’t help but laugh.

“The minute you get out of here,” she told him.

“Great,” he said. “But what if I don’t want to wait that long?”

Behind her, Maddie heard the squeal of shoes on linoleum and then her embarrassment of a brother bellowing,

“Babe! Who has the rings!?”

Nurses, Buck thought, deserved far more praise and credit than they got. All he had to do was tell one of them what was going on, and Maddie was being shuffled back into the employee lockers to change into her wedding dress. Chimney obviously couldn’t change into his suit, but when Buck last checked on him, another nurse was helping him put on the jacket over his unflattering hospital gown without disturbing his IV or any of the wires attached to the machines tracking his vitals.

Now, they were standing in the hall as Maddie finished getting ready, Athena fussing with the train of her dress.

“Kinda fitting that you’re getting married in a hospital,” Buck said, brushing a stray piece of hair out of his sister’s face. “Y’know, considering how much time we spend here.”

“I was just thinking that,” Maddie said with a laugh. “But at least we both walked into this knowing we were getting married today.”

Buck glowered; Maddie just smiled at him.

“You look beautiful,” Bobby told her, rejoining them from checking on Chimney. “Everyone’s ready. Almost everyone. Buck, I think we’re missing someone?”

“Missing?” Buck tilted his head, confused for a moment, before realizing what Bobby meant. “Oh! The uh, the officiant was booked for another wedding, so obviously he couldn’t make it back here. But uh, I was thinking…”

He put on his brightest smile. Bobby heaved out a sigh, as if this was some great chore for him, even while he made no effort to smother a smile of his own.

“Might as well do the whole set,” he said, and held his elbow for Maddie to take. Dressed in a polo and slacks, he looked comically underdressed for the event, just like the rest of them. “Shall we?”

“We shall.” Maddie nodded and slipped her arm through his. She snapped the fingers of her other hand at Buck, who rolled his eyes and handed her a bouquet of flowers. He got them for twenty dollars at the hospital gift shop. “Alright, let’s do this.”

Buck and Athena hurried back to the hospital room, passing the nurses and doctors and some patients that were lining the hall to watch. Eddie was just inside the door, standing next to the chairs that Chris and Abuela were sitting in, and he handed Gavin over gamely when Buck reached for him.

“She on her way?” he asked.

“She’s on her way,” Buck confirmed.

Chimney sat up in the bed and straightened his suit jacket. Hen, the only one of them that never changed out of her wedding outfit, fluffed some of his pillows that were supporting him and then pressed a button on the remote so the bed reclined higher. A couple beats later, Maddie and Bobby entered the room. She slipped his arm out from his to go stand by the head of the bed, while he stopped just short of the foot.

“Dearest friends, family,” Bobby said, the same way he began it years before. “We are gathered here today to celebrate the marriage of Maddie and Howard…”

It turns out that, just like Eddie before them, they were both able to find the proper words.

Chapter End Notes

Did I decide to name this fic after the song that I did just so I could use those specific lyrics for this specific chapter?
Yes. Yes I did.

Chapter 21: Split

Chapter Summary

In which there's a carwreck that isn't just a carwreck, and then there's dinner with the Wilsons.

Typically, when the 118 responded to an emergency, they knew where they were headed. That was the whole point of the CAD system, along with why the second question from a dispatcher was usually “where are you calling from?” But sometimes, like when they were searching for that internet influencer - and oh God, did that description cover Buck and Eddie now? - they were stuck driving around in the hopes of spotting the people that needed them. This time, Buck found himself craning his neck to look out the window of the engine while Toby drove them around in circles near the Sunset Regency Hotel.

Details about what they were looking for were scarce. A traffic accident, three victims - two adults and an infant - and a two mile search radius. They might have been traveling in a red Buick Regal. Hell, there weren't any details on the severity of the accident, so it was very possible that they got back on the road and kept going.

Oh, and it was also an abduction case in progress. That was a not-so-minor detail that they did have.

In the end, it wasn’t even them that found the car. Someone else called in to report it down the embankment, flipped over and on fire. It was within the search radius, and so Bobby directed Toby where to take them. They arrived to find it billowing with smoke at the bottom of a deep incline, where Buck figured it crashed and then rolled and then, quite literally, burned.

“Alright, remember, there are three possible victims,” Bobby reminded them as they clambered out, already dragging out the hoses. “A man, a woman, and their baby.”

Buck helped Toby to get the first hose hooked up, and then he and Eddie made their careful way down the steep slope. Even with the lack of rain, the grass still threatened to have their feet slide out from beneath them. It was only going to get worse once the water started flowing.

Chimney, with the medical bag, made it to the car before they got close enough to start dousing it. He crouched down, just short of it, and started shouting questions at one of the passengers. Then he was blown backwards, almost into Hen, by the sudden explosion of the fuel tank.

“What’s the status down there!?” Bobby demanded over their radios. “Everybody good? Hen?”

Buck would go for his radio and give him the answer, since he could see Chimney sitting up while Hen checked him over, but his hands were busy gripping the hose and keeping it steady. In front of him, Eddie turned it on and started spraying down the flames.

“Were they alive?” he heard Hen ask.

“I saw movement, yeah,” Chimney responded. “Let’s get in there.”

“Chim, it’s still hot!” Eddie shouted at him.

“Then put it out!”

“What does it look like we’re doing?” Buck asked under his breath, and saw more than heard his husband laugh, his shoulders lifting with it.

The problem with hosing down a burning vehicle with passengers inside was that the stream from the hose itself could cause just as much harm. They had to be careful so that they hit the flames, but they didn’t hit anyone inside. Soon, though, they had the flames beaten down enough that Chimney and Hen were able to dash inside and start working to extract victims. Buck and Eddie shut off and dropped their hose to go help, while Ravi slid down to join them with a pair of halligans.

“How’s the driver looking?” Hen asked Chimney, who was crouched down beside the passenger side window, flashlight shining into the vehicle.

“There is no driver,” Chimney said.

“What about the baby?” Buck asked.

“I can see the car seat in the back.”

While Buck went to the back end of the car to try and get a look at the inside, Eddie took a halligan from Ravi and started trying to prise a door open. He grunted and strained, but nothing gave like it was supposed to.

“I think the fire melted the door to the frame,” he complained to Buck.

“We could maybe get the baby out through the back window.” Buck crouched down, trying to look through the space between the car’s trunk and the ground beneath it. “We just need to raise it up.

“Yeah, alright.” Eddie grabbed his radio, thumbing it on. “Cap, we’re gonna need airbags and blocks at the rear of the car.”

“You got it.”

Toby quickly arrived with the requested supplies, and they worked quickly to get the rear end of the car shored up. Meanwhile, Chimney and Hen had extracted the woman, laying her on a backboard while they worked on her. On the street above, new lights and sirens - police, from the flashing blue and the different tone - were arriving. Buck figured it was an easy bet that it was Athena, because she showed up at their scenes an awful lot.

“Can anyone give me a status report on the victims, please?” Bobby asked over the radio.

“The driver is missing, the woman’s been burned badly,” Chimney responded. “We’re stabilizing her and getting her ready for transport.”

“Copy that, what about the baby?”

“Getting to the baby now,” Buck responded. It was awkward to reach his radio with the way he was army crawling beneath the car’s trunk. The back window was already shattered, so all he had to do was poke his head through and… Huh. “Sorry Cap, baby’s gone.”

“Gone? Deceased?”

“No.” Whoops. “I mean not here. The car seat’s empty.”

So there was an injured woman, the victim of an abduction. There was a missing driver, the presumed abductor. And now there was a missing infant. They started the manhunt before Bobby even gave the order, Buck and Eddie directing a couple of guys to help Hen and Chimney get the patient to the ambulance while the rest were sent out in all directions. Additional manpower in the form of several uniformed officers soon joined in.

There were any number of ways that the infant could have ended up outside the car. Once, down in Austin, TK and them worked a MVA where the car seat ended up in a tree of all places. That wasn’t likely in this case, the car seat was still in the car, which… Well, they were left with several options that were just degrees of unpleasantness. Finding the baby might be just as bad as not finding it, especially since, no matter how much he strained his ears, Buck couldn’t hear any crying.

Neither of them were very religious, but Buck still caught Eddie glancing skyward like he was sending up a prayer.

“Sergeant Grant!” one of the cops called out. He was holding something, illuminating it with his flashlight. “I’ve found something.”

Buck bounded over, getting there with Athena and Bobby. The cop had a pacifier dangling from a pen.

“It’s not burnt,” the cop said. “That means they left the car before it caught on fire.”

“The question is,” Athena said, taking both pen and pacifier from her colleague. “Where are they now?”

This way took them to the city proper, and Buck figured that was a good guess. He glanced at Bobby, who gave him a nod, and then he turned to shout for Eddie and the others to come focus on searching in this direction.

They spent a lot of time stomping around in the dark, only to come up with nothing more than that pacifier.

It felt odd, going to dinner somewhere with Chris but without Gavin. The tot didn’t know the difference, not really, he was just perfectly happy being spoiled by his bisabuela and would be going to bed soon anyway. It still left Buck off kilter and with the absurd feeling that he needed to apologize to a two year old for being left out, even though they’d left him at home to do things with just Chris before. Buck was fairly certain that the why was a large part of what made this time different.

Ever since Mara’s blow up with Denny over her blanket, which resulted in the teenager getting several stitches, Buck and Eddie both had been hesitant to introduce Gavin to her just yet. Through no fault of Mara’s, not really, but they were both very aware that anything could be a trigger and also that Gavin didn’t quite understand the concept of being careful to avoid them. Made all the more difficult when Hen and Karen were still figuring the girl’s triggers out for themselves.

This wouldn’t be the first time they’d come over since the Wilsons took Mara under their roof. The last time they’d been over, the first time they’d met the girl, she’d been… Aloof. Distant. Guarded would perhaps be the better word, tracking their movement the entire time they were in the room with her and keeping everything she considered “hers” either hidden away or close at hand. She didn’t trust Buck or Eddie, that much had been very evident.

It made Buck feel better about not introducing Gavin to her just yet, because he got the impression his rambunctiousness would be an added stress that the poor girl just did not need to have sprung on her like that.

They pulled up to the Wilsons’ home this night a little later than they’d meant to, because Gavin had decided to make a bit of a mess just before they left the house and then the traffic was its usual circle of hell. Their arrival time wasn’t too terribly late, though, at least not late enough that Hen felt the need to make sure that nothing had happened to them. Which she absolutely would have; after Chimney’s latest brush with mortality, they were all a bit quick to the trigger with making sure tardiness was simply tardiness, and not something life-threatening.

That was probably why there was a hint of relief on Hen’s face when she opened the door to them.

“Hi, guys.” She stepped aside, opened the door wider. “Come on in.”

“‘Buela sends her love,” Eddie said, handing her the container he brought with them. “In the form of empanadas.”

“I would say she didn’t need to do that, but…”

“But only a crazy person says no to Abuela’s cooking,” Buck told her with a laugh.

Chris made his way past them to the dinner table to sit next to Denny, who immediately began showing him whatever he was doing on the tablet. That had Hen scowling a little.

“Tablet time is over,” she scolded, going to the kitchen island to grab a bowl to put on the table. “Dinner is ready.”

“But we can multitask,” Denny tried to argue, with Chris giving a supportive,

“Yeah!”

“Not when it’s dinner time you can’t,” Hen told him. “No screens.”

“You know better,” Karen added, coming in from the other room and immediately backing up her wife. She grabbed a pot off the stove and carried it over while Denny sheepishly put away the tablet. “Hey, guys. We were about to call Athena to send out a search party.”

“Nah, we just got delayed leaving the house,” Buck told her, pulling out first Eddie’s seat and then his own. His husband smacked him for it even as he smiled at the act. “Gavin had a bit of a, uh, potty emergency.”

“It got everywhere,” Chris cheerfully told them. “It was gross.”

“Where’s Mara?” Eddie asked, leaning in his seat a little to peer into the other room. “Feeling shy tonight?”

“She is sound asleep,” Karen said, and then to Hen, “Maybe we just make her a plate for later?”

Buck glanced between the Wilsons, noticing the flare of exasperation that Hen tried her best to smother as she started loading up her own plate.

“Is she still having trouble sleeping through the night?” he asked.

“Still,” Hen confirmed. “Even with the nanny cam so she can check on us. The therapist doesn’t have any new strategies to help?”

That was directed to her wife, who shook her head.

“He just said she’s still trying to figure out if she can trust us,” Karen said. “And that we have to be patient.”

“We paid him for that?” was Hen’s derisive take on that statement.

Karen scoffed.

“She barely talks to us,” she said. “What makes you think she’s gonna open up to a total stranger?”

“Hey,” Buck spoke up. “Have you uh, have you considered putting a lock on her door? I mean, so it locks from the inside?”

That got him three incredulous stares, from Hen and Karen and from his husband. Buck threw his hands up to defend himself from them.

“Don’t look at me like that, three of us are firefighters, and you’re-” He pointed at Karen. “-a literal rocket scientist. If you need to get into the room, we can get in there, lock or no lock. I was just thinking that, y’know, if part of the problem is that she doesn’t quite trust you yet…”

There were places that Buck stayed at in the past, in that time between Hershey and Los Angeles, that “sketchy” would have been the kindest of ways to describe. More than once he found himself staying up all night because he legitimately didn’t trust that he wouldn’t become the first act victim of a horror movie. He could understand if Mara, who’d been in a group home before the Wilsons took her in and god-only-knew where before then, was having trouble trusting that the safe space she’d been given was actually safe.

“That might work.” Hen let out a sigh. “It’s just… Sometimes, I feel like we’re making progress with her. Others, she’s like a closed book.”

“She has a brother.”

Denny’s quiet statement had them all freezing in the act of serving themselves. Or, well, almost all of them. Chris barely paused before he continued piling food in front of himself, because the kid knew his priorities.

Hen set down the wooden salad servers she was using and leaned forward a bit.

“What’s that, sweetie?” she asked.

“Mara has a baby brother,” Denny repeated.

“A brother? Are you sure?” Karen asked.

“One of the kids in her class was talking about his new baby brother and how he hates him. Mara kind of flipped out.” Denny explained. Buck was mostly caught on the fact that, for him to know about that, that had to mean that Mara was confiding in him. Which meant, in turn, that she trusted at least one of the Wilsons. “Not on him, but she was real mad. And then she told me about her brother. His name’s Tyson.”

“She probably wouldn’t like what Chris has to say whenever Gavin gets into his things,” Eddie mused, which earned from Chris an eyeroll, a scoff, and a drawn-out,

“Dad…”

Maybe this meant that Buck and Eddie needn’t have been worried about how Mara would react to Gavin’s usual Gavin-ness, not if the idea of another child disliking a new sibling made her angry. Then again, maybe she’d have been even more upset at the reminder of her missing brother. Who could say, really.

It still had Buck considering the idea of bringing Gavin with them, the next time they came over.

“Do you know where Tyson is?” Hen asked.

“No.” Denny shook his head. “And I don’t think Mara does, either.”

“Maybe Deidra knows?” Buck suggested. “Or maybe she could find out?”

“Don’t they usually try to keep siblings together, anyway?” Eddie asked.

“Usually.” Hen looked troubled. Karen looked angry. “But… But what about you guys? You’re having your own thing with Gavin, right?”

“Right.” Chris stabbed his fork into his food with a bit too much force as he said it. He wasn’t quite sold on the idea, no matter how many times Buck and Eddie tried to reassure him that nobody was taking Gavin away.

“We’re meeting Amy and her aunt at Griffith Park tomorrow after lunch,” Eddie said. “The Shane’s Inspiration park, I mean.”

“We figured if things go pear-shaped, we could just cut things short and hit the zoo,” Buck added. “Or hell, maybe even if things go well.”

The comment had Chris glowering.

“Well, here's hoping nothing goes pear-shaped,” Karen said.

“Here’s hoping,” Buck agreed, even as he knocked his knuckles against the wood of the table.

Chapter 22: Amy II

Chapter Summary

In which there's a reunion, and Chris wants everyone to know he's there under protest.

Shane’s Inspiration was one of several playgrounds that dotted Los Angeles county that were designed to be accessible to any and every kid. Obviously, the Diazes were frequent visitors of them ever since Carla told Eddie about them. The park they were going to meet Amy and her aunt at was actually the first one of the bunch, from which the others got their collective name.

They left the house just after they ate lunch, and managed to make good time through the midday traffic. Amy and Sylvie weren’t there yet, which was honestly a good thing. Chris was still unsold on this, and was doing his best to make his negative opinions known through vibes alone. Eddie understood, but he also wished that Chris wasn’t quite so obvious about it, because his husband was as anxious as the chihuahua down the road and their son’s clear disapproval of this was not helping any.

“Mi sol,” Eddie said, getting Buck’s attention as he unloaded Gavin. “Go ahead and take him, run some of that energy off. Chris and I will find a table to claim.”

Chris, who’d been about to take off, stopped and turned to glower at Eddie, because he wasn’t an idiot. Buck didn’t notice, though, too busy nodding at the idea and talking to Gavin in his arms.

“You hear that, little guy? Wanna go play for a bit?”

“Yeah!” Gavin cheered, wiggling to be let loose.

Buck laughed but didn’t let him down, instead opting to throw him over his shoulder and jog towards the park proper, Gavin giggling and squealing the entire time. When they reached the street that separated the parking lot from the playground, Buck stopped and exaggeratedly looked both ways, holding Gavin out as he did like he was that monkey from the Lion King showing off Simba.

Eddie rolled his eyes at the other man’s antics, and looked down in time to catch Chris doing the same.

“C’mon.” He lifted out the small cooler they’d packed, checked to make sure the small first aid kit was still in the mesh pocket on the side, and closed the door to the jeep. “Let’s go plant our flag somewhere.”

“You’re both dorks,” his kid complained.

“I remember you used to think we were cool.”

“I also used to think the tooth fairy was real.”

Eddie gave an overexaggerated gasp, hand going to his chest, and said,

“She’s not?”

Chris gave him a look flatter than the entire state of Texas, and then kept moving.

They made their way - slower and with less exuberance - to the park. There were plenty of picnic tables, so even though the place wasn’t empty - it was a saturday in May, and it wasn’t exactly an unpopular park - Eddie was still able to lay claim to an empty one beneath the shade of a copse of trees. Would this be considered a copse? How many trees did it take to make a copse?

A question to have Buck look up later.

“Hey, Chris.”

Chris, about to amble off towards where Buck was helping Gavin down a slide, froze in place and heaved out a sigh. He turned back around to face Eddie, slouching in place as he rested his weight on his crouches.

“Yeah, Dad?”

“Can we talk before you run off?”

“Fine,” Chris huffed.

Eddie set the cooler down on top of the table and took a seat, turned to face Chris. He clapped his hands onto his knees, let a puff of air out of his nose, and leaned forward some.

“You don’t like that we’re doing this,” he stated. Chris just shrugged. “Do you want to tell me why?”

“What if they decide to take him away?” his kid asked in return, the words quiet.

“They won’t.”

“That’s what Denny and Aunt Hen and Aunt Karen thought about Nia,” Chris argued. “And then her mom took her back.”

“Well, that was different.”

“How?”

“Because we adopted Gavin, officially and everything. No refunds,” Eddie said with a shake of his head. “And Nia was taken away from her mom until her mom could get back on her feet, it was never meant to be permanent. Amy gave Gavin up, remember?”

“So- So-” Chris shook his head. “We’re just letting her come back? What if she sticks around for a few months and then just leaves again?”

Oh.

“Okay,” Eddie sighed, laid his hands on his son’s shoulders. “Gavin doesn’t know Amy. She’s as much a stranger to him as she is to you. If she does decide to stick around, maybe that’ll change. But if she doesn’t, if she leaves, he’ll never remember her to be hurt by it.”

“I guess.”

“Chris, this…” Eddie wasn’t sure how to word it, how to address what he knew his son was thinking about. “This isn’t like Mom, okay?”

Chris looked away, the mullish set of his jaw exactly like Eddie saw in the mirror.

“There were so many reasons why Mom left, and why she stayed away for so long. Reasons that have to do with her, and reasons that have to do with me,” Eddie continued. “Amy gave up Gavin because she knew it wasn’t safe for him to stay with her. And she’s doing a brave thing by seeing him again, because he might remind her of something bad, something scary.”

“Gavin isn’t scary.” Chris said it with a bit of a sneer.

“You don’t think storms are scary, either,” Eddie reminded him. Because they lived in a household that had two people and a dog who were not big fans of thunder and lightning. “Amy isn’t going to take Gavin away. If she tries, it would have to be over our dead bodies, right?” Chris gave a firm nod to that. “But she’s not going to. If today goes well, then Gavin’s family just gets a little bit bigger. If it doesn’t, then nothing changes. Okay?”

“Fine,” Chris reluctantly allowed.

“Awesome.” 

Eddie looked up when he spotted motion, spotted Sylvia coming up the path to the playground, a nervous young woman at her side that he just barely recognized as Amy. Chris followed his gaze, twisting around to look at them.

“Is that her?” he asked, his tone making clear what kind of welcome he was ready to give them.

“It is,” Eddie confirmed, lifting his hand to wave. “Would you go get Pops?”

“Yeah, okay.”

A still grumpy looking Chris stared at the two women for another moment, and then he ambled to the playground, where Buck was too focused on Gavin to have noticed the new arrivals. Eddie stood, took a breath, and readied himself for what was coming.

“Pops.”

Buck looked up at the sound of Chris’ voice, hand still gripping Gavin’s little little back as he helped the tot up the short ladder to the top of the slide once more. They hadn’t moved on to any of the rest of the equipment yet, because Gavin was having too much fun sliding down and down again.

“Yeah, bud?”

“They’re here,” Chris said, jerking his head towards the area with the picnic tables.

Sure enough, he spotted Eddie talking to both Sylvie and Amy. Buck heaved in a breath and nodded.

“Alright,” he said, with more surety than he felt. “Okay, Gavin, one more go down the slide.”

Gavin, of course, barely paid attention to him. He was focused on his goal, that of going down just to climb back up. The tot laughed as he slid down, and immediately started sprinting for the ladder once more. He was not happy when Buck caught him before he could, flailing his little legs in protest.

“Sorry, little guy,” Buck said. “We’re done sliding for the moment.”

“No!”

“Yes,” he told him, carrying to the picnic table. Amy was watching them approach, gnawing at her thumb as she did.

“No!”

“Yes.”

The back and forth continued the rest of the short journey, much to Christopher’s amusement. It was a nice way to distract Buck from the anxiety plaguing him, up until he couldn’t be distracted from it any longer.

“Are you arguing with a two year old, mi sol?” Eddie asked, barely hidden laughter underlying the words.

“I am winning an argument with a two year old,” Buck said, and then gave the two ladies a smile. “Hi, Sylvie. Amy, you’re looking so much better.”

She did, indeed, look much better than the last time Buck had seen her, bruised and broken in a hospital bed, but she still looked so very young. Her hair was cut shorter, with a new streak of bright pink, and there was a tiny little ring in her right nostril that wasn’t there before. She had her arms wrapped around her middle, though, and her smile was shy and guarded.

“Thank you,” Amy said. “Is that…?”

“Yeah, this is Gavin.”

Gavin was clutching at Buck’s shirt as he peered at the newcomers curiously with his big blue eyes. Amy’s smile wobbled around the edges. Carefully, Buck knelt down and put Gavin on his feet, though the tot didn’t let go of him.

“Hey, Gav, why don’t you say hello to…?” Buck glanced up at Amy, the unvoiced question hanging between them. Her eyes - a dark brown similar to her aunt’s, not at all like Gavin’s bright blues - went wide and she gave a little shake of her head. “Why don’t you say hello to Amy?”

“H’lo.” Gavin gave a little wave with his free hand.

“Hi, sweetie.” She crouched down, reaching out a hand that didn’t quite touch Gavin’s curls. “You’ve gotten so big.”

An awkward silence fell over the group as everyone watched Amy and Gavin. Buck heard Chris’ crutches tapping on the concrete as he moved to loom over them. He didn’t need to look up to know that his oldest was probably glaring down at Amy, and was happy that she didn’t seem to notice.

“Do you want to show Amy the slide?” Buck asked Gavin. The little tot nodded, his curls bouncing, and Buck smiled at Amy. “What do you say? Wanna go see the slide?”

“Yeah,” she said. “Sure.”

Buck stood back up, peeling Gavin’s fingers off his shirt but keeping hold of his hand afterwards. With a little prompting, Gavin happily led him back to the playground equipment, with Amy hesitantly following along. After a moment, they were accompanied by the sound of Chris’ crutches right behind them.

The moment they got close to it, Gavin was dropping Buck’s hand and rambling up the short, flat steps. Buck stopped right beside it, tall enough that everything was practically below waist height on him. It made it easy to track his kid as he ran through and practically dove onto the slide, giggling as he went down.

“He likes the slide,” Amy remarked, watching as Gavin ran back around to clamber back up.

“Yeah,” Chris answered, a little gruff. 

He slipped off his crutches, setting them against the equipment, and climbed up onto it as well. Instead of going down the slide, which he was very much too big for these days, he found something on the side that clanged and dinged when he pulled levers. That caught Gavin’s attention, the little boy pulling to a stop and coming back to see what his big brother was doing.

“I was… relieved,” Amy said, while she and Buck watched. “When Aunt Sylv told me he was with you, I mean.”

“Because you knew he was safe,” Buck guessed. She nodded.

“I didn’t want to be a mother.” The words were a whisper. “I still don’t.”

“That’s okay.” He tried not to let on how relieved he was to hear that. “You don’t have to be.”

“Papa! Papa!” Gavin was dancing in place, pointing at- Ah.

“You want to go on the swings?”

“Yeah!”

“He just noticed them,” Chris said, sticking close to Gavin as the little guy clambered down, ready to catch him if he tripped in his excitement.

“He’s never been here before,” Buck reminded him. “There’s a lot of new things to see.”

“I guess.”

They made their way over to the swings as a group, Chris grabbing his crutches again, and Buck got Gavin situated in one of the seats. All the while, the tiny dictator impatiently demanded to be pushed.

“You can swing, too, y’know,” Buck told Chris. 

The teenager made a face at him, but then started looking at the other swings in contemplation, because no one was ever too cool or too old for them. Hell, if he thought his butt could fit in the seat, and if there weren’t legitimate concerns that he’d break them with his bulk, Buck himself might’ve had a go. Then again, he’d never claimed to be the most adultiest of adults.

“Papa! Push me!” Gavin demanded, unhappy with the slow pace of things.

“And your dad calls me demanding,” Buck muttered under his breath, and smiled when he heard Amy laugh a little at it. “Alright, little guy, how high do you wanna go?”

“High!”

“You’ve got it.”

It was one of Eddie’s favorite things, to simply sit back and watch his family together. There was a peace to it, a certain calmness that sank to the core of himself and rooted itself there. He would do it all day, if he could, spend his entire time watching Buck or Chris or Gavin, or any of the three of them in combination.

“He doesn’t like us,” Sylvie said. She, like him, had taken a seat on the bench of the picnic table to watch the others.

“Who?” Eddie asked, though he was fairly certain he knew who she was talking about.

“Your other son. Chris?” She glanced at him, and he nodded. “He started glaring the moment he saw us.”

“Yeah. He’s worried, and he’s protective.” Eddie was worried, for the briefest of moments, that Chris was gonna start swinging his crutches when it seemed like Amy was going to touch Gavin’s hair. 

“He’s a good brother.”

“He is,” he agreed.

“I’m not sure how much Amy actually wants to be in his life,” Sylvie told him. “I don’t think she knows, either. Like I told the two of you, she’s mostly just wanted to see for herself that he was safe and alright.”

“You also said you weren’t sure how she’d react to seeing him,” Eddie reminded her. He distinctly remembered the awful revelation about who Gavin got his eyes from. “She seemed like she did okay.”

Nervous, perhaps as anxious about the whole thing as Buck, but it wasn’t too bad.

“She had a panic attack the first time she saw his picture.”

Oh.

“Those aren’t fun,” Eddie said. “The Fourth of July is hell.”

Sylvie eyed him, and then nodded.

“I noticed something, actually. About those pictures, the ones Buck sent me,” she said, focusing back on Gavin and the rest of the group. They were still at the swings, the sounds of laughter carrying on the wind, along with Gavin’s demands to go higher. “There’s a gap. A few pictures of him as an infant, and then a lot of pictures when he’s older.”

Eddie just let out a hum, not giving up any information.

“Buck told Amy that your coworker had him at first,” Sylvie said. “When did you two get him?”

“We got custody of him last January.”

“And who had him before that?”

“Our friend - our coworker - Hen, and her wife.”

“The same ones who had him first, I’m guessing. Did they have him the entire time?” she asked. Eddie just shrugged in response. “I don’t want to know, do I?”

“We don’t even know,” he admitted, not about to tell her about the bruise that was around Gavin’s tiny little arm when he arrived at the Wilsons again.

“Well,” Sylvie said. “He certainly looks to be flourishing with you.”

Chapter 23: Babies Do Not Make For Good Footballs

Chapter Summary

In which Albert still has daddy issues and Buck tackles a guy.

Ravi really needed to brave that damn owl and learn Korean. Actually learn it, not just get by with the fragments of phrases that he picked up like pennies in the street just by eavesdropping on his boyfriend and his boyfriend’s family. And maybe Spanish, too, because he knew that Buck and Eddie had been talking about him the other day, no matter how much they denied it. Why else would they keep looking at him and then laughing?

How would they like it if he started talking about them in Malayalam? Though he’d just be talking to himself, because no one else at the station spoke it. Albert knew about as many phrases in it as Ravi knew Korean ones, for the same reason, which was not at all conducive to gossiping about someone right in front of them.

Anyways, Albert was sitting at the kitchen table, laptop in front of him and chattering away in Korean. Telling his mother about his week, Ravi figured. Myung-Soon Han was speaking from - Ravi glanced at the digital clock on the oven, remembered that neither of them bothered to actually set it, pulled out his phone instead, and then did the math - the far off future of tomorrow morning. Ravi himself sat on the couch, television on low, doing his best not to be too intrusive. He tried to catch the few words he knew in an attempt to know what they were talking about, and only succeeded in confusing himself.

Things between Albert and his mother were… Good? Better than between him and his father, at least. Ravi didn’t know if it was because Myung-Soon was more progressive and accepting than Sang, or if she was simply more willing to be tolerant for the sake of not losing contact with her only son. Buck confided in him, once, that he was pretty certain that was the case with Eddie and Ramon, that Ramon still didn’t like the idea of Eddie being with a man but liked the idea of losing Eddie forever even less.

Regardless of the why, Ravi tried his best to minimize his presence whenever the calls happened. He usually managed to be out somewhere, with his own parents or with friends or out running errands. But sometimes he was stuck haunting the apartment he lived in with Albert, stalking the edges so as not to disturb the shaky peace. Because he didn’t want to interrupt his boyfriend’s time with his mother, when she was so far away and he saw her so rarely, and also because… Well, if she really was being more tolerant than accepting, then he figured staying out of sight, and therefore out of mind, was better than shoving a reminder of it in her face.

Albert loved his mother. Ravi didn’t want to ruin their talks, even more so since Sang was still being an asshole about things.

It sounded like their call was winding down. Ravi was pretty sure they were saying their goodbyes, at least. Unless Albert remembered something else he wanted to tell her and the conversation continued on for another hour. But that didn’t appear the case, because after exchanging a few more words - Ravi was fairly certain he was telling his mother he loved her, it sounded similar to what Al would sometimes say when he didn’t quite have the brain power for English - there was the bloop sound of the call ending, and then Albert shut the laptop.

Then he let out a sigh and let his head fall to the table top with a light thud.

“Uh.” Ravi got up and moved to the seat beside him, to yank at Albert’s hair. “Everything okay, Alley Cat? You’re usually less dramatic after you’re done talking to your mom.”

“Yeah.” Al’s voice was muffled by the wood until he raised his head. “No. Dad was in the room when I called her.”

“Oh.” Ravi had been out for a grocery run, missing the start of the call. By the time he came back, Albert was already in the middle of talking to his mother. “How, uh. How did…?”

“He saw it was me and walked out before I could even finish saying hello.” Albert sounded miserable as he said it. “Mom was upset and pretending not to be, which meant I had to pretend I wasn’t upset either.”

“I’m sorry.” Ravi wrapped an arm around him. His parents had been fine with Albert, taking it perfectly in stride when Ravi finally told them and simply asking to properly meet the “nice young man” from his badge ceremony. But unfortunately, that meant he was at a loss whenever Sang Han did something Sang Han-like. “Do you… Do you want to talk to your brother? Visit the Lees?”

“Howie would just ask me what I expected,” Albert said. “And he wouldn’t be wrong, because what was I expecting?”

“Your dad to love you.”

Albert made a face and pulled away, Ravi reluctantly letting him.

“What time is it?” he asked, grabbing his phone from where it lay next to the laptop. He thumbed it on, glanced at the time. “It’s only six-thirty. C’mon, let’s go do… Something.”

“Like what?” Ravi asked.

“I don’t know. Something. It’s Saturday, it’s Los Angeles. There has to be something we can go do.”

“We can always crash whatever Buck and Eddie are doing,” he suggested. “Get free food, and you can play with the other children.”

“The other children,” Albert scoffed, but nodded all the same. “Sure, yeah, okay.”

“Go change, then,” Ravi told him, shoving lightly at his shoulder. “I don’t mind the lounging chic, but there’s a hole in the crotch of those sweats.”

Albert glanced down at his lap, nodded, and went into their bedroom to change. That allowed Ravi to text Buck without him noticing, sending him a quick warning that Albert’s dad had been an ass again and that they were coming over. Buck replied with a series of emojis that Ravi chose to assume translated to “sure, come on over.”

And then Ravi went to make room in their fridge, because he knew Isabel was going to send them home with leftovers and he was not going to turn that down.

The words “officer down” going over the radio was never a happy occasion. It was moderated this time by the fact it was apparently because of a traffic accident - they ran their cruiser into a civilian's vehicle - instead of anything worse. That still brought several questions to Buck’s mind, though, such as,

“How did this even happen?”

“Dispatch says they were responding to a possible abduction in progress,” Bobby answered over their headsets as they sped through the streets. “It happened in the middle of the intersection.”

“So, what?” Ravi twisted around in his seat, since he was sitting across from Albert, to look at Bobby. “Their sirens were going and the other car just didn’t care? Didn’t notice?”

“Distracted, maybe,” was the captain’s suggestion.

“So many people just don’t get out of the way,” Toby complained, honking the engine’s horn for good measure. 

They arrived at the scene in good time, since they were already in the vicinity, and the brakes squealed as they came to a stop. Buck hopped out, Eddie right behind him, and took in what they were dealing with. The cop’s vehicle, one of the SUVs that the LAPD seemed to favor, seemed to have smashed into the other car with enough force to send them bouncing apart. The front end of the SUV was crumpled, and the driver’s side of the other vehicle was now concave, with bits of glass and metal strewn across the pavement between them.

The cop that called it in was ambulatory, but there was blood all over his face.

“Hen, Eddie, I want you on the driver,” Bobby ordered, pointing at the SUV. Buck could see that the downed cop was slumped in the driver’s seat, utterly still. “Buck, Chim, you’re on the other vehicle.”

“Got it, Cap.” Buck bumped his wrist against Eddie and then went to follow Chimney.

“Tejada, how many we got here?” Bobby asked the second cop, the one that called it in. Tejada’s answer came in the form of bending down at the waist and vomiting all over the pavement, which Buck hopped out of the way to avoid. “Alright. Albert! Come look him over.”

The civilian driver, meanwhile, had crawled to the passenger side and tumbled out onto the road. Buck jogged over just as he was tugging out a car seat with a wailing infant sitting inside it. He looked, frankly, rather rodent-like in Buck’s opinion, and with only slightly less blood on his face than Tejada.

“Oh, hey, let me give you a hand with that,” Buck offered, rounding the car as he did.

The driver shook his head, backing away.

“We’re okay,” he said. “We’re fine, we made it.”

“Sir,” Chimney said. “We should actually take a look at you and your baby.”

“No, no, no.” The driver shook his head frantically, and then told the wailing infant, “It’s okay, Daddy’s here.”

The guy was acting weird, in Buck’s opinion. Definitely stressed, maybe not all there. That and the head injury led Buck to conclude that the guy probably had a concussion, which wasn’t something they could just ignore. Especially with the guy trying to run away from them.

“Sir, we need you to stop,” Buck tried again. “You or the baby might be injured and just not know it.”

“We’re fine!” the guy snapped, suddenly angry. Buck made another mark on his mental checklist of concussion symptoms. Once again to the baby, he said, “It’s okay, sweet pea. Don’t cry.”

Beside him, Chimney suddenly went very still.

“What did you just call her?” he asked the driver. The driver looked back up at them and that was fear in his eyes, now. Chimney saw it, too, because he fell into the same tone and motions he used to soothe patients, patting the air and saying, “Okay sir, just relax”

“We’re a family,” the guy shouted at them. “We’re a beautiful family!”

Buck realized what, exactly, was going on and who this guy was a mere second before he was suddenly throwing the infant - carrier and all - through the air at them. Chimney dove, catching the car seat. The guy turned on his heel and started to run. Buck gave chase, not that the guy was getting very far.

Another cop’s SUV pulled to a screeching halt, cutting him off making and him stumble to a stop to avoid being hit. And Buck, like he was fifteen years younger, viciously tackled the guy to the ground. Maybe it was with a bit too much enthusiasm, since he hit the guy hard enough that he folded, legs going straight up in the air when they hit pavement, but Buck didn’t particularly care about that.

Especially when he saw that it was Athena who’d showed up in uniform.

“This is the guy!” he told her, keeping the squirming little rat in place.

“Turn him over,” she instructed, pulling the handcuffs off her belt. Buck did as he was told, manhandling the man onto his stomach and then helping Athena to get his arms behind his back so she could cuff him. “Is that his car?”

“Yeah,” he answered with a nod.

“We think he took another woman.”

Buck frowned, glanced back at the car.

“It was just him and the baby,” he told her.

“Anybody check the trunk?” she asked. He shook his head. “Alright, sit on him for me.”

“Yes, ma’am.”

Athena got up and hurried towards Bobby, leaving Buck with the kidnapper. The injured, possibly concussed kidnapper, he reminded himself. Remembering Athena’s own words from long ago, and what Hen went through in the more recent past, he hauled the guy over to Athena’s car, so that he was sitting against it.

Hen was busy with the unconscious cop, with Eddie assisting her. Chimney was busy going over the infant. Albert was still tending to the other cop, Tejada. And that was pretty much everyone present that had more than the basic EMT training, so Buck figured this was up to him for the moment.

Buck dug into the pocket of his turnout coat. Not the one with the stash of emergency granola bars, but the one with some of the supplies he snagged the last time he did inventory. Such as the penlight that he now pulled out, clicking the button at the end to turn it on.

“Alright, sir, do you know what day it is?” he asked, flicking the light in the guy’s eyes. Pupil reaction was normal, eyes moved okay to track his movement. “The date? The president?”

A concussion test required the patient to answer questions. If they just kept silent, like this guy was doing as he glared at Buck, then it wasn’t much help. And Buck didn’t know how much of his earlier behavior was symptomatic of the head injury and how much was just because the guy was a nutjob.

“I guess you do have the right to remain silent,” Buck told him as he stood up. He watched as Bobby, carrying a halligan, approached the guy’s trunk with Athena. The captain shoved the tool into the seam and popped it open with ruthless efficiency, and then Athena was reaching in to help a woman climb out. “I don’t think you’re gonna need to say anything, actually.”

The guy just glared.

At some point, their home had become a popular hangout. Buck wasn’t complaining, because that seemed the kind of thing that only crazy people complained about, at least in his eyes. But they had Albert and Ravi over the other day, because Han Sr. was apparently his usual self, and now they were playing host to May and Harry. Okay, Buck was mostly playing host to May, because Chris and Harry were in the living room entertaining themselves with a game.

Little Gavin, of course, was sitting next to them, with his own (non-working) controller and utterly convinced he was playing with them.

“So.” May was sitting at the breakfast bar with Buck while they waited for the lasagna he’d stuck in the oven to finish. It was the first time he’d tried that particular recipe, and it hopefully wouldn’t turn out too bad. “How was the visit with the biomom?”

“It was fine.” Buck felt something brush against his foot, and bent down to pick up Senorita. The cat went boneless in his arms the moment he did so. “Chris acted less like a brother and more of a bodyguard the entire time.”

“Yeah, Harry said that he was very disapproving of the idea.”

“That’s a term for it.” Eddie came in from the backyard, Hoover trotting ahead of him. The dog first made a stop to greet May and Buck, and then he beelined for where the boys were. “Even Sylvie noticed that he wasn’t very happy to see them.”

“Amy mostly watched us play with Gavin,” Buck told May. “Took forever before she was even comfortable with touching him.”

“Her aunt said she had a panic attack the first time she saw a picture of him,” Eddie added.

May’s face twisted up at that, but she thankfully didn’t ask any questions. She’d been a dispatcher longer enough, and been around other first responders enough, to know there was never any pleasant, reasons for that kind of reaction. 

“Is she going to see him again?” she asked instead. “Are you gonna need to get another table for Sundays?”

“Eh, maybe not that far,” Buck said with a shrug. “But she seemed like she wanted to. Right, babe?”

“They didn’t say anything concrete, but yeah,” Eddie agreed. 

“So what is she going to be to him?” was May’s next question. “Like, is he gonna know that she’s his mom, or…?”

“I…” Buck looked at Eddie, who was just as uncertain as he was. “I don’t know, really. She didn’t want to be called Mom. I mean, if he gets older and asks, I am definitely gonna tell him the truth.”

“Most of the truth,” Eddie amended, and Buck pointed and nodded in agreement.

“Most of the truth. He doesn’t need to know the details,” he said. May nodded, understanding the underlying message that she didn’t need to know them, either. “If she sticks around, I’ll leave it up to her what she wants to be to him as he grows up. Aunt, family friend, whatever.”

“One day we’re gonna have to actually make that flow chart,” May said, reaching over to start scratching beneath Senorita’s chin, who immediately started purring and lifted her head up to give easier access. “The family’s getting confusing. Hell, the one for the Wilsons isn’t any better. You heard that they found Mara’s brother?”

“We heard that he got to see Mara again,” Eddie confirmed with a nod. Then he started sniffing the air. “Is that smoke?”

“Is what-? Shit!” Buck shoved Senorita into May’s arms and rushed for the oven, grabbing the mitts and tugging them on before yanking it open. A small cloud of black smoke welcomed him. “Aw, man.”

The lasagna, when he pulled it out and set it on the stovetop, wasn’t quite burnt entirely. There were maybe some bits of it that were salvageable. Maybe.

Buck hung his head with a sigh.

“I’ll order a pizza,” Eddie said, patting him on the back. “Okay, three pizzas.”

Buck nodded and watched him go ask the boys what they wanted on theirs. He glared at the lasagna, tempted to pull a Senorita and just knock it off in a fit of pique. But that would be wasteful. So, instead, he set it on the floor and called for the dog.

Hoover was always willing to clean up the evidence of their culinary disasters.

Chapter 24: Valor

Chapter Summary

In which efforts are rewarded, if maybe a little late.

They had so few good reasons to put on their Class A’s. The last time they’d taken them out of their garment bags, it had been to go to Jones’ funeral. Normally, if there was something that required a bit of dressing up, they stuck the tie onto their regular get-ups and called it a day, like they had for Eddie’s shield ceremony. If they were feeling fancy, they’d add the cap and gloves to the ensemble.

Today, though, was a good reason to pull the Class A out. It was not every day that Buck’s husband got awarded with the Medal of Valor, after all. Buck hadn’t been certain, at first, that he needed to dress up as much as Eddie, since he wasn’t going to be on the stage to pose in front of the cameras. But Bobby told him, and Eddie backed him up, that it was the expected dress for all the firefighters that would be there, and he took their word on it.

Not that Buck really needed all that much convincing to wear it for the occasion. It took more effort getting little Gavin into something formal looking. And honestly, Buck didn’t expect the tot’s clothes to stay pristine longer than five seconds into the ride to the station, but he just looked so darn cute in it. It would be an absolute nightmare to get him out of it when the need arose later, but that was a problem for Future Buck.

Present Buck was too busy taking pictures of his adorable kids - Chris was wearing a suit and looking very suavecito - to care.

The award ceremony was taking place at the station, which had been taken offline and decorated. The trucks and ambulances were parked out front when they arrived, and the bay was filled with tables and chairs. At one end, beneath the loft, was a long table with food - complete with caterers! - while at the other end, its back to the bay doors, was a large stage with a podium and a chairs. There was a line of United States flags along the back of it.

There were a lot of flags hanging up around the station today, actually, both US and California, along with matching bunting everywhere.

They were one of the last ones to show up, though they weren’t close to being late. A consequence of having a little kid to wrangle and living close enough that they were lulled into a false sense of security with how quick they could arrive. Pepa was already there, having claimed a table near the stage with Maddie and Athena. The Lees, with Jee-Yun sitting in Anne’s lap, and the Panikkars, who Buck literally only ever seen once before in his life, had another table nearby, as did the Wilsons, with whom Harry and May were sitting.

There was also everyone else from their shift, and a look at them had Buck feeling self-conscious and uncertain.

“Babe,” he said, glancing around. “I thought you and Pops said that we were all supposed to be in our Class A’s.”

“Uh-huh.” Eddie nodded absentmindedly as he pulled out a chair for Abuela to sit down.

“Okay, well, I’m feeling a little overdressed.”

Albert, chatting away with his brother and Ravi, was not in his Class A, having gone with just the addition of a tie to his usual uniform. So had Toby, and Ortiz, and Clarke, and- Well, every firefighter present that wasn’t either going to be on the stage at some point or Buck. Maddie looked around as well, and then frowned at him.

“You’re not wrong,” she said. Athena, beside her, just looked amused.

“Don’t worry about it, mi sol,” Eddie told him. “We must’ve gotten our wires crossed.”

“Don’t worry about- Seriously?” Buck handed Gavin over to Athena when the kid made grabby hands at her. “I look ridiculous now.”

Eddie gave him a fond glare and tugged at the lapels of his jacket.

“You look very attractive,” he said. “I got an excuse to see you dress up for me, so I’m not seeing the problem.”

“Sit down, Buckito,” Abuela chided. “It’s too late now.”

She wasn’t wrong, though that didn’t magically make Buck feel any less self-conscious. Eventually, through some subtle signal that he missed entirely, everyone started going to their seats. Eddie gave Buck a quick kiss before going to his in the row of chairs that were facing the stage. He ended up next to Tommy Kinard, and the sight of the man did not have the bratty gremlin in his chest waking up to wreak havoc.

There was an empty chair, a sixth one. Someone must’ve miscounted or something.

Buck didn’t recognize any of the people on the stage, with the exception of Bobby and Chief Simpson. Some of them he assumed were people from the city’s government, maybe the union, judging from their clothes. Others, like the older guy sitting next to Bobby - who was looking much better than he did right after his recent, unplanned adventure to Jacumba, the details of which Buck still wasn’t clear on - were in Class A’s as well and were obviously with the department. Some of them were introduced and then gave fancy speeches, but Buck still couldn’t say who they were, because Gavin found these sorts of events as tolerable as Buck himself did, and so he got passed around between laps.

If literally every single person who they trusted to watch him wasn’t either busy - Carla did have other clients, after all - or currently in this building with them, they wouldn’t have brought Gavin at all. As it was, they were, and they did, and so anyone who got annoyed with Buck’s kid making noise and being distracting could just go straight to hell.

It was a relief when Chief Simpson finally stood behind the podium and gave his speech, because Buck knew that meant they were close to the awards being given out and sweet, sweet freedom.

“I’m often asked,” Simpson said, hopefully nearing the end. “What is it that makes us different? What separates firefighters from ordinary citizens? The answer is simple - we will go where others will not. Today, we honor those members of the LAFD who acted so courageously during the Oceanus Blue cruise ship disaster in March.”

Was it uncouth to roll his eyes at his boss’ boss’ boss during the event that his husband was getting a medal at? Probably, but Buck didn’t care. Simpson had been fully against them going to the cruise ship until he could use it for good publicity.

One at a time, they were called up to have the medal draped around their necks, be given a plaque, and pose for the camera with the chief. Hen, then Chimney, then Ravi, then Tommy, and then finally Eddie. Chris, when Buck looked over, was helping Gavin clap.

“Without their singular tenacity and selflessness, and unmatched skill and teamwork, so many more lives would’ve been lost,” Simpson said when Eddie went to stand with the others at the end of the stage. “Firefighters, on behalf of the Los Angeles Fire Department and the City of Los Angeles, we thank you.”

The crowd clapped and cheered, on cue. But, instead of letting them all go, the chief stayed where he was behind the podium.

“But we’re not quite done,” he said. “We still have two medals to give.”

Two? Buck wasn’t the only one confused, judging from the murmuring around him and the others’ faces.

“Several years ago, a tsunami crashed into this city,” Simpson said. Buck felt himself freeze in his chair. “Countless lives were lost that day. But not everyone who was caught in its path of destruction was taken from us. Because one firefighter, still recovering from injuries received months prior, single handedly saved more than a dozen victims. Firefighter Evan Diaz, I would like to honor you with this Medal of Valor.”

Buck didn’t move, not at first. He was too shocked, too caught unprepared. It wasn’t until he felt a push, accompanied with a “Pops, go” from Chris, that he finally went up to the stage. He stumbled through the entire process - medal around his neck, plaque, shake the chief’s hand and pose for the camera - in a daze, though he did his best to smile and at least look like he hadn’t been caught entirely off guard.

Eddie, when Buck joined him and the others, looked far too smug.

“What did you do?” he demanded, keeping his voice to a near whisper.

“Surprised you with a medal.” Eddie shrugged, casual as could be. “I know it’s not a wedding, but it’s the thought that counts. Right?”

The plaques were made of solid wood, and they were sturdy. And, pertinent to the immediate situation, did not break if used as a bludgeon. Eddie did not lose the smugness, even as he cringed away from another hit.

“The quality of teamwork displayed here-” Oh, Simpson was talking again. Buck couldn’t help but notice how this part of the speech didn’t quite flow with the part just before it, with the tsunami. Like the part for Buck was a late addition. Which made him wonder even more just what the hell Eddie (and Bobby, he was definitely in on this) did, exactly. “-is a reflection not only of each individual’s dedication, but the leadership of Captain Robert Nash.”

Oh, Bobby’s face. Buck wondered if that was how he’d looked.

“Captain Nash,” Simpson said, turned slightly to talk to the captain. “For your record of outstanding service and leadership, in and out of uniform, the Los Angeles Fire Department would like to honor you with this Medal of Valor.”

Bobby, after a moment, came unstuck enough to stand and make the short walk to the chief’s side. It was hard to see from this angle, but when Simpson started to drape the medal over Bobby’s head, it looked like the captain shied away from it.
