When the chief sat down, and a shell shocked looking Bobby took his place at the podium, he was holding the medal in his hand.

“Um.” He cleared his throat, glanced between them and the audience. “This isn’t the first time these people have pulled me out of the drink, in more ways than one. Not sure why I deserve a medal for it. In my opinion, you got it right the first six times.”

He set the medal down on the podium and applauded them, with the audience soon joining him.

After that, there was the mingling and the eating. Buck smacked Eddie a couple more times for good measure, to everyone’s amusement, before  they deposited their plaques at the table with Maddie and them. Then they braved the buffet line, Eddie getting plates for the kids while Buck loaded up plates for the two of them. Eddie got done first, heading back to the table to feed the hungry demons, and Buck found himself between Ravi and Tommy.

“It’s crazy, I was pretty sure we were all going to get fired,” Buck commented to Tommy as they waited for Ravi. “Instead, we all have medals.”

“Enjoy it while it lasts,” was Tommy’s cynical sounding response.

“That’s my motto,” Ravi cheerfully added. His plate was nearly overflowing.

“You know you can get Albert his own plate,” Buck told him, lifting up the two he was holding for emphasis. “Right?”

“Oh, no, this is all for me,” Ravi said. “I figured I better get something worthwhile out of all of this.”

“You got a medal,” Tommy pointed out.

“I said what I said.”

They watched him walk off, neatly balancing his plate, to the table where his parents were sitting with Albert and the Lees. Albert immediately reached for some of Ravi's food, got his hand smacked, and fell into overplayed dramatics. Beyond them, Buck saw Simpson talking to the older guy that Bobby had been sitting next to. He’d heard the whispered comments from the others while they sat, and then stood, through the speeches, enough to know who the man was.

“So that’s Gerrard, huh?” Buck asked as he and Tommy went back to the tables to eat.

“Yeah.”

“The way you guys talk about him, I assumed he was dead.”

“Only in our hearts,” Chimney said as he joined them, drink in hand. His sarcastic smile dropped completely when the man in question decided to come over.

“Kinard,” Gerrard said. “I thought you transferred out of here.”

“Several years ago,” Tommy responded, the words stiff. “I’ve been at Harbor Station ever since.”

“That’s right, I heard you got your wings.” He lifted his arms and made little flapping motions with his hands.

“You know,” Chimney broke in. “It’s good to see you haven’t changed at all, Cap.”

“It’s good to see you didn’t forget your training, Han,” Gerrard shot back. He made a pointed look around the station. “These floors are nice and shiny.”

“You taught me right,” Chimney said, stepping past his old captain and walking away. “Whenever I see some filth, I think of you.”

Gerrard watched him go, mouth twisted.

“He always was the funny one, it’s all he had,” he said, before turning back to Buck and Tommy. Well, his eyes were focused mainly on Buck. “You and your husband, do you use the women’s lockers? Or does Nash let you be with the men?”

“The men’s locker room has glass walls,” Buck pointed out, because he honestly couldn’t figure out which of the classic insults, exactly, the old dinosaur was trying to imply with the question. “Were they like that when you were here?”

“Yes,” was Tommy’s immediate answer.

“It’s weird,” Buck said, and then asked Gerrard, “Did you decide that? Because I know sometimes captains get to decide things like that.”

Gerrard didn’t answer. He was just kinda glaring at Buck, his entire face twitching in various spots. After several beats of tension filled silence, Buck just shrugged and moved on with his life. He went to his family, Tommy following behind him.

“So that’s Gerrard over there,” Buck said, setting down the plates and jerking his thumb over his shoulder. “Hen and Chim’s stories didn’t quite capture him.”

“He’s an experience you have to live through,” Tommy commented.

“I’ll pass, thanks,” Eddie said.

“Yeah. Uh, babe?” Buck lifted his medal off his chest. “What the heck?”

“Hey, don’t look at me, mi sol.” His husband pointed at their oldest. “It was his idea, I just helped carry it out.”

“I told you, remember?” Chris said when Buck looked to him. He was happily tearing through his plate of food, deftly avoiding the mess Gavin was making beside him. “You saved me, and you saved them.”

“Oh, you. Come here.”

Buck rounded the table and pulled Chris, squirming and complaining, into a hug.

“I wonder what that’s about,” he heard Tommy say. Buck set Chris free and followed the other man’s gaze, spotting Bobby and Chief Simpson upstairs in the station’s loft. The pair of them looked to be having some sort of argument. Or maybe just a particularly lively discussion.

“Maybe he just doesn’t appreciate a good surprise,” Eddie commented, and then grabbed a napkin to start wiping at Gavin’s face. “Food goes in your mouth, mijo.”

“I guess.” Buck frowned and shrugged. “Alright! Let’s get some food in us before Abuela comes back from wherever she is and makes us pose for twenty-thousand pictures.”

“This is still weird,” Buck said, hours later.

They were in bed, dress uniforms put aside to be taken to the dry cleaners later. Buck was still wearing his medal, while Eddie’s was sitting on the table beside the box that held his Silver Star, along with their plaques. Those would be going on the wall at some point, along with the medals as soon as they found cases for them.

At the moment, Buck was sitting against his pillow and fiddling with his phone, fielding texts from TK and Carlos both. They’d found out about him getting the medal, too, before Buck could actually tell them, because the devil was fast, but whoever was in charge of the department’s social media was faster and had sent him a collab invite before they were even done eating. It wasn’t very surprising, honestly, considering the entire point of that event was to be paraded in front of the cameras like show dogs.

“You deserve it,” Eddie told him.

Buck made a face.

“I was just doing my job. Trying not to die while keeping Chris safe.” He dropped his phone onto the bed beside him and lifted his medal up to stare at it. “Just feels like…”

“Like you got a reward for failing?”

“Yeah.”

Buck never did quite figure out how to forgive himself for Chris falling off the truck that day. He knew Eddie would understand, all things considered.

They fell into silence for a bit, Buck fiddling with the medal. Eddie reached over and took hold of it, tracing his thumb along the edge. When Buck glanced over at him, his lips were curving into a smile.

“What?”

“Oh, just.” Eddie shrugged, smile going wider. “Wondering what you’d look like, wearing the medal.”

“Uh.” Buck gestured at himself. “Like this?”

“Just the medal.”

Oh!

The air was filled with laughter as Buck rolled over on top of him, kicking the covers to the foot of the bed. There wasn’t much room for old guilts after that.

Chapter 25: The Bobby of It All

Chapter Summary

In which we are reminded that middle-of-the-night phone calls are never harbingers of a good time.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

There was something weird going on with Bobby.

Eddie knew, even as the thought latched onto his synapses and refused to let go, that he sounded very much like his husband. Between the two of them, it was usually Buck who was most likely to focus on the nebulous idea of something just being off. Something he couldn’t quite explain, but which he would fixate on regardless. Sometimes it was all in his head and he just needed a hand to guide him away from the edge before he spiraled all the way down to the rocks below. Other times, there actually was a reason to be anxious.

Which of those options described this instance, Eddie wasn’t sure. He wasn’t the one to typically have these sorts of feelings, not like this. He typically just distrusted a person’s motives, their sincerity. This was the equivalent of wetting a finger, sticking it in the air, and declaring that the vibes were off.

But nothing changed the fact that there was something weird going on with Bobby.

It started, Eddie thought on reflection, after the medals ceremony. Or maybe earlier than that, maybe it was after that sudden trip to Jacumba, with Bobby coming back sunburned and dehydrated. Regardless of when it started, though, it was really apparent this shift.

Again, though, there was nothing that Eddie could really point to, no case-breaking piece of evidence that screamed at him to be on alert. With the actress Felisa, he had the benefit of questionable financial history and having known people exactly like her “friend,” Alexis. But this time, with Bobby? He just had the man’s behavior, his actions, to go on.

The captain spent the shift apart from them, detached, when he would never hesitate to join them before. An outsider looking in, as opposed to part of the group. Several times, Eddie caught Bobby watching them go about their business or keeping themselves entertained, an expression on his face that Eddie could only describe as wistful.

When he did interact with them, instead of observing from afar, he’d single them out for it. Eddie saw Bobby with Toby, and then Clarke, and then Ortiz. He sat down to talk with Hen for a bit while she was working through the paperwork to petition for Mara’s adoption. He caught Ravi when the younger man was storing the hoses on the engine.

Ravi said that he’d just offered advice, when Eddie asked him what they talked about. Completely ordinary, an everyday occurrence. It was nothing by itself. It was another example of weirdness in conjunction with everything else.

Another piece of weirdness: they usually did store runs as a team. Today, Bobby went by himself in the captain’s truck. Which, okay, wasn’t cause for worry. It was the exact sort of thing Buck would bring up and then Eddie would rationalize away. Using the captain’s truck to run an errand was a perfectly normal thing for a captain to do.

But in the context…

Buck had yet to say anything about Bobby’s behavior. That could either be because he hadn’t noticed - not impossible, the man could simultaneously be very observant while also being the most oblivious dumbass in the station - or was simply cataloging the evidence, preparing to present his argument to Eddie for why his concerns weren’t overblown and he should be taken seriously. Eddie was assuming it was the former, because his husband was not very subtle whenever he was worried about something and Eddie had yet to see him act the way he would if that was the case.

When the captain returned, Buck jogged over to greet him and Eddie followed. Bobby had paper bags weighed down with ingredients hanging from his hand, and closed the truck door with his elbow.

“So, uh, what are you making?” Buck asked.

Eddie couldn’t deny that he wasn’t curious, too, about what was worth a special store run.

“Not me,” Bobby answered, handing Buck the bags. Buck fumbled to grab them for a moment, face a mask of confusion. “You’re the chef tonight.”

Once again, not necessarily concerning but also very weird. Buck helped Bobby cook meals at the station all the time. But that was the keyword, Buck helped Bobby cook. The captain took pride in feeding his people, and the only time he diverged from the habit was when Athena was suspended and he invited her to cook for them to prevent her from going stir crazy. Even then, he anxiously hovered around the kitchen the entire time, doing his best to inflict his assistance upon her whenever she wasn’t looking.

“The others can help you,” Bobby said.

“Um, okay.” Buck shrugged and grinned, turning on his heel to rush up the stairs to the station’s loft while calling for Albert and Ravi.

Eddie started to follow, but a hand on his arm stopped him in place.

“I was going through some things and found this,” Bobby said, pulling a book out of his pants pocket and handing it to him. “Made me think of you. Just hang onto it, it might come in handy.”

He patted Eddie on the shoulder and left, leaving Eddie unnerved and staring at the worn copy of the Catholic Book of Prayers that the captain just gave him. They’d had conversations before about Eddie’s complicated relationship with religion, so it maybe wasn’t entirely out of left field. But it still felt like he’d just been pitched a curveball, at least.

There was something weird going on with Bobby.

Buck was ready to go home, pretty much. His uniform was folded away in his bag, along with his bottle of bupropion and all the toiletries that he didn’t keep spares of in the locker he shared with Eddie. He was packed up and good to go, all that he was waiting on was his slowpoke of a husband.

Footsteps, soft but noticeable in the quiet of the station, had him looking up. He spotted Bobby stepping into the locker room, already changed out of his uniform.

“Oh, hey, Pops.”

“Hey.” Bobby looked around, frowning a little when he didn’t find whatever he was looking for. “Where’s Eddie?”

“Still in the showers,” he answered. 

The other man nodded.

“You were really on your game this shift,” Bobby said. The bench was between them, Buck noticed, and the captain had stopped just a few steps into the room. “I don’t say this often enough, not nearly, but you’ve come a really long way. You’re not the same impulsive hothead who walked through those doors seven years ago.”

“Has it really been that long?”

“It has,” he confirmed with a nod. “A bit longer.”

“Well here’s to seven more,” Buck said.

“Yeah.” Something crossed Bobby’s face, there and gone again, but Buck couldn’t really put his finger on what it was. “You will make a fantastic captain.”

Buck grinned, ducked his head.

“Thanks, Pops.”

“You and Eddie have any plans for tonight?”

“Go home and sleep,” Buck answered, to the captain’s quiet laughter.

“Then have a good night, kid.”

“Yeah, you too.”

Buck watched Bobby leave, feeling the slightest bit unsettled in a way he couldn’t quite explain. He was so busy trying to untangle that, that he missed it when Eddie got out of the shower, padding into the locker room already mostly changed into his civvies with his uniform bundled up under his arm.

“Something wrong?” Eddie asked him, going to his work bag that Buck had set out on the bench for him.

“Bobby’s being weird,” Buck answered, twisting his mouth up in thought.

“Yeah, he’s been like that all day. No idea what’s up, though.”

There was time, while waiting for Eddie to finish getting ready to leave, to sit back and mull it over. Eddie was right, Buck decided, and wondered when his husband first noticed it. He wondered how he hadn’t noticed it, when it was so obvious in hindsight. Then again, that was hindsight for you.

To the surprise of absolutely noone, probably, Buck couldn’t quite get it out of his mind. It wasn’t helped by the fact that Eddie agreed with him, and even gave him his own points of evidence for the mental murderboard. There was nothing truly concerning, not really, just a constellation of moments of Bobby acting weird. Off in a way that they couldn’t really explain.

Visiting his sister was, as always, a nice little distraction for the anxious little hamster that ran around his brain. Though, considering they were going to introduce Gavin to Mara for the first time ever, it was also its own source of worry, tempered though it may be. Ever since they’d found her brother, and she got to see him again, Mara had opened up and flourished.

Hopefully, that meant there wouldn’t be any more coffee mugs flying through the air.

Of course, Buck felt like an utter ass for ever worrying about it once he actually saw how Mara acted with Gavin. Kind, patient, probably exactly how she treated her younger brother. They all sat at the kids’ table - which was really Maddie and Chimney’s coffee table - and the only point of issue that cropped up was how loud and obnoxious the five of them got. Which wasn’t really much of an issue, in Buck’s humble opinion. He ate with his family and friends, even allowing himself a single glass of wine, and felt his worries drip away.

They hung around even after the food was eaten, and he and Eddie helped clear the table, just talking.

“Okay,” Chimney said, coming out of the kitchen with a tray and making a beeline for the kids. “Who wants dessert? I have freshly baked chocolate-chip cookies.”

He held it out, and the little scavengers swarmed him. Buck and Eddie watched, and then turned to look at Maddie, expressions mirror images of skepticism.

“Since when does he freshly bake?” Hen asked, voicing their doubts.

“It’s more like freshly reheated,” Maddie told them, not even bothering to lower her voice. Buck and Eddie nodded, because that made much more sense. “The actual baking happened at that new market on Third.”

“I turned on the oven,” Chimney argued. “That still counts.”

“Hey, isn’t that the kind of thing we tell Gavin whenever he wants to help?” Eddie asked.

“I think it is,” Buck said.

“Alright, laugh it up you chuckle-fu-”

“Can I have two?” Denny asked, interrupting the certainly child-appropriate thing Chimney almost said. “One for later?”

“Uh, no you cannot,” Karen said. Chimney, who’d been about to hold the tray out for Denny, glanced at Karen and then leaned and whispered something that sounded suspiciously like instructions to meet him in the kitchen. “I heard that.”

Chimney gave her his best fake smile as he came back to the table, setting the cookies down in front of him.

“Karen has preternaturally sharp hearing,” Hen said. “Hears everything.”

Mara got up and came to stand by Chimney’s chair, looking the perfect image of angelic innocence. Buck, who used this trick before to get his way, and had taught Chris how to do the same, was immediately suspicious.

“Can I have another cookie, please?” she asked, sweet as could be.

Chimney looked to Karen, who nodded and shrugged, confirming which child was the current favorite. He held the tray out to Mara, who grabbed one - and then immediately grabbed several more, before dashing off out of the room with Denny. Jee-Yun and Gavin, giggling up a storm, were right on their heels.

“Great, they’re co-conspirators,” Hen complained, though there was an utter lack of heat. She eyed Chris, who hadn’t run off with the others. “Are you going to join the other bandits?”

“We have ‘Buela’s cookies at home,” Chris told her with a shrug.

“What, are you saying hers are better?” Chimney asked, playing up the offense. “That my baking isn’t good enough for you?”

“Yes,” Chris told him, before heading off to where the others had gone.

“That and she doesn’t even ask us for permission before giving them to him,” Eddie added as the others laughed at Chimney’s face.

“I can’t believe how great Mara’s doing now,” Maddie told Hen and Karen, switching topics.

“I think seeing her little brother really helped. I finally feel like she trusts us, and that she wants to be part of our family,” Ken said. A phone started ringing, and she got to her feet. “Oh, that’s me.”

“Cap surprised me yesterday,” Chimney said while Karen went to go answer her phone. “He seemed different.”

“What do you mean?” Hen asked. “He was in a great mood.”

“Maybe too great of a mood. Especially after what he said at the ceremony? I feel like something’s going on with him.”

“You noticed that, too?” Eddie leaned back in his chair, arm casually slung over the back of Buck’s. “He was acting all sorts of weird.”

“I asked Athena about it,” Maddie said. “She said there was nothing to worry about.”

“But?” Hen prompted.

“She seemed worried.”

“Ma hasn’t said anything to me,” Buck told them, chewing on his lip.

“Hen.” Speaking of worried, Karen was off the phone and looking upset. “Th- That was Deidra, uh… Our adoption hearing was canceled, we’re not going before the judge on Monday.”

“What happened?” Hen asked. “They’re rescheduling it?”

“No, Hen, it’s off the docket completely,” Karen said, and now the upset spread to her wife. “The judge has concerns or something about our petition? I don’t- I don’t know…”

“I thought the hearing was just a formality,” Chimney said.

“It is,” Buck answered for Karen. “By the time you’re even at this point, it’s pretty much just a given.”

He and Eddie had spent longer waiting for their turn to go in front of the judge than their adoption hearings actually lasted. They went up, the judge - Judge Carmichael, both times - asked them a few questions, asked Chris a few questions when it was his turn, gave her approval, and then took a picture with them. Not even Buck was worried about it not happening by then.

“Deidra said she’s never seen this happen before,” Karen said. “She- She thinks that someone must have spoken to the judge, but she doesn’t know who.”

Hen’s face turned grim.

“I know exactly who,” she said. “Councilwoman Olivia Ortiz.”

“The mother of the guy that…?” Buck trailed off as she nodded.

“The very same.”

There were probably few things more annoying than being dragged out of a good sleep by a middle-of-the-night phone call. Buck was very tempted to ignore it, already pulling the blanket up to cover his head and burying his face into Eddie’s shoulder, before the little spark of rational thought caught on and reminded him that there was never any good reason for someone to be calling him this late, that it could be important. So he rolled over with a huff and felt around for his phone on the nightstand, knocking his watch to the floor as he did.

“Go for Buck,” was how he answered, voice muffled by his pillow.

“Is this Evan Buckley Diaz?” It wasn’t a voice he recognized. It belonged to a woman, friendly but not cheerful.

“Yeah.”

“I’m calling from First Presbyterian. You’re listed as Robert Nash’s secondary emergency contact-”

That had Buck wide awake. He bolted up on the bed, the sudden motion jarring Eddie into wakefulness, too.

“What happened to Pops?’ he asked, and then- “His secondary contact?”

“His primary is listed as Athena Grant-Nash,” the woman said, which he already knew. “She was admitted alongside him. Mr. Nash suffered a heart attack-”

Buck didn’t hear what else she had to say. All he heard, truthfully, was static and his own heartbeat and his suddenly laborious breathing. His phone probably would have fallen from his numb fingers, if not for Eddie taking it from him first. There was a conversation happening behind him, or one-side of a conversation, but none of his husband’s words were registering with him.

He didn’t know how long Eddie spoke to the stranger on the phone, if it was a few seconds or minutes or even hours. All he knew was that an eternity later, Eddie was wrapping his arms around him and whispering in his ear that he had to-

“-breathe, mi sol,” Eddie was saying, chest pressed against Buck’s back. “C’mon, breathe with me.”

Buck might as well have been trying to suck a bowling ball through a straw, but he did as instructed. He followed the rhythm of Eddie’s exaggerated example, and he managed to heave in one breath and then another. The fuzzy black on the edges of his vision, which he hadn’t even noticed, faded away.

“Pops,” Buck managed to gasp out. “Ma, they-”

“Athena’s alright,” Eddie told him. “It was just smoke inhalation, she’s fine.”

“B- But Pops, he- Eddie.”

His husband fell silent behind him, holding him through the shaking that Buck was just realizing came from him. He spotted his watch on the floor by his foot, and had the idle thought to pick it up before it got stepped on, though he made no move to do so.

“C’mon, let’s get dressed,” Eddie said, even as he still held Buck. “I’ll drive.”

That was definitely for the best.

Chapter End Notes
