Chapter 26: This is the Big One

Chapter Summary

In which Buck and Eddie's morning sucks, but others are having it worse.

Buck took a breath in.

He let the breath out.

The only reason he was still standing, Buck was certain of this, was Eddie, who stayed a constant presence at his side. There wasn’t a moment that passed where he wasn’t touching Buck in some way, a hand on his back, an arm around his waist, a leg pressed against his own. It kept the panic attack, which was lurking just on the edge of Buck’s nerves, from making its return.

It was late, closer to being early. Eddie woke up Abuela, explained to her what was going on, better than Buck was able, and then drove them both in his truck because Buck was in no condition to drive. But it was Buck who took the lead when they arrived, going so fast that he was practically running. The nurse that manned the front desk must have thought him some unhinged lunatic, with how he acted when he demanded to know where Bobby and Athena were.

Athena was their first stop, and also the easiest. She was just asleep, according to the doctor on rotation. She was on oxygen, to help with the smoke inhalation, but she was expected to wake up on her own at any moment. 

The next stop was Bobby, and that was not so easy. Especially with the way even Eddie tensed up as they walked through the halls of the ICU. Buck didn’t really understand why until his husband’s half-whispered comment of,

“Your room was just down there.”

There was an observation window and they crept up to it. On the other side of the glass, Buck could see Bobby lying in the bed. He was intubated, plastic tubes falling like snakes off the bed’s edge and running up to the machine beside him. The hills and valleys of Bobby’s heartbeat were there, visible for Buck to see and track, but he was so very still.

“Mr. Diaz?”

Buck recognized that voice. He turned and spotted Dr. Salazar, the cardiologist he saw after both the lovely experience with Jonah Greenway and lightning strike, standing there in her white coat. She had a clipboard tucked under her arm, and was frowning at them both.

“Are you, um.” Buck cleared his throat. “You’re- You’re Pops’...?”

“Yes,” she answered, stepping up to the window with them. “He had a severe cardiac event.”

“Do you know what happened? I didn’t really get the details…”

Buck trailed off with a glance to Eddie. His husband might’ve gotten more, when he took over the call for him, but he wasn’t certain. He knew there’d been a fire of some sort, but he didn’t remember if anyone had told him what caught on fire.

“The paramedics brought him and his wife in together,” she said, and Buck already knew that but he nodded along anyway. “From my understanding, there was a fire at their home. Captain Nash suffered cardiac arrest at the scene, brought on by smoke inhalation, after getting both himself and his wife out. Paramedics were already there, and they immediately began resuscitation. They managed to restart his heart during transport.”

“H- How long…?”

“It took them almost fourteen minutes.”

The noise that came out of Buck following that statement was not one he was even aware he was capable of making. He blindly groped through the air for Eddie’s hand, squeezing it hard enough that he knew it had to be agony. But Eddie didn’t pull away or make a complaint, instead just asking Dr. Salazar,

“Do you know the damage yet?”

“No,” she answered with a shake of her head. “We won’t know the extent, or if there’s any damage at all, until he wakes up.”

If he wakes up, Buck mentally amended. He knew the percentages, he knew how bad things had been with him and that had only been just over three minutes. Bobby’s heart went for fourteen without beating. Bobby had been dea-

He took a breath in.

He let the breath out.

Turning his back on the doctor, he looked once more through the window at Bobby. The man must’ve been woken up, by the smell of the smoke or the heat of the flames. Buck wasn’t surprised that he’d gotten Athena out, he probably grabbed her and ran the moment he realized what was going on. It was instinct, the kind of thing they would do in their sleep, even if it was their worst nightmare.

Wait.

“Oh God, Harry.” Buck twisted around to search for Salazar, but the doctor was gone. “Eddie, what about Harry? No one’s said anything about him. Why haven’t they-? Where’s-?”

“Buck, Buck, mi sol, breathe.” Eddie grabbed his shoulder with his free hand, stopping his frantic search for someone that could give him answers. “He spent the night at May’s, remember? They spent an hour arguing in that group chat of yours even though they were in the same room, remember?”

“Right, right. You’re right.” He remembered now. They’d been sniping at each other over some movie they’d watched, brought about by a snarky message from Harry. “They don’t- I don’t think anyone’s told them. I need to tell them, Eds. They should know.”

He finally dropped Eddie’s hand to start patting down his pockets for his phone, digging it out in a clumsy rush. Once again, Eddie stopped him with a touch.

“I’ll tell them,” he offered. “I’ll go to May’s, tell them in person.”

“Are you sure?”

“Yeah. You stay here, tell me if Athena wakes up before I get back," Eddie said. “I’ll pick up something better than hospital coffee.”

Buck really had the best husband.

“Thank you,” he whispered, leaning in to kiss him.

Eddie gave him what was probably the best smile he could manage in this context, which came out as more of a grimace, before heading for the elevator. Buck watched until the doors closed and cut him off from view, and then turned back to Bobby. He’d never wanted to know what his family went through after the lightning strike, but it seemed he was finding out regardless.

He took a breath in.

He let the breath out.

The moment the elevator doors closed, Eddie collapsed against the back wall, holding onto the broad railing the ran around the entire thing. He buried his face in his hand, the one that wasn’t bruised from his husband’s crushing grip, and let out a shuddering breath. The time it took to ride down to the ground floor again was a thankful reprieve, a time to let himself go and then put himself back together again.

He was the only thing keeping his husband from falling apart completely, Eddie knew. Buck needed him to be steady, to be the unshakeable rock in the storm. He could be that for him, he would be that for him.

After her freshman year, May decided she’d gotten her fill of the full college experience, moving out of the dorms to an apartment of her own once more. Eddie and Buck, of course, were drafted again to help haul furniture and boxes while the Grant women stood around looking pretty and critiquing their carrying capacity. At this time of day, with the sun beginning to crest the horizon, the city’s traffic was only just starting to pick up, so the drive to May’s from the hospital was not an arduous one.

May’s apartment was on an upper floor, which made moving her in a pain in the ass. But Buck, in a move that was utterly unsurprising, had broken out crime statistics about ground-level apartments, rattling off how much more often they were broken into thanks to the convenience of access. Athena, of course, immediately backed him up, the pair of them ganging up on May until the young woman barely had a choice in the matter.

Eddie took the stairs this time, anything to prolong the journey and put off what he knew was to come.

Harry was the one who answered the door after several minutes of Eddie’s knocking, looking rumpled and rubbing at his eye. The teenager frowned with his entire body at the sight of him.

“Hey, Eddie, what’s-” Harry broke off with a yawn. “What’s up?”

“Is May awake yet?”

“Uh, no . Why?”

“Go wake her up, please,” Eddie said, bypassing the question.

Harry stared at him for a moment before letting him inside and going to his sister’s door, pounding on it with all the grace and respect of a younger brother. It reminded Eddie, briefly, of the way Gavin would shout and slap at Chris’ in the mornings. 

“What!?” May snapped when she opened the door, just wide enough to poke her head out and glare at Harry. Harry, in turn, silently pointed at Eddie. May’s eyes went wide, and then she was closing the door again. A couple moments later, she was stepping out in a robe and a pair of sweatpants that were long enough that the ends dragged on the floor. 

She eyed him up and down, zeroing in on his face. Whatever she saw wiped away her annoyance, replacing it with drawn worry.

“Eddie,” she said. “What’s going on?”

“You should, uh. You probably want to sit down,” he tried, gesturing at the couch, which had a blanket and a pillow on it.

“What,” she barked, “is going on?”

Eddie sighed, dragged his hand through his hair.

“The hospital called Buck,” was how he started. “There was a fire, I don’t know the details. Bobby got your mom out, she’s going to be fine. We’re just waiting for her to wake up.”

“A fire? Where?” she asked while her brother stood in quiet horror. “At home?”

“Yes.”

“And- And Mom’s okay?”

“The doctor said she just had some smoke inhalation.”

“Okay, okay.” May nodded once, and then twice, and then she froze. “Wait, but… Why are you telling us this? Why not Bobby? Why did they call Buck?”

“Bobby had a heart attack,” Eddie told them. May covered her mouth and let out a muffled cry. Harry wrapped his arms around her and pulled her close. “They got him back in the ambulance.”

“How bad is it?” Harry asked.

“Get dressed,” he said, rather than answering the question. May made another noise and Harry screwed his eyes shut, because they both understood what he wasn’t saying. “I’ll drive you both over there.”

May wiped at her face and nodded, turning to go into her room. Harry silently, and a bit robotically, went to the couch and sat down. He grabbed the pillow he’d used the night before, clutching it to his chest.

“I don’t have anything to change into,” he quietly told Eddie. “I didn’t- I mean, I was hanging out with May, we came over here. But I didn’t mean to spend the night, I was just avoiding the house. I didn’t bring anything to change into. I don’t even have my phone charger.”

“That’s okay,” Eddie told him, crouching down in front of him and clasping his shoulder. It was easy to forget sometimes, with how tall he’d gotten, that Harry was barely any older than Chris. He was definitely looking his age now, though. “We’ll figure something out, don’t worry about that right now.”

“How bad was it?” Harry asked. “The fire, I mean. Is it all…?”

“I don’t know.”

“I grew up there,” he whispered.

Eddie didn’t say anything, not that the statement needed him to. He’d seen this happen to utter strangers, it was a regular part of the job, but never to anyone close like this. There wasn’t really much he could say to make this better, not when he was still wrapping his head around it himself.

His phone buzzed in his pocket with a text from Buck. The contents were not surprising, but welcomed nonetheless.

“Your mom’s awake,” he told Harry, who slumped over the pillow in relief. “We’ll go see her and Bobby as soon as May’s ready.”

“Y- Yeah, okay.”

With a stop for caffeine on the way, because Eddie knew he was going to need it.

Buck wasn’t really certain when, exactly, Athena woke up. It was sometime after Eddie left, that much he knew, while he was standing at the window and watching a machine help Bobby breathe. But because he couldn’t really say how long he’d been doing that, how long he’d been standing there, he didn’t know if minutes or hours passed between Eddie’s departure and Athena suddenly showing up.

She was dressed in a robe over a hospital gown, face drawn and exhausted. She barely reacted when Buck drew her in for a hug, his voice cracking on the word “Ma,” and slipped out of his arms to join him in watching Bobby.

“I don’t really know what happened,” Buck told her in a hush. “I- I mean, the doctor- Dr. Salazar, the same one I saw after… A- Anyway, she told me, but I don’t really know. You know?”

The silence was awkward, and it ached.

“They called me, said he had a heart attack,” he said. “I’m probably not- I mean, Eddie would be better- I’ll ask a nurse if they can page Dr. Salazar.”

“Thank you,” she whispered, lips barely moving.

“Right, yeah, of- of course.”

A quick stop at the nurse’s station, to inform them that Athena was awake and seeking to hear about her husband, and Dr. Salazar was on her way.

When Salazar arrived, she joined Athena and Buck at Bobby’s window. Buck stood there and listened to them talk, though the information the doctor gave Athena wasn’t much more than what she gave Buck and Eddie, nor was it very different. Cardiac arrest at the scene, resuscitated fourteen minutes later, won’t know the damage until he woke up. If he woke up.

It was all the same, if worded differently, until Dr. Salazar said,

“Sergeant Grant, I assume you’re aware of an advanced directive your husband has on file here at the hospital?”

“Yes,” Athena answered. “We both do.”

So did Buck and Eddie, ever since the lightning strike last year. With Buck in limbo, with everyone waiting to see if he would be able to breathe on his own again, Eddie had been in uncharted waters. They’d talked afterwards, one of the worst conversations that Buck ever had the displeasure of suffering through, to discuss what they each wanted if they were ever put into that situation again. But at least neither of them would be stuck trying to make those kinds of choices, trying to blindly guess what the other would want and constantly doubting themselves, on the worst days of their lives.

“So, we’re both aware of his wishes should this condition become prolonged,” Salazar said. Athena barely reacted; Buck had to bite his fist. “It’s not a discussion that we need to have right this minute, but if there are other family members that need to be informed, you might want to do that now.”

Athena just silently nodded, her eyes not leaving Bobby. Salazar gave them both a weak smile, before leaving once more.

“May and Harry are already on their way,” Buck told Athena. She cut a glance at him. More at his reflection in the glass than truly at him. “Eddie went to tell them, he’s bringing them.”

Again, a silent nod.

They arrived not long after, May and Harry bursting out of the elevator with a cry of,

“Mom!” 

Eddie followed behind them, considerably slower, with a tray of drinks in his hand. He and Buck watched as the two Grant kids clung to their mother, getting the first bit of actual reaction from the woman since she woke up. She wrapped them both up in her arms, as best as she could, and Buck could hear her whispering to them,

“I’m alright, everything’s alright. Everything’s gonna be alright.”

Eddie stopped a little bit away, and Buck went over to stand by him. He let out a pleased little hum when he was handed one of the drinks. A taste told him that it was made just the way he liked it, with far too much sugar.

“Any change?” Eddie asked.

Buck shook his head.

“Salazar was just here talking to Ma,” he told him. “Nothing different, not really. Uh, Bobby has a directive.”

“I’m not surprised,” Eddie commented. “How’s Athena?”

“Um.” Buck looked over his shoulder at her, allowing her children to cling to them like they were Gavin’s age again and not the adult and teenager that they were. “You know.”

“Yeah,” Eddie sighed. “I do.”

Chapter 27: An Athena Thing to Do

Chapter Summary

In which Buck and Eddie are both doing their best.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Buck was in that twisted, tortuous zone where tired and wired came together. He was too anxious, and the chairs too uncomfortable, to get anything better than a quick and unsatisfying catnap. Athena hadn’t spoken much since they sat down, choosing a chair at the end of the row that allowed her an unfettered view into Bobby’s room. Her children were flanking her, May in the seat beside her and Harry sitting on the floor, the pair of them curled around their mother as best as they could manage.

This was a very specific level of hell, he decided. He remembered those hours after Chimney’s car wreck, when they were all waiting for word from the hospital. The way he was practically clawing at the walls, and how incredibly thankful he was when a distraction - any distraction - came his way. But there was very little here to distract him from his current reality, and he was stuck switching between sitting in the chair, where he ended up constantly bouncing his legs, and pacing the floor like he was stuck to a track.

He was currently doing the latter when he spotted Athena’s captain, Elaine, entering the waiting room with them. She was in uniform, a duffel bag clutched in her hand. Athena didn’t acknowledge her presence until she was taking a seat in a nearby chair with a quiet,

“Hey. How are you doing?”

“They won’t let me in there yet,” Athena answered.

Visiting hours had not officially started, so they were stuck looking from the outside-in. Buck expected that, the moment she was able, Athena would end up doing the same thing everyone said Eddie had done: claim a spot at Bobby’s bedside and then refuse to leave until someone dragged her out. It would take a brave nurse to pull that with her, though. Buck wasn’t certain the hospital had anyone that could do it.

“I pulled this from your locker at the precinct,” Elaine said, gesturing down at the bag she placed on the floor at her feet. “There’s some street clothes, I didn’t think you’d want to sit here in a paper dress.”

“I appreciate that.”

“If there’s anything else you need, anything else any of you need-” Elaine looked at May and Harry in turn. “-just tell me.”

“Thank you.”

“Athena…” Elaine trailed off, and this time the way she looked at Harry and May, as well as Eddie and Buck, was rather pointed.

Eddie managed to figure out what she wasn’t asking before Buck did, getting out of his seat and crossing the short distance to where the Grants were all huddled together.

“Hey, how about we go find a vending machine?” He bent down enough to place his hands on both May and Harry’s shoulders. “Or maybe see if the hospital cafeteria is open.”

“What? No, I-” Harry started to protest, but then his sister, after a glance between their mother and Elaine, cut him off with,

“Yeah, sure. We should probably make sure Mom eats something, anyway.”

Harry was still not quite willing to leave, but he got up with some urging from May. The trio left down the hall, a whispered conversation starting up the moment they were a few feet away. Elaine turned her gaze on Buck, but he was perfectly willing to literally dig his claws into the floors and the walls before allowing anyone to force him away.

Eddie knew that, that was why he hadn’t even bothered trying.

“We need to talk about who may have done this,” Elaine said after a moment and sigh, obviously realizing it would be more effort than it was worth to get total privacy. “The arson inspector is saying the point of origin was near the bedroom.”

Buck briefly wondered if it was Jay Knight that was investigating the fire, the one who investigated all of Dalton Wolfe’s fires. Then the full meaning of what Elaine said struck him. This was an arson. One that someone started in their bedroom. Someone had intentionally done this, someone had tried to burn Bobby and Athena to death.

He sat down hard into the nearest chair.

“He was in the house,” he heard Athena say.

“Who was?” Elaine asked.

Athena turned her head to stare at her captain, seemingly surprised at the question. Buck got the impression that she hadn’t meant to say any of that aloud.

“Whoever did this,” she finally said after a beat of silence. “He had to know the layout of the place, right?”

“Do you have somebody specifically in mind?” Elaine asked.

Before Athena could answer, the door to Bobby’s room opened up and Dr. Salazar stepped out. The three of them leaped to their feet, Buck having to physically hold himself back from rushing the doctor.

“Sergeant Grant,” Salazar said. “You can go on in.”

“Thank you, doctor.” Athena turned back to Elaine. “No, I can’t think of anyone. But I’ll let you know if I do.”

Buck he watched Athena go into Bobby’s room. She barely looked in his direction as she went by, and she closed the door behind her.

This day already felt endless, and noon was still hours away yet. Eddie was fairly certain he was just running on fumes at this point, and far too much sugar and caffeine. Athena was in the room with Bobby when he came back with May and Harry and a small armload of snacks. They weren’t quite certain how many people were allowed in the room at a time right now, and as much as they would like to be in there, too, they all agreed that Athena deserved time alone with him.

At one point, Chief Simpson arrived in person. Eddie had uncharitable thoughts that he was worried about how this could somehow reflect poorly upon the department, but he quashed them as best he could. The chief spoke with Buck and Eddie both, and then May and Harry, before going to the nurse’s station to get a more official update on things.

“Oh, fuck, babe,” Buck suddenly said, looking at his watch. “My meds, I left them at home. Fuck.”

“I’ll go get them,” Eddie immediately offered.

If there was anything they didn’t want, it was Buck missing a dose of his bupropion. Not today, not in addition to all this. By itself, it would be nothing, but with Bobby unconscious in an ICU bed, it was the last thing they needed.

“I really, really love you,” Buck whispered, leaning against him. “So much. I don’t know what I’d do without you.”

“You won’t have to find out,” Eddie promised him, pressing a kiss to the spots above his eye.

He went to the elevator at the same time as the chief was leaving, which led to a short but awkward elevator ride together. If Buck was with them, there’d be an attempt at small talk or at least rambling to fill the silence. Instead, Eddie just tiredly rubbed at his face.

The doors opened up to the front lobby and they stepped out together. The chief headed for the exit, with a perfunctory “firefighter Diaz” said in passing. Eddie was about to go in the same direction as him, which might’ve been awkward, but he was stopped by a cry of,

“Eddie!”

That was Albert. He and Ravi, along with Hen and Chimney, were at the service desk until they started hurrying his way.

“Hen got a call from Ryerson at the One Thirty-three,” Chimney was saying in a rush. “They responded to a fire at Bobby and Athena’s house last night, he said that Bobby went into cardiac arrest at the scene.”

“Ay, Dios.” Eddie groaned, burying his face in his hands. “ I didn’t even- I’m sorry, I’m sorry, I didn’t even think to call anyone. It has been a day.”

“It has,” was Hen’s grim agreement. “When did you get here?”

“They called Buck at- Fuck, I don’t know what time,” he answered, dropping his hands. Hen was looking at him in concern. “Uh, Athena’s fine, some smoke inhalation, but she’s sitting with Bobby right now. Harry wasn’t at the house, he was with May. I went and brought them both over.”

“What about Bobby?” Albert asked. “How…?”

“It- It isn’t…” Eddie rubbed at his neck, trying to work a crick out of it. “His heart was stopped for fourteen minutes.”

Each of their faces reflected the same pained dismay that he felt when he first heard it.

“Like I said, they’re upstairs,” he told them. “I need to go, Buck left his meds at home.”

Eddie started to go but a hand on his arm stopped him.

“How long have you been awake, Eddie?” Hen asked him.

“I don’t know,” he admitted. “It was still dark when Buck got the call.”

There was a conversation happening in front of him, a silent one spoken only in expression, between Hen and Chimney. Usually, he’d make an attempt to try and figure out what they weren’t saying, but he just didn’t have the brain power to do that right now. Eventually, Chimney slapped him on the back and said,

“I’ll drive you.”

“I…” Eddie sighed, shoulders slumped in relief. “Thank you.”

“How much of a mess is Buck right now?” Chimney asked as he steered him towards the exit doors. Hen and the others went on to the elevators.

“I had to take the phone from him because he was having a panic attack.”

The other man winced as they stepped through the sliding doors.

“What about you?” was the next question. “How are you holding up?”

“I’m holding up,” was the answer.

“Kinda what I figured,” Chimney said, and then pointed. “I parked over there, it’s not too far.”

When Eddie left, Buck sat down in a chair and attempted to catch a few Z’s. May took the seat right next to him, and she curled into his side when he raised his arm to let her in. He must’ve managed at least a quick - if uneasy - catnap, because he was suddenly being startled awake by a hand on his knee.

Hen was kneeling in front of him, smiling kindly. On the other side of the room, he saw Albert and Ravi talking to Harry.

“Hey, Buckaroo.”

“Hey, Hen,” he said, rubbing at his eye, and then, “Oh, shit- I totally forgot to- Shit, I am so sorry.”

“It’s fine, we already went through that with Eddie. It sounds like it’s been a long morning for everyone.”

“Yeah, it- it really has,” Buck agreed with a huff of laughter. “Did, um. Did Eddie tell you about…?”

“Yeah, he did,” Hen said. “How are you doing?”

“I’m-” Buck glanced at May, who was shifting enough that he wasn’t certain how awake she was at the moment. “I’m, y’know. Hanging in there. Just waiting for Pops to wake up.”

He tried to put as much cheer into the words as he could manage, and hoped that it didn’t sound as fake as it all felt.

“I’m sure,” Hen said, clearly not believing him. “Which room is Bobby’s?”

“Right there.”

She nodded and stood up, heading for the room he pointed at. The blinds were partially drawn on the windows facing him, so he wasn’t able to actually see inside without getting closer. He watched Hen peek through the window of the door, and then hurry back over.

“I thought Eddie said Athena was sitting with him,” she said in a worried hush.

“She is.” Buck eased his arm from under May, doing his best not to disturb her, and got up to go look for himself. “She- She was.”

But Athena wasn’t in the room now. It was empty except for Bobby and a nurse that was charting his vitals.

“I thought… I thought she’d stay here until he was discharged,” he told Hen, not understanding. “She wouldn’t have just left.”

Except, apparently, yes she would.

Eddie got back from his errand with Chimney to find his husband once more on the verge of panic. He was trying to hide it from May and Harry, an endeavor assisted by the way Ravi and Albert were keeping them distracted, but Buck was rarely ever subtle. The gist of it, which Eddie gleaned from Hen, was that Athena was gone without a word or a trace.

Eddie couldn’t do anything about that, not at this moment. Athena’s sudden disappearance was worrying for him, too, but it was a drop in the bucket today. He could do something about Buck, though.

Buck didn’t fight it when Eddie took him by the wrist and gently pulled him into Bobby’s room. There was only the single chair, the one Athena pulled up to the bedside, and Eddie practically shoved Buck into it. Neither of them said anything, the only noises were the hauntingly, painfully familiar sounds of the monitor and the ventilator. Eddie had to grip Buck’s shoulder to remind himself that while this nightmare was rhyming, it was not the same one as a year ago.

“I- I don’t even know how to contact his brother,” Buck said, breaking the silence. “He would want to know, right? That something happened to his brother. He’d want to know.”

“Probably,” Eddie agreed. He didn’t honestly know, Bobby didn’t really talk about his family in Minnesota all that often. They knew next to nothing about his father, other than him being a firefighter as well, and the only reason they knew the name of Bobby’s brother - Charlie - was because Buck asked him when he realized that he didn’t already know.

Hell, they spent several days in a row in St. Paul, and never once did the idea of visiting Bobby’s family - the living ones - ever come up.

Buck nodded to himself, throat bobbing as he swallowed.

“H- Hey, Pops.” He hesitantly reached over and took Bobby’s limp hand in his own. “I, uh. I don’t know if you’re dreaming like I did. If you are, I hope you’re having a better one. Or- Or maybe not. A good dream, but not- not too good of a dream, okay? I don’t want you deciding that you don’t want to wake up.”

His voice broke a little at the end, and Eddie gave his shoulder a comforting squeeze. Buck’s other hand reached up to cover the one Eddie had on his shoulder.

“Maybe… Maybe I’m in your dream, helping you find whatever you need to wake up, like you and Ma were in mine,” he continued. “Um, Ma’s okay, by the way. I- I mean, I’m sure you knew that already. She was just in here, probably yelling at you to wake up. So you should listen to her, because you know how scary she gets if we don’t.”

“Very scary,” Eddie agreed.

“Yeah, see? Even Eddie knows.” Buck pointed at him, like Bobby was awake to see it. “She… She wouldn’t happen to have told you where she went, did she? ‘Cause she just kinda left, and I don’t know where she went, and I really, really…”

Buck drew in a breath and let it out, his shoulder shaking with it beneath Eddie’s hand.

“I haven’t told you about that documentary I watched, did I?” he asked. “The title was what got me, How to Rob a Bank, and I remembered that time we got framed, right? It’s about some guy in the nineties…”

Eddie stepped away, fully content to let Buck monopolize all the speaking. He listened to his husband fill the unconscious Bobby in on all the details, finding a piece of the wall to lean against as he did. He stuffed his hands into the pocket of his jeans, where he found what his abuela had pressed into his hands before he left the house for the second time.

It dangled from his fingers when he pulled it out, its shape familiar yet foreign at the same time.

Eddie did not have his own rosary, hadn’t since he was a kid still going to church with his folks. This one was a spare of Abuela’s. She’d voiced concern about what had happened to Bobby’s when Eddie told her about the fire, clinging to a small detail just like they all were doing to prevent the horror-shock of it all from overwhelming them.

It might’ve been ash, or it might’ve been tucked away in Bobby’s truck. Eddie wasn’t certain.

He remembered, during that long drawn out time of torment when it was Buck they were keeping vigil over, how Bobby had sat down beside him and quietly began to pray. Eddie’s relationship with God and religion and the church was shaky on the best of days, but he knew how important it all was to Bobby.

There wasn’t anyone here doing it for Bobby, he suddenly realized. Abuela no doubt was, maybe Pepa if she’d spread the news, but while Bobby would sit by their bedsides and pray for their wellbeing, there was no one here to do the same for him.

A small detail, easy to cling to.

It had been decades since Eddie had spoken the words. As he rubbed his thumb against the crucifix on the end, though, they came to him in a rush with the phantom smell of incense and the feel of sweat slipping down his back in the sweltering Texas heat.

“I believe in God,” he whispered, closing his eyes. The words tasted like a lie on his tongue, but he spoke them anyway. “The Father almighty, creator of heaven and earth…”

The good news: Athena was not gone entirely without a trace.

The bad news: Athena was not much better than her husband, or Buck, when it came to certain things.

In any other circumstances, Buck would happily tease her for it.

“Alright, run this by us.” Eddie rubbed his eyes with one hand, and his other was shoved into his pants pocket. He and Buck had switched places with May and Harry, the Grant kids taking their deserved turn at Bobby’s bedside. “What do you think’s going on?”

“Maddie called,” Chimney said. Buck mentally kicked himself, because he really had forgotten to tell literally anyone about what was going on, hadn’t he? “She said she got a weird call from Athena.”

“Weird how?” Buck asked.

“She asked for an address, someone named Amir,” Albert said. “Apparently he worked in the burn unit here?”

“Maddie met him when she came to check on that car crash victim,” Ravi added. “The one whose kid was kidnapped.”

Buck nodded, remembering.

“But the thing is,” Chimney said. “This Amir guy, he’s from Minnesota. And Maddie said he was covered in burn scars.”

“Wait.” Eddie looked between them all. “You’re not saying that this Amir guy…?”

“It certainly seems like it.”

“God.” Buck shook his head, and then he remembered something. “She- She said something to Elaine, when she brought her bag from her locker. When Elaine was talking about this, asking if Ma knew anyone who would want to hurt them. She said he was in the house. I don’t think she meant to say it, ‘cause she played it off afterwards.”

“It would be a Bobby thing to do, inviting him over to try and make amends, “ Eddie said. “No wonder he was acting so weird the other day.”

“Okay, so. We think Ma’s gone after this guy on her own?”

“If she thinks that he’s responsible for what happened to Bobby?” Hen’s face was grim. “That would be a very Athena thing to do.”

“She ran into a burning building once, remember?” Ravi added. “When that sniper guy was holding Bobby hostage.”

And then she killed him, which Buck figured was just good riddance. It probably meant he was an awful person, but the guy was responsible for one of his husband’s near-death experiences.

“She’s in no state of mind to confront him right now,” Chimney said.

Hen nodded in agreement.

“I’m gonna call her captain,” she said, digging her phone out of her pants. “Maybe suggest that she send a black-and-white to this guy’s place, make sure everything’s okay.”

She got as far as waking up her phone before there was a commotion in Bobby’s room. One of the machines started a shrilling tone, and a nurse physically forced both May and Harry out the door as others swarmed the bed. Buck couldn’t see much of anything with all the bodies blocking his view.

“What’s happening?” Buck demanded, rushing over. The only ones who paid him any mind were May and Harry.

“I- I don’t know,” May said. “We were coming over to ask if you’d heard from Mom when the machine started going off.”

They weren’t even allowed to watch it from afar, because the blinds were drawn completely, cutting the view off. The machine was no longer screaming, but that could mean anything. Dr. Salazar came bursting through a set of doors, rushing past their group on her way to Bobby’s room.

Buck was stuck in an endless moment, it felt like. An eternity spent looking at the door, unable to tear his eyes away. Countless possibilities flew through his mind, each worse than the last. He saw visions of a future where he was dragging out the Class A again, this time for a funeral much like Jones’.

He wouldn’t get the full honors, no matter how much he deserved them. No bagpipes, no use of apparatus, no procession from City Hall. All of that was reserved for Line-of-Duties. Not for this, not for people who di-

“Buck.” Eddie’s voice was right in his ear. “Mi sol, you need to breathe.”

He didn’t breathe so much as gulp the air, like he was back in the academy and just finished his first tower run. As he watched, nurses started leaving Bobby’s room, but they shut the door behind them every time and the blinds remained down. And then, finally-

“He can have visitors again,” Dr. Salazar said, stepping out.

Buck was fairly certain he didn’t actually cross the distance, not with how fast he got there. He nearly fell on his face, tripping over his feet as he rounded the corner. Bobby was there on the bed, eyes open and smiling his way.

“Pops!” Buck shouted, rushing over. The others were not far behind him, taking places around his bed.

Bobby looked at each of them, a confused frown growing on his face.

“Where’s…” His voice was hoarse from the breathing tube, and he stopped to clear his throat. “Where’s Athena?”

“She’s out… Being Athena,” Hen answered. “But she’s okay.”

“I’ll get her here,” Chimney said, already putting his phone to his ear as he stepped out.

There was only the one chair still, but Buck forwent it in favor of sitting on the edge of the bed. He listened as Hen caught Bobby up on everything. More importantly, he listened to Bobby respond and ask questions.

And when Athena arrived, escorted by a uniformed officer, none of them got in her way.

Chapter End Notes

This chapter was not supposed to be this long, but I realized the one after it was going to be either far too short or transitionally clunky, so I combined them.
And I am pretty sure the next chapter is going to be the last, which means I need to decide how much I want to screw my future self over by changing the details of a certain cliffhanger.

Chapter 28: With the Weight of Eternity

Chapter Summary

In which the author throws out canon in such a way that will surely cause them much pain later.

Miranda Williams was not well-pleased. She was the opposite of that, in fact. One might even describe her as pissed, and yet that still wouldn’t quite encapsulate the depths of her current rage.

She stormed off the elevator and down the hall, throwing open the door in front of her without any care of who might have been on the other side. There wasn’t anyone waiting to meet with her target, the chairs empty. Only the secretary, a wan little man with a widow’s peak, was there, staring at her in wide-eyed surprise. She ignored him, continuing her march to the other door, the one that led to the actual office.

The secretary tried to stop her, only to have the door slammed closed in his face.

“Battalion Chief.” Chief Simpson set down the pen he was using to fill out something official looking, no doubt an edict on how they could only use the sirens during business hours or something equally as dumb, since he was apparently set on being a dunderheaded fool. “How can I help you?”

“Are you actually this stupid?” Miranda barked, and was still too full of rage to appreciate the way he looked taken aback. “Or are you purposefully trying to screw this department over?”

Simpson sighed, which only served to make her angrier, and asked,

“Do I get to know the reason for this delightful visit?”

“Vincent Gerrard!” she snapped. “You are seriously going to put that dinosaur back as the One Eighteen’s captain?”

“With Captain Nash’s retirement, there is a vacancy,” the chief said. “And there is a concern about a leadership deficit at the One Eighteen.”

“A leadership- Who the hell, Chief? What dumbass are you listening to?”

“The ‘dumbass,’ as you so politely called her, is on the city council.”

Oh. Oh. Now this made some sort of sense. It also just got dumber.

“You’re listening to Councilwoman Ortiz?” Miranda scoffed. “You’re helping her carry out her petty little vendetta against Wilson? Her son was high and drunk, nearly killed at least two people, and then died of a brain bleed after refusing care. And what are you hoping to get out of it, huh?”

He opened his mouth, either to answer or to rebuke her statement, but she held up a hand to shut him up.

“No, no, wait, lemme guess,” she said. “The city council’s about to approve the budget for the next fiscal year, and you want a piece of that sweet, sweet police money pie for the department. Am I close?”

“I would assume that you, of all people, would appreciate an increase in the department budget,” Simpson told her, seemingly unbothered to be throwing an entire station under the bus. “We could get a lot of updated equipment with that.”

“Yeah, we’ve all figured out that you’re more of a politician than a firefighter these days, Chief,” Miranda said. “Fine, help the councilwoman kick one of ours while she’s down, clearly you don’t care. But Gerrard, really?”

“Councilwoman Ortiz suggested-”

“Fuck the bitch!” Miranda snapped. “The increased budget doesn’t matter if we lose it all in a lawsuit, you idiot!”

The chief reeled back, and then stood with a glare.

“All jokes aside, you are toeing the line of insubordination, Williams,” he told her.

“I don’t care,” Miranda shot back. “The only reason we didn’t have to shutter an entire station or three that last time was because Evan Diaz didn’t want the money. We won’t be so lucky this time.”

“Evan Diaz signed waivers that-”

“That he can’t sue us for a workplace accident,” she finished for him. “Not for discrimination. And it won’t just be him. There’ll be his husband, too! And who knows how many people will join in once they start, because after Gerrard was given the boot, we made that station into the one we could point to as a shining example of how progressive this department is. Hell, we’re pimping out the Diazes for PR.”

“And why,” Simpson said, “do you think we need to worry about litigation?”

“Of course you wouldn’t know, you wouldn’t need to know.” Miranda couldn’t quite keep the derision out of her voice. “Thanks to the fact that, back in the day, no one could even comprehend the idea that there’d be women firefighters or - gasp - men who were openly interested in other men, the official department policy on relationships between firefighters is that we don’t have one. We leave it up to the individual captain. If they’re fine letting two of the people under their command work together while dating, or while married, then so be it.”

She watched, pleased, as Simpson’s eyes went wide with realization.

“Gerrard is a racist, sexist, homophobic dinosaur. He is a relic, a reminder of all the worst parts of this department’s past,” Miranda said. “Do you think he’s going to be fine with it? Ravi Panikkar and Albert Han, Evan and Edmundo Diaz, two homosexual relationships in the same station - three of them people of color - that have caused no problems since they were made official years ago. Hell, the department had no problem with the Diazes last summer when one of them was acting captain.

“How much of a shit storm will it be when the Diazes go onto their department approved social media to tell all their fans about how, since the department reinstated a captain that already got the boot for being a bigot once, they can’t work together anymore? Or just start posting videos and stories of Vincent Gerrard’s greatest hits? How big of a field day do you think the media will have when it comes out we’re being sued for discrimination by a group of people you personally awarded Medals of Valor to?”

“And what would you have me do, Battalion Chief?” Simpson asked her. “The city council is in charge of this department’s budget, and Councilwoman Ortiz is a mother in mourning, stuck on the anger stage of grief. It would not take much for her to convince her fellows that we don’t even need the budget we got last year, that the money would do more good with the police. We’ll avoid paying out lawsuits and still have to shutter a station.”

“If you’re too busy quaking in your boots to defend one of ours, fine,” Miranda told him. “I don’t know why Ortiz would want Gerrard to be the captain, whether it's another way to hurt Wilson or because she owes him something or what. I don’t particularly care, either. But there is not a leadership deficit at that station, Nash himself saw to that.”

The office fell into silence, the pair of them glaring at each other. Miranda watched the chief, her boss’ boss, work his jaw, but the man didn’t seem to have any reply to her argument. So, with a nod, she turned on her heel and left the office in much less of a storm than when she entered it.

She was halfway through the outer chamber when the secretary’s phone buzzed and she heard the chief’s voice come through the speaker.

“Look up Evan Buckley Diaz’s phone number,” Simpson said. “And ask him to come in today.”

Miranda smiled, very pleased with herself as she strode toward the elevator.

It had been nearly two weeks, now, since the Grant-Nash home burnt down and Bobby had his heart attack. At first, Bobby had been transferred out of the ICU to the cardiac care unit, but he was soon discharged. There was a flurry in the time since, everyone coming together to get the three of them - Athena, Bobby, and Harry - everything they needed that they no longer had. Michael and David helped with that, sending Harry the clothes that hadn’t made the journey already.

Currently, Bobby and Athena were staying at a hotel, but were looking for a cheap apartment to rent until they found someplace more permanent. Harry was couch surfing, primarily with his sister but he’d stayed at Buck and Eddie’s a few times. In fact, when Buck left the house today, the teenager was sprawled out asleep on their couch, Senorita curled up on top of his head and Gavin slapping at his arm in an attempt to rouse a playmate.

In the calm after the storm, Buck had more brainpower to spare for everything else that was going on. And what was going on was not much better than Bobby’s near death. Apparently, not only had Councilwoman Ortiz put a worm in the judge’s ear to block the Wilsons from adopting Mara, she also spoke to someone at the Department of Child and Family Services. The Wilsons had their license to foster revoked, and Mara was taken away.

Hen, who was acting captain at the 118 until Bobby’s return, reported that Mara was once more in a group home, backsliding into the closed off little girl that she had been when the Wilsons first took her in. Maddie explained to Buck that she and Chimney were petitioning DCFS to be emergency certified, in the hopes that they could take Mara in themselves, but that would be a temporary thing. A tourniquet to a bleeding wound, not something that would stop it entirely.

There was little Buck could do, however much he wished otherwise. But he knew someone that could wield just as much influence as the councilwoman, if not more. The problem was going to lie in convincing her to help. It definitely wouldn’t be for the sake of friendship, not with how much said friendship had deteriorated at this point.

He’d asked her to meet him in a cafe downtown. She wasn’t there when he arrived, and he hoped that just meant he showed up first and not that she wasn’t going to-

“Hello, Buck.” Taylor Kelly’s voice had him looking up from his phone. She pulled out the chair across from him, her smile showing far too many teeth. “Long time, no see.”

“Hi, Taylor,” he said, setting his phone down beside his forgotten menu. “Thanks for coming.”

“Well you said it was your treat,” she pointed out. “How could I say no to that? Hell, how could I say no to you coming back to grovel after all the lovely things you said to me last time we spoke.”

Buck let out a sigh. The last time they’d spoken was right after he’d read that awful book of hers, the one where she laid out far too many secrets and far too many of her opinions, making very little effort to hide the identities of him and his family as she did. It was the reason he and Eddie had become inundated with fans, and why they currently had a LAFD approved social media account.

“You couldn’t be too upset about it, still,” he idly commented. “I saw that you followed me and Eddie.”

“I also did a fun little story about the firefighting husbands and how they went viral,” Taylor said. “Why am I here, Buck?”

“Besides the wine menu?” he joked. She scowled at him, even as she picked it up to look through it herself. “What do you know about Olivia Ortiz?”

“The councilwoman?” Taylor asked. He nodded. “She’s running for mayor. The One Eighteen responded to a traffic accident involving her son - I didn’t do the story on that, don’t worry - where he died at the scene. Medical findings were that he was on drugs and very drunk. What about her?”

“What if I gave you the opportunity to ruin her political career?”

Her eyes narrowed at him, but he spotted the spark of interest that flared to life within them.

“We’re going to order food and wine,” she said, waving down a waiter. “And then you’re going to explain.”

“My treat,” he promised her.

After the waiter came and took their orders, Buck gave Taylor the rundown. He explained to her what happened with Ortiz’s son, the investigation into Hen that followed his death, the fact that she was found to have been of no fault. He told her about Mara, the troubled little girl that the Wilsons took in. And then he told her how the two disparate stories came together, the way Ortiz was waging a vendetta against Hen and hurting Mara in the process.

It was a long enough explanation that the food arrived in the middle of it.

“All of this,” Taylor said, wine glass in hand, “sounds like you’re asking me for a favor, Buck.”

“Maybe I am,” he admitted. “But come on, are you going to tell me that you aren’t itching to jump into it? She’s a councilwoman who’s running for mayor, and she’s using her office to hurt a woman that just won a Medal of Valor for saving people and her literal rocket scientist of a wife. And a traumatized little girl. C’mon, Tay, we both know you want to be the one to run this story. Before someone else jumps on it.”

Taylor sat her glass down with a huff.

“I hate that you know me so well,” she complained. Buck gave her his brightest smile. “Fine. But I want an interview with them both, Hen and her wife. They have a son, right? I want him on camera, too. The whole little family, preferably while crying.”

“I can’t make any promises about Denny or the crying,” Buck told her. 

“Good enough, I’m sure I can talk them into it,” she said.

Buck’s phone began to vibrate against the table. He would ignore it, except the caller ID was saying it was someone from the department. Curious, and a little bit anxious, he answered it with a,

“Go for Buck.”

“Firefighter Evan Diaz?” a nasally sounding man asked.

“This is him,” he said, shrugging at Taylor’s questioning frown.

“Chief Simpson would like for you to come by his office at your earliest convenience.”

“Uh, okay. I’m already downtown,” Buck said, waving down the waiter for the check. He assumed he was right in translating “at your earliest convenience” into “as soon as you physically can.” “I can be there in a bit.”

“Perfect. Thank you for your understanding.”

The line went silent, his screen showing that the call ended.

“What’s that about?” Taylor asked.

“The chief wants to talk to me,” Buck answered. “And no, I don’t know why.”

“Maybe he’s giving you another award.”

“Doubtful.”

Buck arrived at the chief’s office, in the same building as dispatch, a little bit curious and a lot anxious. He left it fuming. Not at the chief, not entirely. Oh, Buck definitely held anger against the man, what with the way he was practically throwing Hen under the bus. He could imagine the driving force behind this decision, Councilwoman Ortiz’s fingerprints were all over it, and it made him sick that even the fire chief was bending under her influence.

But what truly had Buck angry was why she was able to do it at all. What truly made him angry was what the chief had revealed to him during their talk. At any other time, this news would have him proud and excited and wanting to share the news with everyone he knew. But now it just felt dirty and undeserved.

The hotel that Bobby and Athena were staying at was somewhere in the middle tier of quality when it came to Los Angeles hotels. It wasn’t the best, it wasn’t the worst. The two of them were still waiting for the insurance payout, so it wasn’t too surprising. Buck parked the jeep haphazardly and went inside, bypassing the check-in counter and jogging up the stairs. 

He knew which door was theirs, and so he didn’t hesitate to start banging on it.

“What- Buck?” Athena looked surprised to see him. Even more surprised when he stormed past her.

“You left!” he yelled at Bobby, who was sitting on the hotel bed.

“Buck.” The captain- No, the former captain stood up. “What do you-”

“I just had a talk with the chief,” Buck told him. “About how he wants me to be the captain at the One Eighteen. Because you left! You quit!”

“Buck, I…” Bobby glanced over his shoulder, no doubt to his wife for support. But Athena didn’t say anything as she silently closed the door. “I know that this came as a surprise, but I promise you, I’m intending to come back now.”

“Does the chief know that?” Buck snapped. “No, that doesn’t matter. You left, Bobby. You- You-” Buck shook his head, too many words caught in his throat. “We all knew you were acting weird. I didn’t know it was because you were telling us all goodbye. Except that you didn’t, because you didn’t have the fucking balls to say the words.”

“Buck.” Athena’s tone was reproachful. Probably for his language, but he was too angry to care right then.

“He wants you to be captain?” Bobby sounded proud, and it made Buck want to tear his hair out. “I told you that-”

“Were you ever going to tell us - tell me! - about this?” Buck snapped, interrupting the attempt at praise. “Or were you okay with us finding out when we got to the station one day to find someone else there? Did you even care?”

“Of course I care,” Bobby said, quiet and pained.

“You left.”

Bobby didn’t have anything to say to that, it seemed. Buck had run out of words, too, however much he wanted to keep screaming and shouting. It felt a little bit like he was back in the hospital, on the cusp of his family falling apart before his very eyes. Except that it already had, hadn’t it?

Bobby had gone around saying goodbye. But the one he’d given to Buck sounded so very final in hindsight, and so too was the way he gave Eddie his book of prayers. Goodbyes that, with the way their lives were so intertwined outside of the station, were completely unnecessary.

Not unless Bobby was planning on leaving, not just retiring.

Buck swallowed back the scream that bubbled in his throat, shook his head at Bobby, and walked out of their hotel room.

Buck knocked on the door, opening it when Chris shouted for him to come in. He poked his head through, spotting his oldest at his desk.

“Hey, kiddo.”

“Hey, Pops.” Chris smiled at him. “What’s up?”

“Um, I just figured I should give you a heads up,” Buck said. “There might be, hm. Upset shouting in a bit.”

His son gave him a flat stare.

“What did you do?”

“Rude, but fair,” Buck admitted. “I invited your aunts Hen and Karen over, partially so I can give Hen some bad news. That’s one thing. The other thing is that I invited someone else over, and your dad… I don’t think he’ll divorce me for it, but he might buy us whale watching tickets.”

Christopher looked confused by the statement, but he was used enough to his dads’ general weirdness that he didn’t question it.

“I’ll take care of Gavin,” he said instead. The little tot still wasn’t all that fond of voices raised in anger. Neither was Buck, to be honest.

“You are the best kid ever,” Buck gushed, and got an eye roll in response.

There was time, yet, before the guests were scheduled to arrive. Buck sat down on the couch, taking up his discarded needles and half-knitted scarf. He was slowly getting better, mostly in spurts and fits. At least it actually looked like a scarf and not a mess of yarn.

Eddie, unaware of how his day was about to be ruined, joined him shortly after. It was a peaceful period of time where they watched Gavin and Hoover play tug-o-war, the dog holding a piece of rope in his mouth while Gavin - giggling and squealing the entire time - did his best to yank it away. Hoover wasn’t even doing anything, he was literally just holding it.

A light knocking popped the bubble, and Buck withheld the sigh as Eddie got up to answer the door. Only to immediately slam it shut and, without a word, retake his spot on the couch. He picked up the remote and started flipping through the channels, as if the entire event hadn’t happened.

“Babe.” Buck let the sigh out. “Really?”

“What?” His husband’s attempt to look innocent was admirable.

Buck let out another sigh, put away the yarn and needles, and got up. He could feel Eddie’s disapproval radiating from him as he, too, stood up again.

“Seriously, mi sol!? Why? I thought you loved me!”

“I do,” Buck told him, opening the door. Taylor looked more amused than anything, smirking at Eddie as she came inside, adjusting the strap of her purse on her shoulder. “This is for Hen.”

“This is a vieja! I thought she went back to Hell!”

“Nice seeing you, too, Eddie,” Taylor remarked. Her smile at his upset fell away when Hoover trotted over to greet her, giving her far more of a welcome than his owners, much to her dismay. 

Eddie grabbed one of Abuela’s rosaries off the table by the door, thrusting it at the reporter.

“The power of Christ compels you, demon!”

“Has the angry shouting started?” Chris asked, coming into the room. He sounded disappointed, like he’d been hoping for more. “C’mon, Gav, we’ll play in my room.”

Gavin got up and happily ran to his brother, chattering away as he followed Chris back to his room. Which was good, because Buck did not expect Hen’s reaction to Taylor’s presence to be much better than Eddie’s, especially coming right on the heels of what else Buck had to tell her. 

With a lot of grumbling and complaining, Eddie led Taylor to the back patio, Hoover happily following the pair. Just in time, too, because there was soon more knocking almost as soon as they were out of sight. This time it was Hen and Karen on the porch, and he let them.

“Whose car is that out front?” Hen asked.

“Uh, it’s… It’s someone that I really think can help you both,” he told them. “But um. But first I need to tell you something, and I know you’re going to be pissed off, and I’m really, really sorry for it.”

Hen and Karen shared a look.

“What is it?” Hen cautiously asked.

“Pops retired,” he blurted.

“What!?” they both exclaimed.

“Because of the heart attack?” Hen asked.

“No, um. He retired before the fire,” Buck said. “Remember when he was acting weird? It was because of that.”

“Oh,” Karen said, and then, “Who’s replacing him, then? Wait, is it Hen?”

She was getting understandably excited at the prospect. But her wife, who remembered what Buck had said before the news, was already looking quietly crushed.

“It’s not me,” she said. “Is it?”

Buck shook his head.

“Why?” Karen demanded, more upset than Hen. “She’s been acting captain since the fire. Why not her?”

“Councilwoman Ortiz,” Hen guessed. “Please tell me it’s going to be you, instead.”

“Yeah,” Buck admitted, thankful she wasn’t reacting as he feared. “It’s how I found out about Pops, because he certainly wasn’t gonna bother telling me.”

The Wilsons both raised their eyebrows at the anger that seeped into his tone.

“It’s not official yet, there’s, y’know. Paperwork or whatever. I’m going to be Interim Captain Diaz until then,” Buck explained. “But c’mon, Tay- Uh, the person I want you to talk to is out back.”

The two followed him to the backyard, where Eddie was standing as far away from where Taylor sat as possible, arms crossed and scowling. Hoover, meanwhile, had his head on her leg and was happily drooling all over her no doubt expensive pants.

“Taylor Kelly, seriously!?” Hen demanded, coming to a stop. There’s the angry shouting. “Seriously, Buck? Her? Why!?”

“That’s exactly what I asked,” Eddie said, raising his voice a little to be heard. Because he really was at a ridiculous distance from them.

“Because I think she can help,” Buck answered with a shrug.

“Now, now, Henrietta,” Taylor said, smiling at the glare the paramedic gave her. She patted the arm of the chair beside her. “We can help each other. You’ll get your girl back, and I’ll get a nice juicy story to my name.”

Hen and Karen shared a look, an unspoken exchange, and then Hen heaved out a sigh. She grabbed a pair of chairs, positioning them to be as far from Taylor Kelly as possible, before she and her wife sat down.

“Fine,” Hen spat. “For Mara.”

“Perfect!” Taylor reached into her purse and pulled out a tape recorder, turning it on and setting it on the table between them. “Alright, Mrs. and Mrs. Wilson, tell me about Councilwoman Ortiz.”

 

Fin.
